Loop Chronicles Book 5 · A novel that begins at Ezekiel 37:3

Johan Choi

The Dry Valley, Part One

The People Who Do Not Disappear

Chapter 1. The Day, Seven Years Ago

Outside, people die.

I knew that from a very young age. In our settlement, one or two people went missing every winter. When an old person went missing, it was sad but not surprising. When a child went missing, it was surprising and sad. But both happened.

The adults never hid it. They couldn't have. The houses stood so close together that everyone heard whoever was crying at night.

I still don't think that was a bad way to raise a child. I had already learned the word "die" before I was ten. So seven years later, when I first heard the word "preservation," I understood exactly what it promised.

I was deceived precisely because I understood it exactly.

Our settlement had several words for death. Went. Left. Stopped. The adults used them differently depending on the situation. If someone was sick a long time before dying, they said "stopped." If it was sudden, they said "went."

I understood that distinction from a young age. I judged, from which word I heard, whether I should go to that house or not. For a "went," I went that day. For a "stopped," I went a few days later. "Stopped" meant everyone there had already been crying a long time.

Later, in this city, I would see that none of those words is used. Not went, not left, not stopped. With no use for them, the words disappeared too. I would spend a long time looking closely at what moved in to take the place where the words used to be.

There were four of us at home.

My father went missing when I was five. I won't write that story at length here. My mother raised us alone after that. I was the elder brother, and I had one younger brother.

My brother was eight years younger than me. A late child. He was born when I was eight, so I remember the day he was born. His face was red and wrinkled, and my mother laughed, looking at him.

My brother followed me around closely. So closely it was sometimes a nuisance. Wherever I went, he came along. When I went to draw water, when I went to gather wood, when I played with my friends.

There was a time I got angry at him.

"…Don't follow me."

My brother stopped where he was. And he didn't follow. I felt some relief that day, and some guilt by evening. But I didn't apologize. An eight-years-older brother doesn't do that.

I have replayed that scene hundreds of times over seven years. Not because I regret not apologizing. It's that I remember, too exactly, the spot where my brother stopped that day, and that exactness frightens me. The path from our house to the well, in front of the third stone wall. I still know that spot exactly.

My brother was afraid of water.

He was afraid of the sound of the well bucket going down. The rope unspooling makes a long sound, and at the end there's a splash, and the space between them was, for my brother, apparently too long.

I knew this, and I deliberately let the rope out slowly. It was a mean thing to do.

My mother saw it once and slapped my back.

"…Why make something he's afraid of even more frightening?"

"…He'll get used to it soon enough."

"…Is getting used to something the same as not being afraid of it?"

I let that question pass over me at the time. I thought of it again after coming to this city. The people of this city are afraid of nothing. But that isn't because nothing frightens them — it's because there is no longer anything to be afraid of. My mother's question, unchanged, becomes a question about this city.

Seven years ago, the year I was nine, it happened.

A sickness went around the settlement. First it was a cough. Then it was fever. And then people lay down.

The adults gathered and talked for a long time. I never heard the whole of it. They told the children to go outside.

That was when people came from the city.

I want to note first that those people were not bad people. That's the part of this record I most want to be careful with. They didn't come to deceive us. They came to truly help, and they truly did help.

They explained something called preservation. If a sick person was preserved, they said, that person would not get any worse. Not sick anymore, and not gone.

"…Does it cure them?" someone asked.

"…No," the person said. "It doesn't cure. It stops."

I thought that answer was honest. I still think so. That person did not lie.

Nobody in this city lies. I would confirm that for myself, later. And I would learn that a person can end up like this even without a single lie.

That night the adults argued for a long time.

One side said they had to send them. The other side said they must not. Someone from the side against it said this:

"…Stopping isn't the same as living."

Someone from the side for it answered.

"…And is dying living?"

I heard that from outside the door. I was nine, so I only half understood what it meant.

I have carried that half-understood exchange for seven years. And coming to this city, I learn that both sentences are true. Both are true, and they cannot stand together. This book is written in the space between those two sentences.

My mother lay down first, and then my brother lay down.

I did not lie down.

I still don't know why I was the only one who wasn't infected. I've asked. No one could answer. They just said it happens.

My mother took to her bed on the third day. My brother, on the sixth.

In between, I drew water. Four times a day. There was nothing else I could do.

My brother lay there and looked at me.

"…Brother."

"…Yes."

"…I hear the water."

I was holding the bucket rope. I had forgotten he was afraid of that sound.

"…Scared?"

My brother shook his head.

"…I like it."

I didn't understand that answer at the time. I didn't know why a sound he'd always feared had suddenly become something he liked.

Now I know. That sound meant I was there.

The day of departure was set. The people from the city would take the sick with them. Eleven left the settlement. Two left from our house.

I did not go. I wasn't sick.

The morning they left, my mother called me.

"…Anemos."

"…Yes."

"…You stay here."

"…No."

"…Anemos."

My mother said my name twice. Our mother said your name twice when she was angry. But that day, she wasn't angry.

I can still play that voice back exactly. It wasn't a voice of anger — it was a voice asking something of me. I didn't hear that then. I didn't hear it until I was sixteen.

"…That's a place for sick people," my mother said. "You're not sick."

"…Then I'll come later."

My mother laughed at that.

"…All right. Come later."

I was outside the village that day.

I went out because I didn't want to watch them leave. It was the greatest resistance a nine-year-old could make. I sat behind the hill until the sun went down.

When I came back, everyone was gone.

Inside the house, things had been put in order. My mother must have tidied up despite being sick. The blankets were folded, and a jar of water sat drawn in the kitchen.

I looked at that water jar for a long time.

My mother had drawn the water before she left. It was the water I was to drink that evening.

I didn't drink that water for three days. If I drank it, it would be gone. On the fourth day it went bad, and I threw it out. And that day, I cried for the first time.

Seven years later I would come into a city without water. I don't think that's a coincidence. It probably is a coincidence. It just never felt like one to me.

I regretted that choice for seven years. I could have looked at their faces one last time, and I didn't. I could have held their hands, and I didn't.

But writing this record now, I think differently. Sitting behind that hill that day wasn't resistance. I think I already knew, even then. That once those people left, there would be no going back to before. A nine-year-old doesn't know a thing like that in words. But he knows it in his body.

Seven years passed after that.

I stayed in the settlement. I grew up in a relative's house on my father's side, started working at twelve, and at fourteen learned to dig wells.

Outside, people kept dying. Over those seven years I saw seventeen funerals. I counted them. Counting became a habit.

I became someone who counts. That's why numbers appear so often in this record. While you're counting, you don't have to think.

Learning to dig wells at fourteen was an accident. There was an old man in the village who dug wells, and he needed a hand, so I went.

The old man hardly spoke. We dug together all day and he wouldn't say ten words. But there was exactly one thing he said, over and over.

"…You don't find water."

"…Then what do you do?"

"…You make it a way. Water is everywhere already. It just has nowhere to go."

I took that as a technical remark. I thought he meant something about the layers of the earth.

I would learn, three years later, that it wasn't a technical remark at all. In a city without water, standing beside someone digging a well that nobody had asked him to dig.

The old man stopped the winter I turned fifteen. Since he'd been sick a long time, they said he'd "stopped."

I went to that funeral too. The ninth of the seventeen.

Word came from the city a few times during those years.

The preservation had succeeded, they said. Nine of the eleven had been preserved. What became of the other two was not written down.

I would learn later where those two had gone. I'll write that in Part Two.

My mother and my brother were among the nine.

The notice read like this:

The subject has been stably preserved, and a visit may be applied for at any time.

I looked at that sentence for a long time.

At any time. I thought that was a kind thing to say. It really was a kind thing to say. This city truly does let you visit at any time.

But the sentence also meant something else. It meant there was no urgency. Since the other side would not change, there was no reason to hurry.

I did not hurry for seven years. And during those seven years, I was the only one who grew older.

Once I took the notice to my uncle.

"…Can I go now, with this?"

"…You can."

"…Then why haven't you gone, all this time?"

My uncle said nothing for a long while. Then he said this.

"…If you go, you have to see it."

I didn't understand what he meant, and I asked again. My uncle didn't answer.

Seven years later I would give that same answer myself, out of my own mouth. On the day I came out from a visit, turning away from the counter after going to apply for a third visit.

If you go, you have to see it. That's all there is to it.

In the spring of the year I turned sixteen, I packed my things.

My uncle asked why now.

I hadn't prepared an answer. I just said this.

"…I think I can go now."

My uncle looked at me for a long while.

"…What are you going to do there?"

"…Bring them back."

My uncle said nothing to that. And he gave me a water flask. Something given to people from outside when they travel far.

"…I hear there's no water there," my uncle said.

I didn't know what that meant. How could a city without water exist.

I would learn what those words meant on my first day in the city. And I didn't know then, either, that this flask would become the thing I carried to the very end of this story.

On the road, I put my prepared words in order.

I had prepared a great many words over seven years. Things to say to my mother, things to say to my brother. I had even fixed the order. First, greetings. Then, everything that had happened since. Then, the main thing.

The main thing was this.

Let's go together now.

I had prepared those five words since I was nine. I prepared them while digging wells, and I prepared them while watching funerals.

The road took four days.

On the third day I passed through another settlement. I asked the people there which way the Preserve was.

An old man pointed with his hand. Then he asked,

"…Who do you have there?"

"…My mother and my brother."

The old man nodded.

"…We have one too."

I asked, glad to hear it.

"…Do you go visit?"

The old man looked at me. I would remember that look many times, later.

"…I went, at first."

"…And now?"

"…I don't go."

I started to ask why, but I couldn't. It felt like something I shouldn't ask.

The old man spoke first.

"…They're exactly the same."

I heard those words on the road and didn't understand what they meant. I would understand four days later. And I would learn that the old man wasn't a bad person. He didn't stop going because he was tired of it. He stopped because he couldn't bear that he was the only one growing old, every time he went.

In the end, I never manage to say those words myself. To be precise, I do say them, but I learn that the words are of no use at all. It takes this whole book to understand why.

I'll set down one thing here, ahead of time. I thought I was going to that city to wake my family. I was wrong. I was going back to the time when I was not alone.

That those two things are different — I understood that very late.

(Continued in Chapter 2.)

Chapter 2. Seven Years Outside

_Previously_ — Nine-year-old Anemos was alone outside the village the day his mother and brother left for the Preserve. Seven years later, now sixteen, he sets out with a water flask to bring them back. On the road, an old man he meets tells him, "…They're exactly the same." He does not yet know what those words mean.

While I walked the road, I kept walking backward.

My body went forward, but my thoughts kept returning to seven years before. It was a four-day road, and through the third day there was a great deal of time to think. In that time I put those seven years in order.

I wrote "in order," but really it was a matter of counting. I was already a person who counted, even then.

Between the year I turned nine and the year I turned ten, five people went missing from our settlement. Between ten and eleven, three. The year after that, four. I remember all those numbers.

Counting became a habit around age ten. At first I just counted out of boredom. The number of times the well bucket went down, the number of axe-strokes to split wood, the number of steps left until evening. Then at some point I was counting people.

I learned later that this was a way of avoiding grief. Numbers don't cry. Names cry.

My uncle's house had three other children besides me. I was the fourth. Fourth for the rice bowl, fourth for the clothes. My cousins were never unkind to me. They simply never thought of me as a child of their own house. Neither did I.

"…Whose kid are you?" a village child once asked me.

"…I have a mother," I said. "In the Preserve."

The child didn't understand what that meant. Not many people in the village knew much about the Preserve. It hadn't been long since the city people came and took them, and no one yet knew exactly what it meant.

Only I knew. Only I was weighing the weight of that word.

My uncle's youngest was two years younger than me. His name was Gaon. Gaon didn't take to me. For the first few months he wouldn't even look at me.

"…Why won't you look at me?" I asked once.

"…You're going to leave, aren't you," Gaon said. "The kids who were at your house before did too. Whoever we got attached to always left."

I couldn't answer that. Gaon was right. I lived in that house planning to leave someday. There were times I deliberately worked at not getting attached.

I learned very late that the effort had failed. Attachment doesn't fail to form just because you try to keep it from forming. It forms with every shared meal. I ate about two thousand meals in that house. I never counted, but that's what the arithmetic gives now.

At twelve I tried to leave once.

I packed my things and told my uncle. He looked at me for a long while and said this.

"…You'll die if you go alone."

"…I won't."

"…It's four days' walk. Where will you get water?"

I had no answer. I had neither the strength to walk four days nor the sense to ration water. My uncle unpacked my bag again and said,

"…You have to be sixteen at least."

"…Why sixteen?"

"…Because by then you'll be an adult too. It's an adult going to meet adults, not a child going to fetch them."

I didn't understand that distinction then. Now I do. My uncle wasn't worried I'd fail to get there. He was worried about what I'd see once I arrived.

From that year I started counting how many years were left until sixteen. Four years. I crossed those four years off one day at a time.

A friend of mine died the winter I was thirteen.

I won't write the name here. I was told that writing it makes it cry, so I won't write it here. That child was my age, drew water with me, played with me. It started as a cough and stopped in eight days.

While that child was sick, I kept thinking one thing.

Couldn't they be sent to the Preserve?

I said that thought to my uncle. He shook his head.

"…That's not a place you go for this."

"…Why not? My mother and brother went because of sickness."

"…That was because the sickness had gone through the whole settlement, so the city sent people. The city doesn't come because one person is sick."

"…What if we brought them ourselves?"

"…Four days' road, carrying a sick child on your back?"

I had no answer. That child stopped in eight days. I went to the funeral. The third of the seventeen I counted.

That was the day I first learned the boundary of the word preservation. It didn't come to just anyone. Certain conditions had to be met. Nearly a matter of luck.

I would learn later that this boundary was the whole structure of this city. Some come in and some cannot. The standard isn't cruel. It's simply procedure. But I didn't know yet, then, that procedure can be more frightening than cruelty.

After the funeral, Gaon came and sat beside me. He hadn't even been close to that child, yet he came.

"…Brother," Gaon said. "Your mom and your brother aren't going to die, right?"

"…No."

"…Then that's a good thing."

I meant to agree, but I couldn't. Even knowing it was a good thing, the word wouldn't come out of my mouth. I didn't know why, then.

Gaon treated me a little differently after that. I think my effort not to get attached broke apart that day. In the moment Gaon sat down beside me.

At fourteen I learned the trade under the old well-digger.

The old man lived alone, a little way off from the settlement. The village people liked him but never got close. People find someone who doesn't talk hard to be around.

I didn't find it hard. Being beside someone who didn't talk was, if anything, comfortable. If he didn't talk, I didn't have to answer.

We dug all day, every day. Some days the water came quickly. Some days we dug all day and nothing came.

"…Guess there's none today," I'd say on the days nothing came, and the old man would shake his head.

"…It's not that there's none. You just haven't met it yet."

"…What's the difference?"

"…If there's none, you can dig all you want and nothing comes. If you just haven't met it, one more spadeful and it comes."

I took that as a technical remark. Now I know it was the only sermon that old man ever gave me. He hated sermons. And yet that was one.

The old man's hands were cracked. The knuckles were thick and coarse. I would look at that hand and then look at my own. Still soft.

It took three years for my hand to crack like that. I measured it in time. The old man never measured it in time. He just dug.

The old man never once asked me why I was learning to dig wells. I never asked him why he didn't go to the city either. But one day the old man brought it up first.

"…You're going to that city."

I stopped shoveling, startled.

"…How did you know?"

"…You keep looking at that flask. You tend to it even though there's no use for it yet."

That was the first time I realized I had a habit of tending my flask. It was the one my uncle had given me. I wiped it down with a cloth every night. I didn't know myself why I did it.

"…What will you do when you get there?" the old man asked.

"…Bring them out."

The old man planted his spade in the ground and looked at me. A long look. Then he went back to digging.

"…All right."

That was all. He asked nothing more. I stayed confused for a long time, unable to tell whether that short answer meant he was against it or for it.

Now I know. It was neither. The old man already knew what I was going there to do. He already knew what "bring them out" meant. He also knew that telling me anything would be useless. Some things can only be understood by going and seeing for yourself.

The year I turned fifteen, I saw a city person for the first time.

A traveling peddler. He went back and forth between the city and the settlements, selling goods. The city's emblem was stamped on his handcart.

I caught hold of him and asked.

"…Have you been to the Preserve?"

"…Been there. To deliver goods."

"…What are the people like there?"

The peddler thought for a moment, then answered.

"…Quiet."

"…They don't like noise?"

"…No. There's nothing to make noise about."

I found that answer strange. I didn't know what "nothing to make noise about" meant.

I learned the meaning later. A place with nothing to fight over, nothing urgent, nothing to cry about. That was the real meaning of "quiet."

"…Do the people there go on visits a lot?" I asked again.

The peddler looked at me for a long while.

"…Everyone goes a lot at first. Then it tapers off."

"…Why?"

The peddler answered while pulling his cart.

"…You'll know once you go."

I let those words pass too, at the time. A month later I heard something similar from an old man passing through the settlement. I let that pass too. I should have caught it the third time I heard the same words, but I let all three pass.

Four months before I turned sixteen, I received a notice at the settlement hall.

It said nine of the eleven sent to the city seven years before had been stably preserved, and a visit could be applied for at any time. There were no names for the remaining two.

I read that notice three times. The same sentence every time. I think I was hoping the sentence would change. It never did.

That night, for the first time, I did not count toward sixteen. I did not count the four months either. I just started packing.

My uncle watched me do it and said nothing. The man who had told me I was still too young to walk four days, that I had to be sixteen — that man did not stop me from leaving four months early.

I thought that was permission at the time. Now I know. It wasn't permission. It was resignation. My uncle knew I would go eventually, and he knew that pulling four years forward by four months wouldn't change much.

The old man stopped the winter I turned fifteen.

I had stayed by his side for the days before. He said almost nothing to the end. Just once, looking at my hand, he said this.

"…Cracked now."

He'd been looking at my hand. Three years of digging in the dirt had made it that way. I took it as a compliment. In the old man's way, it would have been one.

"…What happens to you now, teacher?" I asked. A foolish question. But the old man didn't call it foolish.

"…I'll stop."

"…If you're sent to the Preserve—"

The old man shook his head. He had little strength left, so it was only the smallest shake.

"…How would this body make four days' road. And I'm—"

He didn't finish. I wondered a long time about the rest of that sentence.

Later, I fill it in myself. It was going to be, I don't want to go. There's no proof. But watching the people I met in this city, that guess slowly became a certainty.

The old man's funeral was modest. A few villagers came. Gaon came too. I cried aloud at that grave for the first time. The ninth of the seventeen funerals, and the first time.

I knew exactly why I was crying that day. It was because I knew that of everything that man had said to me while he was alive, not one word had been empty. I didn't know then whether I would meet another person like that.

The night before I left, I counted those seven years again.

Seventeen funerals. Six years since I learned to count. Two years working under the old man. One meeting with a city person. One day since the notice. Seven years living with Gaon.

And seven years since I had seen my mother and my brother.

I laid those numbers side by side and looked at them. They were all correct numbers, and none of them made sense together. Only the number seven stood out, unusually large.

That night, for the first time, I grew afraid. Not because I didn't know what I would see when I arrived. I was afraid that I would see exactly what I already knew. I didn't yet know a name for that fear.

Gaon came into my room that night and sat a long while without speaking. Gaon, now fourteen, had grown as tall as me.

"…Brother," Gaon said after a long while. "You'll really bring them back, if you go?"

"…Yes."

"…So I'll really get to meet your real family?"

I laughed at that.

"…You're family too, now."

Gaon didn't answer. But I saw his eyes go red.

That night, for the first time, I understood I hadn't been the fourth child in that house. Two thousand meals had made it so. I only understood it the night before I left.

The next morning, my uncle handed me the flask. I took it and set out on the road.

And on that road, as I've already written, I met an old man. What he said still lives in me. Exactly the same. I didn't know what it meant, not until the evening of the fourth day.

The third night, I sat by a fire and thought again about what that old man had said. Our well-digging teacher had said something similar. That it wasn't that the water didn't come, but that you hadn't met it yet.

That night I laid the two sayings side by side. Not yet met, and already met so completely they are the same. I thought, then, that these two were exact opposites. Now I know. In this city, the two become the same words.

That night I opened and closed the flask's cap, again and again. It was still half full inside. I thought I could fill it once I reached the city.

On the evening of the fourth day, I was told, the city would come into view over the hill. I turned my last steps toward that hill.

(Continued in Chapter 3.)

Chapter 3. The Road Up

_Previously_ — Anemos walked the four-day road, retracing his seven years outside: seventeen funerals, the death of the old man who taught him to dig wells, Gaon who had taken him in as family. Leaving all of it behind him, he turned his steps toward the last hill.

From the top of the hill, they'd told him, you could see the city.

The old man had said it. The last old man he'd met. I believed those words and walked toward that hill the whole afternoon of the fourth day.

Around sundown I reached the top.

I remember that moment exactly. I was out of breath, my legs ached, and the flask was nearly empty. And when I raised my head, the first thought that came was not about the city.

It was the thought that it was quiet.

The city sat on the flat ground above the valley.

It was less a wall than a low fence, ringing the place. The roofs stood in neat rows, all of a similar color. Not scattered in every color the way our settlement's were.

I looked at the sight for a long while. At first I thought it was beautiful.

Thinking back now, that impression wasn't wrong. It really was beautiful. The trouble was that I only learned, much later, where that beauty came from.

Before the sun went fully down, I started down the hill. About half a day's distance remained to the city.

As I went down, I listened.

Our settlement made sound even at night. Dogs barking, the well bucket, someone walking, a child crying. I had grown up inside those sounds.

This city had none of them.

I put it down to distance at the time. I thought I simply couldn't hear it yet, still being far off. That it would come once I got closer.

It didn't come, even once I got closer.

I stood a while longer on the hill, until the sun had fully gone down.

Seen from a distance, the city wasn't large. Only a little bigger than our settlement. That surprised me. For seven years I had imagined that city as far larger. I had thought of it as a place that could hold everything, and so guessed its size must be just as great.

I learned later that size didn't matter. What this city held wasn't a matter of volume.

A few streets showed between the roofs. Straight streets. Not a single one curved. I found that strange too. Every street in our settlement curved. As houses were added, the streets grew to follow them.

This city's streets had been laid out fixed from the start. There was no need for them to grow. Since no more people were being born, there was no need to build more houses either.

I found myself wondering, right then, whether there were any children in this city. I'll write the answer later.

I camped that night at the foot of the hill.

I meant to light a fire, but I gave it up. The city was too close. I had learned outside that lighting a fire when a stranger is nearby draws the eye.

I shook the flask. Little water was left. I drank it sparingly.

That night, for the first time, I wondered whether there might be a well inside the city too. I assumed there must be. A place where people lived couldn't be without a well.

I would learn the next day that this assumption was wrong.

The night sky was clear. Many stars. I counted stars until I fell asleep. That night I counted up to thirty-two. I fell asleep before I reached thirty-three.

I dreamed that night. I don't remember what the dream was. Only that when I woke, a feeling lingered as if I had heard the sound of water. I still don't know whether that was a dream or real. That night I was still outside the wall, so it could have been real.

The next morning I stepped onto the road that led into the city.

The road was strangely clean. Our settlement's roads were always cluttered with wheel ruts, footprints, animal tracks. This road had none of that. It looked like a road no one walked at all.

And yet people did walk it. I passed three groups that morning. It was a road that people walked, and yet left no trace. I found that strange too, at the time, and let it pass.

The first I passed was a man pulling a handcart. I stopped him and asked.

"…Is this the road to the Preserve?"

"…It is."

"…How much further?"

"…Half a day."

The man said nothing more and went on. I learned later that his short answer hadn't been unkindness. That was simply all he had to say.

I'll set down here that most of the people I met in this city were like that. Not unkind. Just saying only what was needed. At first it felt cold to me. Later it came to feel, instead, honest.

The cart carried cloth. All of it white or pale gray. I found that strange too. In our settlement people were proud of dyed cloth. Red cloth, blue cloth. The more it caught the eye, the better it was treated.

I would notice later that I had seen almost no eye-catching color anywhere in this city. Brightness wasn't forbidden. No one simply made it. There was no one who wanted to catch the eye.

Before noon, I passed a man walking with two children.

The children walked quietly. In our settlement, children that age would already have run around and shouted a good ten times over that distance. These children simply walked along behind their father.

"…Your children are very well-behaved," I said, striking up conversation as I passed.

The man glanced at me and said,

"…Not well-behaved. Just walking."

I thought that answer strange. I didn't know why "well-behaved" and "just walking" would be different.

I would come to understand the difference later. To be well-behaved means holding back the urge to run. This city's children weren't holding anything back. They simply had no urge to run in the first place.

I learned later that the man wasn't from this city either. He too was on his way to visit someone. That he knew the difference was because he, like me, had come from outside.

The second I passed was an older woman.

She was walking alone. Almost no luggage. I spoke to her too.

"…Visiting someone?"

The woman stopped and looked at me.

"…How did you know?"

"…There's nowhere else to go, on this road."

The woman laughed. A slightly sad laugh.

"…That's right. Going to see my son."

"…How long has it been?"

"…Three months."

I was surprised by that answer. Three months seemed short, I thought. For my family it had been seven years.

"…You go often, then."

The woman said, walking again,

"…I went every week, at first. Now it's once every three months."

"…Why did it taper off?"

The woman didn't answer that right away. Only after a few more steps did she say,

"…Go and see. You'll know once you go."

I was hearing that answer for the third time now. The peddler, the old man, and now this woman. The same words, all three times. Only then did I begin to feel a little uneasy. Why does no one tell me in advance?

Now I know. It wasn't that they couldn't. It was that it couldn't be carried in words. It was something you could only know by going through it, and everyone who had gone through it in this city already knew that.

I walked alongside that woman for a while. She asked me things first — where I was from, who I was going to see, how old I was.

"…Sixteen."

"…Did you come alone?"

"…Yes."

The woman stopped walking for a moment.

"…None of your family helped you?"

"…They did. Gave me the flask, and so on. But none of them came with me."

"…Why?"

I couldn't answer that. I had never once thought, at the time, about why my uncle hadn't come along.

I would learn later. My uncle had been to this city once. A very long time ago, to see his own parents. He never once told me that story. I set it down here for the first time in this book.

The woman spoke again, walking on.

"…I came with my husband, at first. Then he stopped coming."

"…Why?"

"…He found it hard."

I asked no further. I still didn't know what "hard" meant. It felt right, then, not to ask.

Past noon, the city's fence began to come into view.

Seen up close, the fence was low. A height you could climb over if you wanted. I found that strange. It seemed less a fence meant to keep something out than one meant simply to mark a line.

That thought wasn't wrong. This city had no need to keep people out. No one who tried to come in would bring anything from outside better than what this city already gave. The fence wasn't there to block intruders. It was only a thing that drew a line.

Inside the fence, the roofs came into closer view. I looked among them for rising smoke. The kind that comes from cooking. I saw none at all.

I blamed the distance again, then. Too far away to see, I thought. I'll write later about the fact that I still couldn't see it once I was close.

Walking along the fence, I stopped and parted ways with the woman. She went toward a different gate. I learned later that I had never even asked her name.

"…Goodbye," the woman said. "I hope you see something good."

I thought that farewell strange. Hoping I would see something good sounded like a thing you say when you're not yet certain.

I never meet that woman again in this book. But the farewell stayed with me a long time. I heard similar farewells from other people many times later. None of them wished me well with certainty. That was, in their own way, their honesty.

I watched the woman's back until it disappeared toward the other gate. Only then did I walk my road again. Walking alone had already, in that short time, come to feel strange.

A small stream ran beside the road. Glad to see it, I went over and filled my flask.

This was the last water I filled outside. I didn't know then that it was the last.

While filling it, I looked down into the streambed. The water was clear. A small fish passed between the stones. I watched that fish for a long while. It was no different from a stream outside.

I would come to find it strange, only later, that this fish lived just outside the city fence. Nothing at all lived in the water inside the fence. Though I didn't even know yet, then, whether there was a waterway inside.

Following the fence, I came to a gate.

There was no gatekeeper. Instead, letters were written on a wooden board.

Those entering, please register at the reception office.
The Preserve welcomes all.

The lettering was straight. Not a single crooked stroke. I thought of the wooden board at our settlement hall. The lettering there was always crooked. The size changed depending on who had written it.

I read that notice twice.

The Preserve welcomes all. I believed those words exactly as they stood. It wasn't a lie. This city truly did let anyone in. The trouble was that letting anyone in and letting anyone out were not the same statement. I would learn that much, much later.

I crossed the gate.

I have called that moment back to mind many times since. There was no special feeling as I set foot across. No threshold, even. Outside and inside simply ran together.

But the sound vanished completely. Outside the gate, the sound of the stream had been audible. The moment I stepped through the gate, that sound cut off.

I turned and looked back. The stream was flowing just the same. Only it could not be heard from inside.

That was the first time I felt real fear. What I'd felt on the hill had been vague. This was specific. From a place that made sound to a place that did not — I had just crossed a boundary.

I stood at the threshold without moving for a while. I even tried stepping back half a pace. Stepping back, the sound came again. Stepping forward, it cut off again.

I went back and forth in that spot a few times. I know it was a foolish thing to do. But I wanted to be sure, then, whether it was an illusion or not. It was not an illusion.

There was no one on the road but me. The people I had passed earlier had scattered to other gates, or had already gone in. I stood alone before that gate.

I would later come to overlay that moment with the moment I had sat behind the hill at nine years old. Both were moments of deciding whether to cross or not. Nine-year-old me did not cross. Sixteen-year-old me did. That difference is where this book begins.

I took one deep breath and went inside.

Just inside the gate stood a small building. A wooden board read "Reception."

I stood before it. Before going in, I shook the flask once. It was half full.

I never once imagined that carrying this flask would look strange. At the time it was just a flask. Later it would become the one thing in this city that set me apart from everyone else.

Beside the building was a small flowerbed. Flowers had been planted there. White flowers, all similar in color. They hadn't wilted, and there were no insects. I touched one of the flowers. It was real. Not artificial.

I saw that flower again many times later. Always in the same spot, always in the same state. A flower that neither bloomed nor faded. It took me a long while to understand that it was a picture explaining this entire city.

I hung the flask back on my shoulder, pushed the door, and went inside.

(Continued in Chapter 4.)

Chapter 4. A City Without Water

_Previously_ — Anemos reached the Preserve after four days. Everyone he met on the road left him with the same words: "You'll know once you go." The moment he crossed the gate, the sound of water outside cut off completely. He pushed open the door of the reception office and went in.

Inside the reception office it was cool.

One person sat there. Their age was hard to guess. They could have been about thirty, or about forty.

"…Is this your first time here?" that person asked.

"…Yes."

"…Your name and your purpose, please."

I gave my name. When I tried to say my purpose, I paused a moment, not knowing how to put it.

"…My mother and my brother are here. They were preserved seven years ago."

That person wrote something down on paper. Not surprised, not wearing a look of sympathy. Just wrote it down.

I felt that blank expression as coldness, at first. Now I know. It wasn't coldness. In this city, that story was nothing to be surprised at. Everyone had come here to say exactly that.

There was a nameplate on the desk. It read only "Reception Officer." No name.

"…Do you have a name?" I asked.

The person looked up. A face a little taken aback.

"…I do."

"…Then why does the plate only say—"

"…We use only titles here. It's a rule of this place."

I thought that was strange. In our settlement, people were called by name. Even the village head had a name, and everyone knew it.

I would learn later that officials in this city were generally called only by their titles. It wasn't that they had no names. Names were simply not used. I thought a long time about why, but by the end of this book I never got a certain answer.

"…How many days do you plan to stay?" the person asked.

I couldn't answer that question. I hadn't come with any notion of days. I had come only with the thought of bringing them out.

"…I don't know."

"…That's fine. We'll assign you lodging. There's no set period."

"…What about money?"

"…This city has no money."

I thought I'd misheard.

"…What?"

"…There's no currency. Whatever you need, you receive as a ration."

I felt relieved hearing that. Outside, there had been many things I couldn't do for lack of money. Here there was no such thing at all — I only took it as good news.

I would learn later that it was good news and something else at the same time. Having no money also meant there was no way to have more of what you wanted. Here everyone received the same. Because no one wanted more.

After registration, that person guided me to the ration station.

On the way, I asked.

"…Where's the well here?"

The person stopped walking and looked at me.

"…A well?"

"…Yes. To draw water."

"…This city has no well."

I stopped where I stood.

"…None at all? Then how does water—"

"…Whoever needs it receives it at the ration station."

"…Living people need water."

The person looked at me a moment, then answered.

"…Yes. That's what the ration is for. Those who have been preserved don't need it."

I turned that sentence over many times. Those who have been preserved don't need it. I didn't know exactly what it meant, then. I know now. It meant there was a kind of body that could be fine without drinking water.

On the way to the ration station, I saw, up close for the first time, a person of this city — that is, someone who had been preserved.

A woman. Her age was hard to guess. Her walk was a perfectly even pace. Neither fast nor slow. I greeted her without thinking, as I passed.

"…Hello."

The woman looked at me and smiled. An exact smile. Neither too wide nor too small.

"…Yes, hello."

That was all. The woman walked on. I watched her back. It was a fairly strong afternoon sun, but there was no sweat on her forehead. Her face wasn't flushed either.

At the time I just took it as a difference in constitution and let it go. It was only later, asking the reception officer again, that I understood. A preserved person doesn't sweat. Not because they don't feel heat, but because their body has no need to use water that way.

The ration station was a building like a small storehouse. There weren't many people.

Inside, I had the feeling that standing there holding a flask was the one strange thing about me. No one else was carrying a flask.

The station attendant saw me and asked.

"…Here for water?"

"…Yes."

"…How much do you need?"

That question flustered me too. Outside, since there was a well, there had never been a need to decide how much was needed. You simply drew what you needed.

"…A day's worth?"

"…I'll give you a day's worth."

The attendant scooped water from a jar and filled my flask. The exact amount. Neither overflowing nor short.

I admired that exactness at first, then soon found it strange. Well water isn't exact. The amount was different every time you drew it. Sometimes it overflowed, sometimes it fell short. That unevenness was the very nature of well water. This water had come out measured. Water with no room to be uneven.

"…And tomorrow?" I asked.

"…Come again tomorrow and you'll get more."

"…I have to come every day?"

"…Yes."

I thought that was a bother. If there were a well, you could draw a great deal at once and set it by.

I learned later that this way of rationing wasn't about being a bother. In this city there was almost no one, besides me, who had any reason to store water. Everyone needed only to receive what they needed for that day. Storing was something only people who worried about the future did.

Leaving the ration station, I walked the streets.

Buildings lined the roads between the roofs. People passed to and fro. Everyone walked quietly. No one ran.

I looked around for something like a well or a washing place. There was none. No stream either. Not even the sound of running water.

I walked the city for about three hours that day. In all that time I never once heard the sound of water flowing. I don't think anyone who has only ever lived in an outside settlement can fully grasp how strange that is. The sound of water had always simply been there, like air. That day was the first time I ever walked through a place where it had disappeared.

Walking on, I passed a small plaza. A stone structure stood in the middle. Round in shape, hollowed in the center. Glad to see it, I went over.

It was a fountain. A fountain with no water.

The stone floor was bone dry. Not even a trace of moss. I set my hand on the edge of the structure. Not a speck of dust — perfectly clean.

I asked a passerby.

"…Did water used to come out of this?"

The person stopped and looked at the fountain. Looked it over a moment, as if seeing it for the first time.

"…Not sure. I've never heard anything like that."

"…Then why was it built?"

"…Because the shape is pretty?"

That was all the person said before walking off. I stayed behind, looking the fountain over more.

I would learn later that this fountain had never had water in it from the start. This city had a number of things that only kept the shape of flowing water without actually letting anything flow. That day, at the fountain, was the first time I saw it. And later I would see the same shape in something much larger.

There was no one hanging washing out to dry, either. Every garment was clean. I found that strange too, but soon guessed the reason.

A preserved person's clothes don't get dirty. The living were a small minority, and their laundry, it seemed, was handled some other way. I never did see that method, to the end. It was probably a way that used no water.

There was little dust either. Outside, in the settlement, when the wind blew, dirt lifted into the air. Shoes were always grimy. Here, only my shoes were dirty. That evening I looked a long while at the soles of my own shoes.

The lodging was a short walk from the reception office. Inside that building were several rooms, and I was assigned one of them.

On the way in, the door of the next room was open, so I could see inside. A man was unpacking his things. He looked to be in his mid-twenties.

"…First time here?" he asked, looking at me first.

"…Yes."

"…I came the day before yesterday too. Who did you come to see?"

"…My mother and my brother."

The man nodded.

"…I have a brother. It's been three years."

I understood right away what that "it's been" meant. I was already, little by little, learning the way people used that word in this city.

"…Do you go back and forth often?" I asked.

The man's hands stopped their unpacking.

"…This is my third time."

"…Three times in three years?"

"…Yes."

I thought that was few. I didn't say it aloud. The man must have read my expression, because he added this.

"…At first I came every month."

He said only that and went back to unpacking. I asked nothing more and went into my own room.

That night I thought together about that man's "it's been," and about how his visits had shrunk from every month to three times in three years. I still couldn't connect the two, then. The connection wouldn't come until later, until I had stood in that place myself.

As night deepened, I looked out the window. A few lamps were lit along the street. The light was fine, even. It didn't flicker. I had watched lamp-flames flicker in the wind my whole life, but not once did a lamp in this city flicker.

I would learn the reason for that too, later. For now I only note that it was strange. That night I was counting a list of strange things in my mind. No sound, no well, no color, no flicker. Four. I didn't know, then, how much longer that list would grow.

It was a small room. One bed, one table. I sat in that room and set the flask down on the table.

I suddenly thought of the people I had passed that day. No one had carried a flask. No one had a water bottle at their hip. Only I did.

That night, turning the flask over in my hands, I thought: this object had become the thing that marked me here as someone from outside. When my uncle gave it to me, it must have meant survive. Here that meaning had turned into a different meaning. It was like a mark that read: the only person in this city who needs water.

I drank a little water that night. About half of the day's ration.

From then on I began to conserve water. Even knowing there would be a ration every day. A habit worn into the body doesn't disappear just because it's explained.

The next morning, I went back to the reception office to find out the procedure for a visit. On the way I passed the ration station from the day before.

A line stood there. Only three people. I looked closely at those three. They all looked, like me, like people who had come from somewhere else. Their clothes were a little different from the city people's.

I tried, then, to count how many people in this whole city drank water. I couldn't count it. Instead I arrived at this: everyone who drinks water is someone who came from outside. No one was born or grew up in this city. Everyone here had come from somewhere.

Whether that was a correct conclusion, I would only confirm later. For now I set down only the guess I formed that morning.

On the way, I ran into the man from the next room again, the one from the night before. He was already coming back from finishing his visit.

"…That was fast," I said.

"…If you apply early in the morning, it happens that same morning. There aren't many people."

"…How was it?"

The man looked at me a moment. His face searching for an answer.

"…Good."

There was something strange in the way he said it. The word "good" held very little good in it. I didn't notice that at the time. I only exchanged goodbyes and we parted.

I would recall that expression again later. Once, coming back from my own first visit, when someone asked me "how was it," I found I had answered with that same tone — "good."

Reaching the reception office, the same person from yesterday recognized me.

"…Here to apply for a visit?"

"…Yes."

"…I'll walk you through the procedure. You have to see the Curator first."

That was the first time I heard that title. The Curator. I heard the word with the flask still in my hand.

"…What kind of person are they?" I asked.

"…A kind person. You don't need to worry."

That answer was reassuring. But one thing the person added afterward stayed with me.

"…Though you may have to wait a while. They've been rather busy lately."

"…Are there a lot of people applying for visits?"

"…No, it's not that, but…"

The person didn't finish the sentence. I asked nothing more. It seemed like asking wouldn't bring an answer anyway.

I turned that unfinished sentence over all that night. Busy, but not because there are many applicants. Then busy with what? I would learn the answer in Chapter 6. Here I only set down that I wondered.

Leaving the reception office, I shook the flask again. Still about half left. The ration hour hadn't come yet.

In that moment I got some sense of the shape a day would repeat in, in this city. The ration station, the reception office, the lodging. A day spent going back and forth among them. No one else's day had a flask in it. Only mine did.

(Continued in Chapter 5.)

Chapter 5. Perpetuity

_Previously_ — Anemos learned the city had no well. The preserved have no need of water. He became the only person receiving a ration. He was told he would first have to meet the Curator before applying for a visit. The Curator, they said, was busy these days.

The reception officer handed me a sheet of paper.

"…Read this before your visit. Everyone reads it."

It had a title. "Concerning Perpetuity."

I stood right there and read it.

The first paragraph read like this.

Perpetuity is the system operated by the Preserve. It preserves the consciousness and memory of registered subjects without damage. The preserved converse, remember, and judge. They do not disappear, and they do not suffer.

I read that sentence over several times. They do not disappear, and they do not suffer. Those two phrases sounded to me like the best words in the world.

I could find no reason to doubt that sentence then. I can find none now either. That sentence was not a lie. In this city I never once found any evidence that this sentence was false. That is the difficulty of this book.

Holding that paper, I thought of the man in the next room. The one whose brother had "become" preserved three years before. Passing him on my way out of the reception office, I brought up this paper.

"…Have you read this?" I asked.

"…When I first came. Everyone reads it."

"…Do you believe it? What's written here?"

The man thought a moment, then said,

"…There's nothing to believe or not believe. Go talk to my brother and you'll see. It's real."

"…What's real?"

"…The remembering. He remembers everything about when I was little. Even things I've forgotten."

That answer set my mind at ease. The unease that this city might be deceiving me about something eased a little.

I would confirm later that this man's words were accurate. The preserved really do remember. I never once overturn that fact in this book. There was never anything to overturn.

I moved on to the second paragraph.

Perpetuity does not evaluate the preserved. It does not ask their qualifications. It does not weigh what their life before preservation was like, or what weight that life carried. Every registered person is preserved in the same manner, with the same diligence.

Reading that paragraph, I felt strangely at ease.

Until then I had unconsciously assumed this city would screen for something. In our settlement, even to receive a single thing, your qualifications were weighed. Had you been a good person, had you been of use to the village, did you owe any debt. Even of the dead, people said such things. He was a good man. She was a person too soon lost.

This city didn't do that. At first that was simply reassuring news to me. Later I would learn that this was the most frightening part of this city. I won't write that part yet, here.

I asked the reception officer,

"…So you'll preserve a bad person too?"

"…Yes. The registration standard is condition. Not the person."

"…What does that mean?"

"…Whether damage has begun in the living body — that's the standard. What kind of person they were isn't looked at."

That explanation gave me a strange sense of reassurance.

I had long been used to things I couldn't have because I lacked the qualifications for them. Outside, a great many things were a matter of qualification. This city had no such standard at all. I liked that. I still admit that the feeling itself was good.

The third paragraph.

Perpetuity does not judge. It does not ask after sin. It does not weigh. It only preserves.

I read this sentence over a second time.

I would learn later that this sentence stands in exact contrast to the systems of three earlier cities. I won't tell those cities' stories in this record. I'll only note that each of those cities had something that measured. There was a Scales. There was a Roll. Here, there was nothing.

I didn't know, then, how strange that nothingness was. I thought everyone would welcome hearing that there was no evaluation. So did I. I still think, even now, that this sentence itself is a kind one. Kinder than any system I had heard of before.

There was a fourth paragraph too.

The preserved have no need of new experience. This is not a defect but a stability. The purpose of this system is that the state at the moment of preservation remains exactly as it was.

I had no particular thought on first reading this paragraph. The word "stability" sounded to me like nothing but a good word.

I would come to reread this paragraph many times later. That stability and stillness could be the same word — I couldn't think that, then. This is the sentence that hides most quietly in this entire book. I now think it is also the most frightening sentence.

Seeing my face, the reception officer asked,

"…Is something bothering you?"

"…No. It's just… good, what it says."

"…That's right. It's a good thing to say."

The way that person answered, I felt something in the tone. Not so much conviction as repetition. It sounded like an answer given many times before.

I didn't notice that repetition then. Later I would come to make the same repetition myself. It's a good thing to say. Every time someone asked me how a visit had gone. In the exact same tone the man in the next room had used when he told me it was good.

Seeing that I had finished reading the paper, the reception officer asked,

"…Anything you're curious about?"

"…What is Perpetuity made of? Is it a machine?"

"…A bit different from calling it a machine, but you can think of it that way."

"…Who made it?"

The person's face turned thoughtful for a moment.

"…I don't really know that either. I only know it was made a very long time ago."

I didn't find that answer strange. Our settlement, too, had a well that no one knew exactly when had been dug.

I didn't know then that this not-knowing had spread through this entire city. No one knew how Perpetuity had begun. Not even the Curator. Whether they knew and simply weren't telling, or truly didn't know, was never made certain by the end of this book.

I folded the paper and sat a while in the shade of a tree near the reception office. There I overheard two people talking. Both were people of this city.

"…I heard that house's daughter-in-law came by yesterday," one said.

"…Yes, I heard."

"…First time in three years, they say."

"…I see."

That was all. The conversation went no further. I found that strange. In our settlement, that conversation would have gone on much longer. What it meant to come after three years, what had happened in the meantime, why she had come only now.

I recalled that short exchange again later. Those two people weren't curious. They attached no feeling at all to the fact of three years. Neither surprise nor regret. They only exchanged the bare fact.

I know it wasn't rudeness. To those two people, three years and three days truly made no great difference. That not judging could show up this way too — I saw that for the first time in that tree's shade.

"…Can I ask one more thing?" I said.

"…Yes."

"…You said there's no judgment. Then is there no one who's refused preservation?"

"…No one. Anyone who wants it is accepted."

"…What about someone who's accepted without wanting it?"

At that question, the person hesitated a little for the first time.

"…That… it can only begin if the person themselves wants it. Registration requires consent."

"…What if they're in a state where they can't consent? Too sick to."

"…In that case a family member can apply on their behalf."

I nodded at that answer. I thought of how my mother and my brother had been preserved that way. Both had had such high fevers they were in no condition to decide anything for themselves.

I thought, then, that I had fully understood this answer. Later I would learn that this word "consent" is the heaviest word in this city. I'll write that story in Chapter 18.

I asked one more thing.

"…Can I be preserved too? Right now."

The reception officer looked at me. There was no sign of surprise. This question, too, didn't seem to be a first.

"…In principle, yes. I told you the registration standard is condition. Right now you're in good health, so you're not a candidate, but if you wish, you can apply anyway."

"…Even in good health?"

"…Even in good health, we don't block the application. We just don't encourage it, either."

"…Why don't you encourage it?"

The person looked at me a moment, then answered.

"…Because you still have something left to lose."

I didn't understand that answer right there.

I would take those words out and turn them over many times later. You still have something left to lose. I tried, then, to count what I stood to lose. I counted everything that could be counted. That something uncountable remained — I didn't know that, then.

At the very bottom of the paper was a fifth paragraph.

Family and acquaintances may visit at any time. Without any separate reservation or waiting, you may visit whenever you wish.

Reading this sentence, I felt a catch in my throat right there. Any time. I had waited seven years for that word.

"…Is this real?" I asked. "Can I really go any time at all?"

"…Yes. So long as you follow the procedure."

"…Does the procedure take long?"

"…Meeting with the Curator is the first step. After that it goes quickly."

I nodded at that answer. I was glad to hear that only the first step had to be gotten past.

I didn't know then. That this word "any time" carried, along with its kindness, another face. Being able to go any time meant that if you didn't go today, you could go tomorrow; if you didn't go this month, you could go next month. It was a phrase that made all urgency disappear. I would come to see, later, just how slowly that phrase lets a person drift away.

I folded that paper and put it inside my shirt.

I still have this paper. Writing this record now, I spread it open on my desk and read it again. Not one sentence has changed. I have changed.

Walking outside, I turned those paragraphs over again. Does not judge. Does not ask qualifications. Does not ask after sin. Preserves with the same diligence.

That night, for the first time, I thought this: if a system like this had existed in our village seven years ago — no, eleven years ago — could my father have been preserved too? My father's disappearance wasn't from sickness. I said in Chapter 1 that I wouldn't write that story at length in this record. I keep that promise even now.

I'll set down only this much. I can no longer remember my father's face well. I remember my mother's face, but my father's face has gone blurred. I find that difference frightening. The blurring itself. This city was telling me it could take that very blurring away. I was drawn to that promise. I am not ashamed of that pull, even now.

Walking back to the lodging that night, I thought for the first time that this city might be a good place.

That thought wasn't wrong. This city was a good place. I don't want to paint this city as a bad place, to the end of this book. But being a good place, and giving me what I wanted, turned out to be two different questions. I wouldn't understand that difference until much, much later.

Walking to the lodging, I counted. How many sentences on that paper ended in "does not." Six. Does not evaluate. Does not weigh. Does not judge. Does not ask after sin. Does not measure. Does not need.

I walked counting those six negative sentences. I wrote in Chapter 2 that counting was a habit. This time the counting did something else. It confirmed to me that all six were good things to say. That confirmation was, that night, a comfort to me.

Now I count those six negatives again. And I think this. A system that says six times what it doesn't do is a system that has decided, six times, not to do something. That the deciding itself was a choice — it took me a very long time to notice that.

On the way back to the lodging, I passed the fountain again. The waterless fountain I had seen that afternoon.

I stopped in front of it for a moment. And I recalled the sentences I had read that afternoon. Does not judge. Does not weigh. Only preserves.

I opened the flask and drank a little of what remained. Before that fountain, only the sound of my own flask sounded. The cap opening, the water sloshing. In that narrow plaza, that sound rang unusually loud.

I should have noticed something in that moment. That not weighing and having no flow at all might be the same thing. I didn't notice, then. I only walked on thinking those sentences were kind.

I still think those sentences are kind. Even now, having finished writing this whole book. Only now I know that being kind and keeping someone alive are not the same thing.

I closed the cap and hung the flask back on my shoulder. Walking the rest of the way to the lodging, I thought that tomorrow I would meet the Curator. I said that name to myself a few times in my mouth. The Curator.

I said that name then with no feeling at all attached to it. It wasn't until the next day, seeing that face, that feeling began to attach to it.

(Continued in Chapter 6.)

Chapter 6. The Curator

_Previously_ — Anemos read the notice titled "Concerning Perpetuity." It does not judge, it does not ask qualifications, a visit may be made at any time. He felt this system was kinder than anything he had heard of before. The next day he was to meet the Curator.

The Curator's office was a building at the very center of the city.

The building itself was a little larger than the others. But it wasn't ornate. There was almost nothing you could call decoration. I found that, if anything, dignified.

There were six steps. I counted them as I climbed. One, two, three. My hand trembled and I lost count a few times. I counted twice more to be sure it really was six.

I noticed later that this was the only building in this city that looked old. The stone was darker than the other buildings'. No one ever told me why.

The reception officer walked me there and said they would wait outside.

There were a few long benches in front of the building. People sat waiting their turn. I looked over their faces one by one.

One person looked anxious. Their leg was shaking. Another had no expression at all. Their eyes simply faced forward. I wondered why those two faces were so different.

I would learn the answer later. The anxious one was a first-timer. The blank one had come many times before. I didn't even know, that day, which I was closer to. I know now. That day, I was the anxious one.

When my turn came, the reception officer called my name. I adjusted the flask on my shoulder and went into the building.

I stood a moment before the door. There was no marking on the door at all. No name, no title. I thought that strange. Every other place had had a marking.

I would learn later that this door hadn't originally been unmarked. I won't tell that story here.

I knocked.

"…Come in."

The voice was gentle. Age was hard to guess. I pushed the door and went in.

The room wasn't large. One desk, two chairs. Nothing hung on the walls.

One person sat behind the desk. They looked to be around forty. Their clothing was tidy. There was no particular expression on their face. Only their eyes looked a little heavy.

A stack of papers sat piled on one side of the desk. Fairly tall. I wondered what it was but didn't ask.

I would learn later that it was the day's paperwork to be processed. I wanted to count that height, but it wasn't the place for it. Looking back now, that pile of paper was the most honest object showing this person's day.

"…You're Anemos, I take it," the Curator said first. "I heard from reception. Sit, please."

I sat. The chair was hard.

"…You've come to see your mother and your brother, I understand."

"…Yes."

"…It's been seven years."

"…Yes."

The Curator turned over a sheet of paper. It seemed to be my file.

I didn't see, then, what was written on that paper. I never saw it, later either. The Curator never once showed me that document.

"…The road here must have been hard," the Curator said.

"…It took four days."

"…On foot?"

"…Yes."

"…Did you have enough water?"

That question surprised me a little. I hadn't expected to be asked that.

"…Yes, it was fine."

The Curator looked at my flask. I was still wearing it on my shoulder.

"…You seem to carry that around with you."

"…Yes. It's become a habit."

The Curator smiled slightly at that. A small smile.

"…A good habit. Though it's an uncommon object, here."

I still remember that smile. It was the first real smile I saw after coming to this city. The reception officer had smiled too, but that had been an exact smile. This was a little different. I couldn't explain where the difference lay, then. I can't say it exactly even now.

The Curator pressed the corner of an eye briefly with a fingertip. A very short motion. I let it pass, assuming it was fatigue.

"…Are you all right?" I asked.

"…Yes, I'm fine. Don't mind it."

The Curator straightened again. The voice remained just as calm.

I didn't recall that brief motion again until that night. And after that I found myself looking for it every time I saw this person. Sometimes it was there, sometimes it wasn't. I got in the habit of guessing, from that, what kind of day it had been.

The Curator lowered a hand and, arranging the papers again, said,

"…Sorry. Where was I."

"…The application."

"…Ah, right."

In that brief gap I sensed something strange. This person had, until a moment ago, been explaining the procedure with perfect precision, like flowing water. And yet, for just an instant, they had lost their place.

I let that gap pass that day. I thought, people can be like that. But meeting this person many more times later, I came to see that gap arise, each time, in roughly the same spot. While explaining a procedure, for just an instant, looking somewhere else.

"…Let me explain the procedure," the Curator said. "A visit takes just one application form. If you apply today, you may see them today."

"…Do you meet with everyone like this? Every applicant?"

"…Yes. I personally walk you through the procedure for the first visit. From the second on, reception handles it."

"…Why only the first, in person?"

"…Because the first is the most important."

I liked that answer. It didn't sound like a formal answer but one with a real reason behind it.

"…How many people do you see in a day?"

The Curator thought a moment.

"…It differs day to day. Some days many, some days few."

"…Today?"

"…Today's on the busier side."

Hearing that answer, I looked again at the pile of paper on the desk. And I felt a little sorry. I was one of that many.

"…I'm sorry, you must be busy."

The Curator shook their head.

"…No. This is my work. And…"

The Curator paused a moment.

"…This work — it's not the kind of work that can be let fall behind."

I felt something heavy in that tone. It didn't sound like ordinary talk about work.

I only heard it as a sense of duty at the time. Later I would learn it wasn't duty so much as something close to a vow. This person was pressing themselves not to let this work fall behind.

"…Today?"

"…If you'd like."

Something in me swelled right there. Seven years of waiting, and now it would be a matter of minutes.

"…Can I ask something, though?" I said.

"…Yes."

"…I heard at reception yesterday that you've been busy lately. They said it wasn't because there are many applicants, so why—"

The Curator looked at me a moment. Choosing an answer, it seemed.

"…There's some other matter."

"…What matter?"

"…It's a little early to say."

I asked no further. But in that brief silence, I saw something. The Curator's eyes turned, for just an instant, somewhere else. Somewhere in the corner of the desk.

I couldn't read that gaze at the time. I know now. That gaze was directed outside the city — to be exact, below the city. Toward the valley. I wouldn't confirm that until much, much later.

"…Will you write the application?" The Curator held out a sheet of paper.

I took it. There were boxes for name, relation, and the subject's name.

In the name box I wrote my own name. In the relation box I wrote "Son / Elder brother." At the box for the subject's name, my hand stopped.

I won't disclose it here either. I didn't in Chapter 1. I don't write my mother's and my brother's names anywhere in this record. I can't explain the reason exactly myself. I only want these names to be mine alone to say.

I wrote the names. My hand trembled a little.

The Curator saw this and said nothing. Only waited.

I liked that waiting. Someone who doesn't rush you. I hadn't met many people like that.

Pushing the form forward, I asked,

"…Have you been doing this work long?"

The Curator tapped the desk lightly with a fingertip.

"…A long time."

"…How long?"

"…It's been a long time since I stopped counting."

That answer surprised me. I was a person who counted. The words "stopped counting" sounded foreign to me.

"…Why did you stop?"

"…When you keep counting, eventually all that's left is the counting."

I didn't fully understand that, right there.

I would think over those words again and again, later. I have carried numbers through every part of this book. I wrote in Chapter 2 that counting is a way of avoiding grief. The Curator had used that method far longer, far more, than I had. And knew that even that method wears out, in time.

Once the application was finished, the Curator took it up.

"…Are you ready?"

"…Yes."

"…Let me say one thing beforehand."

"…Yes."

"…After you see them, it may be different from what you expected. Don't be alarmed."

I tensed at that.

"…Is she sick?"

"…No. It's not that."

"…Then what?"

The Curator paused a moment before saying,

"…It only means the feelings you've prepared may not all get used."

I didn't understand that, then.

"…I'm not sure what that means," I said.

The Curator looked at me a moment. A face weighing whether to explain further.

"…There's a lot you've wanted to say, all this time, isn't there."

"…Yes. Really a lot."

"…Have you prepared all those words?"

"…Yes. I even decided the order."

The Curator gave the smallest nod at that. A nod that seemed both regretful and resigned.

"…That order — you may not get through all of it today."

"…Why?"

"…You'll know once you go."

Hearing that, I nearly laughed to myself. I nearly counted how many times I'd now heard "you'll know once you go" since coming to this city.

In that moment, for the first time, I faintly sensed that this answer wasn't evasion. Everyone truly could not find a way to explain it. I found that more frightening. It wasn't that there was no explanation — it was that it couldn't be explained.

"…Can I still ask one thing?" I said.

"…Yes."

"…Could it be different in a good way? Not just a bad way."

At that question, the Curator looked at me a while longer for the first time.

"…That, I don't know either. It's different for everyone."

"…What do you think, yourself?"

"…This isn't a place for me to give my opinion."

That answer was firm. I couldn't tell whether it was because of a rule or this person's own choice.

I would learn later that the distinction hardly mattered. Rule or choice, the result was the same. This person never once told me their own opinion, to the end.

I have called this sentence back to mind hundreds of times since that day. The feelings you've prepared may not all get used. It was a warning. A very precise warning. I would live out that warning myself, two hours later.

Gathering the application, the Curator said,

"…It's been received. If you follow the procedure, you should be able to see them within today."

"…How long do I have to wait?"

"…Not long. It just takes some time to prepare."

"…Prepare?"

"…There's a procedure for bringing them in. An officer will guide you shortly."

I nodded at that. As I rose and turned toward the door, the Curator called me back once more.

"…Anemos."

"…Yes."

"…Are you nervous?"

"…Yes."

"…That's all right. Everyone is."

There was something laid under that tone. I couldn't find it strange, at the time.

I know now. That wasn't comfort. It was a confirmation of fact. I didn't have the nerve to guess how many times the Curator had said those same words in that room.

I looked once more around the room. A room with nothing on the walls. One window. Through that window, the roofs were visible.

I would come to that room again, later. Many times. Each time, the room hadn't changed in the least. I thought that was this person's taste. Later I learned it wasn't taste. There was simply no reason to change it.

Before leaving through the door, I bowed once more.

"…Thank you."

The Curator nodded.

"…Go well."

That farewell, too, was short. I faintly sensed, even then, that it wasn't from a lack of sincerity. This person wasn't sparing words — they were choosing only the words that were truly necessary.

Coming out of that room, I thought, for the first time, that this person was to be pitied. I couldn't explain the reason. The thought simply came. Not a grieving person, not an angry person, and yet someone who made me think, this person is to be pitied. I had never met anyone like that until then.

Coming out into the corridor, the reception officer was waiting for me.

"…All finished? The officer will guide you through preparations for the visit now."

I adjusted the flask on my shoulder and followed that person. My heart began to race again.

(Continued in Chapter 7.)

Chapter 7. Applying for a Visit

_Previously_ — Anemos met the Curator for the first time. A person versed in procedure, courteous, yet somehow worn. "The feelings you've prepared may not all get used" — the meaning of those parting words, Anemos does not yet know. He handed in his application. Now only the waiting was left.

The officer was a young woman.

"…This way, please," the woman said. "I'll call you as soon as it's ready."

I followed her to a small waiting room. There were a few chairs, and no one else there but me.

"…How long will it take?" I asked.

"…About half an hour. An hour at the most."

Hearing that span of time, my heart felt strange. I had waited seven years, and now it hadn't sunk in that only half an hour remained.

I still remember exactly how I spent that half hour, sitting in that waiting room. I spent it counting. There was nothing like a clock on the wall. I gauged the time by the sound of my own heartbeat.

I would count one breath in and one breath out and call it one. Counting up to fifty that way felt like about five minutes had passed. That afternoon I counted this way six times. On the sixth, I lost the number and had to start again from the beginning. That was the first failure I experienced that day.

The moment I sat down, I took a piece of paper from my pocket.

The paper where I had written out the words I'd worked over on the road. I unfolded it and read it again.

First, the greeting.

I had meant to open with "I've missed you." Then something like "Have you been well." I had practiced this order the whole four days of walking.

I had reworked that greeting many times too. At first, worried that if I just said "Mom" I wouldn't be able to go on, I'd considered opening with something else instead of the word itself. In the end I decided to simply say it.

I stopped counting how many times, in that waiting room, I silently rehearsed that single word, "Mom." I gave up somewhere around twenty. Counting had lost its meaning. That day I learned, for the first time, that some words come out sounding like the first time no matter how many times you practice them.
