I also looked at the small note I'd written in the margin. "While smiling." I had written those two words down since I was nine years old. I mustn't meet her crying, nine-year-old me had thought.

Seeing that note, I laughed a little in the waiting room that day. It felt like homework nine-year-old me had left for sixteen-year-old me. While smiling. I had no confidence, then, that I could keep that homework.

Second, everything that had happened since.

My uncle's household, Gaon, learning to dig wells. I had meant to tell it all in order, thinking my mother would want to know.

I had broken that second item down in more detail. The story of Gaon coming to think of me as family. The story of the first time I cried aloud, when the old man stopped. Whether to include the story of my friend who stopped in eight days, I had agonized over for a long time.

In the end I decided to include it. It felt like something my mother should hear. I thought that only by knowing everything I'd endured would she understand the words I meant to use to persuade her to come with me.

Looking back now, it was a strange calculation. The calculation that if I told her everything I'd endured, she would do something for me because of it. I didn't even know, then, that it was a calculation.

I had even numbered the order on the paper. One, Gaon. Two, the old man. Three, my friend. Four, how to dig for water. I had changed that order about three times over the four days on the road.

At first I had meant to tell the old man's story first. I wanted my mother, above all, to hear what he had told me — that you don't find water, you make it a way. On the second night I changed the order. It seemed wrong to start with something sad, so I decided to tell Gaon's story first.

At the time I thought reordering it that way was consideration for my mother. Looking at it now, it wasn't consideration — it was fear. I was afraid that if I brought up something sad first, she would grieve, and I'd never get to the main thing I meant to say.

On the third night I changed the order again. I meant to cut my friend's story out entirely. It felt too heavy. But on the morning of the fourth day I decided to put it back in.

Without that story, there was no explaining why I had come this far to fetch my mother. It was only after watching my friend die that I understood the weight of the word preservation. Without that story, I thought then, the main thing I meant to say would lose its force.

Third, the main thing.

Let's go together now. Five words.

I wrote in Chapter 1 that I first settled on those five words that night when I was nine. After that, this line nearly changed several times. At twelve I considered switching it to "I've come to bring you back." At fourteen I thought of adding "I'm grown now too." In the end I returned, every time, to the original words.

I couldn't explain the reason then. I know now. All the other words were words that explained me. "Let's go together now" was the only one that didn't explain me at all. It was a phrase aimed wholly at them. That's why I never changed it, to the end.

I folded and unfolded that paper again and again. My hands grew damp.

Through the waiting room window I could see the yard. A few small trees stood there. Though the wind was blowing, the leaves barely stirred.

I found that strange too, at the time. The same kind as the unflickering lamps I noted in Chapter 4. I didn't know the reason, that day. I know now. This city simply doesn't have strong wind to begin with. Not by chance, but because of the terrain. The valley drew all the wind down into itself.

I watched that tree and counted time. How many times the leaves stirred. When I reached twenty, the officer came back.

"…A little longer, please."

"…Is something wrong?"

"…No. It just takes some time, procedurally."

I believed that. I had reason to. I had already learned that the people of this city don't lie.

I still believe those words. From that day on, I never once heard a lie in this city. Only, the truth wasn't always the shape I wanted.

The waiting room door opened once and closed again. It seemed someone had come into the wrong room and left. In that brief moment I saw a person standing in the corridor. An older man. His face was pale.

I would come to remember that face for a long time. It had no expression to speak of. Neither sad nor glad — just a face that looked entirely used up.

I didn't know, then, what had caused that face. I couldn't have guessed it was the face of someone just finishing a visit. It was only late that afternoon, looking into a mirror, that I found my own face had come to look like that man's.

I also saw what he held in his hand. A small handkerchief. The hand holding it was not the least bit wet.

I didn't find that strange, at the time. I simply couldn't imagine, then, that a handkerchief used to wipe tears could stay dry.

While waiting, I thought about other things too.

Then, for the first time, I tested myself on whether I remembered my mother's voice exactly. I closed my eyes and tried to bring her voice to mind. It came. Exactly, it came. That was a relief to me.

I tried to bring my brother's voice to mind too. He had been eight then, so I didn't know how much his voice would have changed. I tried and gave it up. Only the eight-year-old's voice came to mind. I couldn't imagine what it might sound like now.

I didn't know, then, what that small snag meant. I know now. My brother's voice really was exactly the eight-year-old's voice, still. It wasn't a failure of my imagination. My memory had been exact.

While at it, I counted other things too. The four days it had taken to get here, the three settlements I'd passed through in those four days, the four people whose names I'd heard on the road. The numbers grew the more I counted.

That was strangely reassuring. What could be counted was still something I could hold on to.

I thought about something else too. Would my mother recognize me. I had gone from nine years old to sixteen. I had shot up in height, my voice had deepened, my face had changed. My uncle, a few months before, had looked at me and said, "I wouldn't have known you."

That was the first time I took that worry seriously, in the waiting room. For seven years I had thought only of my mother staying the same, and had hardly given a thought to the fact that I had changed. That was strange. I simply hadn't felt the change, because I saw myself in a mirror every day.

That day, in the waiting room, I asked myself that question for the first time. What if I'm the only one who changed. I would learn the answer soon.

About an hour in, I heard sound from the next room.

Not voices. Sounds like a door opening and closing, footsteps. I listened closely.

I would learn later that this room was the passage leading to the visiting room. That day I counted how many times that door opened and closed. Three times. Three people, it turned out, had gone before me.

The officer came again and said,

"…Almost ready. Just a little more."

"…What preparation is being made?"

The woman looked at me a moment, then said,

"…It takes time, bringing her in."

"…Bringing her in? From far away?"

"…Not that far."

I sensed something vague in that answer. But I didn't press further, then. My mind was elsewhere.

I would only understand what that vagueness was much, much later. I'll write that story later in this book. Here I only note that I failed to notice it that day.

Once the officer left, I was alone again. The room was quiet. So quiet that my own breathing sounded loud.

In that quiet I noticed something strange. Ever since arriving in this city, I had always felt my own breathing, my own footsteps, unusually loud. With every other sound so low, only my own sound stood out. There were times it felt like proof that I was the only living person in this city. It was an illusion. Everyone was alive. Only I was noisy.

I closed my eyes and brought my mother's face to mind again. This time not the voice, but the face. The set of her eyes, her nose, the corners of her mouth. I called up that face piece by piece, as if fitting it together.

I thought I remembered that face exactly. But when I opened the door and actually saw that face, I found there was a very fine difference between the face I remembered and the real one. What that difference was, I'll write in Chapter 9.

I looked over the paper again.

The third item, the main thing. Let's go together now.

I practiced those five words aloud, for the last time, in that waiting room. Very quietly, so only my own ears could hear.

"…Let's go together now."

The words sounded strangely awkward. Words I had practiced for four days — no, for seven years — and yet, spoken aloud, they felt foreign.

I thought, at the time, that the strangeness was from nerves. Later I would learn there was another reason. Those words had been written with a nine-year-old's heart. Spoken from a sixteen-year-old's mouth, they simply didn't fit.

I meant to say it once more but gave it up. I had thought a second try would improve it, but it seemed it would only feel more foreign.

I folded that sentence up as it was and put it away inside myself.

I fidgeted with the flask. Opened and closed the cap.

I drank exactly one mouthful of water in that waiting room. I learned that day, for the first time, that your throat goes dry when you're nervous. I was probably the only person in this city who felt thirst.

Looking at that flask, I had a thought too. Should I show it to my mother. If I told her my uncle had given it to me and I'd carried it all the way here, would she be glad.

I secretly added that as a fourth item. I didn't write it on the paper. Only in my mind. I didn't know, then, that it would turn out to be the most useless item among all the words I'd prepared.

Through the window the sun had tilted a little further. I gauged the length of the shadow. A little past noon. On my second day in this city, at the end of a road I'd walked for four days, I had finally arrived at that hour.

The officer opened the door and came in again.

"…It's ready."

I sprang up at those words. I folded the paper again and put it inside my shirt. My hands trembled.

"…This way, please."

I followed her out into the corridor. My steps felt strangely heavy. Steps I had waited seven years for, and yet now that I actually walked them, my legs wouldn't quite obey.

I can still picture that corridor exactly. It wasn't a long corridor. There were three doors. I stopped before the middle one.

The other two doors were also closed. I wondered whether someone like me was behind them too. Maybe someone else stood there clutching a flask like me, folding and unfolding a paper like me.

I learned later that my guess wasn't wrong. Those three doors in that corridor always held people carrying a similarly weighted burden. I didn't know that, that day. I thought I was the only one carrying this weight.

The officer pointed at the door.

"…Here. Go in."

I took hold of the doorknob. My hand was cold.

I still remember the feel of that doorknob. Smooth. Cold. I took one deep breath before opening that door.

I touched the paper inside my shirt once more. Still there. The greeting, everything that had happened since, the main thing. Seven years' worth of words, even the order all decided.

In that moment I believed that paper would protect me. That if I only spoke in order, only did as I'd prepared, this reunion would turn out just as I had pictured it. I would learn, the moment I opened that door, that this belief was wrong.

The officer said quietly from behind me.

"…Take your time."

Those words were strangely kind. They didn't sound like something said as a matter of procedure. I nodded at them and pushed the door.

(Continued in Chapter 8.)

Chapter 8. My Mother

_Previously_ — In the waiting room, Anemos went back over the words he had prepared for seven years. The greeting, everything that had happened since, and the main thing — "Let's go together now." The officer pointed at the door. He took hold of the doorknob and took one deep breath.

He opened the door.

The room was small. There was one window, and sunlight was coming in. Two chairs stood facing each other.

My mother was sitting in one of them.

I have relived that moment more times than I can count. Even now, writing this record, if I close my eyes the room is still there exactly as it was. The chairs, the sunlight, and my mother.

The room had none of the usual things. No painting on the wall, no vase on a table. Only two chairs and one window. I didn't notice that plainness at the time. My eyes saw only my mother.

My mother was exactly the same.

The way her hair was tied, the way she sat, even the color of her clothes — all exactly as I remembered. For a moment I felt like my breath had stopped.

She saw me and stood up.

"…Anemos."

Her voice was exactly the same too. The very voice I had called to mind with my eyes closed, for seven years.

"…Mom."

I barely managed that one word. None of the greeting I had prepared came out.

She came to me and cupped my face in both hands.

"…You've grown so much."

"…Yes."

"…Have you been eating well?"

I laughed a little at that question. The first thing said after seven years apart was about food.

"…Yes, I've been eating well."

She looked me over for a long time. Up and down, from my face to my feet.

"…Your voice has deepened too."

In that moment I understood how pointless my worry had been, that she might not recognize me. She knew me at once. She saw exactly how much I had changed. But there was no surprise in that recognition. It was as if she were seeing someone she had said goodbye to yesterday.

She noticed the flask slung over my shoulder and tapped it lightly with her fingertips.

"…What's this?"

"…A water flask. My uncle gave it to me."

"…Did you think you couldn't get here without water?"

"…Just habit, I guess."

She smiled a little at my answer. This smile had a different texture from the one before. I didn't notice the difference at the time.

"…Same as ever, you." she said. "Even as a child you always carried everything you needed."

I nodded at that. It was true.

"…Sit." she said.

I sat in the chair across from her. The distance between the two chairs was just enough for a hand to reach across. She took my hand. It was warm. Only then did I realize I had been holding my breath until that moment.

The first thing I checked was the warmth of that hand. I had been afraid it might be cold, or strange somehow. But it was just a human hand. Warm, soft, an ordinary hand.

"…Are you all right?" I asked. "Does it hurt?"

"…No, it doesn't hurt at all."

"…Is anything hard?"

"…No. Everything's fine."

I felt relieved at that answer. And at the same time, something felt empty. Like there was some other question I should be asking, and I didn't know what it was. My mother's expression didn't move at all. To the question about pain, to the question about hardship, the same-temperature answer came back each time. I didn't know then what that emptiness was.

I would only put a name to that emptiness in the next chapter. Here I'll only note that the feeling was there.

"…Weren't you scared?" I asked. "Back then, when the fever got bad and you had to lie down."

She thought for a moment, then said,

"…I think I was scared. But I don't remember it well."

"…You don't?"

"…The feeling from back then is blurry. Because now I'm not scared at all."

That answer struck me as strange. Whether a fading memory of fear was a good thing or a bad thing, I couldn't decide. I remembered exactly how many days that fever had lasted, while my mother barely remembered even the fact that it had been frightening.

I never manage to settle that judgment by the end of this book. Whether a feeling fading is a mercy or a loss — I still don't know.

"…Mom." I called.

"…Yes."

"…Did you know I was coming all the way here? That I'd come."

She looked at me and smiled.

"…No, I didn't know. But it's not a surprise."

"…Why?"

"…Because you're the kind of child who'd come."

The answer was short. But there was some certainty folded inside it. I was glad of that certainty.

"…Did you miss me?" I asked. It wasn't something I had prepared. It just came out.

She paused a moment at that question.

"…Yes. I missed you."

I felt relieved to hear it. But the speed at which the answer came was a little strange. Too exact a speed. Not sorrow, not longing — just the speed of stating the correct answer.

I didn't find that speed strange at the time. I know now. It wasn't a lie. She really must have missed me. Only, that missing probably didn't carry the same weight as the missing I had carried for seven years.

I tried to recall the order I had prepared.

First, the greeting. Already ruined. Second, everything that had happened since.

"…A lot has happened to me since then." I said.

"…Tell me."

"…Um, there's a boy named Gaon, my uncle's youngest…"

"…Oh, that child. Is he doing well?"

I was a little surprised that she remembered Gaon. He had been young even before she left.

"…Yes, he's doing well. He's grown almost as tall as me now."

"…That's fast."

The tone was strange. There was no surprise in that word "fast." It was just a tone that confirmed a fact.

I didn't find that tone strange at the time. I know now. For her, seven years wasn't a fast span of time. The very unit of seven years had no meaning for her.

I started to bring up the old man, then stopped.

I had set that story as the second item under the second heading. The story of the old man stopping. But when it actually came time to open my mouth, my throat closed up.

"…What were you about to say?" she asked.

"…Nothing. Later."

She nodded. She didn't press.

I thought back on that moment later. Why hadn't she asked further? Was it that she wasn't curious, or that she saw I was struggling? I still don't know the answer for certain.

Instead she asked me something else.

"…Where are you staying now?"

"…There's a lodging house in this city. The information office assigned it."

"…Isn't it lonely being alone?"

I laughed at the question. The question of whether it was hard being alone — the very thing I'd just asked her — she was handing right back to me.

"…I'm fine. There's someone in the next room too."

"…Are they a good person?"

"…Yes, seems like a kind person."

She nodded. There seemed to be some relief mixed into that nod. I was glad of it. It felt like proof that she was worrying about me.

I kept that moment for a long time as the best moment of that visit. That brief moment of my mother worrying about me. I thought it was only natural. I know now how uncommon that moment was.

I can still picture her face from that moment exactly. Her eyebrows lightly furrowed, her head tilted toward me. It was the face of someone worrying. Wanting to see that face a little longer, I deliberately answered a little slower.

I couldn't bring up my friend at all. The friend who had stopped after eight days. The moment I tried to say his name, my throat closed on its own.

I never managed to tell that story in this visit, in the end. A story I had been preparing since I was nine, and in front of her I couldn't say a single word of it.

"…How's my brother?" I asked instead.

Her face brightened a little.

"…He's doing well. When I told him he'd see you later, he was happy."

"…Does he recognize me?"

"…Of course."

I felt relieved at that, and uneasy at the same time. My brother was eight. That he would still be exactly that, even after seven years had passed, weighed on me for the first time in that moment.

"…Isn't he having a hard time here either?"

"…No, he's fine."

"…Does he get bored?"

She tilted her head at that question, exactly as before.

"…He doesn't really use the word 'bored,' that child."

I let that answer pass without dwelling on it at the time. Only after meeting my brother in person, later, would I understand what those words meant.

"…Is he nearby right now too?"

"…Yes, remember, today you're to see me first, and your brother a little after."

"…I want to see him soon."

"…He hasn't changed one bit. Be ready to be surprised."

She said that, laughing. A light laugh. I tried to laugh along with it, but it didn't quite work.

I took "he hasn't changed one bit" as just a joke at the time. Two hours later I would learn that it wasn't a joke at all, but an exact fact.

"…Is my uncle doing well?" she asked.

"…Yes. Same as ever."

"…I'm grateful. For taking you in."

"…Yes."

"…It must have been hard, in that house."

I started to say it hadn't been, but just nodded instead.

I don't know why I didn't deny it. I wanted to say it hadn't been hard, but the words wouldn't come. Maybe, in that moment, I was admitting for the first time that it had been a little hard after all.

"…You must have thought of your father a lot too." she said suddenly.

I was startled by that. Eleven years had passed since my father disappeared. For her to bring him up first was rare. In our family, my father was always a subject someone raised carefully — lowering their voice, watching for reactions.

"…Sometimes."

"…Do you remember his face?"

I couldn't answer right away. I hesitated to say it had gone blurry.

"…It's a little blurry."

She nodded at that. She didn't look sad.

"…That can happen."

That calm was strange to me. In our family, talk of my father was always heavy, but the three words that came out of her mouth were far too light.

I thought at the time that lightness was because she was strong. I know now. It wasn't strength. In this city, weight itself was measured differently.

She held my hand a little more tightly.

"…You've done well."

At those two words I nearly cried. I barely held it back.

I remember nine-year-old me writing "while smiling" in the margin of that paper. I kept that homework half done. I didn't cry. I couldn't smile either.

The third item, the main thing, still remained.

Let's go together now.

I kept looking for the moment to say those five words. But I kept failing to find it. My mother was talking about other things, and I couldn't find the courage to interrupt.

"…How is it, staying here?" I asked instead.

"…Good. Everything's good."

"…Isn't it boring?"

She tilted her head slightly at that question.

"…What is there to be bored of."

I felt something blocked in that answer. But I couldn't put my finger on what it was, right then. I would only come to know the name of that feeling after I met Eikon. I'll write that story in Part Two.

I didn't know how much time had passed. The officer opened the door a little and said,

"…Time's up."

My heart sank at those words. Already.

"…Already?"

"…Yes, I'm sorry."

She didn't let go of my hand, and said,

"…Come again."

"…Yes."

"…Let's talk more next time."

I nodded at that. In that moment I realized I hadn't gotten to the main thing. Let's go together now. In the end I never once spoke those five words aloud.

At the door she held me once. Her embrace was exactly as I remembered. Same warmth, same strength.

"…Bye now."

I didn't think, at the time, how strange that farewell was. Seven years since we'd seen each other, and yet the parting words were exactly what you'd say to someone you'd met yesterday.

Before leaving through the door I turned to look at her once more. I wanted to hold her face in my eyes one last time. She was still standing right there. The same posture, the same expression as a moment before.

That feeling was strange too. I would come back anyway, and yet I took in her face as though I would never see it again. I didn't know then where that urgency came from. I know now. My mother had nothing to hurry for. I did.

I stepped out through the door. Looking back, I saw her waving. Her face was smiling.

That smile was exactly the same too. I walked out watching that smile. All the way down the corridor, through the waiting room, until I stepped outside, I couldn't think of anything at all.

Outside, the officer was waiting.

"…You can see your brother in a little while. Please rest a bit, then come."

I nodded and sat back down in a waiting-room chair. I could feel the paper against my chest. I didn't take it out to look at it.

I never unfolded that paper again that day. There was no reason to. The order written on it had already come apart entirely.

(Continued in Chapter 9.)

Chapter 9. Exactly the Same

_Previously_ — Anemos met his mother for the first time in seven years. She wasn't sick or sad, and she welcomed him gladly. He never got out even half of what he'd prepared to say. Before meeting his brother, he had to sit in the waiting room a moment and rest.

Sitting in the waiting-room chair, I did nothing at all.

Even when I tried to think, thoughts wouldn't quite take hold. I just sat there and breathed. I picked up the flask and set it back down, over and over.

I couldn't even count that stretch of time. It was the one moment I gave up counting. I couldn't bring myself to.

I thought of the man next door. The one who had come three times in three years. Footsteps that had shrunk from once a month down to three times in three years.

At the time I guessed that shrinking was laziness. Now I see it differently. It wasn't laziness. Going to see the same exact thing, every time, takes something out of you. What that something was, and where it went, I began to understand for the first time that day.

I thought again of what that man had said to me earlier. "It was good." I had already written down, at the time, how little goodness I could actually hear in that tone. Now I could understand the tone. It wasn't a lie. It really must have been good. Only, that goodness had stopped in the same place, every single time.

I recalculated the man's numbers. Once a month would have made thirty-six times in three years. But it had been three.

Thirty-three had gone missing. Right there, I had no way of knowing where those thirty-three had gone.

After a while I came back to myself a little. And I went back over what my mother and I had said, one thing at a time.

The way her hair was tied. The color of her clothes. The warmth of her hand. The way she smiled.

I went through them one by one, and then I stopped. It wasn't counting. It was checking. And checking, I finally, for the first time, looked something straight on.

All of it was the same.

I called to mind even the folds of her clothes. The way she'd sat, the shape of her collar where it had been creased. Wanting to see how close that was to the last time I'd seen her, seven years ago, I closed my eyes and laid the two scenes side by side.

My mother on the morning she left, and my mother today.

I couldn't find a single difference between those two scenes. Even the color of her hair tie was the same. I knew it couldn't be chance, and yet in that moment I wished it were. If it were chance, it could someday be different.

At first I took that fact only as a good thing.

She hadn't aged. She hadn't changed. So it was a good thing. I repeated that logic to myself over and over in that waiting room. The logic was sound. But my heart wouldn't follow the logic.

I tried to understand why my heart wouldn't follow. My mother mustn't be sick. My mother mustn't be sad. My mother mustn't fail to recognize me. And she had been none of those things. She had been exactly what I'd hoped for.

So why does this feel so strange.

I thought of the people back in our settlement. When they grew old, wrinkles gathered on their faces, their steps slowed, their hair whitened. I had once found that change sad. I had once imagined how my father's face might have changed, if he were still alive.

I used to think that change was proof of sorrow. But in that waiting room I was thinking the opposite. That not changing could be proof of sorrow too. That thought was so foreign to me that I erased it several times over.

Trying to find the answer, I went back over what my mother had said.

"…You've grown so much."

"…Your voice has deepened too."

Those words. My mother had seen exactly how I had changed. And yet she hadn't been surprised by that change. It was there, right then, that I first put my finger on the difference. Seeing a change and being surprised by a change were two different things.

I thought that day I wished there had been a mirror. I wanted to see my own face. I wanted to check for myself how much I had changed in seven years.

Later I saw my own face in the mirror at the lodging house. It was unfamiliar. Someone who recognizes an unfamiliar face and shows no reaction to that unfamiliarity at all. It was only then that I fully understood — that was my mother.

I thought back over the way she had looked at me, in that waiting room. I'd already written that there had been no surprise. More precisely, there had been, inside that gaze, both the recognition that "you've changed" and the acceptance that "I suppose you have," at the same time. There had been no gap at all between the two.

Anyone from our settlement would have had something come out of that gap. A gasp, a laugh, tears. That gap of being startled, "you've grown so much." My mother had no such gap. Recognition and acceptance were one body.

I tried, at the time, to tell myself that wasn't frightening, only different. Writing this record now, I admit that it was frightening.

I opened my hand and looked at my own palm.

There was a callus. A callus from digging wells. Something that hadn't been there seven years ago. I compared that hand to my mother's hand, in memory.

My mother's hand had wrinkles. Wrinkles that had already been there seven years ago. In that brief span I never got to check whether those wrinkles were the same or had grown. Later I regretted not checking.

I know now. Even if I had checked, they would have been exactly the same.

I folded and unfolded that hand again. I could feel the callus.

That feel struck me as unusually strange that day. It seemed like I was the only person in this whole city with a callused hand.

In that waiting room, for the first time, I said the word "exactly the same" out loud.

"…Exactly the same."

It was to myself. No one heard it. But saying it out loud made something come clear.

I had heard that phrase three times on the road to this city. From the peddler, from the old man, from the woman. All of them had only said "you'll see when you get there," and kept that phrase to themselves. It was in that waiting room that I first fully understood what it meant.

I don't resent those three people. I still don't. If they had heard this phrase beforehand, the way I had, they probably wouldn't have believed it either. Some words are just letters until you go through them yourself. That day I passed through the moment when those letters stopped being letters.

I counted back over those three moments. The peddler, the old man, the woman. All three had said it with the same expression.

I couldn't read that expression at the time. Now I could. It hadn't been a warning. It had been resignation.

Being exactly the same wasn't strange in itself. That, on its own, was a good thing. The problem was that the sameness left only me out.

I thought of the trees lining this city's streets. The tree outside the waiting-room window. The one whose leaves barely stirred even in the wind. I had guessed, back then, that it was the terrain, not the wind, that caused it. But another thought crossed my mind, right then.

Maybe those trees don't grow new leaves either. I knew that thought was a groundless leap, and I couldn't shake it anyway. In this city, the boundary between what changed and what didn't was too wide. People, trees, lamps — all of it crowded onto one side.

Only I had changed. My mother had stayed the same. The distance between us was exactly the same now as it had been seven years ago. But I had lived through all seven of those years, while my mother had stood outside them.

At that moment I made a strange calculation.

What if I came back seven years from now. If I saw my mother again at twenty-three. She would be exactly the same then too. I would go through something like this again. Only more apart.

Not seven years, but ten, twenty. I started that calculation and gave it up. I didn't stop because it frightened me. I stopped because there was no need to calculate. The result was already in.

That distance would never close. While I grew old, my mother would stay exactly where she was.

I turned that calculation into numbers. Seven years, then fourteen, then twenty-one. A picture where the gap widened with every multiple.

The picture was simple. But my heart couldn't accept that simplicity.

I pushed the calculation further. When I turned thirty. When I reached my uncle's age. When I was old someday and leaning on a cane. Even then I would come to this city, to this room, and face the exact same face of my mother that I faced now.

Picturing that gave me a strange feeling. It wasn't a frightening picture. But it wasn't a comfortable one either. I tried several words to name that feeling. It wasn't sorrow. Not exactly fear either. The closest word was misalignment. The sense that time itself was misaligned between my mother and me.

I laid that misalignment over something our well-master had once said. That you don't find water, you make it a way. Remembering that, I thought — in this city there was no need to make a way. Nothing needed to flow. My mother, the trees, the lamps — all of it was already standing still. Still water needs no way.

Thinking on that, another thought crossed my mind.

Is this really preservation, or is it something else.

I erased that question on the spot, right away. I thought of the notice I'd read in Chapter 5. The preserved converse, remember, judge. They do not disappear.

That was true. I don't doubt that fact. I still don't. My mother really had recognized me as me, really had remembered Gaon, really had brought up my father. All of that had been real.

But that realness and what I had wanted were wearing different shapes. That was the first day I felt that difference. I couldn't name it yet.

I went back over that notice from the information office, one line at a time. Does not judge. Asks for no qualification. Can be visited any time. Requires nothing new.

Requires nothing new. That day, for the first time, I truly chewed over that sentence. My mother really did require nothing new. The things I had brought her — that I had grown, that I had lived through seven years, the words I had meant to say — none of it filled any lack in her. Because she had no lack left to fill.

It was then that I first thought this: if the things I had brought weren't needed by her, then what had I even come all this way for.

I erased that question right away, on the spot. It was a frightening question. It would only be much later in this book that I would face that question head-on again.

Instead I fled into a different question. What would my brother be like. Would he be exactly the same as my mother too. I already knew the answer. My mother had said, "He hasn't changed one bit." And still, I didn't want to believe that answer until I checked it for myself.

Not wanting to believe is an odd phrase. I didn't want to believe a good thing. Not aging should have been a good thing. And yet, in that moment, I was hoping it wasn't. So that this feeling I had wouldn't be a strange one after all.

Sitting in that waiting-room chair, I opened the flask and took one more mouthful of water. My throat kept going dry. It was a thirst that felt less like a dry body than a dry heart.

That was the first time I felt this thirst. Since arriving in this city there had never been a shortage of water. And yet in that waiting room my throat burned strangely. I didn't know why, that day. Now I can guess. It was the body, telling me — in place of the mind — that something was being lost. Not the body, but the heart.

The officer came back again and said,

"…Shall we go see your brother now?"

I came sharply back to myself at those words.

"…Yes."

Rising, I set aside, for now, the unfamiliar feeling I'd just had. This was time to meet my brother. I decided to take that feeling out again later.

I was used to setting feelings aside like that. It was a method much like counting. If I set something aside, I didn't have to think of it, at least for that moment.

But this time it didn't quite work. Even walking the corridor toward my brother, I couldn't shake that unfamiliar feeling entirely.

Walking the corridor, I looked out the window.

The sky was clear. That I was the only one for whom time flowed under that sky felt strangely frightening.

I've written several times now that I hadn't yet named this feeling. I can name it now. It was loneliness. The kind of loneliness you can feel surrounded by people, loved, even after meeting your family.

That was the day I first knew that loneliness. And I would go on carrying it with me to the end of this book.

Walking, I touched the flask. I didn't open the cap. I just ran my fingertips over its surface.

This flask was, even then, the one thing in this city that belonged to me alone. That fact felt unusually large to me that day. I was the only one whose body was changing, the only one who went thirsty, the only one who had to keep filling something. Holding that flask, I felt like the only living person in this city. I knew it was an illusion. I couldn't shake that illusion that day even so.

The officer walked ahead of me. Following behind her, I took one deep breath.

I thought, at the time, that this breath was to prepare for meeting my brother. Looking back now, it wasn't preparation. I simply didn't want to feel that misalignment I'd just discovered all over again, in front of my brother.

That wish wouldn't be granted. In a little while, I would come to face that misalignment again, in a far sharper shape. In a face that was still exactly eight years old, in a single sentence spoken up at me.

(Continued in Chapter 10.)

Chapter 10. My Brother

_Previously_ — After meeting his mother, Anemos sat in the waiting room and felt, for the first time, a misalignment. She was exactly the same as seven years ago, and that sameness pushed him into an unfamiliar loneliness. Now he goes to meet his brother.

The officer opened the waiting-room door and said,

"…Shall we go now?"

I stood up. The chair I'd been sitting in still seemed to hold some warmth.

I begrudged that warmth, just for a moment. As long as I sat there, I didn't have to do anything.

The officer spoke to me as we walked.

"…Your brother likes to draw."

"…Draw?"

"…Yes. He draws every day."

I laughed a little at that. Even as a small child my brother had been good at drawing. With no paper, he'd always drawn with a stick in the dirt.

Right then I wondered whether the drawings in the dirt and the drawings now would turn out to be the same drawings.

We took a different corridor from the one that had led to my mother. Shorter, with only one window.

Walking that corridor, I counted the doors. There were two. My mother's corridor had had three. I wondered about that difference for a moment, then let it go. It wasn't a thing worth wondering about.

The corridor was quiet. Only my own footsteps sounded.

I've already written several times how loud that sound seemed to me. It was the same that day too. I was starting, now, to try to get used to that sound.

Following the officer, I tried to steady myself.

I told myself, walking: don't freeze up the way I did with my mother. It's fine if I can't say what I prepared — just look well at my brother's face. I held onto that resolve all the way to the end of the corridor.

At the door the officer said,

"…Go on in. He's waiting."

I took hold of the doorknob. The same cold I'd felt at my mother's door. I found that cold a little less unfamiliar this time.

The door opened.

I remember that moment exactly, even now. Inside the room, at the window, a small child was standing.

Eight years old.

The floor was marked with several lines. They looked drawn with fingers, not sticks. Circles and triangles, and shapes I couldn't make out.

Right then I thought of what the officer had said earlier. That he drew every day. In this room with no paper, no sticks, my brother had been drawing on the floor with his fingers. Even the method was exactly the same as it had been outside.

Seeing that face, I stopped breathing for a moment.

It was my brother. Exactly, completely, the brother I remembered. His face, his height, the way he stood.

Even up until I stepped into that room, some part of me had still been imagining otherwise. That he'd have grown a little. That his voice, at least, would have changed. I learned how wrong that imagining had been the moment I opened the door.

My brother had been small-bodied from birth. My mother had once said it was because he was born late. Whenever I'd seen that small body I used to worry. It seemed like he wasn't growing the way other children did.

In that room I thought of that worry again. And I understood, at the same time, how pointless it had been. My brother no longer needed to grow bigger. He was no longer a body that would grow.

I looked around the room once more. One window, two chairs. The same arrangement as my mother's room.

I wasn't even surprised by that sameness anymore. It seemed this city had built every visiting room the same size, the same shape. Only the people were different; the rooms were all the same.

I'd written in Chapter 1 that my brother was afraid of water. Stepping into that room, I found myself listening, out of reflex, for the sound of water. There was none. This room had no water either.

My brother saw me. His eyes went wide.

I tried to find some trace of unfamiliarity in that look. Even the briefest flicker.

There was none. My brother knew me without a moment's hesitation. It was a look like someone seeing a person he had met yesterday.

"…Brother?"

That voice was exactly the same too. An eight-year-old's voice. The very voice I had failed to call to mind, back in Chapter 7.

"…Yeah. It's me."

My brother came toward me. Small steps. I counted them. Six steps. Exactly six steps to cross the room.

I don't remember how many steps it had taken in my mother's room. I hadn't counted. I hadn't had the presence of mind to count, that day. In front of my brother, I had returned to being someone who could count again.

My brother stopped in front of me. And looked up at me.

He looked up at me for a long time.

Starting from my face, down to my shoulders, and back down again to my feet.

I felt my own body anew, following that gaze. A sixteen-year-old's body. A body two heads taller than my brother's.

My brother said,

"…Brother, you got taller."

At that one line, I couldn't say anything at all.

I've tried, more times than anything else in this record, to write this line over again. Every time, I failed. Whatever explanation I put in front of that line, it fell short. So I'll just write it plainly, like this.

Brother, you got taller.

That was true. I really had grown. Nine-year-old me had become sixteen. But the mouth that had said it was still, exactly, an eight-year-old's mouth. In that moment I understood that seven years had passed between the two of us carrying completely different weights.

I stood there holding onto that line for a long time. My brother stood quietly looking up at me while I said nothing.

I couldn't count how many seconds that silence lasted. That was the one moment that day I failed to count. I've written several times that counting was how I protected myself. That method didn't work in that moment.

My brother took my hand. A small hand.

I remember the size of that hand. Exactly the same size as seven years ago. My own hand had grown about twice as big in that time.

"…Brother, why did you come so late?"

I couldn't answer that question. I didn't know how to explain seven years to an eight-year-old.

"…Sorry."

"…It's okay. You're here now."

My brother said that and smiled. A smile with no resentment in it at all.

I was grateful for that smile. And at the same time, it broke me. If there had been resentment, it might have been easier. Resentment is proof that time has passed. This smile had no time in it.

I took my brother's hand in mine. And held that small hand still, inside my own.

I didn't want to let go of that hand. It felt like if I let go, this moment would end.

We sat for a while in two chairs. A room much like the one where I'd met my mother.

"…Brother, want to see this?" my brother said.

He showed me a small stone. I'd seen it before. The stone he'd picked up beside our well back in the settlement.

"…You still have this?"

"…Yeah. I always carry it."

I took the stone in my hand. Exactly the same weight as seven years ago.

Everything was exactly the same. The stone, the hand, the smile, the height. In that moment I confirmed again how exact the misalignment I'd felt with my mother had been. In front of my brother that feeling grew even sharper. The distance between eight and sixteen was crueler than the distance between nine and sixteen.

"…Brother, I draw every day." my brother said. "On the floor. I draw this, and that."

"…Isn't it boring?"

My brother tilted his head.

"…What's 'boring'?"

I couldn't answer that question. It wasn't a question an eight-year-old should be asking back.

Right then I remembered something my mother had said. He doesn't really use the word "bored," that child. I had just let that pass at the time. Now I understood what it meant. My brother didn't know the word "boring" itself. Not that he didn't know it, exactly — he had never experienced the feeling that word pointed to.

My brother pointed at one of the drawings on the floor.

"…Know what this is?"

I looked closely at the drawing. A circle, with two lines running down from it.

"…Is it a well?"

"…Right. I drew you making a well."

I laughed at that. My brother didn't know I had learned to dig wells. There was no way for him to know.

Right then a chill ran through me. What my brother had drawn was our old settlement's well. And yet those words sounded as though he'd drawn it knowing what I was doing right now. I still think it was coincidence. Only, that day, the coincidence didn't feel like coincidence.

"…Brother, remember the sound of the bucket?" my brother asked.

"…Yeah. I remember."

"…I'm not scared anymore."

I couldn't respond to that at all. My brother said it as though it were nothing. As though talking about something that had happened yesterday.

I understood, then. For my brother, that fear, and the moment he'd overcome it, weren't seven years past — they were simply recent. Time hadn't been compressed. It hadn't flowed at all.

"…Are you a grown-up now, Brother?" my brother asked.

"…Not yet. But I'm your big brother."

"…You're my brother, so you're my brother."

My brother said that, laughing. Strangely, that answer comforted me.

Eight-year-olds understand that well. That a relationship comes before a number. A brother is a brother because he's a brother. That simplicity held me up a little, in that moment.

"…Brother, it's the same here every day." my brother said suddenly.

"…What is?"

"…When I wake up in the morning, I'm here, and before I sleep, I'm here. It's always here."

I didn't know what to say to that.

"…You didn't ask if I was bored." I finally managed.

"…You asked that already, Brother."

"…Oh, right."

My brother laughed at that. I tried to laugh along, but it didn't quite work.

I held onto what my brother had said for a long time after. Always here. There was no resentment in those words, no weariness. Just a voice stating a fact. That voice was the most frightening thing of all.

My brother pointed at my flask.

"…What's that?"

"…A water flask. My uncle gave it to me."

"…Is it heavy?"

"…No. Not really."

My brother held out his hand, so I passed him the flask. He held it with both hands, like cradling it.

"…Brother, can I have this?"

I paused for a moment at that.

"…I need this for the road here..."

"…Oh, I see."

My brother handed the flask back without a fuss. Not the slightest trace of disappointment.

That easy acceptance stuck with me strangely. An eight-year-old who felt nothing at all when he couldn't have something he wanted. I had never seen an eight-year-old like that. The children in our settlement cried or threw fits when they couldn't have something. My brother used to be like that too. Not anymore.

I tried to recall the words I'd prepared. But none of them came to me. The words that had scattered in front of my mother felt, in front of my brother, like they had never even existed.

Let's go together now. I couldn't say those five words to my brother either. I couldn't. The child sitting in front of me wasn't a nine-year-old brother — he was an eight-year-old brother. I didn't know what I would even need to explain to him.

I felt for the paper inside my shirt again. I didn't take it out.

I didn't even think, in that room, about taking that paper out. In my mother's room I had at least considered it, briefly. In front of my brother, that consideration didn't even arise. I fully understood, in that room, that some words simply aren't the kind that can be prepared.

"…Brother, next time I'll show you another drawing." my brother said.

"…Okay. I'll look forward to it."

I think back, later, on how easily that answer came out. I'll look forward to it. I said it with no weight at all. I didn't know then how heavy a promise it would become.

I heard again that time was up.

My brother didn't want to let go of my hand.

"…You'll come again, right?"

"…Yes. I'll come again."

"…Soon?"

I couldn't answer that right away.

"…As soon as I can."

My brother, satisfied with that answer, let go of my hand.

I knew, right then, that "as soon as I can" was a heavy promise to me, and just a parting phrase to my brother. To him, soon or late made no real difference. Time didn't brush past that child.

My brother stood up and held me once. His arms didn't even reach my waist.

I gauged the height of that embrace. Seven years ago it had reached my legs. Now it didn't even reach my waist. I knew that calculation was strange. My brother's height hadn't changed at all. It was the calculation that kept changing. What was changing was me.

Leaving through the door, I turned to look at my brother once more.

He stood at the window, waving. An eight-year-old's wave.

I came down the stairs watching that wave. I tried to count the steps. I couldn't. That was the first time numbers failed to help me.

Outside, I gripped the flask tightly. I didn't open the cap.

That afternoon, for the first time since arriving in this city, I felt completely lost. I had set out to find my family, and now, standing in front of that very family, I didn't know where I was supposed to go.

Only then did I open the flask's cap. I took a mouthful of water. It wasn't that I was thirsty. My hands just needed something to do.

That night, lying in the lodging house, I thought again of my brother's one line. Brother, you got taller. I repeated it to myself three more times before falling asleep. On the third time, I cried out loud for the first time.

(Continued in Chapter 11.)

Chapter 11. The Second Visit

_Previously_ — At his first visit with his brother, Anemos was undone by one line: "Brother, you got taller." That night, in the lodging house, he cried out loud for the first time. A few days passed. He decided to apply for another visit.

I waited three days, then applied for a second visit.

Three days. I still remember that number exactly. The first day simply passed. On the second, I held the application in my hand but couldn't make it to the counter. It was only on the morning of the third day that I finally submitted it.

The officer was a young woman again. I couldn't be sure it was the same one. I didn't really look at her face that day.

"…How long will it take?" I asked.

I had asked that same question, word for word, back in Chapter 7. This time, I felt like I knew the answer before I even heard it.

"…About half an hour."

It was exactly the same answer.

Right then I thought of the older man from Chapter 7. That face with every expression used up. I know exactly now what had caused that face. He must have heard this same answer any number of times. The same half hour, every time.

I sat in the waiting room. I didn't take out the paper. This time, I had no prepared words.

To be precise, I did have one. Just one. Let's go together now. I still carried those words. Only, I had no wish to write them on paper again and set them in order.

Through the window I could see that tree. The one whose leaves barely stirred even in the wind.

Confirming the tree was exactly the same, I laughed a little. I felt like I could count the leaves one by one. In fact I did. Just as before, the officer came when I reached twenty.

"…Ready now."

I stood up. My hands didn't tremble this time.

I thought, at the time, it was because I'd grown used to it. I think differently now. Trembling comes from expectation. That morning I already knew what I was about to see.

The corridor was exactly the same. Three doors, the middle one.

The officer said, from behind me,

"…Take your time."

That line was exactly the same too. I wrote, in Chapter 8, how strangely kind that line had felt. Hearing it a second time, something else came before the kindness. This line was part of the procedure. Words said at this exact spot, every time.

I pushed the door.

The room was small. There was one window, and sunlight was coming in. Two chairs stood facing each other.

My mother was sitting in one of them.

I wrote that same sentence in Chapter 8. There was no reason to write it differently. What I saw that day was exactly that.

She saw me and stood up.

"…Anemos."

"…Mom."

Right there I noticed that even the order those two words came out in was the same as last time. She said my name first, and I said Mom after.

She came to me and cupped my face in both hands.

"…You've grown so much."

I froze a little at that.

I had come back after only three days. There was no way I could have grown taller in three days. I did the math right there. There wasn't. There really wasn't.

"…Yes." I answered, exactly as before.

"…Have you been eating well?"

"…Yes, I've been eating well."

She looked me over for a long time. Up and down, from my face to my feet.

"…Your voice has deepened too."

There was no way my voice could have deepened in three days either. A chill ran down my spine the moment I heard that. My mother was looking at me. Looking at me exactly. But that looking wasn't directed at the me standing there now. Words meant for a son she'd parted with seven years ago were coming out again, unchanged.

She noticed the flask slung over my shoulder and tapped it lightly with her fingertips.

"…What's this?"

"…A water flask. My uncle gave it to me."

"…Did you think you couldn't get here without water?"

"…Just habit, I guess."

Even as I gave that answer, I silently said the next line to myself in advance. Same as ever, you. Even as a child you always carried everything you needed. I already knew it before it was even said.

She smiled a little at my answer.

"…Same as ever, you." she said. "Even as a child you always carried everything you needed."

It was exact.

"…Sit." she said.

I sat in the chair across from her. She took my hand. It was warm.

"…Are you all right?" I asked. "Does it hurt?"

"…No, it doesn't hurt at all."

"…Is anything hard?"

"…No. Everything's fine."

I wrote these four lines in Chapter 8 without changing a single word. Copying them out again now, I still can't find a place to change. That day, these four lines really did come out exactly the same.

"…Weren't you scared?" I asked. "Back then, when the fever got bad and you had to lie down."

She thought for a moment, then said,

"…I think I was scared. But I don't remember it well."

"…You don't?"

"…The feeling from back then is blurry. Because now I'm not scared at all."

Listening to that answer, I pressed my fingernails into my palm.

I wanted to confirm, right then, whether this was really the same, or whether I was forcing something similar to overlap in my mind. The pain in my palm was real. And yet my mother's words, as vivid as that pain, were words I'd heard somewhere before. From that moment on, I was certain.

"…Did you know I was coming all the way here?" I asked.

"…No, I didn't know. But it's not a surprise."

"…Why?"

"…Because you're the kind of child who'd come."

Even that answer. I hadn't expected even that answer to be the same. This was a line that came out even when the question changed. I had thought I'd asked a slightly different question from three days ago. And yet the answer wasn't different in the least.

"…Did you miss me?" I asked.

She paused a moment at that question.

"…Yes. I missed you."

I even counted the length of that pause.

One, two. In Chapter 8 too, I had felt that pause. I hadn't counted how long it was, then. This time I counted. It was two. Exactly two.

We talked a little more. About Gaon, about the lodging house, about the person next door. Talking about those things, I tried guessing her answers ahead of time, one by one.

Every time my guess was right, I felt a strange feeling. It wasn't joy. It wasn't relief either. It was closer, if anything, to fear. I knew what would come out of my mother's mouth before she said it.

Once, I deliberately asked something different.

"…Mom, I've been thinking about something lately."

That was the first time I'd said that. It was something I hadn't said last time. I was curious what kind of reaction it would draw.

"…What kind of thing?" she asked.

I couldn't answer. Since it wasn't a question I'd prepared for, the words stuck on my end.

"…Never mind."

She nodded. She didn't press.

That part was exactly like Chapter 8. That day I learned that even throwing in something new only brought us back to the same old place. The problem wasn't my mother. She was always standing in the same place. It was I who threw in something new, and yet that newness kept getting absorbed back into place.

I tried another question.

"…How is it, staying here?"

"…Good. Everything's good."

"…Isn't it boring?"

"…What is there to be bored of."

I had already known that answer too. She had said those exact words in Chapter 8. Hearing it again, I fully accepted that it made no difference how I changed the question. It wasn't the question that was fixed — it was the answer.

I wondered whether the subject of my father would come up again.

"…You must have thought of your father a lot too." she said first.

I had known that line was coming. It was the exact line she had raised first, back in Chapter 8. I deliberately said nothing and waited. I wanted to see whether she'd really bring it up first, on her own.

"…Do you remember his face?" she asked.

"…It's a little blurry."

"…That can happen."

All three lines were exactly the same. I had thought, in Chapter 8, that calm was because she was strong. I know it differently now. It wasn't strength — it was a fixed place. In front of that question, she always stood in the exact same spot.

The officer opened the door a little and said,

"…Time's up."

She didn't let go of my hand, and said,

"…Come again."

"…Yes."

"…Let's talk more next time."

Next time. I had heard that word before too. Next time was always pushed off to a further next time. I started to count how many "next times" this would end up being, and gave it up.

At the door she held me once. Her embrace was exactly as I remembered. Same warmth, same strength.

"…Bye now."

I stopped in my tracks at those two words.

In Chapter 8 I wrote that I hadn't thought, at the time, how strange that farewell was — how it was exactly what you'd say to someone you'd met yesterday, even though it had been seven years. This time, I really hadn't met her yesterday. Only three days had passed. And yet the farewell wasn't different in the least. Only then did I understand. That farewell had nothing to do with time. Whether seven years or three days, to my mother it was the same distance, no different at all.

I came back out to the waiting room and sat down.

Sitting there, I tried, for the first time, to put into words what I'd just seen. The same greeting, the same order, the same pause, the same smile. I counted through that list in my head, up to ten, and stopped. Not because there was nothing more to count, but because I felt that counting further would be frightening.

The officer came over and said,

"…Shall we go see your brother too?"

I nodded.

The officer spoke again as we walked.

"…Your brother likes to draw."

I had heard that same line before. Not a single particle different. Right then I realized this line was something like a script. Not words the officer was saying to me now, but words attached to the procedure itself.

The way to my brother's room was short. I stopped for a moment in front of the door.

By then I half-knew what would happen when I opened that door. And still, I opened it. I learned, for the first time that day, that opening a door knowing and opening it not knowing are two different things.

The door opened. My brother was standing at the window.

His eyes went wide at the sight of me. He came toward me. I didn't count his steps this time. There was no need. I already knew it would be six.

He stopped in front of me and looked up at me.

"…Brother, you got taller."

I couldn't breathe at those words.

The exact same words as three days ago. There was no way I could have grown taller in three days. My brother didn't know that. Or rather, it wasn't that he didn't know — it didn't matter to him. This line came from the same place, always, whether three days had passed or seven years.

This one line shook me more than my mother's "you've grown so much" had. Her words had sounded like inertia. My brother's were different. He was really looking up at me, really feeling that way, and really saying it because of that feeling. And yet the exact same feeling as three days ago was being manufactured, fresh, right there.

I understood, then. This wasn't repetition. It was, every time, the first time. To my brother, I really did look, every time, like I was being seen for the first time. Three days apart or seven years apart weighed exactly the same inside him — new. And that newness carried no memory.

My brother took my hand.

"…Brother, you came again."

"…Yeah."

"…That was fast."

"…Yeah." was all I answered.

I couldn't add anything after that short answer. There was nothing to add. I wanted to say that I'd come back after only three days, but I had no idea what that would even mean to him.

The visit was shorter than last time. I didn't say much. My brother didn't say much either. We just sat there holding hands.

I heard that time was up.

"…You'll come again, right?" my brother asked.

"…Yes. I'll come again."

Giving that answer, I doubted the weight of those words for the first time. I'll come again. How many times had I said that now. I started to count and gave it up.

Leaving through the door, I turned to look at my brother once more. He was waving. The same gesture as last time.

I even felt the angle of that wave was the same as last time. The slight bend of the wrist, the speed his fingers opened. I knew that was a leap, and I still couldn't stop myself from feeling it.

Outside, I walked the road back to the lodging house.

Walking that road, I kept thinking the same thought. Everything I had seen today was exactly what I had seen three days ago. The order, the words, even the length of the pauses. I started to calculate the odds of that being coincidence, and gave it up. I already knew it wasn't coincidence.

Only when I reached the lodging house did I stop. I opened the flask and took a mouthful of water.

That night before I fell asleep, I thought this. If I go next time, it will be exactly the same again. And the time after that, exactly the same again. I couldn't tell whether that thought was sad or frightening. But one thing was clear. I was already dreading the next visit.

That night I thought again of something my uncle had said. If you go, you have to see it, don't you. I hadn't understood those words, walking the four days here. Now I thought I understood a little. My uncle had already known about this repetition. What it meant to see the same thing, every single time.

Even so, I decided not to give up on the next visit. Not yet.

I added that day's visit to the count. One, two.

I didn't know, then, when that number would stop.

(Continued in Chapter 12.)

Chapter 12. I Couldn't Apply

_Previously_ — The second visit hadn't differed from the first by a single word. The same greeting, the same pause, the same farewell. That night, though he was already dreading the next visit, Anemos decided not to give up. Not yet.

I let several days after that simply slip by.

I didn't count exactly how many days. Numbers show up this often in this record, and yet those days alone I couldn't count. It felt like counting them would mean deciding something, so I deliberately didn't.

I spent more time lying in the lodging house, staring at the ceiling. I hung the flask on the wall. Those days had the longest stretch, in all this time, of it not being in my hand.

The person next door knocked a few times. I only answered, and didn't go out.

"…Are you all right?" the person next door asked.

"…Yes. Just a little tired, is all."

It wasn't a lie. But it wasn't a complete answer either. I've written several times now that no one in this city tells lies. Over those days, I learned for the first time that you could go without lying and still not be honest.

The person next door came once more after that.

"…Have you eaten?" they asked.

"…Yes."

That was a complete lie. I hadn't eaten anything that day. In that moment I realized, for the first time since coming to this city, that I had told a complete lie. Trying to imitate the people of this city, I found I wasn't, after all, a person of this city.

In those few days, I went as far as that building's entrance twice.

The first was on the second day. I made it to the door, and then, seeing someone come out from inside, turned right back around. It wasn't anyone I knew. Just a stranger. I was afraid to see that person's face.

Even now I don't know exactly what that fear was. Maybe I was afraid of seeing what expression that person had just come out wearing. Something like the face of that older man from Chapter 7.

The second was on the fourth day. This time I got as far as taking hold of the doorknob. And let go.

I remember the feel of that doorknob. Cold, much like the one I'd felt at my mother's door. I took my hand off that cold.

I told no one about those two failures. Not the person next door, not the officer.

Over those days I thought of that older man from Chapter 7 many times. Three times in three years. I recalculated that number. I'd only gone twice so far. And already I was standing here, stuck in front of a third. By that math, I was faster than he was.

That calculation frightened me. The first time I saw that man, I'd thought he was a different kind of person from me. Someone who'd become that way from being worn down, from age, from years gone by. Over those days I came to see that thought had been wrong. He, too, must have started out exactly like me.

One morning I got up and packed the flask again.

I'd like to write that decision as some great resolve. In truth it wasn't. I got up simply because I couldn't lie there any longer. It felt like if I kept lying there, I would never go again.

I decided to apply for a third visit.

Three. I said that number to myself, once, in my head. One and two I had already counted. I thought it only natural to carry that number forward. I didn't know, then, how far from natural that was.

The application counter was in a different building from the waiting room. I'd passed that counter once, back when I first arrived in this city. At the time I'd thought it was just part of the information office.

A tree stood in front of the building. Much like the one I'd seen through the waiting-room window.

That day was unusually clear. I had never once seen bad weather in this city. No rain, no overcast sky, no strong wind. Every day, a sky of about the same brightness.

I thought even the weather was true to this city's nature. A place where nothing changed suddenly. Even the weather was like that.

I stopped for a moment in front of that tree. I thought about counting the leaves and gave it up. That day, counting felt meaningless. I didn't know, then, why that feeling came over me.

I stood a little longer in front of the door. Not going in, not leaving either.

Those few minutes are the ones I've gone back over more than any other, since. Standing in front of that door for those few minutes was, I think now, the real substance of this whole chapter. Everything else was only the result of those few minutes.

Standing at that door, I thought of the old man I'd met on the road to this city. The old man I wrote about in Chapter 1. The one who'd said his own family had someone here too.

"…Do you visit?"

"…I did, at first."

"…And now?"

"…I don't go."

I've already written that I didn't understand that answer when I heard it on the four-day road here. Standing in front of that door, in that moment, I understood that answer in my body. I had thought not going and not being able to go were different things. That day I learned how close the two really are.

I pushed the door open and went in.

There was no line at the counter. A clerk was sorting paperwork.

Seeing that empty line, I finally noticed. People applying for something new in this city, at all, were rare. Everyone here already lived according to what was fixed. Filling out an application meant you meant to change something. In this city, there wasn't much left to change.

"…Here to apply?" the clerk asked.

"…Yes. For a visit."

"…Which visit is this?"

"…The third."

The clerk nodded and took a sheet of paper from a drawer.

"…Write your name here, and the name of the person you're visiting. Simple."

Simple. I laughed a little inside at that word. I had never seen a procedure in this city that wasn't simple. Visits were simple. Rations were simple. Simple, here, wasn't praise — it was just a word describing a fact.

At the next window, someone was handing over another form. Something like a ration change request. That person handed it over and turned to leave in under thirty seconds.

I envied those thirty seconds. I think, by then, I already knew my own paper wasn't going to finish inside thirty seconds.

I took the paper and went to a small desk off to the side. There was one chair. I sat down.

A pen lay there. I picked it up.

There was nothing else on the desk. One inkwell, a few sheets of paper. I thought that plainness resembled the visiting room I'd described in Chapter 8. This city put in each room only exactly what was needed.

I had thought that restraint was consideration — meant to make deciding easier for a person. I see it differently now. It wasn't consideration. It was this city's nature. Here, there simply wasn't much that was needed to begin with.

The space for a name was at the top. I set the tip of the pen there.

Up to that moment I still thought there was no problem at all. Write my name, write the name of the person I was visiting, submit it, and that was that. I had already done that exact sequence twice. Nothing hard about it.

But the pen wouldn't move.

I tried, right there, to find the reason. My hand wasn't hurt. There was nothing wrong with the paper. I simply couldn't draw the next stroke.

I clenched and unclenched my hand a few times. My fingers moved just fine. The problem wasn't my fingers.

I've since put it this way to myself. My hand was fine. Some nameless part, one I can't even quite call the heart, wouldn't move. I still haven't found a name for that part.

Right then, a great many things passed through my head all at once. In no order.

My mother's "you've grown so much." Those words I'd heard again after only three days.

My brother's "Brother, you got taller." The line I've tried and failed, more times than any other in this record, to write again.

"…Bye now," heard at the end of the second visit. The two words that hadn't differed in the slightest from seven years before.

"…Because you're the kind of child who'd come." My mother had said those words identically, both times.

And my brother, touching the flask, asking, "…Brother, can I have this?"

"…Exactly the same." The words I'd said to myself in the waiting room came back to me too. From Chapter 9.

And the five words I had prepared for seven years. Let's go together now. I had never once managed to bring that phrase fully out into the open air.

I looked at the blank space on the paper. Anemos. I knew exactly how long it would take to write those letters. About twenty strokes, and it would be done.

I couldn't make those twenty strokes.

I took one deep breath. The same breath I'd taken before opening the door in Chapter 7.

Back then, that breath had opened the next door. This time it didn't. I didn't know the difference in that moment. The breath had the same shape, but the place it carried me to was the exact opposite.

I began the first letter.

I drew one circle.

And stopped there.

I looked at that circle for a long time. It was an unfinished letter. Not the start of my name, not the end — just one circle. Right then I thought that circle looked exactly like me. A shape that pretends to be the start of something, when really it leads nowhere at all.

I sat there for a long while, still holding the pen.

I tried to count that stretch of time. I couldn't. It was the second moment I failed to count. The first had been in front of my brother. This time I was alone.

I set the pen down.

I folded the paper again and slid it back toward the counter.

The clerk took it. Asked nothing. Just took it and put it in the drawer.

I've said, many times since, that that moment was the most frightening one of that day. If someone had asked why, I would have made up some answer or other. Because no one asked, I didn't have to give myself an answer either.

"…Come back another time." the clerk said. Not an angry voice, not a sympathetic one. Just a voice explaining procedure.

"…Yes."

I barely managed that one word.

"…Is there any way to know ahead of time, next time?" I asked, mumbling it out. It was a question with no meaning behind it. I just felt like I had to say something.

"…It is possible at any time." the clerk said.

That line. I had already written it down at the end of Chapter 1. It is possible at any time. I had read that line in the bulletin for seven years. I wrote then that it was a kind and a frightening thing at once. Hearing it spoken directly at that counter that day, I felt both of those meanings again, all at once.

I couldn't explain, right there, why I had done that.

Even writing this record now, I still can't, completely.

Since that day I've been asked this question many times again. By myself. Each time the answer came out a little differently, and each time it ended up back at the same place in the end. I don't know. That was the most honest answer.

It wasn't that I was afraid of my mother. It wasn't that I was afraid of my brother either. I still loved those two people. I love them now. But apart from that love, my hand simply would not move on that paper.

I didn't know then that these two things might be the same. Loving someone, and confirming that love once more by repeating it in exactly, precisely the same shape. I would only come to understand the difference between the two much later in this record.

I turned around.

Leaving the counter, I didn't look back.

I still sometimes imagine what that retreating back must have looked like. A sixteen-year-old with a flask on his shoulder, walking out and leaving the form behind. I've never seen that back with my own eyes. But what I did that day was, exactly, one thing.

I did nothing.

I pushed the door open and stepped outside. The tree was still standing right there. I never did count how many leaves it had.

I stood under that tree for a moment. I had no idea where to go.

Right then, for the first time, I truly felt that I had nowhere at all to go in this city. Not the lodging house, not my mother's room, not my brother's room — none of them, right then, was where I wanted to go. I just stood there and breathed.

A few people came and went from that building. Every one of them went in faster and came out faster than I had. Some came out without any paper in hand. Probably they'd come for some other business, not to apply, the way I had.

Watching them, I tried to figure out how long I'd been inside that building. I couldn't. I had no sense of how the time had passed. That was the first day I lost track of time. In a way different from counting.

Part One of this book ends here. I know that's a strange place to end it. Nothing happened, after all. No one was hurt, nothing was broken. I simply turned away from a counter.

And yet I think that turning away was, of everything I had done in this city up to then, the most honest act. I didn't know, in that moment, what I should do. And I didn't hide the fact that I didn't know. At least for that moment.

That night I didn't open the flask's cap. Even though my throat was dry, I didn't open it.

I decided to just let that thirst be. Not doing something was, that day, the only choice left to me.

That night, before I fell asleep, I thought of just one thing. I hadn't accomplished a single one of the reasons I'd come to this city. And yet strangely, that night, it didn't feel so heavy.

That very lack of heaviness was the strangest part of all. And I fell asleep holding onto that strangeness.

I didn't know, then, that this lack of heaviness would take on another name in the part to come. I won't say that name here first.

(Continued in Part Two.)

The Dry Valley, Part Two

Release

Chapter 13. Fifteen Years Old

_Previously_ — I went to apply for a third visit. I took the form from
the window. And then I couldn't write on it, and I turned away. I
couldn't explain to myself why.

It wasn't that my hand shook.

To be precise, my hand was fine. I held the pen properly. I got as far
as the line for my name. I started the first letter of Anemos, and
stopped there.

I tried, right then, to find the reason I'd stopped. It wasn't that my
mother's face had come to me. It wasn't that my brother's face had
come to me. It was the opposite, actually. No face came at all. What
I'd heard at the second visit and what I'd heard at the first played
back, identical, only in a different order.

I didn't know this then. I know it now. What stopped me in front of
that third application wasn't fear. It was that I already knew the
result. My body knew, before I did, that the third time wouldn't be
any different from the first, and it stood my hand still.

I set the pen down and pushed the form back toward the window. The
clerk took it without asking anything. That was the most frightening
moment of the whole day. No one stopped me.

After I turned away, I had nowhere to go.

I didn't want to go back to the lodging. I felt like if I went there
I'd only think about the form again. Instead I walked toward the
plazas. The Preserve had several, and they all looked more or less the
