same. There were benches, there were trees, and no one ever ran.

I tried, later, to count those plazas. I couldn't. They all looked so
alike that I lost track of how far I'd gotten. It was the first time
in that city that counting something had failed me.

As I walked I kept folding and unfolding the form in my hand. The
clerk hadn't said a word. Even when I'd pushed the form back across
the counter, no one had asked why. They'd just taken it and put it in
a drawer. That was stranger, somehow. If someone had asked me
something, I could have had an answer ready.

I thought a long time about that clerk's blank face. I learned later.
Here, it isn't rare for someone to change their mind. No — more
precisely, changing your mind isn't really a thing that happens much
at all. Everyone lives by what they first decided. By this city's
standard, my turning away from that window was probably the rare
thing.

I passed about three plazas. People went by. Everyone's stride was
about the same. Not fast, not slow. I didn't see a single person
running. It seemed like there was simply nothing to run for. It was a
city with no lateness, nothing to chase, nothing to flee.

Among those strides I felt how different mine was. I still walked
fast. It was a habit from outside. When you went to draw water, you
had to walk fast. If you were late, everyone else had already used up
the well.

At the fourth plaza I found a bench. It was in the shade of a tree. I
sat down there.

I still had the water flask with me. The one my uncle had packed for
me. I was the only person in this city who drew water at the ration
station and carried it strapped to my waist. Everyone else had no need
to.

I sat on the bench. I opened and closed the flask's cap, opened and
closed it. It was another habit I had instead of counting. Open, shut.
Open, shut.

"…Why do you carry that around?"

I looked up.

It was a girl. She looked about my age. She was standing beside the
bench, looking at my flask. There was nothing strange in the face
asking the question. Not mocking, not worried either. Just a face that
was curious.

"…To drink."

"…Water?"

"…Yes."

She tilted her head. It really was a face that didn't understand.

"…If you're thirsty you can just go to a ration station."

"…Sometimes I get thirsty while I'm out walking around."

"…Ah."

She didn't look satisfied with that answer. But she didn't press
further either. She just sat down beside me. She hadn't asked if it
was all right, she'd just sat, and strangely, it didn't feel
uncomfortable.

I learned later that this was something everyone in that city had in
common. They don't ask permission. Situations that call for permission
almost never arise there. Taking someone's seat, taking someone's
time — both are rare here.

"…I'm Eikon."

"…I'm Anemos."

"…That's an outside name."

"…Yes."

I didn't ask how she knew that. She must have known from the flask.

"…You're not from here." Eikon said.

"…No. I came for a visit."

"…Who?"

"…My mother and my brother."

Eikon nodded. There was no surprise in the nod. It meant people coming
for visits was common.

"…I came for a visit too. Here." I said it again. "…originally."

"…And now you're not?"

"…I don't know."

Eikon didn't think the answer was strange. She just waited for the
next thing. I couldn't say anything more. She didn't push either.

I remember that silence. Outside, if someone sat quietly next to you
for that long, you'd feel awkward and say something just to fill it. Eikon
didn't. She seemed like a person with no reason to feel awkward.

After a good while, I asked,

"…How long have you been here, Eikon?"

"…Seven years."

I was startled by the number. It was exactly the year my family had
been preserved.

"…So how old are you?"

"…Fifteen."

"…Even seven years ago?"

"…Yes. I was fifteen then too."

It took me a moment to understand what that meant. I'd known, in
principle. That being preserved means you don't age. My mother and my
brother were the same. But those were family, and family was the side
I'd wanted, wanted with all my longing, to stay unchanged.

This girl was a stranger. A stranger staying at exactly the same age
for seven years was strange in a different way.

"…Is it strange?" Eikon asked. She seemed to have caught it from my
face.

"…No. Just."

"…It's fine. Everyone finds it strange at first."

That way of talking stayed with me. It wasn't a hurt tone. It was an
explaining tone. As if she'd organized a reaction she'd seen many
times before and was simply informing me of it.

"…I'm sixteen now." I said. "My birthday passed not long ago."

"…So last year we would have been the same age."

I couldn't do that math in the moment. Eikon did it first.

"…When I was fifteen. I've stayed fifteen the whole time, and you must
have been fifteen last year too."

"…That's right."

"…So we were the same age last year. Now I'm a year younger."

She said it laughing. It was a laugh that found the thing genuinely
funny. I couldn't laugh.

I did the math again right there. It was correct. A year ago we would
have been the same age. But in that year, only I had moved. She had
stayed exactly where she was. I had, in effect, passed her by.

Whether passing her by was a good thing, I didn't know then. I still
don't, really, now.

"…What are you thinking about?" Eikon asked.

"…Nothing."

"…Your face is complicated."

The observation was accurate, so I couldn't say anything. Eikon looked
at my face for a while, then, as if she didn't much care either way,
turned her eyes back to the flask.

"…What do you do here, Eikon? All day."

"…Various things. Meeting people, walking, looking around."

"…You don't work?"

"…There's not really anything to do. There's not much here that needs
making."

"…So aren't you bored?"

Eikon smiled a little at the question. It wasn't a mocking smile. It
was an amused one.

"…It's been a while since I've heard the word bored."

I found the answer strange. It didn't sound like she didn't know what
bored meant — it sounded like she simply hadn't had occasion to hear
the word.

"…Did you used to know?"

"…Yes. Before I came here."

"…And now?"

Eikon looked up at the sky for a moment. Not a face searching for
something — more like a face just resting its eyes.

"…I guess now there's no reason to be bored."

I turned that answer over late into that night. No reason. As if she
meant boredom, and being tired of things, were feelings that only
arose when there was a reason for them. Outside, you got bored even
with no reason. Here, it seemed, without a reason nothing arose at
all.

"…Want to try a sip?"

I opened the cap and held it out. Eikon took it and drank. One sip.

"…How does it taste?" I asked.

Eikon made a face like she was thinking for a moment.

"…Just like water."

"…Isn't it strange, though? Isn't this the first water you've had in
seven years?"

"…What would be strange about it. Water's just water."

I didn't know what to say to that. It wasn't the answer someone who
hadn't drunk water in seven years should give. More precisely, it was
the answer of someone who'd never needed to.

Eikon handed the flask back.

"…Why do you keep carrying that flask around anyway? There must be
times the ration station's closer."

I wasn't ready with an answer to that.

"…I don't know. I just feel better holding on to it."

It wasn't a lie. But it wasn't the whole answer either. I'd never once
thought of throwing the flask away. That the water jar my mother had
filled the night before she left kept coming back to me — that, I
didn't tell her that day.

"…What about your family?" I asked. "Are they here?"

"…Yes. Both my parents."

"…Do you see them often?"

"…Sometimes."

"…Don't you miss them?"

Eikon's face looked at the question for a moment, as if peering into
it. She wasn't angry. The question itself seemed foreign to her.

"…Why would I miss them when I can just go see them?"

I was at a loss for that answer. Outside, missing someone arose
whether or not you could see them. Here, the two seemed bound
together into one thing. If you could see someone, there was nothing
to miss.

"…I couldn't see mine for seven years." I said. "So I kept missing
them the whole time."

"…Ah, that would be different, then."

Eikon said only that much and didn't go on. I was grateful for the
brief acknowledgment, and also let down by it. It was true that it was
different. But exactly what that difference was, she made no attempt
to find out.

I didn't feel, at the time, that she was dismissing me. It was the
opposite, actually. She honestly marked what she didn't know as not
known. Everyone in that city was like that. They didn't pretend to
know what they didn't. I thought that was kindness. Later I would
learn how far away that kindness came from.

Eikon tapped the flask lightly with her fingertip.

"…What are you going to do once you drink all this, though? Are you
going to be here a while?"

"…I don't know. Not yet."

"…Did you stop partway through writing your form?"

I looked at her, startled at how she knew. Eikon shrugged.

"…You have the face for it. People here get it sometimes too. The
face of someone who stopped in the middle of deciding something."

"…People here have that too, sometimes?"

"…Very rarely. There isn't much to decide in the first place, so it's
not common."

I didn't let that pass unnoticed. That there isn't much to decide.
There seemed to be something important folded into that sentence, but
that day I couldn't catch hold of what it was.

The sun had tilted a little. In this city that, too, felt like an
exact clock. The shadows always stretched at the same angle.

"…I have to go." Eikon stood.

"…Where to?"

"…Just. There's somewhere to be."

I didn't ask what that meant. I felt I didn't yet have the right to
ask.

"…See you again." Eikon said. It wasn't a parting courtesy — it was
said in a tone that meant it truly would happen.

"…Okay."

She walked off. I sat on the bench and watched her back. Her walk was
light. There was nothing hurried in it.

Watching that back, I had this thought for the first time. Among the
people I'd met in this city, she was the first who hadn't looked at me
with pity. The Curator, the ration-station clerk — everyone had, in
some way, handled me carefully. Eikon didn't. She just treated me as
someone she was curious about.

At the time, that ease felt good. Later I'd learn why that was so. She
had no reason to be careful with me. A person who lacks nothing has no
need to pity anyone else either.

On the way back to the lodging I passed the ration station. The clerk
saw me and gave a nod of recognition. Everyone recognized me once they
saw the flask.

"…Filling up again today?"

"…No. I've still got some left."

The clerk nodded and went back to their work. That person, too, didn't
look at me strangely. Only it was a different kind from Eikon's. That
person knew I was from outside and so didn't trouble themselves over
it. Eikon had simply been curious, regardless of who I was.

I didn't know the difference then. I know it now. One was a kindness
that acknowledged an exception. The other was the attitude of someone
for whom the very concept of an exception didn't exist.

I got back to the lodging and looked out the window. The city was
still quiet. A quiet with no sound of water in it. I'd started to grow
a little used to that quiet. That was what frightened me. Growing used
to it.

I didn't take the form back out that night. Instead I turned over the
things she'd said. To drink. What would be strange about it. Water's
just water. I guess now there's no reason to be bored.

Those words stayed with me strangely long. Each one, taken alone, was
true. But gathered together, something felt missing. What it was, I
couldn't put into words that day.

That day I noticed, for the first time, that she always paused
briefly before she began to speak. It wasn't that she wasn't in a
hurry. It seemed more like there was simply no reason to hurry in the
first place. I hadn't been in this city long, and already, without
noticing, I'd begun to talk the same way.

(Continued in Chapter 14.)

Chapter 14. I Don't Lack Anything

_Previously_ — I met Eikon in the plaza. Preserved at fifteen seven
years ago, she was still fifteen. When I asked if she wasn't curious
about the taste of water, she said it was just the taste of water.

Three days later I met her again.

In those three days I hadn't done much of anything. I went back and
forth between the lodging, the ration station, and the plaza. I walked
past the window twice, but I didn't go in. The third application, sitting
in that drawer, kept pulling at me, but I couldn't work up the nerve to
ask for it back.

I can count those three days. It was exactly three. But what I
thought about during them, I don't remember well. I think I was
probably trying not to think about anything at all. Trying doesn't
make a thing so, though.

I hadn't gone looking for her. I was sitting in the same plaza, on the
same bench, and Eikon came to me first. She walked up as though she'd
known I'd be there.

"…Carrying the flask again."

"…Yes."

"…Where are you off to today?"

I had nowhere in particular to go. I hadn't gone back to see my mother
and my brother since the second visit. The third application was
still sitting in the window's drawer.

"…I was just walking."

"…Want to come with me? I've got something I'm doing today."

I went along without asking what it was.

What Eikon did wasn't much of anything. More precisely, I wasn't even
sure I could call it doing something.

We passed a garden first — a place inside the city planted with
flowers and trees. Eikon met two people she knew there and talked a
while.

One was an older-looking woman. Looked older was the accurate way to
put it. Since she was preserved she couldn't actually have aged, but
her face gave that impression. The other was a boy who looked younger
than me.

"…This is Anemos. He's from outside." Eikon introduced me.

The woman looked at me and bowed her head slightly.

"…Here for a visit?"

"…Yes."

"…How lovely for you."

There was no malice in it. She meant it, honestly — that it would be
lovely. I didn't know what to say, so I just nodded.

I would hear that phrase a few more times after that day. How lovely
for you. That there was someone to visit — that there was still,
even, someone waiting — was a thing people in that city envied. I
hadn't known it was something to envy.

The boy didn't say much, just waved and went on. Eikon watched his
back and said,

"…He's never had much to say."

"…Are you close?"

"…Yes. I've known him a long time."

"…How long?"

"…I don't know. Five years, maybe?"

I was a little startled by how easy the answer was. It was the first
time I'd heard someone start a sentence about a five-year friend with
"I don't know."

After that we went somewhere like a library. There were a lot of
books. Eikon pulled one out, flipped through a few pages, and put it
back.

"…Going to read it?" I asked.

"…No. Just looked."

"…Not interesting?"

"…It's not that it's not interesting. I just won't read it today."

After that we just walked. It wasn't a walk with a destination. When
there was a road, we walked it; when there was a bench, we sat; then
we walked again.

While walking we passed a fountain. Watching the water shoot up and
fall, Eikon paused a moment.

"…Do you like that?" I asked.

"…Yes. It's nice to look at."

"…So you watch it every day?"

"…Often."

"…Doesn't it get old? Watching the same thing every day."

"…It's the same water, but the shape's different every time. Look."

I looked at the fountain again after she said that. It really was.
The water shot up in exactly the same direction each time, but the
shape it broke into was slightly different every time. I'd never
noticed that before.

I don't forget that moment. It was the first time I felt, concretely,
that the way she saw the world was different from mine. I saw
repetition as tedium. She found something different, every time, even
inside the repetition. Which of us is right, I still don't know.

I counted how many places we'd passed that day. One garden, one
library, two fountains, five benches. But not one of them had been a
destination. I'd never before seen a person walk for five hours with
no destination.

"…Aren't you bored?" I finally asked.

Eikon stopped walking and looked at me.

"…By what?"

"…This. Living like this every day."

"…It's fun, though."

"…What's fun about it?"

Eikon thought for a moment. It wasn't a face avoiding the answer. It
was a face genuinely searching for one.

"…Walking. Meeting people. Looking at books. All of it."

The answer was so simple I found it hard to believe.

"…But it's the same every day, isn't it? Was yesterday like this
too?"

"…Yes."

"…The day before too?"

"…Yes."

"…All seven years?"

This time Eikon actually laughed.

"…I don't remember every single day of the seven years, but they were
probably about like this."

I turned the answer over as we walked. Outside, every day had been
different. One day the well ran dry, one day it rained. One day
someone died, one day someone was born. If a day wasn't different, it
hardly felt like being alive.

There was none of that here. And yet Eikon wasn't bored.

"…Have you never wanted to do something new?" I asked.

"…Something new?"

"…Like, wanting to try something today you didn't do yesterday."

I could see Eikon taking the question seriously. She seemed to have
caught that I wasn't asking to tease her.

"…Not really. There's plenty to do today — why look for something
else?"

"…Just, out of tedium, I mean. Usually."

"…You'd need something tedious first, to be tedious."

I stopped at that. You'd need something tedious first, to be tedious.
It sounded like it didn't add up, and yet it was strangely exact.

That day, for the first time, I felt in my body that time moved
differently for people in this city than it did for me. My time was
headed somewhere. The next visit, the next decision, the next day.
Eikon's time went nowhere. It just was.

We sat again on a garden bench. The sun had passed noon.

"…What do you lack, Eikon?" I asked.

This time I wanted to ask more directly. The earlier questions hadn't
gotten me an answer.

Eikon gave the question a truly thoughtful look. She thought about it
a long time. I took that silence as a silence that was searching for
something.

"…I don't think there's anything."

"…Really?"

"…Yes. I thought about it, and there isn't."

"…Friends? Isn't there anyone you'd like to see more?"

"…If I want to see someone, I can just go see them."

"…What about someone you can't see?"

"…Is there such a person? Here?"

I was stopped short by the counter-question. There wasn't. Inside this
city, anyone you wanted to see, you could see. Not being able to see
someone was a thing that only happened to a person like me, from
outside.

"…What about your parents? Don't you ever think you'd like to live
longer, or stay with them longer? Nothing like that?"

"…If I want to be with them, I go see them. I'm not sure what it would
even mean to want to live longer here."

"…Growing up. Becoming an adult. Aren't you curious about that at
all?"

For the first time, Eikon looked a little amused.

"…What's good about becoming an adult?"

I couldn't answer. I genuinely didn't know. I'd been taught it was
good, but I'd never thought about why. All that came to mind was
getting taller, getting stronger, being able to decide more things on
your own. But trying to explain why that was good, I found myself at
a loss.

"…See?" Eikon said. "Even you don't know, Anemos. So why should I be
curious about it?"

I couldn't argue with that. I still can't. Why becoming an adult is
good — I didn't know then, and I don't know exactly now. I've only
lived believing that it was. Eikon didn't hold that belief at all.

"…Aren't you lonely?" I asked. It seemed like it might be more precise
than saying stifled.

"…Why would I be lonely?"

"…You have time alone too, don't you?"

"…Is being alone the same as being lonely?"

I was stopped short by that question for a moment. They weren't the
same. I'd had a lot of time alone too, but at some point I'd started
to feel lonely. I'd never separated the two, so I couldn't find an
answer.

"…I don't think they're the same."

"…Then why'd you ask me?"

"…Just, I've seen people sitting alone on benches when there aren't
many people around here. They looked lonely."

Eikon thought about that for a moment, then shook her head.

"…They're alone because they want to be. If they didn't want to be
alone, they wouldn't be."

"…Does it always go the way you want it to?"

"…Yes. Here it does."

I felt something like a wall in that answer. It wasn't a wall meant to
pick a fight. She was simply stating a fact as it was, but to me that
fact felt like a wall.

That day, for the first time, I thought it might be possible for
there to be no lonely people in this city at all. Loneliness is born
in the gap between what you want and what you have. A person with no
such gap has no room in them for loneliness to grow.

"…Doesn't it feel stifling?" I asked again. This time with a different
word.

Eikon looked straight at me. There was no defensiveness in her face.
It was a face that was truly curious.

"…By what?"

I couldn't answer.

If she asked me back what exactly was stifling, I didn't know what
I'd say. She wasn't shut in a small room. No one had locked her away.
She went wherever she wanted, as much as she wanted. She saw whoever
she wanted, whenever she wanted. She wasn't enduring anything.

I realized, in that moment, that I myself didn't know what I'd been
trying to ask. Stifling is a word you use when something is blocked.
For her, nothing was blocked. Asking someone with nothing blocked
whether they felt stifled was a question that didn't even hold
together as a question.

"…Never mind." I finally said. "I was just asking."

Eikon didn't press further. She just nodded and looked back up at the
sky.

After a long while I spoke again.

"…When I was outside, something was short every single day. Water was
short, and even sleeping I always wanted something more. It was hard.
But thinking back on it now, I think that was what kept me moving."

"…So lacking something is good?"

"…Not that it's good. Just, that it's what made me do things. Going
to draw water, learning a trade. I moved because I lacked."

Eikon seemed to take that seriously. Then she said,

"…So I don't need to move, then."

There was no sarcasm at all in that. It was simply a conclusion. I
couldn't find anything else to say in front of that conclusion.

I would bring that conversation back up again, even years later. I
wanted to tell her she was wrong. But I couldn't find where, not then
and not fully even now. I had no grounds to tell someone with no
reason to move that they should move.

On the way back to the lodging I made that question over again,
several times. Should I have asked what she lacked? Should I have
asked what she missed? Whatever word I put in, it felt like the same
answer would come back.

By what.

I would go on remembering those two words for a long time. In writing
this book, it's a line I return to again and again. She wasn't asking
to deceive me. She genuinely didn't know, and that's why she asked.
And that was what unsettled me longest of all.

I filled my flask passing the ration station. The clerk asked,

"…Did you walk a lot today?"

"…Yes."

"…Must have been tiring."

I didn't answer that. I wasn't sure whether it had been tiring or not.
My body was fine, but something kept pulling at me, and my steps felt
heavy.

On the way to the lodging I passed the preservation building where my
mother and brother were. I hadn't taken that road on purpose. It was
just the road I always walked.

I stood in front of the building and looked up at the window for a
moment. I didn't even know which window. I was always shown to the
same visiting room, but I'd never thought about where in the building
that room actually was.

That day, in front of that building, I asked this question for the
first time. Did my mother, too, lack nothing, the way Eikon did? Was
she not bored, not lonely, with nothing left to want? After I asked
that question, I stood there for a long while.

Back at the lodging, I opened and closed the flask's cap. That day I
did it maybe thirty times. Even counting was no use. My thoughts kept
going back to that same spot.

By what.

Out the window I could see the city's lights. All the same brightness.
Not a single one flickered. Outside, lanterns flickered often — from
the wind, from running out of oil, from a wick burning short. The
lights here were steady, as if they had no such reasons to falter.

Watching that steadiness, I thought of Eikon. She was like that light,
too. Not flickering because there was no reason to flicker.

I slept badly that night. That was strange — nothing had happened
that day that should have been especially hard. I understood it
later. I'd been searching, inside her answers, for something missing,
and no matter how I searched, I couldn't find it, and that was why I
couldn't sleep. That nothing was missing — that itself was what kept
me awake.

(Continued in Chapter 15.)

Chapter 15. A Sickness

_Previously_ — I spent a day walking with Eikon. I asked whether she
didn't lack anything, whether she didn't feel stifled. She asked me
back, "By what?" and I couldn't answer.

The next time, I went to find her first.

That morning I went to look at the fountain again. Something she'd
said had stayed with me. It's the same water, but the shape's
different every time. I stood in front of it a long while and watched
the shape the water broke into. It really was a little different each
time. But that only made me feel more stifled. She had found something
inside it, and to me it still looked like nothing but repetition.

Standing in front of that fountain, I thought this for the first
time. Maybe it wasn't that I couldn't see it — maybe I was looking for
something that never needed to be seen in the first place. That
thought didn't leave me all day.

I waited in front of the garden. I didn't know whether Eikon would
show up or not — we hadn't made any promise to meet. But about an hour
later she came walking up. There wasn't the slightest surprise in her.

"…Here again."

"…Yes. There's something I wanted to say."

"…You look serious today."

I didn't know what my face looked like. I touched it lightly with my
hand, though it wasn't as if that would fix my expression. I just did
it anyway.

"…Not serious, exactly. I just had a few things I was curious about."

Eikon tilted her head and studied me. Her eyes really were curious. In
front of that gaze I felt the words I'd prepared come apart. I hadn't
meant to be angry, or to press her — but looking at her face, there
was nothing left to do but speak plainly.

Eikon sat down beside me. She didn't press me to get on with it. I was
grateful for the waiting, and also weighed down by it.

"…Last time I asked you something strange. Whether you felt stifled."

"…Yes, I remember."

"…I couldn't answer back then, but I think I understand now. What I
was asking."

"…I," I began. "When I was outside, I always wanted something more.
When it didn't rain, I wanted rain. When it rained, I wanted it to
stop. Whenever I got something, I wanted something else after that."

Eikon listened quietly.

"…That was hard. But I can't really imagine what it would be like
without it. A life with nothing to want."

"…That's me." Eikon said. Her tone was even.

"…Yes. So I wanted to ask. Do you really never want anything? Not
even once?"

This time Eikon thought for longer. I felt that this silence was
different from the ones before. The earlier silences had been
silences trying to understand a question. This one felt like a
silence choosing whether or not to bring something out.

"…Once, I saw someone like that." Eikon said.

"…Like what?"

"…Someone who'd been here as long as I had. One day, that person
started wanting something."

"…What did they want?"

"…I don't know exactly. They couldn't really explain it either. They
just said something was missing. That what was here wasn't enough."

I leaned forward.

"…And then?"

"…That person got stranger and stranger. Didn't seem to sleep well,
wasn't the same even when they met people. Kept trying to be alone."

"…Were they sick?"

"…No. It wasn't sickness. It just seemed like they kept looking for
something. Something that wasn't there."

Eikon's voice stayed even the whole time. An evenness with no grief
mixed into it. I found that strange.

"…What about that person now?" I asked.

"…They applied for release."

That word reached my ears for the first time. Release.

"…What is that?"

"…Reversing preservation. Going back to how you were before."

"…Before?"

"…A living body."

It took me a moment to understand what she meant. Once I did, my heart
sped up.

"…Is that possible? Reversing it?"

"…Yes. It's possible. I heard it's not even that difficult."

"…Then why doesn't anyone do it? Everyone just stays here."

"…Because no one wants to."

I didn't understand that answer right away.

"…No one wants that? To become a living body again?"

"…Think about it. Here, no one gets sick. No one goes hungry. No one
dies. So why would you give that up and go back to a body that can get
sick again?"

I couldn't answer that question at all. As logic, it was correct.
And yet something kept catching at me.

"…So what happened to that person, then? Where are they now?"

Eikon gave a small shrug.

"…I don't know. There's been no word since they applied."

"…No one knows? What happened to them?"

"…Right. Word doesn't really travel from the people here to begin
with. Once you're released, you're off this city's roster."

There was something chilling in that answer. A whole person had
vanished completely, and it was explained this calmly, as if it were
nothing at all.

Outside, I'd watched people disappear many times. They disappeared by
dying. That was a sad thing, and everyone treated it that way. But the
disappearance I'd just heard about was different from that. This
person hadn't disappeared by dying — they'd disappeared in order to
live. And yet no one knew what had become of them.

In that moment I thought, for the first time, that in this city the
direction of disappearing ran opposite to outside. Outside, you
disappeared by dying. Here, you disappeared by living again. Whether
those two disappearances could carry the same weight, I couldn't
decide, then.

"…Isn't that sad?" I asked.

Eikon looked genuinely puzzled.

"…Why would it be sad?"

"…No one even knows what happened to them."

"…That person went because they wanted to. They applied themselves.
It's not something to be sad about."

I wanted to argue with that, but I didn't know where to begin. Nothing
in it was wrong.

"…I think that," Eikon went on, "is a sickness."

"…A sickness?"

"…Yes. Wanting something when you lack nothing. That's a sickness."

"…But why is it a sickness? It's just wanting something."

"…Wanting comes from lacking something, doesn't it? But here, there's
nothing lacking. And if you still want something anyway, isn't
something wrong with your body or your mind? Like someone who isn't
sick feeling as if they are."

I turned that logic over. The logic itself wasn't strange. It was, if
anything, clean. If you feel a lack when nothing is lacking, then
isn't the feeling itself what's wrong? Outside, I'd been taught the
opposite. That it's the person who can't feel lack who's strange. A
person who's full and keeps eating, a person who has plenty and wants
more. Grown-ups clicked their tongues at people like that.

Here, that standard was flipped entirely on its head. Feeling a lack
was the sickness.

I couldn't find an answer to that logic in that moment. I still
can't, fully, now. Even now, writing this book, I don't have a
sentence that truly beats Eikon's.

"…Then could I be sick too?" I asked. "The things I was just saying."

Eikon looked at me closely. There was worry mixed into her eyes. It
was an expression I hadn't seen before.

"…You're a bit different, Anemos."

"…How am I different?"

"…You're not from here. You said you really did lack things outside.
That's not a sickness. That was just a fact."

"…What about now, though? I'm here now."

Eikon didn't answer right away. She looked at me for a moment, as
though studying me.

"…I'm not sure, right now. If you keep wanting something even here, I
can't really judge that either."

I couldn't tell whether that answer was comfort or warning.

"…Did you think that person was sick too?" I asked again.

"…No. That person didn't think it was a sickness. They thought they
were right."

"…What about you?"

Eikon looked at me and said, evenly,

"…I think that person was pitiable. They caught a sickness they never
had."

"…Pitiable?" I repeated. "But you just said it wasn't something to be
sad about."

"…Yes. Both are true. Pitiable, but not sad. They went because they
wanted to."

I couldn't understand that distinction. How could something be
pitiable and not sad at the same time.

I would come to understand that distinction only later. To Eikon,
sadness was a feeling that came from losing something. But that person
hadn't lost anything. They had only gone to get something they wanted,
something they'd chosen themselves. Nothing lost, so no reason to be
sad — that was her arithmetic.

Eikon studied my face and asked,

"…Why do you look so serious?"

"…No reason. It's just — that story sounded a little frightening."

"…What's frightening about it? A story about someone who got sick?"

I couldn't answer that question. I didn't even know exactly what was
frightening about it. Only that evenness, that word sickness, kept
pulling at me.

"…Have you never once had that happen to you?" I asked. "Wanted
something more."

Eikon shook her head.

"…No."

"…Not even once? In seven years?"

"…Yes. You keep asking, but really, no. It's not a lie."

I knew it wasn't a lie. I'd already confirmed, several times over,
that no one in this city lied. That was exactly what made it more
frightening. Because it wasn't a lie.

"…Then maybe that person was like you, at first." I said. "Before they
wanted something."

Eikon paused for a moment at that. It was the first hesitation I'd
seen in her.

"…Maybe so."

"…Then someday, could it be you too?"

"…I don't know. That, I don't know either."

In that short answer I heard, for the first time, something that
wasn't certainty. Until then, every answer she'd given had been
certain. This one wasn't.

I remember that moment for a long time after. It felt like I'd seen a
tiny crack in something that had looked whole. Hardly enough to call a
crack — no bigger than a fingernail mark. And yet it was the largest
thing I saw that whole day.

The sun began to set. The garden's shadows lengthened.

"…I should get going." Eikon said, standing.

"…Okay."

"…Be careful not to catch the sickness, Anemos."

She said it and laughed. It was a joke. A real joke, meant as a joke.

I couldn't laugh.

I kept turning that line over that night. Be careful not to catch the
sickness. She really believed it was a sickness you could catch. And
I, the moment I heard it, thought that maybe I already had.

Walking back to the lodging, I repeated that word to myself several
times. Release. I'd heard it for the first time that day. I didn't
know, then, that it would become the heaviest word of everything I
would hear in this city from then on.

I couldn't sleep even after reaching the lodging. I thought about my
mother and my brother. And I thought about that stranger. The one
who'd caught the sickness. The one who had felt a lack in a place with
nothing lacking.

I didn't know, then, who that person was. Not their name, not their
face, not their age. And yet that person kept overlapping with me. I
hadn't been in this city long myself, and already I kept wanting
something.

I lay in bed fiddling with the flask. Opening and closing the cap. I
counted to about twenty-something and stopped. Counting was no use
that night either.

That night I had this thought for the first time. That I wanted to
give my mother a body again. But the moment I thought it, Eikon's
words followed right behind it. Because no one wants that.

My mother might not want it either. That possibility entered my head
for the first time that night. I tried to push it away. It wouldn't
be pushed away.

It was the same with my brother. I'd never once asked an eight-year-old
what he wanted and what he didn't. I'd just assumed I could take him
along however I wanted. That assumption felt strange, that night, for
the first time.

I hardly slept that night. The moment I opened my eyes the next
morning, I'd decided one thing. To go find the Curator and ask exactly
what release was. That became the next chapter of this story.

Out the window the city's lights were still lit, steady as ever. I
tried to count them and gave up. They weren't countable. They all ran
together, so I couldn't tell where one ended and the next began.

I paced the room a few times. It was a small room; a few steps and
you hit a wall. I circled, and circled, and circled again. Walking
wasn't going to produce an answer, but it was better than sitting
still.

That night, for the first time, I felt this city was frightening. Up
until then I'd only felt it was strange. Quiet, without water, with
everyone looking so alike. But what I felt that night was a different
kind. This city wasn't a bad place. That was exactly what made it
frightening. No one here wanted to leave, and I felt like the strange
one, for finding that strange.

I only closed my eyes around dawn. I slept briefly, then got up and
filled my flask. That day I walked not toward the ration station but
toward the Curator's office. That road felt shorter than any road I'd
walked until then. I hadn't quickened my pace, but I arrived in what
felt like no time at all.

(Continued in Chapter 16.)

Chapter 16. The Word Release

_Previously_ — Eikon told me about someone who had once wanted
something more. That person had eventually applied, and after that
there had been no word of them. She called it a sickness. I
couldn't sleep that night.

The morning air was cold. In this city the air was usually about the
same temperature always, but that morning it felt unusually cold.
Maybe it was because I hadn't slept.

I tried, as I walked, to put last night's thoughts in order. They
wouldn't come into order. What Eikon had said kept surfacing out of
sequence. It's a sickness. Because no one wants that. Why would it be
sad.

That morning, for the first time, I thought this. Whether what I now
wanted to know was really for my mother's sake, or for my own. I
couldn't make that distinction then. I walked on without making it.

I thought, suddenly, of the water jar my mother had filled the night
before she left. I hadn't drunk from it for three days. If I drank it,
it would be gone. Even then, I'd been trying to hold on to something.
It was similar now. Only this time it wasn't a water jar — it was a
person.

As I walked, I heard the sound of gravel under my feet. It was one of
the few sounds this city had. I counted my steps as I listened to it,
and gave up somewhere past a hundred. Counting wasn't going to change
anything.

On the way to the Curator's office I asked a few people about that
word.

The first person I asked was the ration-station clerk. Filling my
flask with water, I asked, almost in passing.

"…Do you know what release is?"

The clerk's hand stopped.

"…I've heard of it."

"…What is it?"

"…I don't know exactly either. I heard it's the procedure for
reversing preservation."

"…Have you done it? Or anyone you know?"

"…No. No one."

It wasn't a lying face. They really didn't know.

The second person I asked was an old man I ran into at the plaza. He
was sitting on a bench, so I sat beside him and asked.

"…Have you heard of something called release?"

The old man looked at me for a long while, then said,

"…I've heard of it."

"…Do you know what it is?"

"…I know the procedure. But I've never once actually seen anyone
who's done it."

"…Why not?"

"…Because no one wants it."

I already knew that answer. It was exactly what Eikon had told me.
And yet, coming from someone else's mouth, it felt different. It
seemed less like one person's thought and more like a fact this whole
city held in common. If one person had said it, it would have been
that person's opinion. Two people saying the same thing felt less
like an opinion and more like this city's rule.

"…Have you ever thought about it yourself, sir? Release."

The old man laughed at that question. It wasn't a mocking laugh. Just
a puzzled one.

"…Why would I think about it."

I stopped walking when I heard that answer. Why would I think about
it. That line seemed to summarize every answer in this city. No one
said it was a bad thing. They just said there was no reason to think
about it.

The third person I asked was a little different. A middle-aged woman
I met at the garden entrance. She said she was on her way to visit her
child. When I asked her the same question, her expression stiffened
for a moment.

"…Why are you asking that?"

"…Just curious."

"…Is it because of family, by any chance?"

I was startled by that question. She seemed to have read something in
my face.

"…Yes. My mother and my brother are here."

The woman nodded, then said in a slightly lower voice,

"…I thought about it once myself. A long time ago."

"…And?"

"…I only thought about it. I never went any further."

"…Why not?"

"…Weren't you afraid? Just thinking about it?"

The woman stopped walking for a moment at that question.

"…It wasn't so much fear. I understood quickly that thinking about it
would do no good."

"…Why would it do no good?"

"…My child was three then. Still three now. What could you ask a
three-year-old?"

I felt struck from behind by that, somehow. I didn't know exactly
where it caught, but it felt like there was another wall inside this
woman's words that I still didn't know about.

She didn't answer that question any further. She just smiled and went
on her way. I didn't notice, that day, what that smile was hiding.

I would come back to that smile later. It wasn't a smile hiding
something. It was simply that she had nothing more to say, and smiled
because of it. People in this city didn't get angry or fumble for
words when they had no answer. They just smiled.

I didn't ask a fourth person. Instead I asked myself. As I walked, I
kept repeating the same question. Why was I holding on to this word
so hard? No answer came. With no answer coming, my feet kept walking
toward the Curator's office anyway.

I counted those four as I walked. The ration-station clerk, the old
man, the woman, and myself. All four had said the same thing, each in
a different way. No one wants it. There's no reason to think about
it. There's no one to ask. I had heard all three of the others, and
still my feet didn't stop.

The Curator's office was the building where I'd applied for my first
visit. Past the window and down the inner corridor was a small room,
where you could meet the Curator.

I'd already been in that room once. The first time I arrived in the
city, when the visiting procedures were explained to me. The Curator
I'd met then had been kind, and had looked tired. That impression
still remained.

The receptionist asked,

"…What brings you in today?"

"…I have something to ask the Curator."

"…Is it visit-related?"

"…No. Something else."

The clerk wrote my name on a form, the same way as any other visitor,
as if there were nothing unusual about it at all. I found that strange,
somehow. Looking back later, that clerk's hand, not yet knowing how
heavy the question I was about to ask was in this city, moved with
the calmest touch of anyone that day.

The clerk went to check something in the back for a moment. I sat and
waited in a chair in the waiting room. It looked like the same seat
I'd sat in the first day. I couldn't even tell whether it had been
months ago or weeks ago. In this city, time didn't count itself well.

In that waiting room, I realized for the first time that I didn't
know exactly how many days I'd been here. I was the one who counted
things, but since coming here, that had stopped working. The days
were too alike to tell apart.

Besides me, there were two other people in the waiting room. One was
an older man, sitting quietly with a folder resting on his knees. The
other was a young woman, tapping her knee with her fingertips again
and again.

Watching those fingertips, I wondered whether she, too, was anxious
about something. But looking again, it didn't seem like anxiety — it
seemed more like a habit. Her expression was calm. That in this city
a body's motions and a face's words could differ from each other — I
noticed that for the first time then.

Each time a name was called, one person went into the inner room.
Watching their faces when they came out, they all looked about the
same as when they'd gone in. No one looked greatly changed.

I thought that was strange, at the time. Outside, if you went into a
government office and came back out, your expression always changed.
Whether the news was good or bad. Here, people didn't change. Probably
because whatever news they heard here fell within the range they'd
already expected. I was the only one about to walk into that room
carrying a question outside anyone's expected range.

The clerk came back and said,

"…Come in."

The Curator was seated inside the room. The same seat, the same
posture as the first time I'd seen him. He smiled slightly at me.

"…It's been a while. Are the visits going well?"

"…Yes. They're going well."

The Curator studied my face for a moment. His eyes had the look of
someone trying to read something. I found that gaze uncomfortable and
also, somehow, reassuring. At least this person didn't look at me
carelessly.

On the desk sat several stacked folders, all with covers of about the
same color. My eyes went to one of them for a moment before I looked
away. It felt like peeking at someone else's papers.

"…What brings you in today?"

I started to bring out the question I'd prepared, and hesitated a
moment. I didn't know why putting that word into the air felt so
heavy.

"…I wanted to ask what release is."

The Curator's expression shifted, very briefly. It wasn't surprise,
and it wasn't discomfort. It looked like something older than either
of those. I couldn't read, at the time, what it was.

The room went quiet for a moment. I couldn't even hear leaves
rustling outside the window. In this city, even the wind was quiet.

"…Where did you hear that word?"

"…From someone I know. Without a real explanation of what it is."

"…From whom, if I may ask?"

"…A girl named Eikon. I met her here."

The Curator nodded slightly at that name. He seemed to know it.
Whether that was because this city was small, or for some other
reason, I didn't know.

"…Eikon is a good friend." The Curator said, with a small smile.
"I've known her a long time."

"…How long?"

"…Seven years. You end up seeing a lot, in this seat, over that
time."

Hearing that, I tried, for a moment, to imagine what this person must
have seen in this room over seven years. I couldn't quite manage it.
Seven years was old enough to have seen every visit, application, and
refusal this city had had since it began.

"…That friend of yours didn't tell you anything bad, Anemos. If
anything, she told you honestly. Everyone here talks that way."

"…I know. I know it isn't a lie. That's exactly why I was more
curious."

The Curator said nothing for a moment. He bought time by pretending to
straighten the papers on his desk. I felt that silence was a silence
preparing an answer.

The Curator stopped straightening the papers and looked at me
directly.

"…Anemos." He finally spoke. "This isn't a question to be asked
lightly."

"…I know. I still want to know."

"…Why do you want to know?"

I couldn't answer that question right away. Why I wanted to know, I
didn't clearly know myself. It felt premature to say it was for my
mother — I hadn't confirmed anything yet. I just felt I had to know.

"…I don't want to stay not knowing."

The Curator looked at me for a while. That gaze felt long.

"…You're asking because of your mother and your brother."

"…Yes. Probably."

"…Is there a reason you said 'probably'?"

I couldn't answer that question right away either. I wasn't even sure
myself whether that was really it. Whether it was for my mother's
sake, or because I couldn't bear not doing something.

"…I don't know. I just felt like I had to know."

The Curator heard that answer and nodded. Something like resignation
was mixed into that nod.

"…All right. I'll explain it to you. But there's something I want to
say first."

"…What is it?"

"…Once you've heard this explanation, you can't go back to not having
heard it."

I didn't quite understand what that meant. But my body tensed before
I did.

"…That's all right. Please tell me."

The Curator still hesitated a moment longer. I saw that hesitation for
the first time then. In this city, people generally didn't hesitate.
If they knew the answer, they said it right away; if they didn't,
they said so right away. This man knew the answer and was hesitating
anyway.

"…This isn't a common thing for me to talk about." The Curator said.
"I've been here for years, and this question has rarely been put to
me this directly."

"…Doesn't everyone ask?"

"…Some do. But most, once they've heard it, don't come back."

"…Why not?"

"…That's something I'd rather you hear for yourself, and judge for
yourself."

"…Is it that frightening a story?"

The Curator didn't answer that question right away. He tapped the
edge of the desk lightly with his finger. It was the first time I'd
seen someone in this city make a gesture that looked that restless.

"…I'm not sure frightening is the right word. But it isn't an easy
story."

I let that warning pass lightly, at the time. Why the Curator had
bothered to say it first, I would only come to understand properly
chapters later. After hearing what came next, I could never go back
to who I had been before.

Outside the window, the shadows shifted a little. There was no sound
of the sun moving, but the light in the room changed, little by
little. I watched that change and waited for his answer.

The Curator straightened in his seat. He pulled one folder toward
himself but didn't open it. He just rested his hand on top of it.

"…I'll start from the beginning." The Curator said. "It's a simple
procedure. Simple, and not simple."

I wanted to write that contradiction down exactly as he said it. Not
understanding what it meant made me want to, more.

"…What do you mean?"

"…You'll understand once you hear it."

I set the flask on my knees and wrapped both hands around it. There
was no particular reason. I just wanted something to hold on to. The
flask's surface was cold. That cold was the only sensation in that
room I could actually feel in my body.

I still remember that posture. Both hands wrapped around the flask. I
sat in that room in that posture, waiting for what would come next.
And what came was, in small ways, different from what I'd expected,
and different from what I'd feared.

The Curator drew a breath. And began to speak.

(Continued in Chapter 17.)

Chapter 17. It Can Be Reversed

_Previously_ — I asked the Curator what release was. He warned me
that once I heard the explanation, I couldn't go back to not having
heard it. I said I wanted to hear it anyway, and the Curator began
to speak.

The light in the room had turned a little more yellow than before.
It was the only proof that time was passing.

The Curator took his hand off the folder. And looked at me directly.

"…Release is possible."

He said that first. I held my breath and listened.

"…The procedure isn't difficult either. You fill out the form, wait a
few days, and undergo the operation. Nothing about it is blocked."

I read that sentence over to myself, several times. Possible. Not
difficult. Nothing blocked.

"…Really?" I asked. My voice shook.

"…Yes."

I nearly stood up out of my seat. Something inside me came loose all
at once. For seven years I hadn't known any way to "wake" my mother
and my brother — and there was a way. And not one that was blocked,
not one with a difficult procedure either.

In that moment I felt like I was back at nine years old. That day
I'd sat behind the hill until the sun went down. Back then there had
been nothing I could do. But now it was different. There was
something I could do. And it wasn't even hard.

My grip tightened on the flask. My hand opened and closed without my
noticing. The habit of counting something didn't come this time.
There was nothing to count. There was only the feeling that
everything was already settled.

I didn't know, then, exactly what to call this feeling. I know now.
It was hope. The first hope I'd felt in a concrete shape in seven
years. And how thin that hope was, I wouldn't know until I sat in
this same room again, a few days later.

"…So I can apply right away, today?"

"…The application itself, yes, that's possible."

The Curator's tone was strangely careful. I didn't notice that
carefulness at the time. I was too happy.

"…What's the procedure? Where do I get the form?"

"…Wait a moment, Anemos."

The Curator raised a hand to stop me.

"…Before I go over the procedure, there's something you need to know
first."

I stopped short at that. Something that had just come loose tightened
back up again.

"…What is it?"

"…You need to know exactly what release reverses, first."

"…Isn't it reversing preservation? That's what you just said."

"…That's right. But what that exactly means — everyone gets it wrong,
at first."

I didn't know what that meant. Reversing something. Going back to a
state without preservation. I didn't understand what was complicated
about that.

"…A preserved person, in their current state, speaks, remembers,
recognizes you, Anemos." The Curator said. "That's true. It isn't a
lie."

"…Yes. I know."

"…Release takes that person out of that state. Back, into a living
body again."

"…That's a good thing." I said. "It's living again."

The Curator looked at me for a moment. There was something complicated
in his eyes. I couldn't tell whether it was sorrow or carefulness.

"…Yes. It's living again."

He said it while looking down at his hands. That gesture looked to me
like someone measuring something — how much to say, how far to go.

"…If my mother lives again," I said, "she'll eat beside me, sleep
beside me, grow older. That's what happens, isn't it?"

"…Yes. That's what happens."

"…That's what I wanted. That's exactly what I've wanted for seven
years."

The Curator nodded at that. Even nodding, his eyes didn't look away
from me. But what was in those eyes wasn't only agreement. I felt that
difference in the moment, but I couldn't put a name to it.

He said that much and stopped. I waited for what came next. But the
next thing didn't come right away.

"…But?" I pressed.

"…But I'll tell you that in the next chapter."

I didn't find that sentence strange at the time. I thought he was
just tired and sparing with his words. Looking back now, the Curator
was, in that moment, being careful with me. I think he believed that
if he told me all of it at once, I wouldn't be able to bear it.

Unable to stand the silence, I asked again.

"…Where do I get the application form, anyway?"

"…You can get it here. Only —"

"…Only what?"

The Curator opened a drawer and took out a single sheet. He didn't
hand it to me — he set it down in front of himself.

"…The application itself isn't difficult. Name, relationship,
subject's information. That's all there is to it."

I looked at the paper. It really did look simple. It had only a few
fields.

Name. Relationship to the subject. Subject's full name. Subject's
preservation number. And a single signature line at the bottom. That
was all.

I traced each field with my eyes, one at a time. Writing my own name
in the name field wouldn't be hard. In the relationship field I could
write "son." In the subject's name field I could write my mother's
name. And I thought I'd need to write one more of these, with my
brother's name in it.

Looking at that form, I thought this for the first time. Could a
sheet of paper this short really hold the weight of seven years?
Looking back now it's a laughable question. The paper was never meant
to hold the weight. It was only a road it let something pass along.

"…Is that all? I just write this?"

"…Procedurally, yes."

I let that phrase, procedurally, pass by unnoticed at the time. Later
I would learn it was the heaviest phrase in this whole chapter.

"…Why doesn't anyone do it?" I asked. "When it's this simple."

The Curator didn't answer that question right away. He smoothed the
edge of the form with his fingertip.

"…What's simple is the procedure. The decision isn't simple."

"…What decision?"

"…I'll tell you that later."

I felt a small stifling at that answer. But hope was bigger than that
stifling. The fact that there was a procedure, that it wasn't blocked,
was so large that I could hardly hear anything else.

Writing this passage now, I want to grab that earlier self and shake
him. Why didn't I press harder? Why did I let "the decision isn't
simple" pass by like that? The answer is simple. I had just seen hope
for the first time in seven years, and hope makes a person hurry. It
doesn't make a person doubt. At the time, that seemed only natural.

"…If the procedure is this simple," I asked again, "why is there no
one in this city who's been released? From what I've heard, anyway."

The Curator's expression showed a small surprise at that question.

"…It's not that there's no one. It's just extremely, extremely rare."

"…How rare?"

"…In all my time in this seat, you could count them on one hand."

I wanted to ask for the exact number, but I was afraid to ask. I
didn't know, at the time, where that fear came from. I know now. My
body already knew, before I did. That the number wasn't a good
number.

"…I want to write it today, even." I said.

"…Anemos."

"…Yes?"

"…Don't be in a hurry."

That struck me as strange. I'd waited seven years already. I thought
I had every right to hurry. This man didn't know my seven years. He
didn't know the three days I'd gone without drinking water, the
seventeen funerals, the promise I'd broken — "I'll come back later" —
and never kept.

But in that moment, I didn't say any of that out loud. I had a vague
sense that it wasn't because he didn't know those things that he was
telling me not to hurry.

"…Why shouldn't I hurry? If it's something that can be done, isn't
it better to do it quickly?"

The Curator looked at me for a moment, then said this.

"…This isn't the kind of thing that's better done quickly."

I didn't fully understand that, at the time. Not understanding it,
some corner of my mind kept holding on to the good news anyway.
Possible. Not difficult. Nothing blocked.

"…Have you seen a release, Curator?" I asked. "Actually seen one."

The Curator closed his eyes for a moment, then opened them.

"…I have. Several times."

"…What was it like?"

"…That's hard for me to say right now."

"…Why?"

"…Because you don't know the whole of it yet, Anemos. If I tell you
only part of it, you might misunderstand."

I felt a little stifled by that answer, but I didn't press further.
I knew he wasn't doing this to toy with me. No one in this city ever
lied. That concealing and lying were different things — I sensed that,
vaguely, in that moment.

The Curator held the form out to me. His fingertips lingered on it for
a moment.

"…You can take it with you. Just don't write on it today." The
Curator said.

"…Why not?"

"…Because it won't be too late to write it once you've heard the
rest."

I nodded, obediently, at that. I'd been told not to hurry again, but
in that moment it didn't bother me much. I took the form and tucked
it into my coat. Its weight was so light I could hardly feel it was
real.

I was still smiling as I walked out of that room. The Curator never
once smiled with me. I didn't notice that difference at the time. I
should have. I didn't.

"…Come back next time." The Curator said. "I'll tell you the rest
then."

"…What's the rest?"

"…What release truly means."

I nodded at that. I was curious, but for now, what I'd heard today
was already enough to make me happy. I stood and said my goodbye.

"…Thank you."

The Curator nodded. There was no smile on his face.

Walking out of the room, I looked at the copy of the application in my
hand. Name, relationship, subject's information. Looking at those
fields, I imagined writing my mother's name into them.

I smiled the whole afternoon that day. I smiled at the ration-station
clerk, smiled greeting people I passed in the plaza. I thought I
should tell Eikon this news when I saw her. Thinking that, my steps
were unusually light that day.

That lightness disappears completely in the next chapter.

I went straight to the garden from there. I looked for Eikon. Luckily
I found her sitting on a bench.

"…Eikon."

"…Your face is different today."

"…I have good news."

Eikon looked at me with an interested expression.

"…What is it?"

"…Release is possible. The procedure's simple too. The Curator told
me himself."

I said that expecting she'd be glad the way I was. But Eikon's
expression barely changed.

"…That's already known."

"…What?"

"…That it's possible. That's not new."

I felt the air go out of me all at once. To me it was news that
overturned the world. To her, it was something she'd already known
all along. That the same sentence could carry such different
temperatures felt strange to me.

"…Then why hasn't anyone told me?"

"…Because you never asked. People here don't tend to bring up things
that aren't needed."

The sun slanted in through the garden trees. In that light I looked
at Eikon's face again. There was no change in her expression. Not
joy, not surprise, not disappointment — just her usual face.

"…Aren't you glad about this news?" I asked.

"…Why would I be glad? It has nothing to do with me."

"…It's glad news for me."

"…I know. I can tell from your face."

She said that, looking at me closely. There was something like worry
mixed into her eyes. It was a look I hadn't seen before.

"…But I'm a little worried." Eikon said.

"…About what?"

"…The Curator didn't tell you everything, did he? Just the
procedure."

I was at a loss for words at that question. She was exactly right.

"…How did you know?"

"…He always does that. He never tells you everything at once."

"…Why?"

Eikon shrugged.

"…I don't know either. But he must have a reason for doing it that
way."

I let that comment pass by unnoticed that day. It was only when I sat
in that room again a few days later that I learned the reason. And
once I knew the reason, I came to understand, too, why Eikon hadn't
been as happy as I was that day.

Eikon pointed at the flask.

"…Give me a sip."

I opened the cap and held it out. She drank a sip and handed it back.

"…How does it taste?" I asked. The same question as last time.

"…Just like water."

"…Same answer as before."

"…Same water."

I laughed at that. She laughed too. That laugh was the easiest moment
between us that whole day.

On the way back to the lodging I took the copy of the application out
again. A fold had already formed in it, from being held too long in
my hand.

I imagined writing my own name into the name field. Son, in the
relationship field. My mother's name, in the subject's name field.
Just imagining it made my heart race.

I slept well that night. It was the most comfortable night's sleep
I'd had since I came to that city. When I opened my eyes the next
morning, I felt like the world had changed. I didn't open and close
the flask's cap once that day. There was nothing to count, nothing to
bear. How short that feeling would turn out to be, I couldn't have
imagined then.

(Continued in Chapter 18.)

Chapter 18. They Become Able to Die Again

_Previously_ — I heard from the Curator that release was possible.
The procedure was simple too. I took the application and was happy.
The Curator said he'd tell me the rest next time.

Two days later I sat in that room again.

In those two days I took the application out and put it back about
three times. It wasn't that I was wavering over whether to write on
it. I just wanted the paper in my hand. I even traced the spot where
my mother's name would go, with my fingertip.

I still remember those two days exactly. They were among the
happiest two days of my life. And that they were also the last time I
would ever be happy in so simple a way — that, too, I know now.

I didn't wait long in the waiting room this time. The receptionist saw
my name and showed me in right away, as though she'd known I was
coming.

The corridor leading to the room felt long that day. It wasn't more
than a few steps, but each step felt like it was preparing me for
something. I didn't know what.

The Curator was sitting in the same seat as last time. He nodded when
he saw me. He didn't smile.

"…You're here."

"…Yes. I came to hear the rest."

The Curator pulled a folder toward himself. This time he opened it.

"…I told you last time. That release means returning that person to a
living body."

"…Yes."

"…What that means, exactly — I'll tell you today."

I set the flask on my knees and wrapped both hands around it. The
same posture as last time.

"…A released person becomes, again, a fully living body. They
breathe, blood moves through them, an injury hurts, they age. They go
hungry, they go thirsty, and when they're tired, they sleep."

"…Yes, that's a good thing, isn't it."

The Curator looked at me for a moment. In his eyes I felt something
heavy approaching.

"…And from that moment," the Curator said, "they become able to die
again."

At first I didn't understand what he meant, in a strange way. To die.
Wasn't that obvious? If you're alive, someday you die. I'd known that
since I was very small.

"…Right. That's just natural, isn't it. Everyone who's alive is like
that."

"…Anemos." The Curator said. "The people here right now don't die."

I looked back over that sentence.

"…They don't die?"

"…No. In the preserved state, they don't die. They don't get hurt,
they don't fall ill, they don't age. That's what Perpetuity does."

I already knew that. I'd already seen it — that my mother and my
brother hadn't changed one bit in seven years. But that this connected
to the words don't die — I felt that, truly, for the first time, right
then.

"…So my mother right now," I said slowly, "is in a state where she
can't die?"

"…Yes."

"…But if she's released?"

"…From the moment she's released, she becomes a body that can die
again."

I repeated that sentence silently to myself. Becomes a body that can
die again. It was a strange sentence. Becoming able to die sounded
like gaining something, and yet at the same time I already knew it
was a loss.

The room went quiet. In that silence I heard that sentence again and
again in my head. They become able to die again.

I've gone back to that moment countless times since. Even now, writing
this book, I can still hear that sentence. They become able to die
again. I couldn't admit, right away, that it was bad news. Admitting
it took time.

"…So," I said. "If I get my mother back, I could lose her again?"

"…Yes. That's right."

"…How soon? Can she live a long life, at least?"

"…No one knows that. She'd become the same as everyone else. She
could have an accident, she could fall ill, she could live a long,
long life. She'd become just the same as what you saw outside,
Anemos."

Something in me caved in at that answer. There wasn't a single
certainty left. My mother, right now, was certain. Nothing had
happened to her in seven years, and nothing would happen to her going
forward. Release would make that certainty disappear.

I thought of the seventeen funerals I'd counted. The old man, the
neighbor's child, my father's distant relative. All of them had been
alive, and then they'd died. That was what being alive meant. It meant
that someday you joined that list.

In that moment I asked myself this question for the first time.
Whether I truly wanted to put my mother on that list. That I couldn't
immediately answer yes to that question is something I'm ashamed of,
even now, thinking back. Ashamed, and yet an honest hesitation.

"…Even so," I said. My voice cracked. "Even so, she'd be alive. She'd
really, truly be alive."

"…That's right."

"…Then I still want to do it."

The Curator nodded at that. There was no anger in his face, no attempt
to stop me.

"…Why are you so certain, Anemos? That your mother would think the
same way?"

"…That's," I started to speak, and stopped. Had I really been
certain? I asked myself that question for the first time. "…I don't
know. I just assumed, of course, that she would."

"…I understand. That feeling. But I still haven't told you
everything."

"…There's more?"

"…Yes. The most important part is still to come."

I drew a deep breath. My body knew, before I did, that something
heavier was coming.

The Curator poured a glass of water and drank it. Watching that
motion, I felt that even he found this conversation hard. He must
have done this many times, and yet each time didn't seem to get any
easier.

"…Anemos, everything I've told you so far has been the procedure.
From here it's the real reason. Why no one ever manages to see an
application through to the end."

"…Release," the Curator said, "requires the consent of the preserved
person themselves."

"…Consent?"

"…Yes. You can write the application, Anemos. Because you're family.
But that alone isn't enough. The subject themselves must consent
before the procedure can go forward. One more signature is needed.
Not your signature — the subject's signature."

That didn't surprise me much.

"…Isn't that only natural? It's their own life."

"…That's right. It's only natural."

The Curator said that and paused for a moment. He looked down quietly
at his hands on the desk.

"…But here's why that becomes a problem."

"…A preserved person," the Curator went on, "lacks nothing. They
aren't sick, aren't hungry, aren't lonely, aren't afraid. There's
nothing they need."

"…Yes, I know. I've met someone like that too. She told me she
lacked nothing."

"…Then try asking that person for their consent. Do you know what
they'll say?"

I couldn't answer.

"…When you ask for their consent," the Curator said, "most of them
ask you this.

Why?"

I heard that and couldn't say a word.

I truly couldn't say anything at all. I tried to open my mouth, and
nothing came out.

I've tried to rewrite this scene more times than any other in this
book. I wanted to write it more dramatically. I wanted to write that I
argued back, that I said something. But that would be a lie. In that
room, I truly said nothing at all.

I didn't know how much time had passed. There was no clock in the
room. I only gauged the time by the feeling in the fingertips holding
the flask. I sat there until my fingertips went numb.

The Curator waited for me. He didn't press.

"…Why, they ask?" I finally managed.

"…Yes. Why should they become a body that can get sick again? Why
should they become a body that can die again? It's good now — why?"

I tried, hard, to find something to argue back with that question.
The harder I tried, the less came out.

"…That's," I said. "That's not living, is it. That's not really
living."

"…That's true too," the Curator said. "But it doesn't land, for the
person themselves. They already feel that they're doing fine. They
speak, they remember, they meet people. If they ask you what living
is, then, if that isn't it — I can't answer that either."

"…And," the Curator added, "if you say that in front of them, they'll
answer you like this. Then what am I now?"

I imagined that question turned on me. My mother, looking at me, asking
that. Just imagining it closed up my throat.

"…I can't answer that question either." I admitted.

"…It isn't only you, Anemos. No one can."

I tightened my grip on the flask. My knuckles went white.

"…So," I said, "no one is ever able to be released? If none of them
give consent?"

"…That's right. That's why there are many applications and almost no
completions. Most stop right in front of the subject's consent."

"…What do you think, Curator," I asked, "the people who made this —
why do you think they built it this way? Why did they make consent a
requirement?"

The Curator thought about that a long while.

"…I think it was respect. At the start, at least."

"…Respect?"

"…Yes. It's their body. Family shouldn't be able to change a person's
body just as they please. That's probably why consent was made
mandatory."

I nodded at that. It made sense. And at the same time, it felt more
frightening for making sense.

"…The people who first made this can't have meant harm." I said. It
was as much a thing I was saying to myself.

"…That's right. They didn't mean harm. It was probably a very good
intention, if anything. Meant to keep anyone from reversing a person
carelessly, to make sure that person's own will was respected."

"…But that respect," the Curator went on, "ended up becoming a door
that, in the end, no one walks through. Asking for the consent of a
person who lacks nothing is the same as leaving a door open while
already knowing no one will walk out of it."

I sat there a long while, unable to say anything.

I think that was the day, in that room, that I finally saw this city
clearly for the first time. Up until then I had suspected the city was
hiding something. That it was fake, a trick. But it wasn't. This city
hid nothing at all. It had simply arranged things so that no one had
any reason to leave. And that was far more complete than hiding
anything ever could have been.

"…So what can I do?" I asked.

"…You can ask. Your mother. Your brother. I can't promise you an
answer."

"…And if they refuse?"

"…Then the application stops there. There's no way to force it
forward."

"…What if it's not a refusal, just — no answer at all? If they just
keep asking back, 'why'?"

"…That's a refusal too. Not answering is the answer."

That sounded cruel to me. But I knew he wasn't saying it to be cruel.
It was simply a fact.

"…I'm still going to ask." I said.

"…Of course. You should ask. I'm not stopping you. This is something
you have to go through yourself, Anemos."

"…Then why are you looking at me like that?"

The Curator paused for a moment at that question.

"…Like what?"

"…Like you're worried."

The Curator heard that and smiled faintly. It was a smile I hadn't
seen before. A sad smile.

"…That's right. I am worried. But that's not a reason to stop you.
I don't have the right to stop you, either."

My legs wavered slightly as I stood. I didn't know if it was from
sitting too long, or for some other reason. At the door, the Curator
said one last thing.

"…Anemos."

"…Yes."

"…This isn't me telling you to give up. I just want you to go in with
your eyes open."

I nodded at that. What exactly I should have been braced for, I didn't
know at the time.

As I left the room I looked again at the application in my hand.
Name. Relationship. Subject's full name. Subject's preservation
number. The signature line.

That signature line looked different to me now. It wasn't a field
for my signature — it was a field that needed my mother's.

That day I didn't go to the ration station, didn't go to the plaza. I
went straight to the lodging. I shut the door and sat looking at that
form for a long while. Why. Those two syllables kept circling in my
ears.

Until then I'd thought I'd spent seven years preparing. That day I
learned that all of that preparation had been for a different
question entirely. I had prepared for how to find my mother. That my
mother might not want me — for that, I had never once prepared at
all.

Outside the window, the city's lights came on, one by one. I started
counting them and gave up again. That day, counting anything was no
use.

I opened the flask's cap. I drank a sip of water. The water felt
unusually cold. This was what being alive was. Feeling cold as cold.
And, someday, that feeling stopping too.

That night, for the first time, I became afraid of seeing my mother
again. Until then there had only been longing. From that night on,
fear grew alongside it. Fear that I would ask, and hear that answer:
why. Fear of not knowing what would become of me, if I heard it.

I go into the next chapter without having overcome that fear. I
didn't overcome it and go. I simply carried it with me. Looking back
now, carrying that fear with me was, itself, the best I could do at
the time.

(Continued in Chapter 19.)

Chapter 19. Why

_Previously_ — I heard from the Curator what release required. Once
released, a person becomes a body that can die again. And it
requires that person's own consent. He told me that when you ask
someone who lacks nothing for their consent, most of them ask back,
"Why?" I couldn't say a word.

After I walked out of that room in Chapter 18, for days I heard only
those two syllables — why. When I fell asleep, when I opened my
eyes. They sat on top of every other thought I had.

I still don't know what to call those days. Too frightened to call
them days of preparation, and yet I kept preparing something, too
frightened to call them only days of fear. Probably they were both. I
meet that kind of time more than once in this book.

It took me three days to file for a visit.

To be precise, I could have filed that same day. The three days were
the time I spent standing at the counter with the form in my hand and
turning back.

I didn't count those three days. My habit of counting didn't help me
that time. I learned then, for the first time, that there is a kind
of fear that can't be turned into a number.

During those three days I saw Eikon once. Not in the garden — in
front of the ration station. I was filling the flask when she came
up.

"…Haven't seen you around lately," Eikon said.

"…I've had some things to think about."

"…What things?"

I didn't answer right away. I didn't want to talk about the
application. But Eikon didn't press. She just looked at my flask.

"…You've been opening and closing that a lot lately," she said. "I
can tell by the sound."

That surprised me a little. I hadn't even noticed I was doing it.

"…I guess so."

"…People trying to decide something do that. People here don't have
much deciding to do, so most of them wouldn't know. I learned it
watching you, Anemos."

I couldn't smile at that. Eikon studied my face and went on.

"…Are you going to ask her? Your mother."

I nodded at that.

"…When?"

"…Day after tomorrow, probably."

Eikon chose her words for a moment. It was a different kind of
silence from her usual one. She usually put a thought straight into
words. This time she was choosing something.

"…What are you going to ask her?" she finally said.

"…Whether she wants to do it."

"…That's all?"

"…There's more, after that. I've been preparing it for seven
years."

Eikon heard that and said nothing back. I couldn't tell if that
silence was worry or indifference.

"…Why aren't you answering?" I asked.

"…No reason," Eikon said. "I was going to say I hope it goes well. But
I'm not sure anymore what that would even mean."

Something cold moved through me at that. If even this girl didn't
know what "going well" meant, then what, exactly, was I walking
toward?

I didn't hold on to that question properly, at the time. I should
have. If I had, I might have prepared a little differently the next
