day. But I let that coldness pass through me and go. Going back over
the words I'd prepared, in order, felt more urgent.

On the fourth day, I finally filed the form. My hands weren't
shaking. That itself felt strange.

The visiting room was the same room as always. One wooden table, two
chairs, one window. I had the grain of that table's wood memorized by
then.

The door opened and my mother came in.

"…Anemos."

"…Yes, Mother."

My mother smiled. The same smile as the last three visits. A smile
full of gladness, without a flaw in it.

"…You're looking well."

"…So are you."

This exchange, too, was identical. I'd grown used to the sameness by
then. But that day the sameness reached me differently. For the first
time, some part of me tried to guess what lay behind that smile.

I unhooked the flask from my belt and set it on my knees. I touched
the cap with my fingertips. I didn't open it. I just needed something
to hold on to.

I've already written, more than once in this book, that moment of
holding the flask like that in that room. I did the same thing in
front of the Curator. Writing this account, I counted how often that
habit shows up. Already several times, in this book alone. When I'm
afraid, I hold something in my hand. It was the only method I knew.

My mother saw me fiddling with the flask and said nothing about it.
She just waited. A waiting that didn't hurry, the way people in this
city wait. That day I found the waiting both a comfort and a fear. I
would have to say it eventually.

We sat down. My mother spoke first.

"…How have you been lately?"

I didn't answer the way I usually did. Usually I just said I was
doing fine. That day was different.

"…Mother." I said. "There's something I need to tell you."

My mother's expression shifted, slightly. Not a worried expression.
Just an attentive one.

"…What is it?"

I tried to bring out the words I'd prepared. Seven years' worth of
words. But they lost their order in my mouth.

"…I — Mother, you can." I stumbled over it. "You can be turned back
into a living body again."

My mother heard that and said nothing for a moment. I was afraid of
that silence.

"…What do you mean?" my mother asked.

"…There's something called release. It reverses preservation. Then
you'd really be alive again, Mother. With me."

Saying that, I felt my own throat close up.

My mother looked at me for a moment. There was surprise in her eyes.
Not a bad surprise. Just the surprise of hearing something
unexpected.

"…So there is such a thing," my mother said. "I didn't know."

That was the first moment I felt something strange. My mother hadn't
known, either. Seven years in this city, and she hadn't known there
was a way to turn her own body back. Was it because no one had told
her, or because it made no difference to her whether she knew?

"…Would you want to do it?" I asked.

This question was the point I'd prepared for. I'd hovered outside a
counter for three days for this question, waited seven years for it.

My mother heard the question and looked at me steadily. Not an angry
face, not a sad one. Just a curious one.

"…Why?"

I lost my breath at that single word.

It was different from the Curator's "Why?" That one had been a
businesslike question. My mother's "Why?" was gentle. A tone that was
truly asking out of curiosity. That made it more frightening.

"…Why would I need to become that again?" my mother asked again.
"There's nothing wrong with me right now."

"…But," I said, "this isn't really living, is it."

"…Isn't it?" my mother asked back. "I'm talking with you right now,
like this. I see your face, I hear your voice. If this isn't living,
then what is?"

I couldn't answer that question. Just as I hadn't been able to answer
in front of the Curator, this time too I said nothing at all.

I knew, in that moment, that I'd hit the same wall a second time.
The first time, in front of a stranger. This time, in front of my
mother. The same wall, and the second time hurt more.

My mother watched my silence and reached out to touch the back of my
hand. Her hand was warm. As warm as it had been seven years ago.

"…Anemos. I'm not in pain right now. I'm not afraid, I'm not sad. You
know that too."

"…I know."

"…So why does that become a problem?"

I couldn't answer that question either. I'd felt that it was a
problem, and yet I couldn't explain, right there, why it was one. The
more I searched for words to explain it, the more those words sounded
like they were about my own situation, and not hers.

"…Mother." I finally managed to say. "I waited seven years."

"…I know."

"…I want you to live with me again. Eat meals together, sleep under
the same roof. Just an ordinary life."

My mother heard that and closed her eyes for a moment, then opened
them.

"…That means a lot to me. It really does."

"…But?"

"…But I already see you like this, don't I. Not often, but I can
always come."

"…That's not the same as living together."

"…What's different about it?"

I lost my words again at that question. I'd thought I knew what was
different, but when it came time to actually say it, nothing came
out.

"…Anemos." My mother said my name. Not with anger in it. Like that
morning — a voice that was asking something of me.

"…Yes."

"…Why do you want this so much?"

I hadn't expected that question. My mother wasn't asking why for her
own sake — she was asking why for mine.

"…I," I said, "want you to be alive again."

"…That's not an answer. That's what you want. It isn't a reason."

I had nothing to say to that. She was right.

"…I'll think about it," my mother finally said.

That surprised me a little. It wasn't a refusal.

"…Really?"

"…Yes. I'll think about it. But I can't promise anything."

"…Why?"

This time it was I who used those two syllables. The question I'd
heard from the Curator, I threw it back at my mother this time.

My mother smiled faintly at that.

"…I don't know that yet myself. I'll have to think about it to find
out."

My mother turned her eyes toward the window for a moment. Past that
window there was always the same tree. I'd already seen that tree
many times. Even on windless days, its leaves stirred slightly. I'd
thought that strange once, but that day the thought didn't come.

"…Are you going to ask your brother too?" my mother asked, turning
back to me.

I hadn't expected that question.

"…Yes. I mean to ask him."

"…He's only eight."

"…I know. But it's his own matter, so I have to ask him."

My mother looked at me for a long time at that. A look I couldn't
tell apart — worry, or something like pride.

"…What did you know, when you were eight?" my mother asked.

I lost my words at that question. I thought of myself sitting behind
the hill at nine. What had I known, back then? Even now I don't know
exactly.

"…I don't know."

"…Neither do I," my mother said. "That's part of why I'm a little
worried."

"…About what?"

"…About what you'll do, once you hear his answer."

I had no answer to that either. My mother was already worrying about
a conversation I hadn't even had yet. I didn't find that frightening
so much as strangely comforting. For the first time in seven years,
my mother was worrying ahead of time, for my sake.

"…You don't have to worry," I said. There was no basis for it.

"…That's the kind of thing people say without any basis," my mother
said, smiling.

That smile put me a little at ease. As long as there was a smile,
nothing had collapsed yet — or so it seemed.

A notice came that the visit was ending. We said goodbye the way we
always did.

"…I'll come again," I said.

"…Yes. Come again."

My mother smiled and waved. The same gesture as the last three
visits. Watching that gesture, I felt that nothing had changed. The
content of our conversation had been entirely different, but the way
we parted was exactly the same.

Leaving the visiting room, I felt the strength go out of my legs. I
leaned against the wall for a moment.

I stood there a long time. I didn't count how long. I didn't want
to. I just wanted to feel the coldness of that wall, nothing else.

My mother hadn't refused me. That, somehow, made it heavier. If it
had been a refusal, I would have grieved and it would have been over.
"I'll think about it" was a sentence that let me neither grieve nor
be at ease.

That night I took out the words I'd prepared, again. Seven years'
worth of words, piled up. That night I finally admitted, properly,
that not one of them had been of any use.

Back at the lodging, I took out the copy of the application again. I
smoothed the crease with my fingertip. In the name field there was
still only my name. The signature line was empty.

I looked at that blank line for a long time. My mother's "Why?"
seemed to hover over it, still. I wanted to fill it with something.
But there was nothing to fill it with.

I folded the form back up. And for the first time, I decided to count
the words I'd prepared over seven years, one by one. I counted them
off on my fingers. Five. Five sentences.

Let's go home now. I missed you. This isn't really living, is it. I
want you to be alive again. And the last one, held so long that it
had stopped being a sentence and become something closer to a weight.

I counted all five of them that night, but I couldn't bring myself
to take any of them out. To take one out I'd have had to hold it in
my hand, and I felt certain that the moment I did, it would break. I
decided to take them out one at a time, the next day. Even if I had
to watch them break, I had to watch.

Outside the window, that same steady light was still burning. I
didn't count it. That night, counting anything was no use. I've
already written that once, earlier in this chapter. It's worth
writing again here, because that night truly was like that.

I fell asleep with the five words still hooked on my fingers. The
next morning those fingers were still folded the same way. It seemed
that even in sleep, some part of me had gone on counting. Five
fingers, five sentences. I met that morning without having properly
looked at even one of them yet.

(Continued in Chapter 20.)

Chapter 20. The Things I Had Prepared

_Previously_ — Instead of an answer, my mother said, "I'll think
about it." That night I counted the words I'd prepared over seven
years. Five of them. I fell asleep without having properly looked
at even one.

The moment I opened my eyes that morning, I unfolded my fingers
first.

Five. Exactly as I'd folded them the night before. I decided not to
pick any of them at random. I would go in order. The oldest one
first.

I still remember that morning's resolve clearly. Deciding on an
order gave me, at the time, the feeling that I was in control of
something. I wasn't in control of anything at all, and yet the order,
at least, was mine to decide.

Counting the five of them gave me a strange feeling. Everything I'd
counted up to then had been out there, outside me. Funerals, steps,
the plaza, laughter. What I was counting this time was inside me. It
was the first time since I'd come to this city that the things I
counted had moved inward.

Looking back now, that difference is the whole point of this
chapter. Counting what's outside is observation. Counting what's
inside is interrogation. That morning, I began to interrogate
myself.

The oldest one was these five words.

Let's go home now.

Earlier in this account I wrote that I'd been preparing this line
since I was nine. That I'd prepared it while digging the well, while
watching a funeral. But taking it out again that morning, I found
that this line had never come with a why attached. To the nine-
year-old I'd been, it was simply obvious. You go together because
you're family. It was a sentence that needed no reason.

Now it was different. The moment I said this sentence out loud, my
mother would ask. Why. I didn't already have that answer. A reason
that hadn't been needed at nine had become necessary at sixteen. A
strange thing. The longer time went on, the more certain my love had
become, and yet the words to explain that love had only grown more
scarce.

I said the five words over to myself, several times. Not out loud —
only inside my mouth. Each time I said them, the words grew a little
lighter. Not lighter because they'd had weight and lost it, but
lighter because they'd become useless.

I recalled the place where I'd first held that line. Beside the well
in the settlement, the third stone wall. The same spot where my
brother had once stopped walking. I tried to imagine what expression
the nine-year-old me had worn there. Probably none at all. Back then
there'd been no need for an expression. It had simply been a fact. We
were family, and family goes together.

Now I had to add an explanation on top of that fact. A fact that
needed explaining might, perhaps, no longer be a fact at all. I
stopped myself in the middle of that thought. It seemed to be going
too far.

That morning I set this first line down completely, for the first
time. Not thrown away. Only, I admitted that it could no longer serve
as an answer. This line belonged to a time before the question. Now
was a time that had the question. That the same words could carry a
different weight depending on the season — that was something I
learned for the first time that morning.

The second and third had already been tried. I'd used them both the
day before.

"This isn't really living, is it." My mother had asked back what,
then, our talking together right now was, if not living. I hadn't
been able to answer.

"I want you to be alive again." This one had ended more quickly.
My mother had pointed it out on the spot. That's what you want,
that's not a reason.

I looked the two of them over again. Looking them over didn't change
anything. What broke yesterday didn't mend itself today. I spent
about an hour just confirming that fact.

I thought again of the old man I'd met on the road. The one on the
way to the settlement who didn't go to visit his family. "Because
it's always the same." I felt I understood that a little now. My
mother had smiled the same way yesterday and today. Only I kept
bringing different words each time. And the different words kept
breaking on the same spot. It was in that moment I first had the
premonition that I, too, might end up like that old man. That someday
I might run out of words to prepare, and after that, might simply
stop going at all.

I don't write that hour out in detail here. There's nothing to
write. A stretch of time spent staring again at something already
broken looks, from the outside, like doing something, but is really a
time of doing nothing at all. I learned that fact that day. And even
learning it brought little comfort.

Leaving the lodging, I opened and closed the flask's cap over and
over. I did it about three times and stopped. Counting, opening and
closing — both were just gestures to get me through the time. The
gestures alone solved nothing.

The fourth was a line I hadn't tried yet.

I missed you.

This was the one I'd felt most sure of. It wasn't logic. It was
simply a fact. I really had missed my mother for seven years. There
seemed to be no place for a why to get a grip on this one. To have
missed her was to have missed her. True no matter what anyone said.

I applied for another visit, to test the line. This time the wait
wasn't long. The attendant led me to the same room I always sat in.
My mother was standing at the door, waiting for me.

"…Here again," my mother said. There was no surprise in it.

"…Yes."

"…What's the matter?"

"…Nothing," I said. "I just missed you."

My mother smiled at that. A soft smile.

"…I'm right here," my mother said. "You're looking at me right now,
too."

For a moment I couldn't answer that at all.

"…So I'm looking at you right now, too," my mother went on. "Isn't
missing someone what you say when you can't see them?"

I heard that and froze on the spot.

She was right. Completely right. I was looking at my mother right
now. My mother was looking at me. We were, right now, in the middle
of looking at each other. "I missed you" was a sentence that no
longer held true, in this very moment.

I watched these five words break during that short visit. The line
I'd been most certain of broke the most easily. The line I'd believed
was fact and not logic turned out, I learned then, to hold true only
under a certain condition. When the condition was gone, the fact was
gone too. I hadn't realized that in this city, that condition was
always met. The sentence that a visit is possible any time — I'd read
it since Part One, and only now did I truly understand what it
meant.

"…So when can I see you next?" I finally managed to ask.

"…Whenever you file for it."

"…That's it? If I can see you any time, then what more is it that
I'm even asking for?"

My mother didn't answer that question. Instead she looked at my face
for a long time. There was something like worry in her eyes.

"…Anemos, are you all right?"

I couldn't answer that question either. Whether I was all right or
not, I didn't know myself.

My mother reached out and touched the back of my hand again. The
same warmth as last time.

"…Don't prepare too much," my mother said. "Words get heavier the
more you prepare them. I'd rather you came to me light."

I didn't understand what that meant right away. I understood later.
My mother had already guessed what I was doing.

I left the visiting room and walked toward the garden. I thought
Eikon might be there. She was. Sitting on her usual bench, looking up
at the sky.

"…That face again," Eikon said, looking at me.

"…What face?"

"…The face of someone with something broken in them."

I sat down beside her. And I told her what had just happened. That
I'd said I missed her, and my mother had asked back whether she
wasn't right there in front of me.

Eikon listened to the whole thing and stayed quiet for a moment.

"…Your mother's right, isn't she," she said.

"…I know. That's what makes me angrier."

"…Angry?"

I was surprised by the word that came out of my own mouth. I hadn't
meant to say angry.

"…No, not angry," I corrected myself. "Just — it's true, and I don't
want to accept it."

Eikon looked at that distinction with something like curiosity.

"…Why don't you want to accept something true?"

"…Because if I accept it, then I'm wrong."

"…What's so bad about being wrong?"

I lost my words at that question. Outside, being wrong was
something shameful. That's how the adults had taught it. But in this
city, apparently even that idea sounded foreign.

"…Have you ever been wrong about something?" I asked.

Eikon thought for a moment.

"…I don't know. I don't think I've had much occasion to be."

"…Why not?"

"…If you don't want anything, there's nothing to be wrong about.
You have to expect something, to be wrong."

That answer left me at another loss for words. I'd been losing my
words in front of this girl's sentences a lot, lately. This time was
no exception.

"…Today," I said, "I confirmed that everything I'd prepared was
wrong."

"…So what do you do now?"

"…I don't know."

Eikon heard that answer and looked toward the flask. She held out
her hand. I opened the cap and passed it over. She took a sip and
handed it back.

"…How does it taste?" I asked. A question that came out of habit.

"…Just like water."

That answer made me smile a little. In a day that had broken
entirely, that one familiar answer, at least, had stayed the same.

"…By the way," Eikon went on, "you said five."

"…Right."

"…You've talked about four. That leaves one."

My body went stiff at that. I'd never told her about a fifth one, and
she'd counted exactly right.

"…That one's not yet," I said.

"…Why not?"

This time I didn't answer. Eikon didn't press either. She just looked
back up at the sky. I was grateful for that silence. In this city,
someone not pressing you could be kindness, and it could be
indifference too. That day, whichever it was, I was grateful for it.

Walking back to the lodging, I looked at my last finger.

The fifth line still had no name. I'd never turned it into a
sentence before. That day, for the first time, I tried.

I rewrote it several times. Once it came out like this: Then I'm
alone. I revised it. Without my mother, I become alone. I revised
it again. Because I don't want to be alone.

I started to read each of these sentences out loud, one by one, and
stopped myself. It felt like saying them aloud would make them real.

I thought about telling Eikon first, at least. In front of her,
almost anything was relatively easy to say. But it seemed this one
line, even in front of her, wouldn't come out. The other four had
been words aimed at my mother. This one was aimed at myself. Bringing
out a line aimed at yourself, to someone else, first — that took a
different kind of courage.

I wrote only the first character of that sentence in the corner of a
sheet of paper. I. My hand stopped there. I didn't write the rest.
I stared at that one character for a long while, then folded the
paper and put it in the drawer.

That night I never finished the fifth line. I know now that it
wasn't that I couldn't finish it — it's that I didn't want to. This
one line was the one I couldn't test in front of my mother. If this
one broke, I would have nothing left. The other four, I could at
least pretend were for my mother's sake. This one had been, from the
start, for my own.

That night I opened all five fingers again. Four were already
broken. One I was still holding closed. Holding it, only — I had no
courage to use it.

Writing this book now, I can finish that fifth sentence. The boy I
was that night could not. This account is, among other things, the
road toward that finishing. I don't want to get too far ahead of
myself, so I'll leave it at this much here. A day comes, eventually,
when this last finger has to open too.

I looked out the window. The light was as steady as ever. Watching
it, I thought about what I should do the next day. I had no more
words left to bring to my mother. Four of the five I'd prepared had
broken, and one I'd hidden away myself.

That night, for the first time, I thought of my brother. I knew I
would have to ask him the same question I'd asked my mother. I also
remembered what my mother had said in the visiting room, with worry
in her eyes. What you'll do, once you hear his answer.

I brushed that worry off lightly, at the time. My brother was
eight. I thought, if anything, it would be easier with him. I thought
there'd be no need for complicated logic, no words that could break.
How wrong that thought was, I would learn the next day.

That night, for the first time, I decided not to prepare any words.
I'd tried five, four had broken and one I'd hidden, and now there
were no more words left to prepare. Strangely, that felt like a
relief. Having nothing left to prepare felt, somehow, like having
nothing left to fail at.

How wrong that feeling was, I didn't know then. Having no prepared
words didn't mean there was no failure to have. It was closer to the
opposite. Going in unprepared, I would hear, that place, an answer
of a kind I had never written down before.

I folded my five fingers again. This time with no order, all at
once. And I turned off the light. In the dark I laid that hand on my
chest and lay down. Five fingers, still there, carrying five weights.

(Continued in Chapter 21.)

Chapter 21. My Brother's Answer

_Previously_ — The five words I'd prepared for my mother broke, one
by one, or were hidden away by my own hand. I decided to go and see
my brother next, carrying no prepared words at all.

Before I went to my brother, I went first to see the Curator.

There was something I needed to ask. Whether an eight-year-old could
give consent at all. Saying that question out loud, I felt how odd
it was, even to myself. Why had I assumed that something not even an
adult could do might somehow be possible for a child?

The Curator didn't answer right away. He turned over a few pages of
paper.

"…It's not a procedure that stops at age," he finally said. "Consent
is consent. Old or young."

"…But?"

"…There's no but. Not procedurally."

I felt I'd heard that phrase, "not procedurally," somewhere before.
I'd already heard it once, in Chapter 17. Then as now, there was
always something more behind those words.

"…Anemos," the Curator said. "What an eight-year-old understands
isn't mine to judge. That isn't my place."

"…Then whose place is it?"

"…Yours. You're his brother."

That weighed heavily on me. It was a sentence that handed the weight
over to me. I wasn't ready to take it up yet.

"…What if," I asked, "my brother says no?"

"…Then it stops there. You can ask again later, but there's no way
to force it forward."

"…What if he says yes?"

The Curator thought a little longer about that question.

"…If he says yes, the procedure moves forward. Except."

"…Except what?"

"…We confirm it once more, on our end. Directly with the subject.
Not through the family."

Hearing that, I felt some relief. It sounded like the judgment
wouldn't rest on me alone.

"…How do you confirm it?"

"…We simply ask. Whether they truly want it. But Anemos," the
Curator looked straight into my eyes, "that confirmation isn't
everything. It confirms the form of it. It doesn't confirm the
content."

I didn't fully understand what that meant, at the time. I would
understand it a few hours later.

The corridor to my brother's room was the same one I'd walked in
Chapter 10. I'd already counted its steps once before. This time I
didn't count. I had no room left over for counting.

The door opened. My brother was standing by the window. He ran to me
when he saw me.

"…Brother!"

"…Been doing well?"

"…Yeah. Did you get taller again?"

I smiled at that. There was no way I'd grown taller in a few weeks.
But he asked every time, in complete earnest. I knew it was this
child's only way of measuring time. My height was his clock.

My brother took my hand. That small hand. Still the same size.

"…Want to see this?" he said. The stone. The one he'd picked up by
the well.

"…Still have it, huh."

"…I carry it all the time."

I took the stone in my hand for a moment. Then I sat down. I had
something to say today.

"…Listen," I began. "There's something I want to ask you."

"…What?"

I'd thought all night about how to explain it, but I never found the
right words. In the end I started with the easiest thing.

"…Would you like to be able to grow again?"

My brother's eyes went wide.

"…I can grow?"

"…Yeah. There's supposed to be a way."

"…Can I get tall like you?"

"…Yeah."

My brother's face lit up. Watching that face, I felt my heart drop.
I hadn't expected he'd be glad about it so easily.

"…But," I went on, "if you do that, some other things come with it."

"…Like what?"

I stopped for a moment. I never did find, all the way through, how
to say this part.

"…You could get sick. If you got hurt. And someday…" I couldn't
finish the sentence.

"…Stop?" my brother asked.

I was startled by that word. It was the word I'd written back in
Chapter 1. In the settlement, that's what they called it — being ill
a long while and then going. My brother still remembered the word.

"…Yeah. Someday that could happen too."

My brother heard that and got a thinking look on his face for a
moment. The face of an eight-year-old working hard to weigh
something.

"…Did Mom stop?" my brother asked.

"…No. Mom's still here, just as she is."

"…So if I stay the same too, I won't stop?"

I couldn't answer that question. It was exactly right. My brother
was already stating, on his own, something close to the conclusion.

"…What do you think, brother?" he asked.

I hadn't prepared for that question. I'd come here resolved to carry
no prepared words, so I had no answer for this one either.

"…I don't think I should say what I think," I said. "This is your
own matter."

"…But do you want it?"

I went a long while without answering that one, too. I wanted it. Of
course I wanted it. But it felt wrong to say so. I'd already learned
this once, in front of my mother. What I want isn't a reason.

"…I," I finally managed, "want what you want."

My brother heard that and stared at me for a moment. Then he smiled.

"…Then it's simple."

"…What is?"

"…I just want what you want."

My breath caught at that, all at once.

"…What do you mean?"

My brother said it like it was obvious.

"…If you'll do it, I'll do it."

I heard that single line and, at first, I was glad.

Truly glad. My mother had said, "I'll think about it." I'd heard
Eikon's "What's that got to do with it?" more times than I could
count. Here, for the first time, I heard "I'll do it." I nearly rose
from my seat.

I remember those few seconds even now, exactly. Something inside me
cheered without a sound. There it is. My brother wants this. I
repeated that sentence to myself, over and over.

In that moment I was already picturing the next scene. Myself
running to the Curator's office. My hand writing my brother's name on
the application. Even the scene of holding my brother's hand and
leaving a mark for him on the signature line. All of it passed
through my head in a few seconds. I was startled myself at the
speed. What I'd imagined in those few seconds felt more vivid than
anything I'd prepared over seven years.

But that cheer didn't last.

"…Why did you answer that way?" I asked. My voice shook strangely.

"…Because it's what you want."

"…No, I asked what you want."

My brother tilted his head.

"…If you want it, I want it too."

I heard that and looked down at the stone still in my hand. I'd been
gripping it this whole time without noticing. Its shape had pressed
into my palm.

"…Not that," I asked again. "Without me — just thinking about it on
your own — how do you feel?"

My brother put on a serious, thinking face at that question. He
thought a long while.

"…I don't know. I just never thought about it, because you were
here."

I had no words for a long while, in front of that answer.

I've rewritten this scene more times than any other in this
account. In my first draft, I wrote that I withdrew the application
right there on the spot. That was a lie. In reality, it wasn't so. In
that moment, briefly, very briefly, I thought about simply accepting
that answer as it was.

I set that thought down here honestly. I thought about taking my
brother's "I'll do it" straight to the Curator. Procedurally, there
was nothing wrong with it. My brother had answered. If you'll do it,
I'll do it. All I had to do was write that answer on the form,
exactly as it was. Probably no one would have stopped me. Even if the
Curator confirmed it himself, my brother would clearly give the same
answer in front of him too. If you'll do it, I'll do it. That
confirmation confirms the form, not the content — those words, only
then, did I finally understand completely.

I'll also note that during those few seconds, my hand was already
moving toward the application in my pocket. My fingertips touched
the edge of the paper. I pulled my hand back right there. I didn't
know exactly why, at the time. I know now. The moment I pulled that
paper out, I would have become someone who kept, on the surface, what
this city calls respect, while betraying it completely underneath.

I took my brother's hand again. That small hand.

"…I'll ask you again. Later," I said.

"…Can't we do it now?"

"…No, not now."

My brother's face fell. Watching that face, my chest tore. This
child had been trying to give me what I wanted. And I was refusing
the gift.

On the way back to the lodging I thought again of the Curator's
words. What I'd heard in Chapter 18. "A preserved person lacks
nothing." And why consent was a problem in the first place. I'd never
once applied those words to my brother. My brother wasn't someone who
had never wanted anything — he was someone still learning, from me,
how to want.

That night I saw that difference clearly for the first time. Eikon's
"What's that got to do with it?" came out of her having no lack.
My brother's "I'll do it" came out of having no direction of his own.
Neither one was consent. And yet I had to admit that my brother's
answer was, by far, the more tempting of the two. Because at least it
had been a yes.

That night I took out the application again. I looked a long time at
the space where my brother's name would go. My hand moved over it
several times. I didn't write.

That night I thought again, for the first time, about the word
"respect." The Curator had said once that the consent requirement
had probably come out of respect. A respect meant to keep anyone from
turning a person back carelessly. That night I also saw the other
side of that word. To take my brother's "I'll do it" and carry it
forward would have been, at the same time, both keeping respect and
completely destroying it. The procedure would have been followed. But
the thing the procedure was meant to protect would not have been.

The next morning, I went to the Curator's office.

"…I asked my brother yesterday," I said.

"…What did he say?"

"…He said, 'If you'll do it, I'll do it.'"

The Curator heard that and showed no expression at all. Not a face
that was judging. Just a face that was waiting.

"…Does that count as consent?" I asked.

"…Procedurally, yes."

There it was again. Procedurally.

"…But what I want to ask," I went on, "is whether that's real
consent."

For the first time, the Curator smiled, faintly. A sad smile.

"…It sounds like you already know the answer to that, Anemos."

I had no answer to that. He was right. I already knew the answer.

"…I'd like to withdraw the application," I said.

"…Your brother's?"

"…Yes. It hadn't even been filed yet, but I wanted to make it
certain."

The Curator took something out of a folder and set it in front of
me. A form confirming the withdrawal. I wrote my name on it. My hand
didn't shake. This time, I was truly glad that it didn't.

"…You did well," the Curator said.

"…At what?"

"…There have been people, sometimes. People who, when the child
answers that way, go ahead and carry it through as it stands."

I looked up, startled at that.

"…There have been people like that?"

The Curator nodded. He said nothing more. I asked nothing more. It
was a weight I could understand without asking.

Leaving that room that day, I felt for the first time that I had
chosen something myself. Up until then, everything had been
happening to me. I'd been asked, "Why?" I'd been given, "I'll think
about it." This time was different. I could have taken my brother's
"I'll do it," and I set it down myself instead. It was the first
choice I had made in this book.

I didn't go to my brother that day. Instead I left him a letter. I
asked the attendant to pass it along.

"I'll ask you again later. Next time, think it over by yourself,
without me there. You can take as long as you need."

Writing that letter, I felt that this line, of all things, was more
honest than any word I'd prepared over seven years. It was honest
because I hadn't prepared it. I caught that irony that very day.

Passing through the garden, I ran into Eikon. Not on the bench —
we met in the middle of the path.

"…Your face is different again," Eikon said. "Different from
yesterday's face."

"…How's it different?"

"…Yesterday's was a broken face. Today's is…" she chose her words
for a moment. "…a face that put something down."

I thought that phrase was exactly right. I'd picked something up
today and set it back down. The feel of it was still in my hand.

"…What did you put down?" Eikon asked.

I told her briefly about my brother. The answer, "I'll do it," and
withdrawing it.

Eikon heard the whole thing and said nothing for a long while. Then
she said this.

"…I honestly don't really see why that's a problem. Your brother
said he wanted to."

"…He said he wanted to because of me. Not because he wanted to,
himself."

"…Is that different?"

I couldn't answer that question right away. In this city, that
distinction seemed to be a foreign one. For Eikon, wanting and having
a reason always came together. If you want something, there's a
reason; if there's no reason, you don't really want it either. But
with my brother, the two had come apart.

"…It's different," I finally said. "I only saw that difference
clearly myself, for the first time, today."

Eikon heard that answer and tilted her head. Not a face that
understood, but a face that simply accepted not understanding.

"…You've been picking out nothing but hard things to do lately,
Anemos."

I smiled a little at that.

"…Yeah. I have."

I remember that short conversation for a long time after. Eikon
didn't understand my choice. And yet she didn't argue against it. It
was the first time I felt what it was like to be respected without
being understood.

(Continued in Chapter 22.)

Chapter 22. Looking Down

_Previously_ — My brother said, "If you'll do it, I'll do it." I
decided not to accept that answer as consent, and withdrew the
application myself. For the first time I felt that I had chosen
something.

After I parted from Eikon, my body felt strangely light and heavy at
once.

The lightness was from withdrawing the application. It felt like I'd
finished something. The heaviness was from the feeling that
finishing it had solved nothing. Not with my mother, not with my
brother — I still had nothing, either way.

That afternoon I didn't go straight back to the lodging.

If I went back, I felt I'd end up counting something again. Five
fingers, the blanks on a form, the number of my brother's steps. That
day I didn't want to count anything. So I just walked. With no
direction chosen.

I remember that walk strangely well, even now. It's one of the few
scenes in this account where I walked with no purpose at all. Up
until then I'd always been going somewhere. To the visiting room, to
the Curator's office, to the garden. That day was different.

As I walked, the streets grew steadily less familiar. At some point I
realized I was moving away from the city's center. Fewer buildings,
fewer people. No more benches from the plaza, no more garden trees.

Even though I'd been in this city a fair while, I'd never once come
this way before. The visiting room, the Curator's office, the ration
station, the garden — the paths I walked always connected those four
places. This road led to none of them.

Even the ground underfoot was different. The streets at the city's
center were all packed hard, but here bare earth showed through in
patches. I walked on that earth. The feel of it under my feet was
familiar again, after a long while. It was close to the feel of the
roads outside.

The people I passed grew fewer too. At first there'd been one or two,
here and there, and then at some point there was no one at all. That
absence felt, strangely, like a relief. It was a road where no one
studied my face.

Walking that road, I learned for the first time that this city had
something like an "outskirts." At the center, people gathered.
Visiting, walking, crossing paths. At the outskirts, there was no
one. A place with no reason to go. At the time I thought that was
simply a fact of geography.

When I reached the edge of the city, at first I didn't realize that
was where it ended.

The ground simply vanished there. More precisely, the ground suddenly
bent downward. I stopped in front of it.

A low railing ran along that boundary. Not high. About waist height.
Not so much a barrier meant to keep you from crossing, as a height
meant only to mark that this was a boundary. I set my hand on that
railing. It was cold.

I stepped forward and looked down.

At first I couldn't see anything. More precisely, it took my eyes a
while to adjust to that landscape. Eyes accustomed to the city's
light couldn't read the colors below right away.

Slowly, shapes came together.

It was a wide valley. Wide enough that I couldn't quite see its end.
The floor was a dry, pale color. I couldn't tell if it was the color
of soil or of ash. Here and there were darker marks, deeper in tone,
but from up here I couldn't tell what they were.

There were no trees. Or — there were, but only a few, standing far
apart, and they had no leaves. Only branches remained, standing bare.
I couldn't tell whether those trees were dead or only sleeping.

The cliff was steeper than I'd expected. I tried to gauge the height
from the city's ground down to the valley floor. It was higher than
any hill I'd seen in the settlement. Layers showed in the cliff face.
Soils of slightly different colors, stacked in horizontal bands. Red
near the top, growing paler going down, until near the bottom it was
almost white. I could only guess that those bands were the mark of a
very long stretch of time. It was a kind of layering that nothing the
old man had taught me could fully explain.

The dark marks scattered across the floor kept catching my eye. They
came in every size. Some were very small, some as large as a
gathering of several people. From that distance I couldn't tell
whether they were only shadows, or the trace of something else. Not
being able to tell, if anything, held my eyes there all the more.

I've redrawn that first sight of the landscape in my mind many
times. Writing this account, I've felt the temptation to attach some
grand emotion to that scene. But to be honest, in that moment I
wasn't afraid. I wasn't sad, either. I was simply — strange. Down
there was a color that existed nowhere else in this city.

A wind blew upward from below. A dry, lukewarm wind. A wind I could
hardly feel anywhere else in this city. I stood there a long while,
taking that wind on my face.

I looked at the ground beneath my feet again. Even the ground right
in front of the railing had a different texture from the ground
farther inside the city. Softer, and it crumbled a little underfoot.
I tried to find, with my eyes, exactly where that boundary began. I
couldn't find it. Between the city's hard ground and the valley's dry
ground, there was a stretch that shifted gradually. Ground that
belonged to neither side.

There was no sound, either.

More precisely, it was a different kind of absence than the city's.
The city was quiet because there was little sound. Down there seemed
quiet because there was nothing, in itself, to make a sound. I
couldn't put that difference into exact words in that moment, but my
body felt it.

Out of habit, I walked along the railing counting my steps. Twenty to
the left, thirty to the right. I was trying to find the valley's end.
I couldn't. Both ways, it kept running on past what I could see.

I gave up trying to gauge its size. Right there, for the first time,
I admitted that some things can't be gauged.

A person was walking past nearby. An older man, carrying a small
basket, on his way somewhere. I called out to stop him.

"…Excuse me."

The man stopped walking and looked at me.

"…What's down there?" I asked.

The man glanced briefly where I was pointing. Then he looked back at
me. His expression didn't change much.

"…The valley."

"…What's in it?"

"…I don't know."

I was a little surprised by that short answer.

"…You don't know? Living right here?"

"…I've never been down."

"…Not even once?"

"…No."

"…Why not?"

The man smiled faintly at that question, for the first time. Not a
mocking smile. Just the smile of someone finding the question
unfamiliar.

"…There's no reason to go."

I turned that answer over in my mind.

"…Aren't you curious?" I asked.

"…Being curious and having a reason to go are different things."

That distinction was unfamiliar to me. Outside, if you were curious,
you usually went and looked. Curiosity itself was the reason. I
wanted to check that once more.

"…Have you never once wanted to go down?"

The man took the question seriously. There was no sign he was
brushing it off.

"…I think maybe I thought about it once, as a child. But I don't
remember well."

"…Why didn't you go?"

"…Because I didn't know what would be down there — would that do,
as an answer? But that's not quite the exact reason. More precisely,
nothing changes whether I go or not."

"…Why does it matter, whether something changes?"

The man smiled a little at that question.

"…It doesn't matter. That's exactly why I don't go, either."

I couldn't find another question to ask, in front of that circular
logic.

"…Has anyone ever been down there? Anyone at all?"

"…Well. I've never heard of it."

"…Not a single one?"

"…Not a single one."

That answer gave me a strange chill. Since this city was built — or
at least, as far as this man knew — no one had ever set foot in that
valley.

The man, having said that, walked on. He adjusted the basket in his
hands, without looking at me as though I were especially strange.

Watching his back, I felt something cold pass through me. At first
I thought it must be a forbidden place. I expected a wall, at least,
or a sign. There was none. Only a low railing. Anyone who set their
mind to it could climb over. And yet no one did. Not because it was
blocked, but because there was no reason to go.

I'd already met that kind of wall in this city more than once.
Release was the same. Nothing blocked it, and yet no one did it. The
valley was the same. Nothing blocked it, and yet no one went down. In
that moment I sensed, only dimly, that those two walls were built
from the same material.

I drew one deep breath. And I stayed exactly there, standing still,
for a long while.

The wind kept blowing. I tried to count it. One gust, two. I counted
a few times and gave up. Wind wasn't something you could count, the
way steps or laughs were. It kept blowing and stopping, and there was
no telling where one ended and the next began.

In front of that wind, I had a strange thought. I didn't yet know
that my name meant wind. That meaning wouldn't come to light until
Part Four. But rewriting this scene now, it's hard for me to simply
pass over that coincidence. I stood there a long time, for the first
time, in front of something that couldn't be counted. And later I
learned that the name of that uncountable thing was also my own name.
For now I'll set down only this much, and save the rest for when the
time comes.

Far off, a single bird flew across above the valley. Even seeing a
bird in this city was rare. I watched to see whether it would fly
down into the valley. Near the edge of the cliff it turned and flew
back toward the city instead. It didn't go in.

Not even that bird went in. In the moment, I let that pass as
unremarkable. Later it came back to me. If even a thing that could
simply fly over never crossed in, then maybe it wasn't blocked at
all — maybe there truly was nothing inside it. I would soon learn how
wrong that thought was.

I leaned against the railing again and looked down.

The sun was sinking little by little. The valley's colors began to
shift. The dim gray-brown of daytime caught the evening light and
slowly reddened. I watched that change for a long while.

There was one spot where an especially dark shadow lay long across
the ground. I tried to tell whether it was the shadow of something,
or just a shadow made by a fold in the land. The distance was too
great.

In that moment I thought, very briefly, about going down. I didn't
hold on to that thought for long. Not that day. I didn't yet know
what was down there, and even if I had, that day was already full
with a different weight. My brother's letter, the withdrawn
application. That was as much weight as the day could hold.

Instead I reached my hand out over the railing. As far as my hand
could reach, there was only empty air. I opened and closed my hand in
that emptiness a moment. Nothing to grasp. Even knowing that was
obvious, I wanted to confirm it.

I only understood that gesture later. I hadn't wanted to touch the
valley. I'd wanted to confirm, with my own body, that I couldn't
reach it yet. My heart had already crossed that edge; my body was
still inside this city. I felt that gap for the first time that day.

I stood there and watched the valley turn completely red. And then
watched the red turn back to gray, and then to dark. Once the dark
settled in, the valley became invisible altogether. The boundary, the
cliff, those dark marks — all of it. As though none of it had ever
been there. That vanishing felt, to me, larger than anything I'd seen
by daylight.

Walking back, I thought again of that man. There's no reason to go.
That day, for the first time, I understood fully that this sentence
applied to almost everything in this city. There was no well, because
there was no reason for one. There was no release, because there was
no reason for it. No one went down into the valley, because there was
no reason to. All of it was explained by the same sentence. It isn't
needed.

I felt, for the first time that night, how frightening a sentence
that was. Bad things, mostly, have a reason behind them. If there's a
reason, there's something you can fight. If there's no reason, there
is no target for the fight at all. That night, for the first time, I
understood that this city had no target to fight.

Back at the lodging, I opened the flask. Not much water was left. I
didn't drink it all. I left some. I didn't know why. I just wanted to
leave some behind.

Outside the window, the city's lights came on one by one. I followed
the order they lit with my eyes. Always the same order. Watching that
familiar order, my mind kept drifting back to the dark gray and the
red I'd seen earlier. This city's lights and that valley's sunset
felt like two different languages.

Before falling asleep that night, I had this thought for the first
time. I'd thought this city was simply spread out flat, not rising
above anything. But in fact there was another layer, underneath.
That day I saw that layer for the first time. Having seen one, it
seemed there might be others. That night, for the first time, it
occurred to me that this whole city might be a far thicker place than
I'd known.

Lying under the covers, I couldn't fall asleep for a long time. When
I closed my eyes, I saw that reddening valley again. I'd never once
seen that color, anywhere in this city. The city allowed only an
exact brightness, an exact shadow. Down there, there was no such
exactness. I set it down here honestly — that irregularity frightened
me, and yet I found, strangely, that I couldn't look away.

(Continued in Chapter 23.)

Chapter 23. The Ones It Failed

_Previously_ — For the first time I stood at the city's edge and
looked down into the Dry Valley. No one ever went down there. Not
because it was blocked, but because there was no reason to go. I
couldn't fall asleep with that landscape still in my eyes.

The next day I went back to that edge.

I hadn't decided to go. I'd set out to fill the flask at the ration
station, and my feet turned that way on their own. I decided not to
fight the direction my body wanted.

When I reached the railing, someone was already there.

It was Eikon.

I was a little surprised.

"…Do you come here often?" I asked.

"…Sometimes."

"…Why?"

Eikon shrugged at the question.

"…No particular reason. I just end up here if I walk long enough."

I didn't quite believe that at face value. There weren't many places
in this city people went to without a reason. But I didn't press
further.

We leaned against the railing side by side. The valley was the same
as yesterday. The dry color, the sparse trees, the dark, stained
marks.

"…I saw you standing here yesterday," Eikon said. "From a distance."

"…Why didn't you come over?"

"…You looked like you wanted to be alone."

"…But you came today."

"…Today you didn't look like that."

That distinction made me smile. This girl always read people that
way. Accurate, in a way that was a little frightening for being so
accurate.

"…Who did you meet here yesterday?" Eikon asked.

"…A man. Carrying a basket."

"…Ah, him." Eikon seemed to know who I meant. "He comes here often
too. Like me."

"…Why?"

"…I don't know. I never asked. People don't ask that much, in this
city."

Hearing that, I realized for the first time that I wasn't the only
one who stood at this low railing. It seemed the people of this city,
too, sometimes walked out here, for no reason at all.

"…What's that?" I asked. The same question I'd asked the man
yesterday.

Eikon didn't hesitate at all with that question.

"…The valley."

"…I know that. I mean what's down there. Those dark marks."

Eikon looked down for a moment. Then she spoke, in a tone as
unaffected as ever.

"…Traces."

"…Traces of who?"

"…Of the people whose preservation failed."

I heard that and my body went stiff. Eikon's tone hadn't changed in
the slightest. The exact same tone she'd use to talk about the
weather.

"…Failed?" I asked back. "It can fail?"

"…Very rarely. It isn't something that happens often, to begin
with."

"…How does it fail?"

Eikon thought for a moment before answering. A face choosing her
explanation, not a face of discomfort.

"…Preservation starts with registering the person who's brought in.
Their name, their record, who they are. If that registration doesn't
finish properly, the preservation doesn't finish properly either."

"…What happens if it doesn't finish?"

"…It simply doesn't happen. That person doesn't remain in the
preserved state."

"…Then what? What happens to them?"

"…Just — as they were before. A body that gets sick, grows old,
stops."

Hearing that answer, I felt the strength go out of my knees.

"…How often does that happen?" I asked. "That kind of failure."

"…I don't know exactly. But it's supposed to be very rare. Rare
enough that most people here go their whole lives without hearing of
it even once."

"…But you know about it."

"…I heard it as a child. From my parents. Just that such a thing
exists."

"…Weren't you scared? Back then?"

Eikon looked into that question for a moment.

"…I was a child too, back then. Not so much scared — I just thought,
so there's such a thing in the world too. Most people here take it
that way. Like bad weather. Not common, but not something that never
happens either."

That comparison sent a chill through me. Bad weather. This city
called a person's complete disappearance something on the order of
weather.

"…Sometimes records get damaged, too," Eikon went on. "Among people
registered a very long time ago, sometimes the record ages until it
can't be read anymore. If that happens, the preservation tied to that
record ends along with it."

"…When does that happen? At registration, or later?"

"…Mostly at registration, they say. Damage happening later is
supposed to be almost unheard of."

That answer gave me a very small measure of relief. It didn't apply
to my mother and brother. Their records had been intact for seven
years.

"…So," I asked again, "do all those failed people end up down
there?"

"…Yes. More precisely, their traces do. Whether it's the body, or
whatever's left."

"…Why there, of all places?"

Eikon couldn't answer that question.

"…I don't know. It's just always been that way. I only know what
I've been taught."

"…Doesn't it make you sad?" I asked. A different version of the
question I'd already thrown at her once, in Chapter 14.

Eikon looked genuinely puzzled at that.

"…Why would it be sad?"

"…A person died. That's what failing means, in the end, isn't it."

"…It failed. No one did anything wrong. The registration didn't go
through, and that's just what happened."

That distinction felt foreign to me. Outside, failure and fault
often sat in the same place. When something failed, people went
looking for someone to blame. In this city, that link itself seemed
not to exist.

"…Have you ever been down there?"

"…No. There's no reason to go."

Hearing that answer, I heard the man's words from yesterday layered
over it. There's no reason to go. It felt like this whole city had
memorized the same sentence.

In that moment a number came to me.

Eleven.

Seven years ago, eleven people came from our settlement to this
city. They were the sick. I'd received word that nine of them had
been preserved. What had happened to the remaining two was written
nowhere in that bulletin.

I stood there and counted that number again. Eleven minus nine.
Two.

"…Eikon." I asked. My voice shook. "If — say a person came to this
city, and no word ever came about whether they were preserved or
not. What would that mean?"

Eikon looked at my face for a long time at that question. A face
that had noticed something.

"…That usually means they failed."

I heard that answer and had nothing to say.

I unhooked the flask and opened and closed the cap. My hands were
shaking. I gripped the flask harder to try to stop the shaking.

"…It's not certain, though," I finally said. "That no word means
failure. It could be some other reason."

"…What other reason could there be?"

I couldn't answer that question. No other reason truly came to mind.
I'd simply hoped there was one.

"…What did the bulletin say, exactly?" Eikon asked.

I still had that sentence memorized. A sentence I'd read over and
over.

"…'The subject has been stably preserved, and a visit may be
requested at any time.' That's what was written for the nine. For
the other two, nothing at all was written."

Eikon heard that and nodded. There was no hesitation in that nod.

"…Then they failed. There was nothing to write, so nothing was
written. If it's good news, this place writes all of it down."

I wanted to argue with that certainty, but I had no words to argue
with. I think I'd already known it myself. I'd only not wanted to
admit it.

I set this down beforehand, in Chapter 1. That of the eleven, nine
were preserved, and what became of the remaining two was never
written. And I wrote that I would learn later where those two had
gone. This is that place.

I don't know those two people's names. I don't know their faces. I
know nothing about them, beyond that they were carried away from our
settlement together. But in that moment I knew that somewhere down
there, there might be traces of those two people whose faces I'd
never known.

"…Is there any way to confirm it?" I asked. "Whether those two are
really down there."

Eikon shook her head.

"…Without a name, there's no way to confirm it. They were never
registered here to begin with."

I felt that answer as cruel. Eikon's tone was still not cruel at
all. Only the fact was.

We stood there in silence for a moment. The wind blew up from below
again. I couldn't tell whether it was the same wind as yesterday, or
a different one.

"…Are you all right?" Eikon asked. For the first time, something
like worry showed on her face as she looked at me.

"…I don't know."

"…Did I say something wrong?"

"…No. You just told me the truth."

Eikon looked a little relieved at that. Watching her face, I
confirmed again that she hadn't meant to hurt me. She'd only said
what she knew.

That day, for the first time, I saw Eikon's composure differently.
Up until then, I'd only thought that composure came from having no
lack. That day it was different. That composure existed, too,
because death itself had already become a very distant thing in this
city. The preserved don't die. The failed aren't even registered.
This city had never had a place set aside for grief, from the start.

"…Are you going to go down?" Eikon asked.

I couldn't answer that question right away.

"…I don't know."

"…What would change, if you went down? You don't even know their
names."

I couldn't answer that question either. She was right. Going to look
for two people whose names I didn't even know made no sense. And yet
in that moment I felt some corner of my heart already leaning that
way.

Eikon stood quietly beside me. She didn't hurry me. She just looked
down with me.

That afternoon I went to find the Curator. There was something I
wanted to confirm.

"…You said nine of the eleven sent word back," I said. "Why did the
other two never send any?"

The Curator took out a folder and leafed through it. It took a
while, as though the record were an old one.

"…This is a record from seven years ago. Here it is." He set his
finger on a page. "Nine people, registration completed. The
remaining two…" He paused a moment. "…it says here, registration
incomplete."

"…What does that mean?"

"…Exactly what Eikon told you. It means the preservation procedure
never finished going through."

"…What were those two people's names?"

The Curator looked over the page again. He shook his head.

"…Without registration, there's no name either. I'm sorry."

That "I'm sorry" put me at ease, oddly enough. It was the first time
anyone in this city had apologized to me. Everyone else had only
stated facts. This man stated the fact and was sorry about it too.

"…Are they down in that valley too?" I asked.

"…Probably. I can't promise you that. But it's by far the most
likely thing."

I heard that answer and rose from my seat. There was nothing more to
ask. There had been — but it felt like nothing more would change the
answer.

At the door, the Curator called me back once more.

"…Anemos."

"…Yes."

"…Not many people come to ask about this kind of thing."

"…Why not?"

"…Most people just remember the number nine. It's easier to forget
the two."

I had no answer to that. I too had nearly forgotten those two, for
seven years. I'd lived content with the number nine.

I went back to that edge again. Eikon was already gone.

I looked down alone again. The dark marks were still there. I
couldn't tell which of them, if any, were the traces of the two
people who'd come from my settlement. It seemed I would never be
able to tell.

I didn't go down into the valley that day. It wasn't yet the time.
But from that moment on, what lay down there was no longer, for me, a
strange landscape. Down there might be the trace of someone I knew.
No face, no name, but someone I knew all the same.

Walking back to the lodging, I counted those eleven people again. I
didn't remember their faces exactly. I'd been nine. But a few pieces
remained. Someone's cough, a child carried on someone's back, an old
woman waving from a doorway.

I couldn't tell which of those fragments belonged to those two
people. I probably never will. I found it strangely troubling that I
couldn't write their names in this account. I had never learned how
to mourn someone with no name.

That night, for the first time, I felt relief that my mother and
brother had been among the nine. And I was ashamed of that relief.
The boundary between nine and two had been pure chance. I couldn't
tell whether I should be grateful for that chance, or sorry for it.

That day, for the first time, I saw a small gap in this city's
perfection. Perpetuity has never once broken its promise. The
preserved truly do not disappear. But that promise applied only to
those whose registration was finished. For those never registered,
there had never been a promise to begin with, to keep or to break. I
would only understand later that this gap wasn't a flaw in this
city, but proof that this city was honest. This city never made a
promise it couldn't keep.

(Continued in Chapter 24.)

Chapter 24. The Sound of Water

_Previously_ — I learned what had become of two of the eleven who'd
come with us from the settlement. The traces of those whose
registration was never finished go down into the Dry Valley. I
didn't go down that day. But the landscape was no longer strange to
me.

The days after that passed without much happening.

More precisely, nothing much seemed to happen, from the outside. I
didn't go to see my mother, or my brother. I ran into Eikon twice or
so, but we didn't talk long. I counted those days. Four of them.

I felt the temptation to skip these four days in this account.
Nothing had happened, after all. But the fact that nothing happened
was itself a rare thing in this book. Up until then I'd run into
something new every day. Why, I'll do it, the ones it failed. For
four days I ran into nothing at all. I only walked, went to the
ration station, and slept.

During those four days I went to the valley's edge twice more. No
one was there. I stood alone, looked down, and came back. I wasn't
trying to find anything. It only seemed that the landscape had found
its place inside my days by then.

I didn't take out the application again during those days, either.
Four of my five fingers had broken, and one I'd hidden away myself.
My brother's application I'd withdrawn with my own hand. I wanted to
set it all down for a while. Setting it down didn't mean giving up.
I just wanted to breathe a little.

Looking back on those four days, I think they were the quietest
stretch in this whole book. Quiet didn't mean peaceful. I spent those
days still waiting for something. I didn't know exactly what.

On the third day the attendant at the ration station greeted me a
little differently.

"…You've been quiet lately," he said.

"…Yeah. I guess so."

"…Are you all right?"

I couldn't answer that question right away. It wasn't common for
someone in this city to ask after me first.

"…I don't know. I think I'm waiting for something."

"…For what?"

"…I don't know either."

The attendant didn't add anything more to that answer. He just
filled the flask and handed it back. Taking the water, I felt again
how strange the word "waiting" sounded in this city. People here
rarely had anything to wait for. They could get what they wanted when
they wanted it.

On the fourth day I ran into Eikon in the garden. She spoke first.

"…You haven't been asking lately," Eikon said.

"…Asking what?"

"…You used to ask all the time. Doesn't it frustrate you, isn't it
sad, that kind of thing."

I smiled a little at that. She was right.

"…I think I understand now. Even without asking."

"…Understand what?"

"…That you really are all right."

Eikon heard that and stared at me for a moment. Not a surprised
face. Just a face confirming something.

"…Finally figured it out."

I laughed again at that answer. This time she laughed too. A short
laugh. And yet it was the lightest moment of those few days. I wanted
to hold on to that lightness for a long while, but it wasn't the kind
of thing that stretched the longer you held it.

On the evening of the fifth day, I was sitting by the window in my
room.

It was not long past sunset. The city's lights were coming on one by
one. I followed the order with my eyes for a while, then stopped. It
was an order I'd already counted more than once.

The flask sat beside me. Nearly empty. I fiddled with its cap,
thinking I'd have to fill it the next morning.

I still remember that evening clearly. I thought it would be an
evening with nothing to it. It had been that way four days running,
so I assumed the fifth would be the same.

A little wind came in through the gap in the window. Not a common
wind, in this city. I leaned into it slightly.

That was when I heard the sound.

At first I didn't know what it was. Very faint. Mixed in with the
city's other sounds, I couldn't be sure whether it was really a
sound at all, or just my own imagining.

I held my breath and listened.

It came again. A little clearer this time. Something being dragged.
Then something unspooling. Long, at a steady pace.

In that moment I felt my body react before my mind did. Before I
could think, my hand had already gone to the windowsill. I knew this
sound before I'd even realized I knew it.

It was the sound of a well rope unspooling.

I'd heard that sound since I was born. At our settlement's well,
several times a day. The particular friction of the rope paying out,
and the creak of the wooden pulley taking it up. Then a short
stillness. And at the end, far off and small, a splash — water
touched.

I knew the order of that sound exactly. The drag, the unspooling, the
stillness, the splash. I'd grown up counting those four beats. The
same beats my brother had once been afraid of.

I already wrote down this sound in Chapter 1. The sound my brother
had been afraid of, until one day, out of nowhere, he said he liked
it. Because it meant his brother was there, and that was good. I had
never once forgotten that sound, in seven years.

I closed my eyes and gave myself over entirely to the sound. The
drag — I knew that was the rope going over the pulley. The unspool —
the bucket falling, the rope paying out behind it. The stillness in
between was the time the bucket spent crossing empty air. Its length
told you the depth of the well. I counted that stillness. One, two,
three, four. Only after four did the splash come.

Four beats. I remembered that number as though it had been carved
into my fingers. Our settlement's well had been three. This one was
deeper than that. I don't know why that difference stayed with me so
strongly. Probably because that single number told me this wasn't the
well I knew — it was an entirely new well.

I thought, too, of the water jar my mother had filled the night
before she left. The water we'd left three days undrunk, gone bad on
the fourth day and thrown out. I still remembered the weight of that
jar exactly, its smell, the moment I'd cried for the first time,
pouring that water away. I couldn't say why that memory rose up now,
of all times. But the sound kept calling it back.

And that sound, I was hearing again, in this city without water.

I sprang up from my seat.

I looked out the window. The sound seemed to be coming from the
east side of the city. I couldn't judge the exact direction. But
from the nature of the sound, it wasn't close by. And yet, in this
city's stillness, it carried through with perfect clarity.

I opened the window a little wider. The sound grew louder. The drag,
the unspooling, the stillness, and this time, a little louder — the
splash.

In that moment I realized how long I'd been waiting for this sound.
Realizing it startled me. It was strange, to have been waiting for
something without even knowing I was waiting for it.

I looked around the room. I wondered whether anyone else had heard
this sound. I looked out over the city through the window. Everyone
was moving about as usual. No one stopped walking. No one turned
toward the sound.

That day I confirmed, with my own eyes, that this city truly did
not hear sound. It wasn't that they couldn't hear it. That sound was
clearly loud enough to hear. It was only that no one had any reason
to respond to it. However loud a sound might be, if it was a sound
no one needed, it wasn't a sound at all, in this city.

Footsteps sounded on the floor below. Another lodger. I threw the
window open wide and called down.

"…Do you hear that?" I called down.

The person stopped walking and looked up at me.

"…Hear what?"

"…That sound, over there. Sounds like something dragging."

The person made a show of listening for a moment. Then shook their
head.

"…Hm. Can't say I do."

For a moment that answer made me doubt whether I'd misheard. But the
sound was still there, clear as ever. The drag, the unspooling, the
stillness, the splash. I couldn't tell whether that person truly
couldn't hear it, or heard it and simply let it pass.

"…Right. Sorry to bother you," I said.

The person nodded once and walked on. I watched their back and left
the window open instead of closing it.

I stood there and listened to nothing but that sound for a long
while.

The drag. The unspooling. The stillness. The splash.

I counted the beats with my fingers, pressing down on each one.
Once, twice, three times. Someone was drawing water again and again.
It wasn't a sound that ended after one round.

I think now that the repetition was, more than anything else, the
proof. If it had happened only once, it could have been chance. Could
have been the sound of something dropped by accident. But the sound
kept going. It was the rhythm of someone at work. I know that
rhythm. Different from the rhythm I learned at the old man's side,
and yet the same kind of rhythm. Not hurried, and yet never
stopping.

This city has no well. I'd known that fact from my very first day in
the city. The ration station handed out water. The preserved needed
none, and the living received only their allotted share and drank it.
I'd been the only person in this city who carried a flask around with
him.

And yet right now, somewhere out there, someone was drawing water
from a well.

I stepped back from the window and stood in the middle of the room.

I ran through a few possibilities. Had the ration station brought in
some new equipment? No — that sound wasn't coming from the ration
station's direction. The direction was wrong. Was someone digging
something, on their own? But no one in this city dug into the
ground. There was no reason to.

Crossing out those possibilities one by one, I found only one left
standing. Someone was digging a well. Something no one had done since
this city was built, someone was doing right now.

I tried to imagine who that person might be. It didn't seem like
anyone from this city. People here always said there was no reason
to dig a well. That meant it would have to be someone from outside,
like me — and yet I'd never once seen another outsider in this city
besides myself.

In that moment, for the first time, I found myself curious. That
there might be another person from outside, here in this city besides
me. I felt that curiosity outweigh any fear. A strange thing. A
stranger was doing a strange thing, and I wasn't afraid. If anything,
I was glad.

I tried to count how much time had passed since the sound began. I
couldn't count it exactly. While I was listening to the sound, I
couldn't count anything else.

I picked up the flask. Nearly empty. I stood there a moment, feeling
that lightness in my fingertips.

In that moment I ran back, one by one, through everything that had
happened to me in this city. My mother's "Why?" My brother's "I'll
do it." The withdrawn application. The trace of two people whose
names I didn't know. For the first time I strung all of it together
into a single line. This city was a place without thirst. Without
thirst, there was no wanting; without wanting, there was no consent.
I couldn't lay out that connection in exact words, in that moment.
But I felt it in my body.

I would unpack that evening's thought in much greater detail, much
later. Here I set down only the thread of it. That the sound of
water had returned to a city without water. I believed then, and I
believe now, that it wasn't a coincidence.

Writing this chapter, I've thought many times about why I didn't
run straight out that night. It wouldn't have been strange if I had
bolted the moment I heard the sound. But I didn't. I stood there and
wanted to listen a little longer. Before confirming anything, I just
wanted to listen.

The drag. The unspooling. The stillness. The splash.

I heard those beats again and again. Outside, the city's lights began
going out, one by one. This city's night slept on a fixed schedule.
But that sound alone never went out. It continued late into the
night.

I lay down without closing the window. I wanted to fall asleep to
that sound.

Closing my eyes, I saw the well at the settlement again. The path my
brother used to walk behind me on. My mother's words, patting his
back. Why make something scarier when he's already scared. I hadn't
thought of those words in a long while. This city was a place afraid
of nothing. Because there was nothing here to be afraid of — I'd
already written that, back in Chapter 1. But the sound I was hearing
now seemed to be waking something this city had forgotten long ago.

That night I didn't yet know what this sound was waking. If I had
known, I wouldn't have fallen asleep so easily. Looking back now, I
think that ignorance was, if anything, the last peace I was allowed
that night.

The sound of water went on past midnight. The drag, the unspooling,
the stillness, the splash. Instead of counting those four beats, I
simply let my body go. It had been a long time since I'd heard a
sound without counting it — only listening.

I slept deeply that night, for the first time in a long while. It
was the most restful sleep I'd had since coming to this city. The
next morning, I decided to follow that sound. That is what happens
in the next part of this account.

Ever since I came into this city, I'd felt as though I were
standing in front of a door. In front of the people who do not
disappear, in front of the words it can be reversed*, in front of
the question why. This time was different. This time, for the
first time, someone on the other side of the door was already
making a sound of their own. All I had to do was follow it.*

(Continued in Part Three.)

The Dry Valley, Part Three

The Valley

Chapter 25. The Well-Keeper

_Previously_ — an unfamiliar sound came from outside the city. I knew it
at once, because I'd grown up outside. The sound of a bucket going down,
a rope paying out, and at the end, a splash. Someone was drawing water.
This city had no well.

I followed the sound.

I left the dormitory and walked east. In this city, you almost never have
to follow a sound while you walk. There's so little sound to begin with.
Footsteps, sometimes a door opening and closing — that's all. So an
unfamiliar sound was easy to track, even from far away.

On the way I passed three people. All walking the same direction. No one
hurried, and no one looked at me. I noticed that none of the three so
much as turned toward the sound. They must have heard it. In a city this
quiet, there was no way they hadn't.

I thought it was strange, at the time. I understood later. It wasn't
strange. People in this city simply don't listen to sounds they don't
need. Not that they can't hear them — they choose not to. It took me a
while to see the difference.

It was at the eastern edge of the city, well past even the distribution
station. There had originally been nothing there. I'd passed that stretch
of ground on my first day here. Dry soil, a few weeds — that was all.

Now there was a pit.

About waist-deep on me. Wide enough that two people standing across from
each other would touch shoulders. Beside it, the dug-out earth was piled
like a small mountain, and a bucket and rope hung over the mound.

The water flask at my waist swung against my hip. My uncle had given it
to me before he left. "No water there, they say." I hadn't known what
that meant, then. I only understood once I reached the city. There really
was no well. Only the distribution station.

I had never once emptied that flask all the way. I'd drink half, then
refill it at the station. But in that moment, for the first time, the
flask felt heavy. I didn't know why.

There was a man in the pit.

Tall, broad-shouldered. Old sun on his forearms. Even from a distance I
could see the thick calluses on the hand gripping the shovel. His clothes
weren't the clothes of this city. The color had faded, and the knees had
been patched twice over.

I stood there a long while, watching.

He gave no sign of having seen me and kept digging. Drive the shovel in,
step on it, lift the earth, throw it up. There was a rhythm to it.
Unhurried, but never stopping.

I know that rhythm. I learned it at fourteen. There are two kinds of
digging. Digging in a hurry, and digging built to last. The hurried kind
wears out in an hour. The lasting kind goes all day. This man was the
lasting kind.

"…If you're just going to stand there watching, pour some water."

The voice came up out of the pit. It took me a beat to realize he was
talking to me.

"…What?"

"You've got a flask. That one."

He meant the flask at my waist. I was the only person in this city who
carried one around. The preserved didn't need water, and the living took
what they needed from the station, as they needed it. I was the only one
who kept a flask on me all the time.

"…I'm going to drink this."

"…I know. Just give me a little. The dirt's too dry to dig."

I unhooked the flask and went to the edge of the pit. The man set his
