shovel down for a moment and held out his hand. I passed it over. He
poured half of it onto the dirt floor and struck the spot with the shovel
a few times. I watched the earth go soft.

"…Thanks." He handed the flask back. "Speon."

"…Anemos."

"…Anemos." He repeated the name once. Then he picked the shovel back up.
"Wind."

I didn't answer that. It was the first time I'd met someone who knew what
my name meant. No one in this city had ever asked. They had no reason to.

I let that one line pass by, at the time. I understood later. Speon
wasn't a man who said what a name meant carelessly. When he said it,
there was always a reason. It was only much later that he told me what
the reason was.

I kept standing there.

I looked down into the pit. The soil was red on top and gray farther
down. I knew what that meant — the old man had taught me. Red earth is
the dry layer, gray earth is the trace of where moisture once passed
through. Gray means water crossed this ground at some point.

"…Has water gone through here before?" I asked.

Speon looked up at me, still holding the shovel. For the first time his
face showed a little surprise.

"…How'd you know that."

"…The color of the dirt."

"…Someone taught you."

"…Yes. An old man." I paused, then added. "He's gone now."

Speon nodded. He didn't say anything comforting. He just nodded. I found
that easier, somehow. In this city, people didn't know how to say
comforting things anymore. There was nothing left to be sad about. Speon's
nod was better than any comforting word.

I wrote earlier that I counted that old man's funeral as the ninth of
seventeen. I'll set it down again here. He died in winter. On the last
day he called me over and had me hold his hand. A man who rarely spoke
said a little more that day. "…One of the wells you dig is sure to come
up dry." "…Why?" "…So you'll have a reason to dig the next one." I didn't
understand it then. I thought success every time would only be better. I
know now. A person who only succeeds stops. You need one failure mixed
in to keep digging.

"…There was a water channel down here." Speon started digging again as
he said it. "It got blocked, a long time ago. Earth built up."

"…Why did it get blocked?"

"…Because no one used it. Unused things get blocked. People and water
channels are the same that way."

"…There's no well here," I said.

"…I know. That's why I'm digging."

"…Did someone ask you to?"

Speon stopped digging and looked up at me. His eyes were a light brown.
His face showed no irritation at all.

"…No."

"…Then why dig?"

He drove the shovel into the earth pile. Then he straightened his back.
It made a sound. It meant he'd been bent over a long time.

"…Water that just sits rots." He said it. "It has to flow to stay
alive."

I didn't understand what he meant. This city didn't even have anywhere
for water to sit still in the first place. There was no well. And yet
Speon spoke as if he were talking about water that was already sitting
somewhere, stagnant.

"…This city doesn't have water," I said. "There's nothing to sit still."

"…There is." Speon said. "It's not only water that's missing."

Then he picked the shovel back up. He didn't explain any further. I
didn't fully understand what he meant, not that day.

I know now. The water he meant wasn't well water. This whole city was
standing water. People sitting exactly as they were seven years ago.
Things that never changed. That was what Speon was talking about, and I
was still listening as if it were only about a real well.

I started counting the mounds of dirt.

Each swing of the shovel brought up one heap. I have a habit of counting
things. Somewhere around the hundredth swing, I asked.

"…Have you dug a lot of these?"

"…A lot."

"…How many?"

Speon's face went thoughtful for a moment. He didn't look like a man who
kept count. And yet his answer was exact.

"…Thirty-seven."

"…Did they all work?"

"…No. Thirty-one came up with water. Six didn't."

"…What about the ones that didn't?"

"…Left them as they were. Thought about filling them back in, but I
didn't."

"…Why not?"

"…Someone might come dig further, someday. Water I couldn't find, someone
else might."

I liked that answer. A man who admitted failure without sealing it back
up afterward. I'd seen very few people like that. In this city, failed
things went down into the valley. They weren't left standing so someone
could try again.

"…Where are you from?" I asked.

"…Here and there." Speon gave a short laugh. "Once, in some city, I saw
a bridge that only had half of itself built. I had three friends then,
and one of them helped finish that bridge."

"…And now?"

"…Scattered. Everyone went their own way. I'm a well-digger, so I keep
moving."

I didn't ask who those three friends were, that day. I had no reason
to. It sounded to me like an ordinary old story of Speon's, and I still
didn't know then what he'd done before he came to this city. Later I
would hear that bridge story again. That belongs to the last pages of
this record.

Still, I couldn't make myself leave. I wasn't sure why, even to myself. I
just liked the sound of the digging. Earth being lifted, the occasional
knock against a stone, the low sound of Speon's breath going out. It
wasn't the sound of water, but it was a sound for water's sake.

"…How many people have come?" I asked. "To watch you dig here."

"…You're the first."

"…No one's come at all?"

"…A few passing by. They looked, then moved on."

"…Didn't they say anything?"

Speon lifted a shovelful of earth as he answered.

"…Nothing to say, I guess. No need for it."

I heard that and looked back toward the city. Far off I could see the
Curator's building. People walked by, quietly. That was how everyone in
this city moved, always — quiet, unhurried, only as much as was needed.

Every time I look back on this moment, I feel a strange unease. I
expected the city to get angry. A well was, after all, a thing this city
had declared it didn't need. But no one got angry. That was somehow
heavier. If they'd been angry, there would have been something to fight.
With no anger, there was nothing to fight at all.

The sun began to set. Speon kept digging.

"…What about food?" I asked.

"…I've got some." He tipped his chin toward a small sack beside the
earth pile. "Dried stuff."

"…Water?"

"…I'll have to take a bit more off your flask."

I laughed. It was the first time I'd laughed since coming to this city.
It lasted a few seconds. But it was a real laugh.

I counted those few seconds afterward. In seven years I never counted
the days I laughed. There was nothing much to count. Coming to this city
gave me a reason to count — laughter had grown rare. I wrote earlier that
counting had become a habit of mine; from that day on, laughter became
one more thing I counted.

I unhooked the flask again and handed it over. Speon drank a little and
gave it back.

"…Will you be back tomorrow too?" I asked.

"…I'm going to be here the whole time."

"…How long is that?"

"…Until the water comes."

"…When's that?"

Speon pressed his palm flat against the dirt floor. Then he closed his
eyes for a moment. He looked like someone listening for something. I
didn't know, at the time, what that gesture was.

"…Don't know." He opened his eyes. "Water comes out when it comes out."

"…You can't rush it?"

"…Rush it and all you get is a collapse."

I heard that and looked at the walls of the pit. They really were packed
firm and solid. The mark of digging that hadn't been rushed.

I found myself wondering again about the gesture I'd seen earlier.

"…You put your hand down and closed your eyes before. What was that?"

"…Listening."

"…To what?"

"…Where the water wants to go."

I didn't know what that meant, so I stayed quiet. Speon motioned for me
to put my own hand down and try. I crouched and did it. The dirt was just
dirt. Cold and hard.

"…I don't hear anything."

"…No one does, at first." Speon said. "It's not listening, really. It's
waiting. If you keep your hand there long enough, the earth forgets what
it's been hiding. Then you hear a little."

I couldn't say I understood that. But strangely, the words frightened me.
Waiting until the earth forgot what it was hiding — somehow, that sounded
like this city's story.

I didn't see the connection then. This city, too, had been hiding
something for a long time. It wasn't that the people wanted nothing —
they had forgotten how to want. What Speon said to the earth, I would
later hear him say to the whole city.

That day I didn't go back to the city. I stayed sitting beside him a
while longer.

Speon didn't say anything more to me. He just kept digging. I watched. It
had been a long time since I'd had anything to watch. There wasn't much
worth watching in this city. Everyone moved exactly the same way.

Walking back to my assigned room that night, I remembered something my
mother once said to me. When my brother was afraid of the sound of
water, she told me, "Getting used to something and not being afraid of
it aren't the same thing." I wanted to hand that line back to Speon that
day. This city had gotten too used to living without a well. But that
didn't mean it didn't need water.

I didn't say it that day. I couldn't say it the next day either. It
would be a long time before I could say it at all.

On my way back to the dormitory I looked back three times. The sound of
digging still came from the pit. It was growing dark, and still Speon
didn't stop.

Listening to that sound, I had a thought for the first time.

There's someone in this city who keeps going, even when he's told he
isn't needed.

That thought stayed in my head all that night. I still didn't know what
it meant. I only held onto it, for a long time.

This was around my twenty-third morning in the city. Every day since
I'd arrived, I'd counted something — the number of visits, the time I'd
spent standing at the window, the count of sentences my mother had said
identically. But that night, for the first time, I counted something
else. I couldn't count how many times Speon swung the shovel, but I
counted exactly how many times I laughed. Once.

One. But that one was a different kind of number from the ones I'd
counted before. Not a number that only went down. A number that could go
up.

Just before the sun finished setting, one person passed by.

A girl. A year younger than me, walking slowly — the same unhurried walk
everyone in this city had. I knew her face.

It was Eikon.

She stopped for a moment in front of the pit. She looked at Speon, then
at me, then back at the pit. She said nothing. Her face showed almost
nothing. Not surprised, not curious — just a face that was looking.

I thought she'd say something. But she said nothing at all and started
walking again. A few steps on, she turned back exactly once.

I remember that retreating figure for a long time afterward. Not
surprised, not dismissive. Just the back of someone who had looked at
something that had nothing to do with her. I didn't know it that day, but
that would become how this whole city treated Speon. Not angry, not
stopping him — just regarding him as something that had nothing to do
with them.

"…Someone you know?" Speon asked.

"…Yes. Fifteen years old. Preserved seven years ago."

Speon drove the shovel in once more.

"…This city must have a lot of people like that."

"…Yes."

"…Does everyone look at it like that?"

I couldn't answer right away. Thinking about it, it really was true. No
one got angry, no one was curious. Everyone gave it exactly that much
attention — the attention you'd give something that had nothing to do
with you.

"…Yes. Everyone's like that."

Speon heard that and paused his hand for a moment. Then, as if it were
nothing much, he went back to lifting earth.

"…Guess I'd better keep digging, then." He said it. He didn't explain
why. I didn't know the weight of that line yet, either.

Once the sun had fully set, stars came out. Almost no one in this city
looks at the stars. The preserved don't go out at night without reason,
and the living go to sleep at their set hour. But that night I sat by the
pit and looked at the stars for a long time.

Outside, I'd looked at stars every night. The old man taught me
directions by the constellations. In the settlement, that had been
useful knowledge. In this city, it was useless. No one here ever needed
to find their direction at night. Wherever you went, the path was
already set.

That night I found myself thinking, after a long while, about useless
knowledge. The names of constellations, the knots the old man taught me,
how to read the color of soil. Since coming to this city I'd thought all
of it had become useless. Sitting beside Speon that night, I felt for the
first time that it wasn't that. It hadn't become useless — it had simply
had nowhere to be used. Speon was making a place for it to be used
again.

The next morning I went back to that spot. On the way, I didn't prepare
any words to explain why I was going. I couldn't remember the last time
I'd gone somewhere that didn't require me to have words ready.

The pit was a little deeper than the day before. Speon was already down
inside it. He saw me and, without any greeting, said this.

"…Flask."

I laughed and unhooked it.

That day I went by way of the distribution station. In front of it, as
always, a short line stood waiting. Only the living stand in that line.
Standing in it, I had a strange thought for the first time. The station
hands out water. Exactly as much as is needed. A well doesn't hand
anything out. It just comes up. Whoever needs it draws it themselves.

I thought about that difference for the first time that day. Rationing
is handing out something fixed. A well is simply something that's there.
One is given by whoever manages it; the other is given by the ground
itself, for nothing. This city only had the first kind. Speon had brought
the second.

The station attendant looked at my flask and asked.

"…Refilling again?"

"…Yes."

"…You filled it yesterday too."

"…I shared it."

The attendant tilted his head. His face said he didn't understand what
sharing a portion meant. Here, no one ever had reason to give their share
to someone else. Everyone needed only exactly their own.

I took the water and walked back toward the pit. On the way I passed the
spot where Eikon had turned back the day before. No one was there. Only
a couple of footprints left in the dirt.

I looked at those footprints a moment. Then I kept walking.

When I reached the pit, Speon was already down halfway again. The depth
had grown by a hand's width since the day before. I sat down beside it
and checked the color of the soil. Still red. Gray was still a long way
off.

"…How much more do you have to dig?" I asked.

"…Don't know." Speon said. "Until the red earth ends."

"…How much is that?"

"…You find out by digging."

I liked that answer better than any answer I'd heard in this city. This
city knew everything in advance. Visiting hours, ration amounts,
procedures. There wasn't a single thing done without already knowing how
it would turn out. Speon was doing this without knowing. And even so, he
never wavered.

I couldn't put that difference into words then. I can now. This city
moves knowing the outcome already. Since the outcome is fixed, there's
nothing new to learn. Speon moves without knowing the outcome. So every
day is a day of learning something new. I wanted to stay beside that
kind of day.

(Continued in Chapter 26.)

Chapter 26. We Don't Need One

_Previously_ — Speon began digging a well at the eastern edge of the
city. No one had asked him to. "Water that just sits rots. It has to
flow to stay alive." That was all he said. Every day I went and watched,
sharing my water with him. Eikon passed by once and looked without a
word.

From the second night on, word began to spread.

The way rumor traveled in this city was different from outside. Outside,
a rumor grows as it spreads. A story about one person dying can go around
a village once and come back as three people dying. Nothing like that
happened here. However many people passed a sentence along, it never
lost a single syllable from the original. Someone's digging a well at the
eastern edge. That sentence went all the way around the city and came
back unchanged.

I found that remarkable. A city that doesn't lie has rumors that travel
honestly too. They don't swell and they don't shrink. So in this city
there was never any reason to be frightened by a rumor. You were only
ever as frightened as the fact itself. And in this case, the fact wasn't
a frightening one. Someone's digging. That was all it was.

On the third morning, someone came.

Not the station attendant. His clothes were different — gray, with a
small emblem on the collar. I'd seen that emblem before, on the
Curator's building.

The man stopped in front of the pit and looked down at Speon.

"…What are you doing here?"

"…Digging a well." Speon answered without pausing his shovel.

"…Do you have a permit for this?"

"…No."

The man nodded. His face wasn't angry. Just a face confirming a fact.

"…This city doesn't need a well," he said. "The preserved have no need
of water, and the living are supplied their fixed ration at the
distribution station."

"…I know that."

"…Then why are you digging?"

Speon paused his shovel for a moment. He brushed off dirt and looked at
the man.

"…Not needing it isn't the same as not digging."

The man nodded again at that. He didn't argue. He just wrote something in
what looked like a notebook.

"…Understood. This isn't dangerous work, so I won't stop it. I'm simply
informing you. It isn't needed."

Then he turned and left.

Watching him walk away, I felt a little foolish for having expected
something momentous. Only then did I notice my palms had gone sweaty. I'd
seen this kind of situation many times outside. Someone does something
without permission, people come to stop them, an argument breaks out,
sometimes it even comes to blows. I must have been waiting for a scene
like that. But nothing happened here. A man came, stated one fact, and
left.

I've gone back over that exchange in my head more times than I can
count. I'd been expecting a fight. I'd expected him to say get a permit,
or stop this. He demanded nothing at all. He simply stated a fact and
left. It isn't needed. That single line sounded more absolute than any
order could have.

That afternoon, someone else came.

An older woman. She was passing by, then stopped and peered into the
pit. There was a little curiosity in her face — a rare expression in this
city.

"…What's this?" she asked.

"…A well," Speon answered.

"…What's a well?"

I was startled by the question. It was the first time I'd met someone who
didn't know what a well was. Speon wasn't startled at all.

"…A way of getting water from the ground."

"…Ah." The woman nodded. "I thought water came from the distribution
station."

"…Station water comes from somewhere too. It's stored there. This is
water that just comes straight up from the ground."

The woman thought for a moment.

"…So you don't need to store it?"

"…No. Because it keeps coming."

The woman turned that over in her expression. Then she said,

"…But I've never once been short of water. I always get exactly what I
need, when I need it."

"…I'm sure you have," Speon said.

"…Then why is this needed?"

Speon picked his shovel back up.

"…Maybe you'll never need it."

The woman turned away, looking satisfied with that answer. It hadn't
been an argument. It was a conversation in which both of them had
agreed the other was right. I found that stranger still.

People in this city don't lie. So the woman's "I don't need it" had
been true too. She really had never once gone without. And Speon hadn't
lied either. "Maybe you'll never need it" was something he really
thought, when he said it. Both of them were honest. And yet the two of
them were looking at entirely different things.

That evening, Eikon came again.

This time she wasn't passing through. She walked straight up to the pit.
She saw me and, instead of a greeting, asked this.

"…Why are you helping?"

"…It's fun."

"…What's fun about it? Why make something no one needs?"

I didn't know what to say to that, so I looked at Speon. Speon kept
digging and just listened to the two of us talk.

"…It might turn out to be needed," I said.

"…I don't lack anything," Eikon said. Her voice wasn't angry. It was the
voice of someone genuinely curious. "It's not just me. Everyone here is
the same way. So why is this needed?"

I hadn't prepared an answer to that question. I couldn't say she was
wrong. It was true — no one in this city was going without water, no one
was struggling for it.

"…I don't know," I said honestly.

Eikon nodded at that, as if satisfied. It wasn't that she was mocking me.
It was more like a confirmation that her own thinking had been right.

"…Then why dig, if you don't know?"

"…Because Speon is digging."

"…Is that a reason?"

I couldn't answer that one either. Eikon looked down into the pit for a
moment. Then she turned and walked away. She hadn't been angry, and she
hadn't been mocking. It was simply the walk of someone who hadn't gotten
an answer to her question and was going back.

I turned that question over all that night. Is that a reason. Truly, I
had no reason beyond the fact that Speon was digging. And yet it hadn't
felt like an insufficient one. That was strange. It couldn't be
explained by logic, but my mind was already made up.

On the fourth day, the station attendant himself came.

When I went to refill my flask, he spoke first, before I could.

"…That man digging the well — do you know him?"

"…Yes."

"…We've had a few inquiries. Whether the ration needs adjusting."

"…What did you tell them?"

"…That it doesn't. The current ration is sufficient."

I wanted to say something to that, but nothing came out. It was true that
the ration was sufficient. No one died of thirst. No one got angry
because the line came up short. The system was running perfectly.

Why does a perfectly running system need new water? I ran into that
question every time, and every time I stalled. This city had no words to
answer it with. The ration wasn't lacking. Whatever the well was needed
for, it wasn't a shortage. I still hadn't managed to put that into
words.

As he filled my flask, the attendant added,

"…But he keeps digging, I hear."

"…Yes."

"…No reason to stop him. It's not dangerous."

"…No."

"…It's just a little remarkable, that's all. Doing it while knowing it
isn't needed."

Hearing that, I realized this was the first time someone in this city had
felt Speon to be 'remarkable.' Not anger, not interest — some feeling
somewhere in between.

Late that afternoon, three children came running up.

There were children in this city too. Living children, not yet
preserved. They were a little different from the adults. They crouched
in front of the pit, eyes bright, and asked.

"…Is real water actually going to come out of this?"

"…It will," Speon said.

"…When?"

"…Don't know."

"…If you don't know, why dig?"

I nearly laughed at that. The children were asking the same question the
adults asked, only in a different tone. The adults asked it and turned
away. The children asked it and didn't turn away.

"…Because it's fun." Speon said. "Want to try?"

One child reached for the shovel, but another grabbed his arm.

"…No. We have to be back at our set time."

"…There's still time left."

"…Still no."

Listening to that exchange, something in me turned strange. These
children were growing older. Not yet preserved, and maybe someday to be.
By the time this well was finished, some of them might already be doing
something else entirely. Or they might already have gone toward the
Preserve.

I remembered those children's faces for a long time afterward. Curious
faces, and yet faces that turned back exactly on schedule. This city was
precise even with its children. It didn't stop their curiosity. It only
allowed exactly as much as was set.

The children waved and ran off — right on time, exactly. Watching them
go, I thought about how, at that age, I'd played in the water without a
thought for the time.

On the fifth day, I asked Speon.

"…People keep saying it isn't needed. Are you all right with that?"

"…I'm fine."

"…Doesn't it make you angry?"

Speon drove the shovel into the dirt pile and rested a moment.

"…No reason to be angry. They're right."

"…Right? But it's needed. Water is."

"…Not right now. Really. The ration is enough, and the preserved don't
drink. What they're saying isn't a lie."

That answer frustrated me.

"…Then why do you keep digging?"

Speon looked at me. There wasn't a trace of irritation or impatience in
his eyes.

"…Not needing it now and never needing it aren't the same thing."

I turned that over for a long time. That it wasn't needed now was a fact
everyone in this city agreed on. That it would never be needed — no one
had ever confirmed that. This city believed it would go on forever exactly
as it was now. And that was the city's pride, and its premise. That
nothing changes.

"…This city doesn't look like it's going to change," I said.

"…It looks that way," Speon said. "But looking like it doesn't change
isn't the same as not changing. It just means the reason to change
hasn't come yet."

I turned that over for a long time too.

I heard it, at first, as an argument meant to persuade. I know better
now. It wasn't persuasion. Speon wasn't trying to convince anyone. He
was simply doing what he believed was right, whether people said it was
needed or not. Persuasion demands a response. Speon demanded no response
at all.

Through those six days, I measured the pit's depth every day. There was
no proper ruler, so I used my own forearm. The first day, waist-high; the
second, chest-high; the third, half my own height; the fourth, a little
past half. By the fifth day, Speon had to stand fully inside the pit
before even his head was visible.

I wrote down every one of those numbers. I didn't know why, at the time.
Thinking of it now, it's because this city had exactly one thing that was
changing. The people didn't change. The ration didn't change. The visit
procedures didn't change. Only the pit grew a little deeper every day. I
was watching something change, for the first time in a long while. That's
why I wanted to count it.

That evening, I walked along turning over what the station attendant had
said. Remarkable. Doing it while knowing it isn't needed. The words kept
circling in my head. Only then did I realize how rare a feeling
remarkable was in this city. Surprise was rare, fear was rare, joy was
rare. The people of this city lived among things they already fully
knew. Being remarkable is a feeling that needs something unknown to exist
at all.

I pictured the attendant's face again. He'd said the ration was
sufficient, and in the same breath called it remarkable that Speon kept
digging. Both feelings lived inside one person at once. I think that was
the most honest reaction this city had. Not needed, and still
remarkable. Neither one was a lie.

When I reached the pit, Speon was still digging, late into the evening. I
stood beside him that night, for the first time, and told him everything
I'd seen so far.

That evening, for the first time, I got angry at Speon. Or not exactly
angry — my words just came out sounding angry.

"…It's strange," I said. "No one gets angry, no one stops him, they just
say it isn't needed. I find that more frightening than anything."

"…Why is that frightening?"

"…If people got angry, you could fight them. There's nothing to fight
here. Because everything they're saying is true."

Speon set down his shovel and looked at me for a long while.

"…Are you talking about this city right now, or about the well?"

I couldn't answer that right away. It was both. Or maybe it had never
been two separate things to begin with.

"…I don't know," I said.

"…Neither do I," Speon said. "But once you do know, that's when the
reason to dig becomes real. Right now it's just your body doing the
digging."

I fell asleep without understanding what he meant.

On the sixth morning, a piece of paper was stuck into the ground in front
of the pit.

It was an official document from the Preserve. I read it first — I read
faster than Speon did.

Notice regarding excavation work at this location. The current water
supply system is operating normally across all sectors, and no
assessment has found that additional water sources are needed.
However, as the work itself has been confirmed to pose no hazard, no
separate action will be taken to stop it. — Office of the Curator

I read the document aloud to Speon. He listened without stopping his
digging.

"…What do you think?"

"…About what?"

"…Doesn't it make you angry?"

"…Why would this make me angry. It's all true."

"…It says they're not even going to stop you."

"…Right. They won't. In this city, people stop something when they have
a reason, and don't when they don't. Right now there's no reason to
stop me."

I set the paper carefully on top of the dirt pile, weighing it down with
a stone so it wouldn't blow away.

I still have that document. It's a document I can't resent anyone over.
There's not one lie in it, and not one threat. It simply states the
facts, exactly as they are. That's how this city fights. It fights by
not fighting.

There was a signature at the bottom of the document. Office of the
Curator. Seeing that name, I thought of the Curator himself. The man who
had explained the release procedure to me. Kind, and worn thin. I found
myself wondering whether he'd written this document himself, or whether
someone had written it for him.

I didn't hold onto that question for long, at the time. I learned later.
He was a man who wrote documents like this in his own hand. He didn't
leave it to others. Whatever he decided, he put his own name to it. I
would come to understand why, much later, the first time I saw his face
turn sad.

I read the paper over once more. Every sentence was exact. No
exaggeration, no understatement. I couldn't explain, at the time, why
that exactness felt so chilling. I can now. An exact statement has no
gaps in it. And with no gap, there's nowhere to dig in.

That afternoon, I sat on the edge of the pit for the first time, swinging
my legs, and thought.

No one in this city hated Speon. No one tried to drive him out. Everyone,
politely, precisely, only ever said it wasn't needed. And every word of
that was true.

I sensed then, dimly, that this was a stronger defense than any
argument. Getting angry means acknowledging your opponent — admitting
they're worth fighting. This city didn't grant Speon even that much
acknowledgment. It simply treated him as something that had nothing to
do with it.

Speon accepted that treatment. He never felt wronged by it. I felt, for
the first time, caught between those two postures. The city's quiet
indifference, and Speon's quiet persistence. Neither one showed anger.
And yet they were exact opposites.

As the sun began to lean toward evening, Speon climbed out of the pit and
brushed off his hands.

"…Want to try digging a little yourself tomorrow?" he asked.

I couldn't answer right away. I wanted to. But it struck me as strange
how quickly I'd come to want it. I'd come here to bring my family back. I
hadn't come here to dig a well.

"…I'm not here to work," I said.

"…I know," Speon said. "What does that have to do with trying a shovel
once?"

I couldn't answer that either. I'd thought there was some connection.
When I tried to put it into words, there wasn't one.

"…I'll think about it."

Speon smiled. It was the first time I'd seen him do that. Deep lines
gathered at the corners of his eyes — the lines of a man who had laughed
a great deal, over a long life.

"…No need to think. Your body will decide on its own."

I heard that and refilled the flask and handed it to him. He drank and
gave it back. We sat a while without speaking.

On the way back to the dormitory, I went back over everything that had
happened in those six days, one by one. The official who'd asked for a
permit, the old woman who hadn't known what a well was, the station
attendant, Eikon, the children, and one sheet of paper. No one had gotten
angry. No one had stopped Speon. And yet, over those six days, the whole
city had been saying the same thing to him, over and over. That it wasn't
needed.

That night I thought I finally understood why those words frightened
me. It isn't needed wasn't a wrong thing to say. That's why it couldn't
be argued with. Watching a man keep digging, surrounded on every side by
words that couldn't be argued with — that was the first time I wanted to
go on watching someone for a long, long while. And I still didn't know
where that feeling had come from.

Lying in my room, I looked at my palms. Clean hands, without a single
blister. Looking at them, I counted, for the first time, how long these
hands had gone doing nothing at all. From the day I arrived in the city
to today — twenty-nine days. In all that time I had walked, stood,
stood in lines, filled out forms. The only things these hands had ever
gripped were paperwork and a flask.

I fell asleep looking at that hand. The next morning, the moment I
opened my eyes, the first thing that came to mind wasn't my mother, and
it wasn't my brother. It was the pit. I didn't think that was strange, at
the time. Looking back on it now, I should have.

(Continued in Chapter 27.)

Chapter 27. Digging Together

_Previously_ — for six days the city never got angry at Speon. It only
told him, precisely and politely, that it wasn't needed. Speon never
argued and kept digging. I carried water to him every day. Lying in my
room, I looked at my clean palms for a long time.

The night before, I couldn't fall asleep for a long time.

I kept going back over what Speon had said. No need to think. Your body
will decide on its own. I hadn't believed it. I'd always been a person
who thought first. Sitting behind that hill at nine years old, and later
setting the order of every word I meant to say on the way — both had
been the result of thinking. I had never once let my body decide on its
own.

But that night, strangely, my body won. The moment I opened my eyes in
the morning, my feet turned toward the pit first. Thought followed
after.

Leaving the dormitory, I thought about my mother and my brother. I could
apply for a visit any time. Any time at all. Since they never changed,
if I didn't go today I could go tomorrow, and if not tomorrow, the day
after. Whether that thought made things easier or harder for me, I
couldn't tell at the time.

The next morning, I picked up a shovel.

I woke the moment the sky lightened. It had been a long time since that
happened. Until then I'd always woken at a fixed hour, around when the
distribution station's bell rang. That day I was already awake before
the bell even sounded.

No one had told me to. There was a spare shovel Speon had left beside the
pit. I'd only looked at it for days. That day my hand simply reached for
it first.

"…Want to try?" Speon asked.

"…Yes."

I gripped the handle. It was a familiar weight in my hand. The wooden
handle had worn smooth. I knew right away that the smoothness wasn't
strange to me at all. My body already knew the angle between palm and
handle, even the direction the weight leaned.

I hadn't made anything with my hands since coming to this city. I'd
signed my name on forms, opened and closed the flask's cap, knocked on
doors. That was all. I'd been a person who worked with his hands since
fourteen, but in this city there was nothing for those hands to do.

I gripped the handle with both hands and weighed it. A little shorter
than the shovel I'd used outside. The blade was thinner. It didn't seem
like something made in this city, but something Speon had brought with
him. The handle was chipped in places — marks left by long use.

"…How old is this?" I asked.

"…This shovel? About twelve years."

"…That's old."

"…Good wood lasts. No reason to change it."

I heard that and set the blade against the dirt. Before the first stroke,
I paused a moment to steady my breath. It was such a small motion, and
yet strangely, I felt tense.

I drove the shovel into the earth.

I stepped on the upper edge of the blade and put my weight into it. The
feel of the soil splitting traveled up through the sole of my foot. I
pulled the shovel back. A clump of earth broke free.

It was a simple motion. And yet my body remembered it exactly — the
angle, the timing of the force, how to use my back. I felt my body move
before my mind had even finished thinking.

"…You're good at this," Speon said.

"…Did some of this before."

"…The old man taught you?"

"…Yes."

I took a second stroke. A third, a fourth. I was counting in my head.
By the tenth my breath had grown a little short. By the twentieth my
shoulders had started to ache. By around the thirtieth, my breathing
eased again instead. A rhythm was building. Unhurried, but never
stopping — the same rhythm Speon had shown me that first day.

Sinking into that rhythm, I felt something shift. Since coming to this
city I had been a person who did nothing but wait. Waiting for a visit,
waiting for an answer, waiting for a procedure to end. There was no
waiting in digging. Dig, and earth came up. The result came right away. I
only realized, in that moment, how much I had missed that.

I took a small knife out of my pack and hung it at my waist. It had been
the old man's. He'd given it to me before he died. The handle was wood,
worn glassy smooth from long use. I hadn't taken it out once, in all my
time in this city. There had been no need to. In this city, there was
almost nothing to cut or shape. Everything came already made.

That day I used the knife to smooth a rough patch of wood grain on the
shovel handle. Speon saw and asked.

"…That's a good knife."

"…It was his. The one who died."

"…Take care of it."

"…I will."

About half an hour later, Eikon passed by.

She stopped again this time too. But this time she was watching me, not
Speon. Watching my hands, to be exact — watching me hold the shovel.

"…Why are you doing that?" she asked.

"…Helping."

"…You didn't come here to live, Anemos."

"…No."

"…So why are you doing this city's work? It isn't even needed."

I answered without pausing my digging.

"…It's fun."

Eikon's face went a little strange at that answer. Not angry, not
mocking. A face that truly didn't understand.

"…Are fun and needed different things?"

I stopped digging at that question. It was a good one.

"…I think they're different," I said. "Something needed is something
you'd be in trouble without. Something fun is... something you don't
need, but want to do anyway."

"…Why do something you don't need?"

I couldn't answer that. Eikon didn't wait for an answer and started
walking again. I watched her go and drove my shovel back into the earth.

I held onto that question for a long time. Why do something you don't
need. It really wasn't a strange question in this city. Here, every
action had to have need attached to it as its reason. Fun wasn't a
reason. That day, for the first time, I felt strange to myself, doing
something for no reason but fun.

Around that time I began to feel that time moved differently inside the
rhythm of digging. Over the past month in this city, I had mostly
experienced time as waiting. I'd submit a visit request and wait days.
Hear the answer and wait more days. Inside that waiting, time crawled.
A day would pass with nothing happening, and vanish without my even
knowing whether it had been there at all.

Something like a bell sounded once from the direction of the Preserve's
building. The sound announcing visiting hours. I heard it, and I didn't
stop digging. It was the first time that had happened. Since coming to
this city, every time that sound rang, my body had turned toward it on
reflex. Not that day.

I only realized it afterward. That morning, for the first time, I let
that bell pass right through me. I felt like I should have felt guilty. I
strangely didn't. That was the stranger part.

While I was digging, time went differently. Every time I set the shovel,
I could see with my own eyes how much earth had come up. Dig ten times,
and there was ten strokes' worth of pit. A hundred times, a hundred
strokes' worth. It was time that accumulated into a result. I was
feeling that accumulation for the first time in a long while.

I couldn't put that difference into exact words that day. I can now.
Waiting time is time that passes over me. Digging time is time I make
myself. Since arriving in this city I had lived entirely inside the
first kind of time. That day, for the first time, I touched the second.

About an hour in, my palms began to burn.

I had no gloves. Speon took off his own and gave them to me.

"…I'm fine," I said.

"…Blister now, you'll be useless for days. Put them on."

I took the gloves and put them on. They were a little large. Still, the
fit felt familiar. It felt like returning to my fourteen-year-old self,
my fifteen-year-old self.

I want to describe that feeling exactly. It wasn't that I hadn't used my
body in seven years. I'd kept working the whole time — drawing water,
gathering wood, digging wells. But every bit of that work had been for
survival. Work someone told me to do, work I had to do. This digging was
different. No one had told me to. Nothing would happen if I didn't. I was
simply doing it because I wanted to.

Nothing like that had happened once in seven years. So I give this
chapter its title on that account. For the first time in seven years, I
used my body.

At noon, Speon said we should rest.

We leaned against the dirt pile. He shared out some dried food. Hard,
strongly salty. Outside food. Nothing like the taste of this city's
rations.

"…This city's food has no taste," I said.

"…It's not tasteless, it's just always the same," Speon said. "Same
ration every time, same ingredients every time. No reason for it to be
good or bad."

"…And this?"

"…This came out salty by accident. Nobody measured the salt going in."

I laughed at that.

"…A mistake?"

"…Something like that. But it's not bad, is it."

I chewed the salty food and turned that thought over. A mistake, and not
bad. This city had no mistakes. Everything was exact. There was nowhere
for a mistake to happen.

That was the first time I missed the thing called a mistake. A mistake
was proof that something was alive and moving. After seven years spent
among things that were only ever exact, one salty bite of food felt
strangely welcome.

Outside had been full of mistakes. The day my mother put salt in the soup
twice and it turned too salty. The time, after my father was gone, when
my mother tried fixing the roof for the first time and made the rain
leak in some new, wrong place. The time I tied the bucket rope wrong and
dropped the bucket to the bottom of the well. Things like that happened
all the time. And every time, someone laughed, or got angry, or fixed it.
That commotion was ordinary life.

This city had none of that commotion. No leaking roof, no dropped
bucket, no oversalted soup. Everything went exactly according to plan. I
only learned, coming to this city, how quiet that kind of perfection
was. A perfect place had nothing to talk about. The words people shared
shrank right along with it.

That afternoon, looking at the half-eaten salty food, I felt, for the
first time since coming to this city, like telling someone what had
happened that day. I told Speon the story — the time I tied the bucket
rope wrong. He laughed out loud. I remember that laugh being louder than
any sound I'd heard in this city.

That afternoon, I got a blister. The gloves hadn't helped. A small,
clear blister rose on the inside of my palm, just below the thumb.

Speon took my hand and looked it over.

"…That's a lot of work for a first day."

"…It hurts."

"…It's supposed to. That's how a hand gets made new."

"…What do you mean?"

"…Once the blister breaks and a callus grows in, that's a well-digger's
hand. Until it does, it isn't."

I heard that and looked at my hand. It was still a hand that was nothing
much. But that one blister felt like the start of something.

I thought of my brother. He used to sit beside me every time I drew
water and watch my hands. When a blister came up he wanted to touch it,
but I wouldn't let him, because it hurt. He still kept watching from
beside me, all the same.

"…What's wrong with your hand?" he asked me once.

"…It's from working."

"…Will I be like that someday too?"

"…You don't have to be."

"…Why?"

I hadn't answered him then. My brother had been preserved at eight, so
that hand was still, even now, the same smooth hand of an eight-year-old.
Without blisters, without calluses — a hand that had never made a single
thing.

Thinking that, I stopped my hand. A strange guilt rose in me. While
blisters rose on my hand, my brother's hand stayed exactly as it was. I
was growing, and he wasn't. That fact suddenly weighed on me, all over
again.

I had dug a well with this hand at fourteen. But that hand had been
gone a long time already. Over seven years, through other kinds of work,
calluses had risen and worn away, risen and worn away again. Coming to
this city, they'd disappeared altogether. That day, I had started making
that hand again.

Speon wrapped my hand with a scrap of cloth instead of a proper bandage.
His fingers were clumsy at it. He was a well-digger's hand, not a
bandaging hand, it seemed. The knot came loose twice before it finally
held on the third try.

"…You're no doctor," I said, laughing.

"…Never had much use for doctors. Get hurt, wrap it up, keep working."

"…Doesn't it hurt?"

"…It hurts. But it's better than stopping work. Stop, and you start
thinking too much."

I said nothing to that. Stop, and you start thinking too much. I heard
that as being about me. Since coming to this city, I'd spent most of my
time stopped. And in all that time, I'd thought far too much. Words I'd
prepared, words I hadn't managed to say, words prepared all over again.

Speon said it as if it were nothing. But it stayed with me from that day
on. Stop, and you start thinking too much. Every time I turned that
sentence over, I realized all over again how long I'd been stopped in
this city.

As the sun leaned toward evening, the station attendant passed by and saw
us. He stopped and watched for a moment.

"…Digging together?" he asked me.

"…Yes."

"…You don't even need it."

I answered without preparing anything, just what came out of my mouth.

"…I'm not doing it because I need it."

The attendant paused a moment at that. His face looked like he'd never
heard those words before.

"…Then why?"

"…I just want to."

He seemed to turn that over for a while, then nodded without saying
anything more and walked on.

I remember that moment for a long time. It was the first time I'd
confirmed for myself, out loud, that I wasn't doing this because I
needed to. In this city, every action had to have a reason — need,
procedure, regulation. That day, for the first time, I did something
with no reason at all. And I learned how freeing that could be.

Even after the attendant walked off, I stood there a while longer,
looking at the dirt on my hands. Earth was packed deep under my
fingernails. It didn't look like it would come off easily even washed. I
didn't mind that. If anything, that dirt felt like proof I'd done
something that day.

Speon kept digging the whole time. I glanced at the side of his face for
a moment. Sweat had mixed with dust and left streaks across it. He showed
no sign of tiring. He really did seem like someone who could do this work
all day.

"…Isn't it hard?" I asked.

"…It's hard."

"…But you don't look like it's hard."

"…Hard and unbearable are different things," Speon said, driving the
shovel in. "This is a hard I can bear. There's a hard that can't be
borne too. That's a different story."

I thought about that distinction for a long time. People in this city had
no hard things at all. They were people who didn't even know what hard
meant. Speon knew what hard was, and kept choosing it anyway. I didn't
know, at the time, exactly what that difference meant.

Writing this record now, I take that thing Speon said out again. A hard
that can be borne, and a hard that can't. I went on, after that, telling
these two apart for the rest of my life. Which kind was the hard of
walking back from visiting my mother? I didn't know then. I would only
learn it much later in this book.

The pit grew noticeably deeper over that one day. Twice as fast as Speon
working alone. I felt proud of that. It was the first time I'd felt
proud of anything since coming to this city.

By evening, when I set down the shovel, my hands were shaking. My arms
ached. My back hurt. I welcomed all of it.

"…Coming back tomorrow?" Speon asked.

"…Yes."

"…What about your family?"

I couldn't answer that right away. It was true, I'd come here to bring my
family back. And yet all that day, I'd barely thought of them at all.

"…I can visit any time," I said. "Since they never change."

Speon heard that and looked at me for a long while. His face looked like
he wanted to say something, but in the end he said nothing.

"…But," he said, starting again. "That they never change — is that a
good thing, or a bad thing?"

I couldn't answer that right away either. I'd always thought it was
good. Not disappearing, not suffering, not being forgotten. But faced
with Speon's question, that answer suddenly wavered.

"…I don't know," I said. It was the answer I'd been giving most, these
days.

"…Not knowing is fine," Speon said, standing and shouldering the shovel.
"Better than pretending to know everything."

"…See you tomorrow, then."

Walking back to the dormitory, I kept looking down at my hand. Blisters,
streaks of dirt, earth packed under my nails. A hand I couldn't have
imagined, just days before.

On the way I passed the distribution station. The door was shut. For the
first time, I walked past without filling my flask. There was still
about half a day's water left in it. That half-full flask seemed to show
how much I'd moved that day. I'd drunk far more than usual, and there was
still water left. Working the body harder should mean more thirst, not
less — I wondered briefly why there was any left, then let the question
go. It wasn't one that needed an answer.

I slept deeply that night, for the first time in a long while. No
dreams. The next morning when I opened my eyes, the first thing I felt
was my hands aching. Strangely, I liked that ache. It was proof the body
was alive.

I started to count that ache and stopped myself. That day I learned,
for the first time, that some things are better left uncounted.

From that day I went to the pit every morning. The first few days my
body was so worn out I collapsed into sleep each evening. Then my body
began to adjust to the work again. Blisters broke, and thin calluses
settled where they'd been. Every time I touched those calluses, I felt
like I was meeting my fourteen-year-old self again.

In those twelve days, I never once applied for a visit. I knew it was
strange and didn't stop anyway. I sensed, dimly, that I was avoiding
something. I wouldn't learn what it was until I went down into the
valley.

On the twelfth day, the pit had grown well past my own height. Speon
built a ladder and set it in place. Climbing up and down it, I found
myself feeling, all at once, that this was no longer just a pit. It was
turning into something else.

(Continued in Chapter 28.)

Chapter 28. Going Down

_Previously_ — I went to Speon's pit every morning. Blisters rose on my
hands, and calluses settled in. For twelve days I didn't apply for a
visit. I sensed, dimly, that I was avoiding something. The pit had
passed my own height, and a ladder had gone in.

On the morning of the thirteenth day, I didn't go to the pit.

My body was turned in a different direction from the moment I opened my
eyes. I decided not to fight that feeling. Speon had been right. There
were times the body knew something before thought did.

Instead I walked to the edge of the city. I hadn't been that way in a
while — to the spot where I'd first looked down into the Dry Valley, and
where Eikon had told me what that valley was. I met no one on the whole
walk there. The city was unusually quiet, that early in the morning.

I stood at the edge and looked down.

The valley was the same as ever, that day too. Dry ground, faded soil,
a landscape without a trace of moisture, however far you looked. I'd
looked down into it several times before. But that day it looked
different.

I thought for a long time about where that difference came from. I know
now. Before, I had only ever looked down the way you look at something.
That day, for the first time, I was imagining myself going down into it.

For twelve days, digging that pit, I had gone down and down every day.
The moment it passed my own height, I'd started climbing up and down a
ladder. The digging itself had already been a movement downward. That
morning, for the first time, I felt that fact clearly. I had already
been practicing how to go down. It just so happened the thing I'd been
going down into was a hole in the ground.

Writing this sentence now, I think that coincidence might not have been
a coincidence at all. I'm not certain. But this much is true: a body that
had spent twelve days repeating the same downward motion, every day,
walked one morning toward a far larger downward, on its own.

Writing this record, I often go back and count coincidences like that
one. Meeting a well-digger. My hands remembering their work again beside
him. And then, twelve days later, heading toward the valley. Take away
any single one of them, and I wouldn't have been standing at that edge
that day.

Standing at the edge, I said nothing for a long time. Wind blew. That
was rare in this city. It came up from the direction of the valley — dry,
lukewarm wind. I let it hit my face and closed my eyes.

I went back to the pit and told Speon.

"…I'm going to go down into that valley."

Speon stopped digging. He looked at me for a long while. Not a surprised
face. If anything, the face of someone who'd been waiting.

"…Why?" he asked.

"…I don't know. I just feel like I have to go."

"…Looking for something?"

I couldn't answer that right away. I didn't know myself what I was going
to look for. Eikon had said there were traces there of people whose
preservation had failed. I couldn't tell whether I wanted to see those
traces, or whether I was looking for something else entirely.

"…I don't know," I said again.

Speon nodded.

"…Will you come with me?" I asked.

He drove the shovel into the earth pile. Then, for the first time, he
went quiet for a long while. That silence was unfamiliar to me. Speon
was usually quick to answer. A question would come and an answer would
follow, almost right away. This time was different. He touched the
pit's wall once, as if stroking it, looked up at the sky once, and only
then opened his mouth.

I was counting the length of that silence. By the time I reached ten, he
finally spoke.

"…No." He said it, at last.

"…Why not?"

"…That's a place you have to go."

I didn't understand that at once. I couldn't help asking again.

"…What does that mean?"

"…I'm a man who digs wells. I can decide where the water goes. But why
you have to go down into that valley isn't something I can decide for
you. Only you know that."

"…I don't know it either."

"…Then you go not knowing. That's how it should be."

I wanted to argue with that, but I had nothing to argue with. Speon kept
smoothing his shovel and neither pushed me nor tried to stop me.

I didn't understand it then. I understood later. Speon was a man who
drew water toward a place. He wasn't a man who led people toward one.
If I had to go down into that valley, it had to be on my own feet. Not
led there by anyone's hand.

I stood there a while longer. From inside the pit I could hear Speon
digging again — earth being lifted, feet pressing down. That familiar
rhythm only made me more restless. As long as that sound went on, I kept
feeling like I was putting something off.

"…Scared?" Speon asked, without stopping his digging.

"…Yes."

"…Scared of what?"

I couldn't answer right away. I couldn't tell whether it was the valley
itself that frightened me, or what I'd see there, or the fear of coming
back up somehow changed.

"…I don't know."

"…So you're afraid of not knowing." Speon said. "That's a fine kind of
fear to have. The bad kind of fear is being afraid of what you know."

"…What's the difference?"

"…Fear of what you know keeps you from going at all. Fear of what you
don't know just means you're afraid while you go."

I couldn't say I understood that distinction. But hearing it, my feet
felt a little lighter.

I asked Speon one last thing.

"…What if I don't come back up?"

Speon's hand stopped. For the first time, his face turned serious.

"…Then I'll come down."

"…You just said I had to go alone."

"…You not coming back up alive, and you not being able to come back up,
are different things," Speon said. "If you see something down there and
decide not to come back up, that's your own to decide. But if you're
hurt and can't come back up, that's mine to go for."

I liked that distinction. It wasn't a promise never to come for me. It
was a promise to respect me.

"…So you're worried?" I asked.

"…I am. But being worried doesn't mean I can go in your place."

I heard that and looked at Speon's face again. It was a worried face.
And yet that worry didn't hold me back. That was the first day I learned
that both things could live inside one person at once.

"…Do I need to prepare anything?" I asked.

"…Water." Speon said it shortly. "Not much else."

"…Is it dangerous?"

"…Don't know. No one's ever gone down."

That answer surprised me a little. He'd always seemed, until then, like
a man who moved only once he knew something.

"…You haven't gone either?"

"…No. Thirty-seven wells, and I've never once come across a valley like
this one."

"…Then why tell me to go, without telling me anything?"

"…Because there's nothing I can tell you. If it were something I knew,
I'd teach it to you gladly enough. This is something I don't know."

I turned that over. Since coming to this city, I'd always been the one
learning something someone else knew. Procedure, from the Curator. This
city's logic, from Eikon. Digging, from Speon. The valley was a place no
one knew anything about. I found, strangely, that I liked that.
Somewhere I didn't have to follow in anyone's footsteps. Somewhere my
own first footprint would become the only record of that ground.

I can only say it now — that it actually put me at ease. If it was a
place no one knew, there was nothing shameful in not knowing it myself.

I filled my flask and shouldered the small sack Speon had packed for me.
A little dried food, a coil of rope.

"…Why the rope?"

"…Don't know. There are times it's good to have."

I laughed at that. It was such a Speon answer. A man who prepared without
certainty. And yet his preparations always fit exactly what was needed.

Before heading back to the dormitory, I ran into Eikon. By chance. She
was standing where she always walked.

"…Where are you going?" she asked, looking at the sack on my shoulder.

"…The valley."

Eikon's expression stiffened for a moment.

"…Why?"

"…I don't know. I'm curious."

"…There's nothing to see there," she said. "They're just failures."

"…I'm curious what failure looks like."

Eikon looked at me for a long while. Then she said this.

"…Don't come back a different person."

I didn't understand what that meant and tried to ask her again. But she
turned and walked off before I could.

I turned that line over for a long time afterward. Don't come back a
different person. In this city, changing at all was itself something to
be feared. Eikon had shared that fear with me. I couldn't tell, at the
time, whether it was worry or warning. Thinking of it now, I think it was
both. And either way, it meant she was thinking of me.

I opened the sack once more and checked what was inside. Rope, dried
food, a small knife — the old man's knife. Packing it, I felt somehow
steadier. I thought for a moment about how the old man might have walked
a road like this one. He probably would have just walked, without a
word.

On the way I passed the distribution station. The attendant saw me and
asked.

"…Where are you off to? You filled your flask twice."

"…I'm going down into the valley."

The attendant's face moved, for the first time, in a big way. A face of
surprise.

"…There? Why?"

"…I don't know. I just feel like I have to go."

"…No one goes there."

"…I know."

He looked at me a moment, then, without a word, filled my flask to the
very top. More than the regulation amount. I noticed and said nothing.
He said nothing either.

I remember that moment clearly. In a city that ran on exactness, that
day he stepped just slightly outside the regulation, for the first time.
No one made anything of it. It felt to me like proof that something was
still left in the people of this city. Some small wish to slip, just a
little, unnoticed, past what was set.

I thanked the attendant and started walking again. I half-waited for a
moment to hear him call after me. No sound came. I decided that wasn't a
bad thing either.

"…When are you coming back up?" he asked.

"…I don't know."

"…All right. Go, not knowing."

I heard that and started walking. A few steps on, I looked back. Speon
was already digging again. He hadn't seen me off. He simply went on with
his own work.

That retreating figure didn't hurt me. It was easier, if anything. Being
seen off gives you one more reason you have to come back. Speon hadn't
loaded me with that reason. My coming back had to be something for which
the reason had to already be mine.

The path down into the valley began at the city's edge.

At first it was a gentle slope. I walked carefully over loose gravel.
The sound of earth crumbling underfoot rose up. It was a sound I'd never
once heard inside the city — every path there was packed hard and firm.

Scattered dry brush lined both sides of the path. Branches without a
single leaf on them. I touched one. It rustled as if it would snap, but
it didn't. It just held on, dried out. That stayed with me, strangely —
things standing there a long time, neither dead nor alive.

After a while the slope steepened. I used the rope several times, hooking
it into cracks in the rock and lowering myself down. It was a method the
old man had taught me. My hands remembered it exactly.

That was when I understood why Speon had packed the rope. He'd said he
didn't know, but he'd sensed, dimly, that a path like this might exist.
Saying you don't know and preparing nothing at all are different things.
I turned that difference over, going down and down.

I began counting my steps. Counting was an old habit of mine. Every
hundred steps I stopped for a sip of water. Two hundred, three hundred.
The path kept leading down, and down. At first I kept looking back
toward where the city had been; at some point I stopped looking back at
all.

I came to a stretch thick with rock. I climbed down bracing myself
against them with my hands. Every time the healed-over blister marks on
my palms touched stone, they stung. I bore the pain and kept going down.
The pain almost seemed to show me the way — the farther down I went, the
worse it hurt. Hurting meant I was doing something.

I remembered something the old man used to say. On rough ground, read it
with your hands first. Hands are more honest than eyes. Following that,
I checked the rock face with my fingertips before setting my feet down.
A few times my foot slipped, but I never fell hard.

Around the six-hundredth step, I realized for the first time that I'd
been walking without looking back at all. My eyes had been forward the
whole time.

As I went down, the air changed.

The city's air was always cool and dry. The valley's air was drier
still. My throat felt scratchy with every breath. I took a sip from the
flask. The water seemed to be going down far faster than it had in the
city.

The sound was different too. The city had at least a little sound —
footsteps, a door. The valley had none at all. My own breath and my own
footsteps were the only sound there was.

I thought I'd heard that silence somewhere before. It took me a long
time to place it. The night before my mother fell ill, just before
sickness swept the settlement — the whole village holding its breath.
That silence. The silence right before something happens.

The light was different too. The city's sunlight was always the same —
falling in exact, predictable brightness, morning like morning and
evening like evening. The valley's light changed color with every angle
it struck. Rock-shadows stretched long, then shortened; dust caught the
sun and drifted in a pale haze. Watching that shifting light, I found
myself thinking this valley felt, strangely, more alive than the city
did. It was a strange thought. I'd been told this was a land of dry,
dead things.

A few times something crumbled underfoot. I stepped on it without
thinking, believing it was stone, and only realized afterward that it
wasn't. Each time, I stopped and looked down. I didn't yet have the
nerve to find out what it was. I just kept walking.

Once I nearly took a bad fall. A rock that looked solid crumbled soft and
dry beneath me. I threw out my arm and caught the rock beside it. My
heart pounded for a long while after. I sat down there a moment to catch
my breath.

That was the first time I truly felt this path held real danger. Until
then I'd said I was afraid, but it hadn't been a danger my body had
lived through. In that brief moment when the rock crumbled, I felt that I
was truly alone here. No one to help. That fact frightened me, and yet,
strangely, it also felt clear.

That was the first time I understood, in my body, the difference
between this city and this valley. The city had no danger. In exchange,
nothing ever happened. The valley had danger. And inside that danger, I
felt, more clearly than ever, that I was alive.

Sitting there catching my breath, I opened the flask and took a sip. More
than half the water was already gone. I gauged what was left and started
walking again.

Standing back up, I looked at my palms. Bracing myself against the rock
had torn the healed blister-marks open again. No blood. Just thin,
peeled skin left behind. I opened and closed my hand a moment, checking
the feeling in it, and walked on.

The slope went on. I used the rope a few more times. Each time, my hands
grew a little surer at the knots. What had taken two tries to tie
correctly at first came together in one, later on. My body seemed to
have fully remembered the work all over again.

Before reaching the bottom, I looked back up one last time.

The city was up there. Small and quiet. That was the moment I first felt,
truly, how far I had come — from where my mother and brother were, from
where Speon was, from the distribution station and the Curator's
building.

For a moment I thought about turning back. That it wasn't too late yet.
But my feet kept going down. Speon had been right. My body moved before
thought did.

Dust rose low underfoot and settled again. I stood there and thought of
my mother and my brother. At this very hour, they would be there, with
the same faces, in the same room, waiting for me to come. Whether it
could even be called waiting, I wasn't sure. They didn't wait. They
simply were there. I turned that difference over once more and kept
walking.

The slope beneath my feet began, little by little, to level out. I could
feel I was nearly there. My last few steps came strangely slow. There was
no reason to hurry. If anything, it felt like I shouldn't.

My foot finally touched the valley floor.

The soil was different from the city's. Lighter. It broke apart under my
foot and scattered like powder. I stood there and caught my breath for a
moment.

I looked all around.

It was different from what I'd imagined. I'd expected something
frightening, something terrible. But what first came into view was
simply dry ground stretching wide open — dry ground that seemed to run
all the way to the horizon.

And scattered across it, there were things.

I hadn't gone close enough yet to know what they were. But I could tell,
even from a distance. Those things had once been something.

I turned back and looked up at the path I'd come down. The city was no
longer visible. The slope blocked the view. Strangely, that put me at
ease. Not being able to see the way back didn't mean I couldn't go back.
It only seemed to mean that, for now, I should look only forward.

I closed the flask's cap and fastened it firmly at my waist. Then I took
a step. Dust rose quietly underfoot and settled again.

I stood there for a long while. I was afraid to take the next step. It
would be a long time — not until I had properly walked the valley — that
I understood exactly what that fear had been for.

I took my first step toward the scattered things.

(Continued in Chapter 29.)

Chapter 29. Bones

_Previously_ — I finally reached the floor of the Dry Valley. Speon
hadn't come with me. "That's a place you have to go." I took my first
step toward the scattered things. I hadn't yet gone close enough to
know what they were.

The floor of the valley was lower than the city, wider, and quiet.

Even the angle the light came in was different from the city's. The sky
showed as a thin strip between the cliffs, and the light falling through
that gap colored the whole valley a pale, milky white. The shadows were
thick and long. I stood there a moment and let my eyes adjust to that
light.

The cliffs rose high on both sides. They blocked off the upward direction
where the city was, so that now only a narrow band of sky was visible. I
followed the grain of the cliff with my eyes. Layers of differently
colored soil were stacked one on another. A red layer, a brown layer, a
gray layer. A cross-section with a long span of time engraved into it
exactly as it had happened.

Near the base of the cliff, things that looked like roots showed through
the soil. Traces of something that had tried to grow and hadn't finished.
I followed those roots with my gaze. I couldn't see where the ends led.

There was a smell in the air. The city had almost no smell at all. In a
place where everything was managed to an exact, regulated quantity, it
seemed smell didn't survive either. The valley had a smell. The smell of
dry earth, of old wood, and another smell besides that I couldn't quite
explain. Not sweet, not rank — just very, very old.

The first thing I saw was a shoe.

Only one. Half buried in the soil, its shape still intact. The leather
was dry enough to crumble at a touch. The laces had rotted through and
snapped. I crouched down in front of it. Inside, the shape of a foot
still faintly remained. No one was wearing it. Only the trace of someone
who had.

I reached out to touch it, then stopped myself.

A few steps further on there was another shoe. This time a pair. Set
side by side. As if someone had taken them off, set them neatly, and
walked away somewhere. That neatness stayed with me a long time. It was
that day I learned, for the first time, that what is arranged carefully
outlasts what is left in disorder.

There was a small shoe too. A child's. I stood longest in front of that
one. The toe was especially worn through. It must have belonged to a
child who ran a great deal.

Walking a little further, I came to scraps of cloth.

Things that had once been clothing. All the color had faded until it was
hard to tell the cloth from the soil. In one spot several pieces lay
overlapping. I guessed they must once have been a single garment. The
place where a sleeve had been, the place where a collar had been. It had
settled into the ground in the same shape a person once wore it.

Every time the wind blew, those scraps of cloth stirred lightly. As if
something in them were still alive. And yet they looked as if they would
crumble the instant a hand touched them.

Further on the scraps grew more numerous. In some places several layers
of clothing lay folded over one another. I thought that must not be one
person's trace but several people's, overlapping in a single spot. As
time had piled up in layers, so had the traces.

Some cloth still kept a trace of color. A scrap with a reddish tint, a
thread that still held a bit of blue. I couldn't gauge how long that
color had managed to hold out. Everything else had already returned to
the color of the earth, and only that small bit of color was still
holding on.

Walking further, small objects began to appear.

A button. A comb. A broken piece of wood. Some I could tell the original
use of; some I couldn't. I walked among them and counted. Ten, twenty,
thirty. I didn't stop counting. If I didn't count, I couldn't bear it.

In one spot there was a small bundle of paper. It looked as if it would
crumble if I unfolded it, so I didn't. A faint trace of what had been
writing showed through. I couldn't tell whether it had been a letter or
a list.

Around the spot where the paper bundle lay, small shards of glass were
scattered as well. They looked like they had once been a bottle, though
only a few fragments still kept a recognizable shape. I picked one up,
then set it back down. It felt as though the things here ought to stay
exactly here.

On the ground lay a single thin cord. Rotted down almost to a thread. One
end was driven into the earth; the other lay on the soil, connected to
nothing. It looked like a cord that had once been tied to something.

Beside it, small stones were set out in a row. Not arranged by chance.
Someone had deliberately stood them up like that. I followed the row.
Twelve of them. I couldn't tell what they had been counting, or marking.
Only the number twelve was left behind.

Further along there was something that looked like a small toy. It
seemed to have been carved from wood, though almost none of its shape
remained. An animal? A person? I crouched in front of it a moment, then
stood back up.

The ground grew darker in color the further I walked.

From red soil to gray-brown soil, and further still, soil that was
nearly white. I remembered what Speon had said about soil colors. Gray,
he'd said, was where water had passed through. But this white was
different even from that. It didn't look like a trace water had passed
through — it looked like a trace of where water had drained out
completely.

Every time I set my foot down, that white soil rose like powder and
settled again. Every breath brought a little of that powder against the
back of my throat. It tasted dry.

Here and there along the valley wall there were things like shallow
hollows. I couldn't tell whether they'd been made by people or had
formed naturally. I looked briefly into one of them. Something was
stacked deep inside, but it was too dark to make out what. I didn't go
in.

There were more traces of small pools scattered across the ground. Some
large, some very small. Regardless of size, the inside of each held the
same cracked pattern. A place where water had been and then vanished
leaves the same trace no matter its size — I learned that that day.

I looked back at my own footprints left in that white soil. A long trail
stretching behind me. It was the only new footprint pressed into this
valley. Every other trace here was old; only mine had just been made.

Every time the wind blew, the edges of that footprint crumbled a little
more. I walked a few more steps and turned to check. The print behind me
was already going soft at the edges. It was then I learned that this
valley didn't hold onto even a new trace for very long.

There were a few things whose shape had survived whole.

Something that had been a tree. A tree that must once have been large
stood now hardened gray. Down to the tips of its branches, it was still
exactly there. Only the leaves were gone. I laid a hand against the base
of it. I could feel the grain of the bark just as it was. It was no
different from touching a living tree. Only there was no warmth in it.

That tree wasn't alone. A little further off, several more like it were
standing. It might once have been a forest. I walked among them, stepping
through the lattice pattern their branches' shadows made. Through the
leafless branches, the sky showed in small broken pieces.

One tree's base had split open. Something was inside the crack. I bent
down and looked. A small bone. It looked like a bird's. Where a nest
should have been, there was no nest — only its trace remained.

Beside it was the mark of a small pool. I could tell at once it was a
place water had been. The cracked pattern on its floor spread out like a
spider's web. The trace left by soil shrinking as water dried away. White
powder had gathered in the gaps between the cracks.

I ran my fingertips along that cracked pattern. The depth varied from
place to place. The deepest crack went in about the width of a finger
joint. I tried to gauge how large that pool must once have been. Walking
its edge, it came to about thirty of my steps. Not a small pool.

At the very center of the pool's floor, in the spot where water must
have lingered longest at the very end, a small mark remained. A round,
shallow depression. I recognized it as a footprint and stopped walking.
It was the trace of someone who had stood beside the last of the water,
until the very last moment.

Walking further, the valley widened.

The cliffs on either side drew back, and my view opened up. I tried to
take in, all at once, everything scattered across that wide space, but I
couldn't take it all in. I couldn't see an end to it. I had no way to
gauge how long, or how widely, these traces had been accumulating.

The oldest things had already decayed to where they were barely
distinguishable from the soil. Traces holding only the barest hint of
shape, that would collapse at a touch. The most recent things still kept
a form you could recognize as having once belonged to a person.

Watching that difference, I felt that this valley moved by the way time
accumulates. The city was a place where time had stopped. The valley was
a place where time flowed, very slowly, but unmistakably.

Inside the widened valley, spots crowded with traces and spots utterly
empty of any trace alternated one after another. Every time I passed one
of the empty spots, I felt, strangely, a heavier weight settle on me. A
spot with traces told you, at least, that something had been there. An
empty spot told you nothing at all.

At the center of one empty spot, one very small thing lay by itself. A
ring. The metal hadn't decayed; it remained intact. I looked down at that
ring for a long time. Something was engraved on the inside, but soil had
gotten into it and I couldn't make it out. I left that unwiped too, and
moved on.

It was around then that things that looked like bones began to appear on
the ground.

At first I wasn't sure they were really bones. The color was close to the
soil's, and their shape looked on the verge of crumbling. But looking
closer, they were unmistakably bones. Some long and slender, some short
and round. In some places they still held a complete shape; in others
they were scattered.

I walked carefully among them. I tried not to step on any. In some spots
they were gathered together in a single place, as if someone had once
lain down right there. I stopped in front of one such spot.

That kind of spot wasn't only one. As I walked I started counting them.
