Five, ten, seventeen. Some spots lay close together. I thought they might
have been a family. Some lay far off, alone.

Some were divided up. Places where no complete shape remained — only a
few fragments scattered here and there. I noticed the degree of
scattering was different from place to place. In some spots the
fragments were still gathered close, as if scattered only recently; in
others they'd been scattered so long ago that the fragments had spread
far apart from each other.

In one spot, unusually small ones were gathered together. I knelt down
in front of it. Judging by the size, they belonged to a child. I couldn't
move from in front of that spot for a long time.

Writing this chapter, I think this is as far as the most accurate
description I'm capable of can go. I don't intend to carry it further
into words. Some scenes become a lie the moment you add explanation to
them.

I passed that spot and kept walking. At some point, without my noticing,
even the habit of counting my steps had stopped. There is a point counting
cannot carry you past — I learned that for the first time, that day.

Along one wall of the valley there was a row of shallow hollows worn
into the rock. They looked like places where people had once sat, backs
leaned against the stone. I started to count those worn hollows one by
one, then gave it up. I didn't want to count again.

Below the wall, beside those hollows, a thin groove ran a long way. It
looked like a mark scraped long and slow by a fingernail. I didn't follow
that groove. I only swept it with my eyes and passed on.

Every time the wind blew, dry earth spun low across the ground.

Inside that spinning, small things rose and settled again together.
Threads, thin fragments of bone, unnameable bits of debris. I watched
them catch a brief glint in the air and then sink back down to the
earth.

As the sun lowered, long shadows fell across that white soil. The
shadow of a tree, my own shadow, and the shadows of the scattered
things. Looking only at the shadows, it seemed as if something were
still standing there.

The lower the light dropped, the deeper the colors grew. Things that had
been plain white, plain gray by day caught the sunset and turned orange,
turned red. Watching that change, I understood that this valley wore two
entirely different faces, by day and by evening. The valley by day
looked like a land of dead things. The valley by evening looked like
something was burning.

In that light, I felt, for the first time, that this valley was
beautiful. The moment that thought came to me, I was startled at myself.
I couldn't tell whether I was allowed to find a place like this
beautiful. But the sunset asked no permission of mine before it kept
deepening into red.

I walked further into the valley.

The sound of things crumbling underfoot went on and on. Dry leaves, dry
earth, dry bone. The sounds were all much the same. I tried to tell them
apart, then gave that up too. There was no need to tell them apart. They
were all the same sound.

The further I walked into the valley, the sparser the traces actually
became. I thought that was strange. I'd assumed the farther from the
city, the more would have accumulated — it was the opposite. The mouth
of the valley held the densest crowd of traces, and going inward they
thinned out more and more.

I didn't understand the reason then. Only, it occurred to me that this
sparser space might actually hold the oldest things of all. Whatever had
arrived here first might have gone in deepest, and been worn by wind and
soil the longest, until by now it left almost no trace at all.

There was something on the ground that looked like a dry riverbed. I
could tell from its winding shape that it was a place water had once run.
I followed that dry course. The soil underfoot was a little firmer there
than elsewhere. Earth packed down long ago by a current.

Far off, deeper in the valley, I saw something moving.

It was too far to make out what it was. I stood in place and looked that
way for a long time. The movement was slow and steady. Different from
something merely stirred by the wind.

I stood still and kept watching that direction. The sky was nearly dark
by now. Stars were beginning to come out, one by one. The same stars I'd
seen from the city's edge. That familiarity was, in a small way, a
comfort.

The movement didn't vanish. If anything it grew clearer, little by
little. I started walking that way. The sound of dry things crumbling
underfoot went on. I decided not to count that sound.

The further I walked, the fewer the scattered traces became. In their
place, the ground grew firmer. I didn't yet know what that change meant.
Only, I could feel that I was drawing closer to something different.

Until that moment I had believed no one else was in this valley besides
me. It didn't take long to learn how wrong that belief was.

(Continued in Chapter 30.)

Chapter 30. The One Already There

_Previously_ — Far off, deeper in the valley, I saw something moving.
It was too far to make out what it was. I started walking that way.
Until that moment I had believed no one else was in this valley besides
me.

The closer I got, the more that moving shape was a person.

I was sure of it by the time we were about a hundred steps apart. I
recognized it not by the walk itself but by the rhythm of it. An animal
doesn't walk that way. Only a person walks like that — stopping, then
going again, lowering the body, then standing back up.

I started counting my steps. Ninety-nine, a hundred. Counting helped
press the fear down. Eighty, seventy. The number shrank as the distance
shrank.

He didn't seem to have seen me. Or — he'd seen me, but it didn't seem to
concern him. He walked slowly among the scattered things. Every so often
he stopped and looked down at something. Then he walked on again.

I understood then that his was not the walk of someone searching. A
person searching looks around. He didn't look around. He walked the way
someone walks who already knows exactly what is where.

I tried to count his steps. Fifty, sixty. But at some point I lost the
count. The timing of when he stopped wasn't regular. In some spots he
stood a long time; some he simply passed by. I tried to start counting
again, and gave it up.

That was the first time in my life I gave up counting something. It
wasn't that I couldn't count it. I felt, in my body, that counting simply
didn't fit this person. In the city, everything could be counted. The
number of visits. The benches in the square. The number of times my
mother said the same sentence. This man's footsteps were unlike any of
those.

Looking down, I saw my own footprints falling in a row alongside his.
Mine were clear and distinct. His pressed in and then blurred almost at
once. I couldn't tell whether that was the wind, or simply the way he
set his feet down. I'd learned the day before that this valley didn't
hold even a new trace for long, but his footprints seemed to vanish
faster than that, faster than mine.

About thirty steps off, I stopped walking.

I wasn't sure it was right to go any closer. The valley had none of the
city's rules. No one here told you who anyone was, or what you were
allowed to do.

He was the one who stopped first.

He didn't turn all the way around. Only his head tilted slightly this
way. In the dimming light, I couldn't make out his face well. I couldn't
guess his age. He seemed old, and at the same time not so old.

His clothes weren't the city's clothes. Nor were they like the outside
clothes Speon wore. The color was almost indistinguishable from the
soil. I couldn't tell whether that was from long wear, or whether that
had always been their color.

"…Hello," I said first.

He said nothing back. Only he turned his head a little further this way.
I could tell he was looking at me, but there was no surprise in that
look.

I would go back to this many times afterward. That he wasn't surprised,
that day. A person had appeared in a valley no one ever comes to, and he
showed no surprise at all. As if he'd already known, even if not today,
that someone would come sometime.

I took a few more steps closer.

The sound of dry things crumbling came from underfoot. That sound rang
unusually loud to me. It was probably because of the different kind of
breathing I was hearing for the first time since I'd come to this
valley. Not my own breath. Someone else's breath.

"…Were you already here?" I asked.

"…Yes."

His voice was low. Not angry, not glad to see me. Just a voice giving an
answer.

"…I'm Anemos."

At that he nodded once. He didn't give his own name in return. I thought
that was strange, but I didn't ask again. I seemed to have learned
somewhere, without knowing where, that this was the courteous thing to
do.

In this record I keep calling this person "he." I can't explain exactly
why. Only, I felt for the first time that day that there are people it
isn't right to give a name to.

He started walking again. It wasn't a walk meant to shake me off. It was
simply the walk of someone continuing what he'd already been doing.

I followed a few steps behind.

Every place he stopped seemed to have a reason. In some spots he
lowered himself and looked a long time at something on the ground. In
others he reached out and touched a dry branch, very lightly. He didn't
break it. It looked like a gesture only meant to confirm that it was
still there.

"…What are you doing?" I asked.

"…Walking."

"…Just that?"

"…Yes."

That answer was so simple it made me want to ask more, rather than less.

"…Do you walk like this every day?"

"…Nearly."

"…Why?"

He stopped walking and looked at me. The first time our eyes properly
met.

"…Why would I need a reason not to?"

I had no answer to that. He was right. Walking didn't need a reason. In
the city it would have needed one. Here, apparently, it didn't.

I thought, at the time, that this man resembled Speon. A man who kept
going without needing a reason. I learned later that this wasn't quite
right. Speon was making something. A well, a channel for water. This man
made nothing. He only walked. I didn't understand then how large that
difference was.

I walked alongside him. He didn't stop me from coming up beside him. Nor
did he welcome it. He simply let it be.

We walked a while without speaking. It struck me as strange that this
silence didn't feel uncomfortable. Walking quietly like this beside
someone I'd only just met wasn't a common thing, even outside.

He stopped again in one spot. In front of a small heap of bones. I
couldn't remember whether I'd passed this spot before. Inside the
valley, every heap looked alike, and then looked entirely different from
every other.

He crouched down in front of it. He reached out and touched the soil,
lightly. He didn't dig into it. It was only a gesture of stroking the
surface.

"…Someone you knew?" I asked.

"…No."

"…Then why touch it?"

He drew his hand back and stood up.

"…Does touching have to be the same as knowing?"

Again I had no answer to that. This was a man who answered a question
with a question. I couldn't tell whether that was evasion, or whether he
was genuinely asking out of curiosity.

The sun went fully down.

Stars began coming out above the valley wall. The same stars I'd seen
from the city's edge. That familiarity was, again, a small comfort.

"…How long have you been here?" I asked again.

This time I asked it a little differently. The first time it had been
"were you already here." This time it was "how long." I felt that the
difference mattered.

He said nothing for a moment.

It wasn't an angry expression. I studied his face closely. There really
was no emotion in it at all. No irritation, no displeasure, and no trace
of amusement either. Just empty — but not an emptiness that was cold.
An emptiness that was quiet.

"…I don't know," he said.

"…You don't know?"

"…I stopped counting a long time ago."

I stopped short at that answer. It was something I'd heard somewhere
before. The Curator had said something like it. That it had been a long
time since he'd stopped counting. At the time I'd thought it was a way
of avoiding grief.

This time something was different. This man didn't seem to not count
because he was avoiding grief. He seemed not to count simply because
there was no need to.

"…Longer than seven years?" I asked. I thought if I gave him a specific
number, he might answer.

"…Maybe."

"…Ten years?"

"…I don't know."

I realized then that this conversation was strange. In the city, when
you asked with a number, people answered with a number. Exactly. This
man, asked with a number, didn't answer with a number. And yet he wasn't
lying, either. He really seemed not to know.

"…Then what can you tell me?" I asked.

For the first time he tilted his head slightly. It wasn't a gesture of
amusement. It was only the gesture of someone hearing the question
again.

"…I was here first," he said. "Is that enough?"

"…First?"

"…Before you came."

I thought that wasn't really an answer. And yet, strangely, I didn't
want to press further. I felt, in my body, before I thought it, that he
wouldn't say anything more than that.

I return to that moment often, afterward. He wasn't angry. He turned the
question aside, and there wasn't a trace of irritation in it. He didn't
laugh either. I'd seen plenty of people who deflect while laughing. This
man didn't run away into laughter either. He simply turned it aside, as
it was. I felt that was a far more honest way to do it. It's a strange
thing to say — honest, while turning something aside — but that is
exactly how it felt to me.

I undid my flask.

"…Would you like some water?" I asked.

He looked at me a moment. Then he shook his head.

"…You don't need any?" I asked again.

"…I'm fine."

It was a phrase I was used to hearing in this city. I don't need it. I'm
fine. But the way this man said it was somehow different from the way
the city's people said it. In the city, people's "I'm fine" meant they
really were filled up, and so they were fine. This man's "I'm fine" — I
didn't know how to put it. It wasn't that he was full and so fine. It
was more that he was a person who simply had very little need at all.

I drank a mouthful of water by myself. My throat was dry.

"…And food?" I asked. "You don't eat?"

"…Sometimes."

"…Only sometimes?"

"…Yes."

I noticed that he didn't look hungry. His body was thin, but it didn't
look weak. I thought that was strange, but this whole valley was
strange, so I let it pass as one more strangeness among the rest.

Night settled fully in.

I thought briefly about whether I should find the way back to the city.
But my body pushed that thought aside. Climbing a dark slope was
dangerous. And more than that, I still wanted to be here a while longer.
I didn't know the reason for that, even then.

"…Can I stay here tonight?" I asked.

He looked at me. Neither permission nor refusal in the look.

"…That's for you to decide."

"…Isn't it dangerous?"

"…What is?"

"…Sleeping here."

"…I sleep every day."

That surprised me a little. It was the first time I learned that this
man slept here.

"…Where?"

He pointed a hand toward the distance. I looked that way. It was too
dark to see anything.

"…There's a hollow over there."

I thought of the shallow hollow I'd seen in Chapter 29 — the one too
dark inside to make anything out, the one I hadn't gone into. Knowing now
that he slept there, I was glad I hadn't looked inside it. I would have
felt as if I'd peered into someone's room without asking.

"…Aren't you cold, there?" I asked.

"…I'm used to it."

"…Is being used to it the same as not being cold?"

Only after I'd said it did I realize it was something my mother had once
said to me. When my brother had been afraid of the bucket's sound at
the well, my mother had asked me: is being used to it the same as not
being afraid?

At that question he looked at me a little longer than before, for the
first time.

"…No," he said. "It isn't the same."

That answer surprised me. In the city no one would have answered that
way. Everyone said that once you got used to something, it was fine. This
man kept the two things apart.

I held onto that short answer for a long time afterward. This man never
got angry and never laughed, and yet somewhere in him he was exact,
precisely exact. I felt, dimly, even then, that this exactness was
different from the city's exactness. The city's exactness came from
knowing everything. This man's exactness seemed to come from having
lived through something, for a long time.

I settled myself down on the bare ground. He sat a few steps off. Not
side by side, but not far either.

"…Will you walk like this again tomorrow?" I asked.

"…Yes."

"…Can I come with you?"

This time he looked at me a little longer.

"…If you want to."

I liked that answer. It neither stopped me nor led me on.

More stars came out. I tried to count them, then gave it up. It wasn't a
number that could be counted.

"…Won't you tell me your name?" I asked, last.

He was silent for a moment. There was no anger in that silence either.

"…Why would I need a name?"

I had no answer to that. He closed his eyes after that. I couldn't tell
whether he was sleeping or simply had his eyes shut.

That night, before I fell asleep, I had a strange thought. This man was
unlike anyone I'd ever met. The city's people gave short answers because
they wanted nothing. This man gave short answers for some other reason.
I didn't know what it was, then. The next day, he would ask me a question
first. Standing in front of that question, I would come to see part of
that reason.

The wind flowed low along the valley wall. Dry things rustled. I closed
my eyes listening to that sound.

I woke once in the night. I didn't know how long I'd slept. Opening my
eyes, I found he was still sitting in the same spot. His eyes were
closed. He might have been asleep; he might only have been sitting
there.

I looked at his back for a moment. The angle of his shoulders, the way
he sat. There wasn't a single tremor in it. I wondered whether a living
person could stay that unmoving for that long.

Every time I recall that night since, it's that stillness that comes
back first. I had lived believing that every living person trembles a
little. Everyone outside was like that. Breathing shakes you; cold shakes
you. That night, for the first time, I fell asleep beside someone who
did not tremble at all.

I closed my eyes again. When I next opened them, it was morning.

(Continued in Chapter 31.)

Chapter 31. Can These Bones Live

_Previously_ — I met him in the valley. When I asked how long he'd been
here, all he said was, "I was here first." Not angry, not laughing
either. I spent that night beside him. I fell asleep watching a back
that never trembled.

When I opened my eyes in the morning, he was already up.

He stood not far off, looking deeper into the valley. I watched his back
a moment, then pulled myself up. My back was stiff from a night lying on
bare ground.

"…Sleep all right?" he asked, without turning around.

"…Yes."

"…Not too cold?"

"…A little."

He nodded at that. It wasn't a worried gesture. Just a confirming one.

The morning light lit the valley differently. Not the red of the evening
before, not the dark of night. A white, cold light. Under it, the
scattered things showed more clearly than before.

I remember that morning light for a long time afterward. It was a light
that hid nothing. Evening light made everything beautiful. Morning light
simply showed things as they were.

We walked together.

Much the same walk as the day before. He went ahead, and I followed a
few steps behind. He stopped now and then to look at something. I
stopped with him each time.

As we walked I asked him a few more things. Mostly he answered short. To
some questions he gave no answer at all, and simply kept walking. I was
coming to understand, more and more, that silence too was a kind of
answer.

"…How many people are in this valley?" I asked. "Traces like this one."

"…That's not a thing you can count."

"…Why not?"

"…The traces overlap. You can't tell where one ends and another begins,
or whether it's the same person."

Hearing that, I thought of the spots I'd seen in Chapter 29 — where I'd
counted up to five, ten, seventeen, and then stopped. I'd already learned
there was a point past which counting couldn't go. This man seemed to
have known that from the start.

"…Aren't you curious?" I asked. "Who all these people were, who came
here."

"…I am."

"…Then why don't you look into it?"

"…Searching and walking are different things."

I kept walking without understanding that answer.

We walked a long while, and came to a wide, open space. Wider than the
one I'd seen the day before. A place where bones had gathered in
unusual numbers. In some spots they still held a whole shape; in others
they were scattered.

He stopped walking there. He stayed stopped a long time.

I stood beside him and looked at that landscape with him. I didn't speak.
It felt like a place where speaking wasn't allowed.

I still can't describe that silence exactly, even now. It wasn't a sad
silence, and it wasn't a frightening one. It was just a very large
silence. Standing in front of that size, I felt very small.

He lowered himself and sat on the earth. I sat down beside him.

The wind blew once. Dust rose low and settled again. Inside that dust,
small things rose together and came down again. Threads. Thin fragments
of bone.

He watched that scene for a long time. I watched along with him.

After a long while, he spoke.

"…Anemos."

It was the first time he'd said my name. It landed on me, strangely,
with weight.

"…Yes."

He didn't go on right away. He took up a handful of soil, then let it
run out through his fingers. The dry powder fell slowly.

"…Do you think," he said, "these can live again?"

I understood the question. Every single word in it was plain. And yet it
took time to take in what it meant.

These. I looked at the scattered things in front of us. Bones, rotted
scraps of cloth, crumbled shoes. Things that had once been a person.

Live again. That phrase rang strangely inside me. Living again, from a
state opposite to being alive. I felt there was something more inside
that question. A feeling that this wasn't only about this valley.

"…I don't know," I said.

It didn't take me long to say the words. But saying those three, I felt
strangely ashamed. As if I should have offered something more
convincing.

"…What do you mean, you don't know?" he asked. It wasn't a pressing
tone.

"…I really don't know," I said. "If I say yes it feels like a lie, and
if I say no it feels like a lie too."

He said nothing to that answer. Only he glanced at me for a moment.
There was something in that look, and at first I took it for
disappointment.

I would learn later it wasn't disappointment. I couldn't tell the
difference then.

"…Why not say no?" he asked. "That's just bone. Look at it. It's dry."

At that I looked again at the scattered things. Truly dry. They looked
very far from anything living.

"…Even so, I can't bring myself to say no," I said.

"…Why not?"

I tried to find an answer to that question. I looked for it and didn't
find it.

"…I don't know. I just can't."

He traced something in the dirt with a fingertip. A meaningless line.
Then he wiped it away.

"…Then why can't you say yes?" he asked again.

I couldn't answer that one either, right away.

"…I've never seen anything like this," I said. "Outside, when a person
died, they were just dead. I've never seen anyone live again."

"…What about the city, then? There are people there who live again,
aren't there. Release, I think it's called."

I was startled by that. It was strange that he even knew the word
release.

"…That seems like something different from living again," I said. "It's
turning a preserved person back — not bringing a dead person back."

"…Why is that different?"

I stopped a long while at that question. The preserved really do not
disappear. They exist in a state that has not died. But the things in
this valley had disappeared. Only traces remained. I'd thought that
difference was very large, and yet when I tried to explain it, the words
wouldn't come.

"…I don't know," I said again. "I thought it was different, but now I'm
not sure."

Writing this passage, I meet myself again as I was that day. I kept
saying, over and over, that I didn't know. I was ashamed. Since coming
into the city I had always been the one learning something, never the
one giving the answer. But in front of this man, even what I'd learned
was no use. None of it could answer that question.

He asked again. This time the same question in slightly different
words.

"…What I mean is — do you think that becomes a person again," he said,
pointing his hand at the scattered things. "Breathing, walking. So that
if you called its name, it would turn to look."

I stopped again in front of that specific picture. Breathing, walking, so
that calling a name makes it turn. I laid that picture over my mother
and my brother. My mother didn't breathe. More precisely, she had no
need to breathe. My brother was the same. And yet both of them turned to
look, if you called their name.

The things in this valley didn't turn, however you called. I knew that
difference. And still no answer came.

"…I don't know," I said, a third time.

For the first time he looked at me a little longer.

I didn't look away. There was no reason to. I truly didn't know, and I
didn't want to hide that.

"…That's all right," he said.

That surprised me.

"…What is?"

"…Not knowing."

It took me time to understand that.

"…Shouldn't I give the right answer?" I asked.

"…What's the right answer?"

I couldn't answer that question either. Yes didn't feel like the right
answer, and no didn't feel like it either. Neither one was it, but then I
didn't know what was.

"…Saying I don't know," he said, "is sometimes the right answer."

"…Does that really count as an answer?" I asked.

"…It does."

"…How? You haven't said anything at all."

He picked up a small stone from the ground. Held it a moment in his
hand, then set it back down.

"…Not knowing isn't the same as saying nothing," he said. "It's not
lying that you know."

I turned that over for a long time.

"…I wanted to say yes," I said, honestly. "It felt like I should. I want
those people to be alive again."

"…Then why didn't you say yes?"

"…Because it would have been a lie. I don't actually believe that's
going to happen."

In that moment I felt I'd been honest with myself for the first time.
What you wish for and what you know are different things. I wished. But
I didn't know. That day was the first time I'd said those two without
mixing them together.

In that moment I thought of that night seven years before. The adults
arguing behind the door. One side had said, "Stopping isn't the same as
living." The other had asked back, "And is dying living, then?" At nine
years old I'd only half understood those two sentences.

Standing in front of this valley, I felt for the first time why both of
them were right. Stopping and living are different — that's true. The
city had shown me that. But dying isn't living either. These scattered
things had shown me that. Both sentences were true, and neither one was
the answer I wanted.

"…I've been carrying those two sentences around for seven years," I
said. I told him briefly about that night.

He only listened, all the way through. Even after hearing it all, he
didn't say which side was right.

"…Both are right," he said. "That's why they don't stand together."

"…They can't stand together?"

"…No. But that's not the problem. The problem is that you have to keep
living between the two of them."

I turned that over for a long time.

"…You could have said no," I said. "That would've been easier. It's
just bone, after all."

"…Then why didn't you?"

"…That felt like a lie too."

As I gave that answer, I tried to find the reason I felt that way. I
found it.

"…I've seen water running toward this valley," I said. "It hasn't
reached here yet. Speon is digging the channel. Even before the water
arrives, I already felt like something here was alive. I don't know why
that is. But I don't want to call that feeling a lie either."

Hearing that, his eyes widened slightly, for the first time. The
closest thing to a surprised expression I'd seen on him.

"…That," he said. "That's what not knowing is."

I didn't fully understand what he meant. But I could feel it was
something important.

"…Will these bones live again, or won't they?" I finally asked again.
This time it was my turn to put the question to him.

He shook his head.

"…I don't know either."

That surprised me.

"…You've been here longer than I have."

"…Is being here long the same as knowing?"

I nearly laughed at that question. I didn't laugh. It wasn't a laugh
that belonged in that place.

"…I just walk," he said. "Beside these. I don't pretend to know."

I felt, then, that I understood a little of why this man was here. I
still can't explain it. Only, he wasn't someone who held the answer to
this valley. He was someone who kept carrying the question of this
valley, on and on.

We sat in that spot a long while longer.

The wind blew a few more times. Dust rose and settled. I watched that
repeat. I didn't count it.

"…Are you disappointed?" I asked. "That I couldn't give an answer."

"…No."

"…Why not?"

"…Not many people have given the answer, I don't know."

That surprised me a little.

"…What did other people say?"

"…Yes, or no."

"…Which one more?"

"…No, more. Then yes."

That order struck me, strangely, as sad.

"…And I don't know?"

He looked at me a moment.

"…You're the first."

I couldn't answer at all, hearing that.

I keep this sentence among the ones I hold onto longest in this whole
book. You're the first. I still don't know, even now, whether that was
praise or just a fact. Only, from that day on, I came to know that saying
you don't know something isn't a shameful thing. An honest not-knowing
is better than a hastily built knowing.

He stood up. He started walking again.

I sat there a little longer, then stood and followed.

"…Do you ask other people this question too?" I asked.

"…Probably."

"…Why did you ask me?"

He answered without stopping his walk.

"…Because you brought water with you."

I didn't know what that meant. Whether he meant the flask, or something
else. He explained no further.

I turned that over for a long time. I still haven't fully worked it
out, even now. Only, later in this book, I would come to a place that
brought that line back to me again.

We walked together the rest of that afternoon. We didn't return to that
question again. Both of us seemed to know there was no need to.

As we walked, I asked myself that question again, silently, more than
once. Do you think these can live again. The answer still didn't come.
But the not-coming was less uncomfortable now than it had been at first.
I couldn't explain why, that day.

I can explain a little now. Even not-knowing becomes bearable once it
finds its place. That afternoon, for the first time, I was able to walk
inside a state of not-knowing — without rushing to find an answer.

When the sun rose high, he stopped walking and looked at me.

"…Going back to the city?" he asked.

I couldn't answer right away. Wanting to stay here longer and knowing I
had to go up were both in me at once.

"…Yes," I said finally. "I think I should go."

"…All right."

He didn't try to hold me back. He didn't try to stop me either.

"…Can I come down again?" I asked.

"…If you want to."

I nodded at that answer. Then I turned and started toward the slope I'd
come down. A few steps on, I looked back. He was already walking again.
Among the bones, without a tremor.

(Continued in Chapter 32.)

Chapter 32. Back Up

_Previously_ — He asked me: "…Do you think these can live again?" I
couldn't answer. He didn't fault me for that. "You're the first." By
afternoon I decided to go back to the city.

The road up felt longer than the road down had been.

The same slope, the same rocks. And yet my feet kept feeling heavy. I
couldn't tell whether that was my body's exhaustion, or something else.

I used the rope a few times. The knots came together in one try now. My
hands seemed to have fully relearned that work. I smiled a little,
watching them. The twelve days of shoveling hadn't gone to waste.

Climbing that slope, I kept turning that question over the whole way.
Do you think these can live again. The question was under my feet the
entire climb. Every time I set a foot down, it asked itself again. I
decided not to look for an answer. As he had told me to, the night
before.

Around the six-hundredth step, I looked back.

The valley lay down there below. Wide, quiet, dry exactly as it had been
the day before. I tried to find him in that view. I couldn't. Either he
was too far off, or he'd gone behind some fold of ground.

I felt a strange disappointment then. It hadn't even been a full day,
and already it felt natural to me that he would keep being there. I
would go through that disappointment again many times, later. I'll write
more about it in Chapter 47.

I turned and kept walking.

By the time I reached the city's edge, the sun had passed a little
beyond its height.

I caught my breath. I brushed off the dust. There was white powder from
the valley on my hands and my clothes. I didn't brush all of it off. For
some reason, I didn't want to.

The city spread out before my eyes.

The buildings, the square, the distribution station, the Curator's
building far off. All exactly as it had been. I confirmed that with my
own eyes. Nothing had changed. Not the roof colors, not the width of the
streets, not the way people walked.

Even the light was different. The valley's light had come filtered
through white powder; whatever it fell on lost a shade of its color. The
city's light was yellow and clear. I stood under that light and looked at
my own hand. The white powder caught in the back of it stood out
strangely. Even the air was different. The valley's air had been heavy
and dry; here it was light, and smelled newly washed.

And yet it looked different.

I tried, for a long time, to rewrite this sentence. I wanted to explain
exactly how it looked different. After rewriting it many times, I finally
accepted that this sentence was the most accurate one there was. Nothing
had changed, and yet it looked different. Beyond that, I have no
explanation.

I stood there and tried to count the city. How many roofs there were, how
many alleys. I'd done this several times before. The numbers had always
come out the same. I counted again, that day. The roofs came out the
same number as ever. So did the alleys, and the square.

Only after confirming that not one of those numbers had changed did I
understand why I'd felt uneasy. I think I had secretly hoped something
would have changed. Seeing that nothing had, I felt, if anything, even
more like a stranger here.

At first I thought it might be the light.

I wondered whether my eyes, having grown used to the valley's white
light, were simply taking in the city's familiar light differently. But
as I walked I learned that wasn't it.

I passed the distribution station. The attendant recognized me and
nodded. The same greeting as always. And yet, receiving it, I felt it as
something strange. This man had greeted me exactly the same way
yesterday, and the day before that. I noticed it, freshly, as if for the
first time.

I passed the square. There were the benches. A few people sitting on
them. I looked at them. They all sat quietly. No one running, no one
hurrying.

I must have seen that scene dozens of times. It had been over a month
since I'd come to this city. And yet that day the scene looked strange
to me, as if I were seeing it for the first time. To eyes that had just
come from the valley, this quiet read differently.

One person was standing in front of a flowerbed. A small flowerbed. One
of the few in this city — planted not with flowers, but simply with dry
grass. That person was holding a watering can. Giving it exactly the
regulation amount of water. I watched that motion for a long time.

That flowerbed wasn't receiving water because it needed it. It was
receiving it because that was the set procedure. I noticed that
difference for the first time that day. There was no one watering
anything in the valley. No one had set a schedule for it there. And yet
I'd felt something alive there. This flowerbed, receiving its exact water
every single day, gave off no such feeling at all.

Passing in front of the Curator's building, the clerk at the front desk
recognized me and gave a light greeting. Receiving it, I thought of my
mother and my brother. At this very moment, the two of them were still
in that room, in the same posture as yesterday. My steps slowed a little
at that thought. Then I kept walking. I didn't want to go that way,
today.

I walked along the channel.

Speon's channel. It was running all the way to the city's edge now. I
walked listening to it. The sound of water was exactly the same as
yesterday. And yet even that sound was heard differently.

I had a thought for the first time then. It wasn't the sound that had
changed. It was my ears. I had heard true silence, in the valley. A
silence with no sound in it at all. Coming back to the city, even this
small sound of water rang loud.

Walking, I heard, freshly, sounds I'd never before picked out one by one.
The click of the distribution station's scale. The sound of someone
opening and closing a door. Footsteps far off, all of them small and
careful. It had been over a month since I'd come to this city, and even
having lived that long among these sounds, I had never once listened to
them separately, one by one.

I went to the pit. Speon was there, smoothing the soil.

"…Back," he said, setting his shovel upright. It wasn't a worried face.
But there was something in the eyes that looked at me.

"…Yes."

"…All right?"

I couldn't answer that right away.

"…I don't know," I said. "Whether I'm all right or not."

Speon nodded at that answer. He didn't press further.

"…What was down there?" he asked.

I didn't know how to answer that question. Bones, traces, and a person. I
didn't know which of those to say first.

"…I met someone," I said.

Speon stopped shoveling.

"…Someone?"

"…Yes. Inside the valley. Walking among the bones."

Speon looked at me for a long time. It was a surprised face, though not
enormously surprised.

"…What kind of person?"

"…I don't know. He didn't tell me his name."

"…What did he say?"

I stopped for a moment at that question. I wasn't sure I could carry that
question over to Speon — do you think these can live again.

"…He asked me one question," I said. "I still haven't answered it."

Speon didn't press further at that. He just nodded.

"…There are questions you don't answer," he said. "Not everything has
to have an answer."

Strangely, that was a comfort to me.

On the way back to the dormitory, I stopped in front of the tub of water
they kept for washing. A large tub. The water lay still inside it.

I looked at my own face reflected on it.

My face had tanned a little over the past few days. Dried dust still lay
in a thin film on my cheeks. My eyes looked a little more sunken than
before. I looked at that face for a long time.

It was then I understood, exactly, for the first time, that the city
hadn't changed at all. The city hadn't changed one bit. What had changed
was me. A face that had spent a day in the valley simply looked
different to eyes that had come back to the city. But knowing that didn't
make the strangeness go away.

I scooped water in my hand and washed my face. The dust washed down. The
water clouded and then cleared again. I looked into that tub a long
while before I moved on.

I walked the city the whole afternoon.

No particular purpose. I just wanted to walk. As I walked I kept
comparing things. The valley's soil against the city's streets. The
valley's silence against the city's small sounds. The valley's scattered
things against the city's ordered people.

I knew that comparison wasn't fair. One was a land of failures, and the
other was a city of successes. And yet I couldn't stop making it.

Passing in front of the Curator's building, I stopped walking a moment. I
thought of my mother and my brother. I thought about going to visit.
But my feet wouldn't move that way.

I didn't know the reason then. I learned it later. I didn't want to see
my mother while I still hadn't found an answer to that question. The
question and my mother had, somewhere along the way, become bound
together as one.

I saw the Curator climbing the steps to his building. He didn't seem to
notice me. He was climbing with a folder tucked under his arm. His pace
was slower than usual. I watched his back for a moment.

I saw something in that back, though I couldn't put a name to it then.
A weight different from mere tiredness. I would only come to know what it
was in Chapter 34. Writing this passage now, I find myself wanting to
count how many times a day he climbs and descends those six steps. I've
never counted it.

That evening I met Eikon.

By chance. She was in the spot where she always walked. The moment she
saw me, her expression changed slightly.

"…You're back," she said. "You're still you?"

I laughed a little at that question. I remembered what she'd said before
I went down to the valley. Don't come back a different person.

"…I don't know," I said, honestly. "I'm not sure I know myself well
either."

At that answer, Eikon's face took on a slightly strange look.

"…Isn't that the same as becoming a different person?"

I couldn't answer that right away.

"…What was down there?" she asked.

"…Bones. Traces. And there was a person."

"…A person? In that valley?"

"…Yes."

Eikon's face stiffened slightly.

"…There can't be a person there."

I said nothing to that. I wanted to ask why she was so certain, but I
didn't.

"…What did that person say?" Eikon asked.

I hesitated a moment. I wasn't sure I could carry that question over to
her either.

"…He asked me — do you think these can live again."

Eikon's face showed nothing at all, hearing that. After a long while, she
said this.

"…That's a strange question."

"…Why?"

"…The answer's obvious. Bones don't live."

I wanted to say something to that, but the words wouldn't come. I
couldn't call her wrong. But I didn't want to say she was right either.

"…Is that what you think?" I asked.

"…Of course. It's right that they don't live."

I envied that certainty. At the same time, it frightened me a little.

"…Why couldn't you answer, Anemos?" Eikon asked. It wasn't a mocking
tone.

"…I don't know either. I didn't want to say yes, and I didn't want to
say no."

"…Why not?"

I thought about that question for a long time.

"…If I say no, it feels like admitting those people are really over.
I couldn't do that."

"…And if you say yes?"

"…That feels like a lie. Not something I said because I really believe
it — just because I wish it were so."

Eikon looked at me steadily for a moment, hearing that.

"…That's strange," she said. "I've never once had to think about that."

"…Why not?"

"…Because for me there's only one answer. There's nothing to think
about."

Listening to that, I felt that in her world there was no place at all
for not knowing. I felt both envy and sadness at that. I couldn't gauge,
then, which was larger.

I kept turning that conversation over on the way back. Eikon had said no
without a moment's hesitation. I couldn't. I would come to understand a
little more where that difference came from, in the next chapter.

That night, I lay in the dormitory looking up at the city's familiar
ceiling.

The night before, there had been the valley's stars. Tonight it was just
a ceiling. I turned that difference over for a long time.

That was the first night I didn't feel this city was holding me
prisoner. Instead I felt this city was putting something off. I couldn't
put into words, then, what it was putting off. I can now. This city was a
place that deferred the question. The valley was a place that kept
asking it.

I looked at my palms. I could feel the thin callus that had settled over
the old blister marks. I started to count how many times that hand had
touched the city's walls that day, and gave it up. The days I gave up
counting were growing more frequent.

I lay down with my head on the flask. Water sat half-full inside it. I
thought about where that water had come from. The distribution station.
The valley's water hadn't reached that far yet.

I didn't know, then, that this water would one day run all the way to
that valley. If I had known, I wouldn't have slept as easily as I did
that night.

Before falling asleep, I brought that question to mind one last time. Do
you think these can live again. This time too, I made no answer to it.
Instead I simply set the question down beside me and closed my eyes.

I dreamed that night. A dream in which the valley and the city ran
together. My mother was walking across the white soil, and my brother was
holding the water flask. I didn't remember all of the dream when I woke.
Only one scene stayed with me clearly. My mother turned to look at me and
said something, but no sound came.

(Continued in Chapter 33.)

Chapter 33. Eikon's Logic

_Previously_ — Back up from the valley, the city looked different
though nothing about it had changed. I carried the question over to
Eikon. "Do you think these can live again." She'd said no without a
moment's hesitation.

The next morning, I went to find Eikon again.

It wasn't that there was anything left unsaid from the day before. To be
exact, I didn't know myself why I'd gone looking for her. Her certainty
just kept sitting on my mind. That face saying no, without hesitation.

She was on the bench she always sat on. She saw me and nodded slightly.
No surprise. In keeping with this city's people.

"…Back again," she said.

"…Yes."

"…Because of what we talked about yesterday?"

I was a little surprised she'd guessed that.

"…Yes. That's right."

On the way there I'd passed Speon's pit. The sound of running water came
from it. Listening to it, I'd thought — everyone always said it was a
good thing when water appeared where there had been none. But that
reasoning didn't work on Eikon. For her, everything was already there.

I sat down beside her. I set the flask on my knees. My hand fiddled with
the cap.

"…There's something I wanted to ask yesterday, and didn't," I said.

"…What is it?"

"…Is there really nothing you wish for?"

Eikon's face took on a thinking look at that question. Not an angry
expression. A face that was really thinking it through.

"…What do you mean, wish for?" she asked back.

"…Wanting something more, because something's lacking."

"…I don't lack anything."

I'd heard that answer from her many times before. But that day it sounded
a little different. I wanted to ask her more.

"…Really?" I asked. "Not one thing?"

Eikon looked straight at me.

"…Yes. Really."

"…For example?" I asked. "Something you'd like to eat, something you'd
like to wear — nothing at all?"

"…There's nothing I'd like to eat," she said. "What the distribution
station gives is enough. I've never wished for anything more
delicious. It's the same with clothes."

I lost words in front of that answer. Outside, wanting something more
delicious, something warmer to wear, was simply a given. Only then did
it really sink in that here, that given didn't hold at all.

"…Can you explain it to me?" I asked. "Why you don't lack anything."

Eikon was quiet a moment. Not a silence for choosing her words — a
silence more like rethinking the question itself.

"…I've never been hungry," she said. "Never been sick. Never been
lonely. My parents are here, and I'm still me. What more would I need?"

"…But isn't there something you wish for anyway? Anything at all."

"…No," she said. It wasn't a firm tone. Just a tone stating a fact.
"Wishing means wanting something you don't have right now. There's
nothing I don't have right now."

I tried to find something to argue against that. I couldn't find it.

"…Is there nothing you're curious about?" I asked again. "Something new,
something you've never seen."

"…There are things I'm curious about. I'm curious about that person you
say you met, yesterday. But curious and lacking are different things."

"…How are they different?"

"…When you're curious, it ends once you know. When you're lacking,
filling it just makes more come up. From what you've said, that's how it
sounds for you."

Something in me flinched at that. It was an exact hit. Outside, filling
it had always just made more come up. In this city, there was nothing
like that.

"…So you've never once gone through that? Filling something and having
more come up."

"…No. Not since I came here."

"…And before?"

Eikon closed her eyes a moment, then opened them.

"…My memory's hazy. It was seven years ago. But I probably did. Everyone
does."

I turned that over for a long time. It was the first time it occurred to
me that she might once have been like me.

"…So why would I need more?" she asked, first this time.

I stopped at that question.

"…What do you mean?"

"…I know you keep looking at us thinking it's a pity. You try not to
show it on the outside, but it shows anyway."

I felt ashamed at that. I wanted to deny it, but I couldn't.

"…But me," Eikon said. "I don't lack anything. Why would I need more?"

I read that sentence over to myself several times. I don't lack anything.
Why would I need more. It had the shape of a question, but it wasn't
really a question at all. It was the speech of someone who already had
the answer.

I hold onto this sentence longer than any other in this record. Writing
this book, I tried, over and over, to argue against it. I failed every
time.

"…If you had more," I tried to go on, "you'd learn more, and grow more,
and—"

"…More what?" Eikon asked. It wasn't a taunting tone.

I stopped at that question. Grow more. But why should you grow. Why is
growing better than not growing. I tried to find the answer and realized
it had always been so obvious to me that I'd never once had to put it
into words.

"…I don't know," I said, honestly.

Eikon smiled a little at that answer. Not a mocking smile.

"…You say I don't know a lot lately too."

"…I guess I do."

"…I'm not sad," she said again. "I'm not suffering. I'm me. The same as
I was seven years ago. Why is that bad?"

I had no answer to that question either.

"…I didn't say it was bad," I said.

"…But you think it. You look at me and feel sorry for me."

I started to deny it, then stopped. It was true.

"…I'm sorry," I said.

"…There's nothing to be sorry for," Eikon said. "I just think it's
strange. I'm fine, so why does everyone look at me feeling sorry?"

In front of that question, for the first time, I saw this whole city
differently. Maybe it wasn't this city that was to be pitied. Maybe it
was me, looking at this city and finding it pitiable. I tried to push
that thought away for a long time.

"…If you're really all right," I asked, carefully, "why did that person
apply for release, back then? You told me about it before. The one you
called sick."

Eikon's expression shifted slightly, for the first time.

"…That person was sick."

"…Sick with what?"

"…I don't know. She just turned strange. She started wanting things,
over and over. She hadn't been like that before."

"…What did she want?"

"…I don't remember. Anyway, then she applied, and after that she was
gone."

That word — gone — stuck with me.

"…How can you be sure it was a sickness?" I asked.

Eikon looked at me. For the first time something other than certainty
flickered a little in her eyes.

"…I can't be sure," she said. "But there's no other word for it. If
someone who was fine suddenly starts wanting something, what else would
you call it?"

I had no answer to that either.

"…Have you ever been afraid?" I asked. A question that came to me
suddenly. "Seven years ago, before you came here."

Eikon paused a moment at that question.

"…Maybe. I don't remember well."

"…Not even what you were afraid of?"

"…The dark, maybe. Doesn't everyone get afraid of that?"

"…And now?"

"…Now there's no reason to go anywhere dark. I sleep at the set time,
at night."

Something bitter came over me at that answer. It wasn't that the fear
had gone away — it was that there was nothing left to be afraid of at
all. I remembered feeling this same distinction with my mother and my
brother. This city hadn't overcome fear. It had simply removed everything
there was to fear.

In that moment I understood how honest that word "sickness" really was.
Eikon wasn't using it out of malice. In her world, it truly was a word
with no other name to give it. When a lack appears in a world with no
lack in it, there's no word for it but sickness.

"…I don't think it's a sickness," I said, carefully.

"…Then what do you think it is?"

I stopped a long time at that question.

"…I don't know," I said finally. "But I don't think 'sickness' is the
right word."

Eikon showed no particular reaction to that. Not angry, not agreeing
either. Just a face that seemed to set the words aside somewhere inside
her.

"…You know," Eikon said, after a long while, "I don't understand why you
keep trying to shake me."

I was startled by that.

"…I wasn't trying to."

"…You are. By asking so many questions."

I didn't know what to say to that.

"…I'm sorry," I said again.

"…I told you there's nothing to be sorry for," Eikon said. "But know
this much — I'm not shaking. No matter how much you ask."

For the first time, I felt something else behind her certainty. It
sounded almost like a defense. And yet the certainty was still perfectly
clear. It was strange that the two could sit together.

I didn't know, then, how to hold those two things together. I still
don't fully know now. Only, having finished this whole book, I've come to
think this was one of the most honest moments in it.

"…But," Eikon said again, "why do you keep trying to shake me? I don't
shake you."

I thought about that question for a long time.

"…I guess it's because I'm worried about you," I said.

"…There's nothing to worry about, I keep telling you."

"…I'm still worried."

At that, for the first time, Eikon's face softened, just a little.

"…You're a strange person. Worrying about someone with nothing to worry
about."

"…I guess I am."

"…I don't lack anything. Why would I need more?"

Eikon said it once more. This time it wasn't asked of me — it sounded
more like something she was confirming to herself.

I tried again to argue against that sentence. I thought about bringing
up love. That love doesn't need a lack. But before I could say it, I knew
it wouldn't persuade her. She was loved. Her parents were still beside
her. Even love, for her, was already something fully filled.

In that moment, for the first time, I admitted I had no words that
could beat this logic. She wasn't wrong. She wasn't suffering. No
argument had any power in front of her satisfaction. I think this is the
most frightening fact in this whole book. I still think so now.

In that moment I thought of my brother. Preserved at eight, he stood in
the same place as Eikon. An eight-year-old who lacked nothing. I
remembered the answer I'd gotten when I first brought up release to him.
"I'll do it if you do it." I'd withdrawn my own application because I
couldn't accept that as consent. Standing in front of Eikon now, I felt I
understood that reason a little more clearly. My brother hadn't answered
because he had no lack to answer from. It was the same with Eikon. Both
of them weren't refusing to want something — for both of them, the very
motion of wanting had become unnecessary.

I saw that difference clearly for the first time, in that moment.
Refusal and having-no-need are different things. My brother and Eikon
had never refused. They simply had no reason to want. I had confused the
two for a long time.

"…What about longing?" I asked, last. "Missing someone who's gone. Isn't
there any of that either?"

Eikon looked at me for a moment.

"…There's no one for me to miss," she said. "My parents are here. I'm
here. There's no one who's gone, so there's no one to miss."

I was completely out of words at that. For longing to exist at all,
something had to be lost first. In her world, nothing had been lost.

"…Yeah," I said finally. "I think you're right."

Eikon looked a little surprised at that. She didn't seem to have
expected I would concede so easily.

"…And yet something's still left over?" she asked. For the first time
she turned the question back on me.

"…Yeah," I said. "Something's left."

"…What is it?"

"…I don't know. I'd feel better if I knew what it was, too."

The sun was going down. Eikon stood up.

"…I hope you find that answer," she said. An unexpected thing to say.

"…Why?"

"…So you'll stop shaking me."

I laughed at that. She smiled, just barely, too.

"…I'll come again," I said.

"…All right. But I'm not going to change."

She walked off. I watched her back and turned that sentence over again.
I'm not going to change. I understood that it wasn't a threat, and it
wasn't a warning either. It was just her stating a fact.

I stayed sitting there a long while after. I could feel the bench
cooling. I watched the people passing by. All of them wore the same face
as Eikon. A face that lacked nothing. Those faces looked a little
different to me now. Before, I'd thought of them as empty faces. That
day I understood they weren't. Not empty — full. Only what filled them
held none of the things I knew.

Since I'd come to this city, I'd kept looking at these people with
pity. That day, for the first time, I thought that the pity might be a
lack on my side. Was it not because something was missing in me that I
looked at them and found them pitiable? I held onto that question a long
time, and set it down again without an answer.

That night, I tried to write Eikon's logic down on paper. I lack
nothing. Therefore I have no reason to want more. There was no flaw
anywhere between those two sentences. I sat a long time in front of that
perfection.

(Continued in Chapter 34.)

Chapter 34. The Curator's Sorrow

_Previously_ — Eikon stated her logic plainly. "I don't lack anything.
Why would I need more?" I couldn't argue against it. She said she
wasn't shaking. I felt something else behind that certainty.

The next day I went to see the Curator.

I had no particular business. It wasn't a visit application either. I
simply couldn't stop thinking about that back I'd seen climbing the
steps the day before.

The clerk at the front desk looked a little puzzled to see me.

"…Not a visit today?"

"…No. I just came to see him."

The clerk went off to ask inside. I sat on the long bench and waited.
I didn't count the chairs, always twelve of them. I felt no need to
count.

The Curator's room was exactly as it had been.

One desk, two chairs. The walls still bare. The stack of papers looked
a little taller than before.

Every time I walked into that room, I felt anew how completely
unchanged it was. The first time, the second time, today. I used to
think this was simply his taste. Now I know better. It wasn't that he
had no reason to change it — it was that he had no time to. The stack of
papers in that room had never once gotten smaller.

The varnish had worn off the corner of the desk, at the spot where he
rested his hand. One armrest of his chair shone unusually smooth on one
side only — the side, it seemed, he leaned on when he signed. Looking
at those spots, I felt, for the first time, as though I had actually
seen with my own eyes how long this man had sat at this desk.

The Curator saw me and rose slightly from his seat.

"…Anemos. It's been a while."

"…Yes."

"…I heard you went down to the valley."

I was startled at that.

"…How did you know?"

"…There's a record that you filled your flask twice at the distribution
station." The Curator smiled slightly. "This city keeps a record of
even that."

I felt that smile was a little different from his usual one. I didn't
know then what was different about it.

"…How was it?" the Curator asked.

I couldn't answer that right away. I didn't know where to begin.

"…There were a lot of bones," I said. "And I met someone."

At that the Curator touched the desk with his fingertips. A very brief
motion.

"…Someone?"

"…Yes. He was walking there."

The Curator said nothing for a moment. I felt that silence resembled
that gap I'd seen in him before — that instant, while explaining a
procedure, when his eyes had drifted somewhere else, very briefly.

"…Did you know?" I asked. "That there's a person down there."

The Curator nodded. Then he spoke very slowly.

"…I knew. I've known for a long time."

"…Who is he?"

"…I don't know either," the Curator said. "I've never asked him his
name."

"…Why not?"

"…I thought he wouldn't answer, even if I asked."

I laughed at that. It matched exactly what I'd experienced in the
valley.

"…Have you been down there?" I asked.

The Curator looked out the window a moment. Only rooftops were visible
through it. Not a direction that looked toward the valley.

"…Yes," he said. "Many times."

I was startled by that. I'd heard that no one in this city went down.
There was no need to.

"…Why do you go down?"

The Curator didn't answer that right away. He squared the corners of the
stack of papers, though they were already square.

"…Because it's my work," he said.

"…Is that your work? Going down into the valley?"

"…No one assigned it to me," the Curator said. "I just thought someone
had to know. What's down there."

I turned that over for a long time.

"…What is down there?" I asked. "As you've seen it."

This time the Curator was silent for a long while. I nearly started
counting that silence. Then I stopped myself. For the first time,
counting something in front of this man felt like rudeness.

"…Failure," he said finally.

Something in me went rigid at that word.

"…Are the ones who failed, down there?"

"…No," the Curator said, shaking his head. "It isn't the people who
failed."

"…Then what failed?"

The Curator looked straight at me. For the first time, I saw something
unhidden in those eyes.

"…I did," he said. "I'm the one who failed."

I had no answer to that.

"…What do you mean?" I managed. "What did you fail at?"

The Curator laced his fingers together. He sat like that for a moment.

"…When I first came to this post," he said, "I thought preservation was
a completion. Keeping a person from pain, keeping them from
disappearing. I believed that was the best thing we could do."

"…You don't now?"

"…I still think that. Preservation really is a good thing. No one is
in pain, no one disappears. That's not a lie."

"…But?"

"…But I learned, later, that believing that was the whole of it — that
was my failure. That not being in pain and being alive are different
things — I didn't know that. Not for a very long time."

"…Perpetuity doesn't fail," the Curator went on. "That's a fact.
Preservation is exact. It does exactly what it promises. No
disappearing, no losing memory, no pain. It has never once broken that,
not a single time."

"…But?"

"…But believing that was the whole of it — that was my failure."

I didn't fully understand that sentence at the time. I know now. This
man wasn't doubting the system. The system worked perfectly. What he
doubted was himself — himself, who had believed a perfect system to be
only that: perfect.

"…What's down there," the Curator said, "isn't something preservation
failed to produce. It's a place that shows what preservation left out."

"…Left out what?"

The Curator opened his hand, then closed it again.

"…I don't know exactly, either. That's why I go down. To find out."

I felt that answer was strangely honest. This man never seemed ashamed
to say he didn't know. I'd sensed before that he didn't tell me
everything he knew. But that wasn't him hiding something — it was that
there really were things he didn't know.

"…The first time I asked you," I said, carefully, "when you told me
release was possible. You didn't look glad."

"…No."

"…Why not?"

The Curator looked at me a long while at that question.

"…While I'm in this post," he said, "my work is to explain this
procedure exactly. As it is. Without hiding anything, without
exaggerating anything. That's my job."

"…Yes."

"…But every time I do that work, I know exactly what I'm saying."

I hadn't yet felt the full weight of those words.

"…What do you mean?"

For the first time I saw that his eyes were wet. He wasn't crying. Just
something heavy around them.

"…Every time, I recommend preservation to people," he said. "Because
it's my work. And I know what that means for that person. They will
never be in pain again. They will never disappear again. And they will
never, ever again, laugh or cry."

I couldn't breathe at that.

"…Knowing that," the Curator went on, "I do that work every day."

"…How many times a day?" I asked.

"…Usually three or four," he said. "Sometimes more than ten."

I was startled by that number. This man repeated words of the same
weight ten times a day. I tried to count how many years that had
accumulated over, and gave it up. It seemed a number too large for
counting to mean anything.

"…Then why do you keep doing it?" I asked. I didn't mean it as an
accusation. I was truly curious.

"…If I don't, someone else will," the Curator said. "And I — at least if
I'm the one doing it, I can do it without hiding anything."

"…Does that comfort you?"

"…No," he said, right away. "It's no comfort. It's just the best I can
do."

I couldn't say anything in front of that answer. This man didn't look
for comfort in his own work. He didn't justify his own work either. He
simply knew the work was sad, and didn't stop because of that sadness.

"…What about kids like Eikon?" I asked. "Do you know them too?"

"…Yes. I know all of them."

"…How do you feel, seeing them?"

The Curator looked out the window a moment.

"…At first I was glad," he said. "It was proof that preservation was
working well. No pain, not forgotten. That was the goal of my work."

"…And now?"

"…I'm still glad," he said. "And at the same time, I'm sad."

I started to ask how those two feelings could exist together in one
person, and gave it up. I already knew the answer. I'd heard something
like it from Speon. Worried, but that doesn't mean I can go in your
place. Hard, but that doesn't mean I can't bear it. This man was holding
two things together in the same way.

"…I'm not going to quit this work," the Curator said. "That's the most
honest answer I can give you."

"…Why not quit?"

"…Because if I quit, someone who doesn't grieve as much as I do will do
this instead."

My throat tightened at that. This man was using his own sorrow like a
resource. He seemed to believe that if someone who grieves does this
work, the work becomes, if only a little, less cold.

I came to see this man differently, that day. At first I'd seen him as
a kind man. Then I'd seen him as a tired man. That day, for the first
time, I saw him as a sad man. And I saw that his sadness didn't stop him.
I think the last of those three is the heaviest.

Elsewhere in this record, I've heard about other officials in other
cities. One believed the system so completely he never doubted it. One
didn't believe it, but followed it anyway for lack of any alternative.
Another didn't even know what shame was. Every time I hear those
stories, I think of this man. He was unlike all three. He believed the
system — because the system really was exact. And at the same time,
knowing exactly what that exactness meant for a person, he kept doing the
work every day. I think that is the hardest place to stand. Believing,
and grieving.

From the stack of papers on his desk, he pulled out one sheet, then put
it back without showing it to me. I didn't ask what it was. But
watching that gesture, I remembered what Speon had said — that this man
wrote every document himself instead of leaving it to anyone else. That
he staked his own name on anything he decided.

"…Did you write that yourself?" I asked.

"…Yes. I write every document I sign, myself."

"…Why? You could leave it to someone else."

"…If I don't write it myself," the Curator said, "it feels like I'm not
taking responsibility for it."

My throat tightened again at that.

I looked down at the floor by the window. It was worn far more than the
rest of the floor. It looked like the spot he stood in every time he
looked outside. I knew that kind of wear didn't come in a few days. It
takes a long time, a very long time of standing, to make a floor look
like that.

"…Thank you," I said. I wasn't sure exactly what I was thanking him for.

"…For what?"

"…For being honest with me."

At that the Curator smiled, very slightly, for the first time. A sad
smile.

"…Anemos, you're the first person I've said this to."

I asked, startled.

"…You never told anyone?"

"…No. No one ever asked."

That sentence rang, to me, like Eikon's had. It's been a while since
I've heard the word bored. No one in this city ever asked each other
things like this. They were people with no reason to ask.

"…Can I ask one more thing?" I said, standing to leave.

"…Yes."

"…You said you'd seen someone released. The first time. Can you tell me,
now, what that was like?"

The Curator looked at me for a long while.

"…I trembled," he said. "Seeing someone who could die again. But that
person smiled, for the first time. I still remember that face."

I couldn't ask anything to that.

"…What happened to that person afterward?" I asked.

"…They lived. A few years," the Curator said. "And then they stopped."

I recognized that word. It was a word I'd used outside. The word for
someone who passes after a long illness. It was the first time I'd heard
anyone in this city use it.

"…Where did you learn that word?" I asked.

"…That person taught it to me. After the release. They asked me to use
it, when they died."

"…Why?"

"…They said they wanted to leave it behind as proof they had lived."

I caught my breath at that.

"…Was that smile a good thing, or a bad thing?" I managed.

"…Both," the Curator said. "I still don't know the answer."

Leaving that room, I thought again of the day I'd first felt sorry for
this man. That day I couldn't explain why. I can now. I think I was
already sensing this man's sorrow, even then. It just took twenty-eight
chapters to give it a name.

(Continued in Chapter 35.)

Chapter 35. The Water Reaches

_Previously_ — The Curator spoke his own sorrow for the first time. He
said he kept doing his work knowing exactly what it was. "If I don't,
someone else will. At least I can do it without hiding anything." I
carried those words back to the pit with me.

That afternoon, the sound from the pit changed.

I felt the difference from far off. It wasn't the sound of a shovel. It
was some kind of splashing.

I hurried my steps. The weight that had sat on me ever since I'd left the
Curator's room lifted, all at once, at that single sound. I had no time,
then, to think about where that lightness had come from.

When I reached the pit, Speon was standing inside it. Water had risen to
his ankles. He saw me and grinned.

"…It came."

I didn't understand that right away.

"…What did?"

"…The water."

I looked down into the pit. Water was rising from the bottom. Not clear
— closer to muddy. But it was unmistakably water. It moved, it caught
the light, it flowed.

I remember that moment exactly. I'd seen water many times since coming
to this city. The distribution station's water, the water in my flask.
All of it water that had been held somewhere. The water I saw that day
was different. It was water rising up out of the ground. Not water
someone had set there — water that simply came, from there.

"…How long has it been?" I asked.

"…About an hour."

"…Why didn't you call me?"

"…There wasn't time to," Speon said, laughing. "First it just got
damp, and then all at once it surged up."

I crouched down there and looked at the water. I reached out to touch
it and stopped.

"…Can I touch it?"

"…You should be the first."

I dipped my hand into the water. It was cold. Grains of soil slipped
between my fingers. I let myself feel that touch for a long time.

Outside, I'd never once thought of this touch as anything special.
Water had been everywhere. In every well, along every riverbank. It was
only here, in this city, that I felt as if I were relearning this
sensation from the beginning. That it was cold. That it flowed. That it
slipped between my fingers. Things that had once been simply given were,
here, new and astonishing, one by one.

I felt something strange in that moment. This water came from the
thirty-seventh well — thirty-second, out of the thirty-seven he'd dug. I
could count that number. But the water itself couldn't be counted. It
just flowed. It was the first thing to arrive in this city that couldn't
be caught by a number.

Water kept rising the whole afternoon. Speon shaped the walls of the pit
and steered where it would run. I scooped soil out beside him.

Toward evening, people began to gather, one by one.

The distribution station attendant came first. He looked into the pit
and his eyes went wide.

"…It really came."

"…Yes," Speon said.

"…Amazing."

The attendant said only that and stood there a long while. He didn't
drink it. He just watched.

Next came the older woman — the same one who'd once asked Speon what a
well even was.

"…Well," she said. "It really was coming, after all."

"…Yes," Speon said. "I told you."

"…I believed it, but seeing it for myself is different."

She didn't drink either. She stood at the edge of the pit and simply
looked down at the water for a long while.

The official in gray came too. The same one who had asked about the
permit on the first day. He still had his collar's insignia on. He
stood in front of the pit and looked at the water for a long time.

"…Congratulations," he said to Speon. It was a businesslike tone, though
not as stiff as before.

"…Thank you," Speon said.

"…This will need to go into the record now. As a successful excavation
case."

"…You're welcome to."

The official took out his notebook and wrote something down. Watching
him, I remembered him writing "Not required" on the first day. I was
curious what he was writing now, but I didn't ask.

"…Is this water fit to drink?" he asked.

"…It'll take a few days to clear," Speon said.

The official nodded and closed his notebook. Then, like everyone else,
he left without drinking it.

The children came too. The same ones who'd once been caught reaching for
a shovel. This time no one stopped them.

"…Can we drink this?" one child asked.

"…Still a little cloudy," Speon said. "Once it settles you can drink
it."

The children looked disappointed at that, then quickly turned to
watching the grains of soil swirl on the water's surface. Stirring it
with a finger, watching it settle.

Watching those children, I saw for the first time, in this city, a face
purely enjoying itself. Not enjoyment born of need — just enjoyment born
of wonder. I was glad to see that face.

Eikon came too.

I recognized her coming from far off. Her walk was as unhurried as ever.
She stood in front of the pit and looked down at the water for a long
while. Her expression barely changed.

"…It really came," she said. The same words the attendant had used, but
with a different temperature.

"…Yeah," I said.

"…It's interesting, I guess," she said. "But I don't understand why it's
drawing this many people."

"…You don't find it interesting?"

"…I do. But interesting and liking are different things."

I laughed at that. It had the same grain as what she'd said before —
that curious and lacking were different things.

"…Want to try some?" I asked.

Eikon shook her head.

"…I don't need it."

"…What about just drinking it out of curiosity?"

"…Curiosity is satisfied by looking," she said. "Drinking is a
different matter."

That distinction was exactly like her. I didn't press her further. She
stood a little longer, then left quietly, like the others. Once, on her
way, she looked back. There was something in her eyes, but I couldn't
read what it was, that day.

I would recall that look again, later. In Chapter 44, I learn its
name.

By the time the sun had gone fully down, nearly ten people had gathered.

That was a large number by this city's standard. I was surprised,
counting it. No one had called anyone, and yet this many had come.

I noticed the way they stood too. Not crowded close together. Spaced at
a regular distance, each looking down into the pit on their own. No one
pushing, no one trying to get ahead. That spacing was exactly this
city's people. Even in front of something astonishing, they didn't
hurry.

They all wore a similar expression. A face that didn't need this, but
had come to see it because it was interesting. I felt a strange comfort
among those faces. This city's people weren't entirely numb. There had
just been very little, for a very long time, to move them.

No one drank. I didn't find that strange. It was to be expected. This
city's people weren't thirsty. The distribution station's water was
enough. This new water wasn't need — it was something else.

It was only then I realized that none of these people knew what it felt
like to be thirsty. The sensation of thirst itself had vanished from this
city long ago. I thought that maybe they hadn't come here because they
were thirsty, but because some faint memory in their bodies still
recalled, dimly, what thirst had once been.

I thought a long time that night about what that something else was. I
can say it a little now. It was proof. Proof that in a place everyone had
called unnecessary, something could really come out of the ground. There
was no need to drink it, but there was a reason to want to see it.

People began drifting off, one by one. At night everyone went in at
their set time. That exactness felt, that day, a little regrettable to
me.

The last one left was the distribution station attendant. He crouched
at the pit's edge and looked at the water for a long time.

"…What happens to it now?" he asked.

"…Let it settle," Speon said, "and then we'll have to cut a channel
for it."

"…To where?"

"…Haven't decided that yet."

The attendant nodded. Then he reached out his hand.

He held his hand out and paused for a moment. I didn't know what that
pause meant. It looked like hesitation, or maybe just a moment spent
making up his mind.

He dipped only the tips of his fingers into the water.

A very brief motion. Not meant to drink. Just a gesture to confirm, with
his fingertips, that the water was really there.

A drop gathered at his fingertip and fell.

The ripple spread to the edge of the pit and faded. I followed that
small ripple with my eyes for a long time. That was the only motion left
on the water's surface.

He looked at his hand a moment. His wet fingers. Then, without a word,
he drew his hand back.

I remember that gesture exactly, even now. He said nothing. He didn't
drink. He just dipped his hand in and took it out again. I think that
motion is the biggest thing that happened in this city that day. No one
would call it that, but I do.

He hadn't dipped his hand in because he needed to. He was a man who
received water from the distribution station. He lacked nothing. And
yet he dipped his hand in. There was no reason for that gesture. Or
rather — there was a reason, but it was the kind of reason "need" can't
explain.

"…It's cold," he said. I couldn't tell whether he was speaking to
Speon or to himself.
