"…Yes," Speon said. "Water from underground is always cold."

The attendant stood up. He wiped his wet fingers on his clothes. Then he
looked at me a moment.

"…Anemos, have you tried this water?"

"…Not yet. I only touched it."

"…Why not drink it? You're the one who wanted it."

I couldn't answer that right away.

"…It doesn't feel like the time to drink it, yet," I said.

The attendant tilted his head at that. His face said he didn't
understand. But he didn't press further, said goodbye, and left.

After the sun had set fully, one more person came, last of all.

I recognized the silhouette from far off. The Curator. He had no folder
with him. Empty-handed, unlike his usual self.

"…I heard the news," he said, standing in front of the pit.

"…Already?" I asked.

"…Word travels fast in this city. It just never swells."

He looked into the pit for a long time. The water dimly reflected light
in the dark.

"…How do you feel?" I asked.

The Curator's face took on a moment of choosing his words.

"…It's strange," he said. "I've told people today, more than once, that
this city has no need for water. It's the truth. But seeing this now, I
feel something else."

"…What?"

"…I can't explain it," he said. "It's just — good."

That sentence felt heavier to me than anything I'd heard from anyone
else all day. This was a man who knew the weight of every procedure he
carried out. A man like that had just said, without explanation, that it
was simply good.

He didn't dip his hand in. He just stood there a long while, looking
down. Then he said goodbye and turned to walk away.

I watched his back for a long time. I thought of what he'd told me that
afternoon. If I don't, someone else will. At least I can do it without
hiding anything. That night I thought a long time about how those words
and this — standing here doing nothing but watching — could live inside
one person. Some people were already wet, without ever dipping their
hand in.

Only Speon and I remained. The water in the pit was still, little by
little, rising.

"…Why didn't you drink it?" Speon asked.

"…I don't know," I said. "It just doesn't feel like it's time yet."

"…Not a bad answer."

I looked at the stars reflected in the water. Even in that still-cloudy
water, a few stars shimmered on the surface.

That night, I kept coming back to the attendant's gesture. He hadn't
drunk it. He'd just dipped his hand in. I think this whole book is inside
that small motion. Even a person who says he needs nothing still reaches
out his hand. Without explaining why. Of the nearly ten people who came
to the water that day, in the end only that one had reached out. Counting
that number, this time, I didn't find it lacking at all.

The sound of water ran all through the night. Small, steady. I walked
back to the dormitory listening to it. For the first time in days, I
walked without counting anything.

On the way I passed the Curator's building. One light was on. His room.
I looked up at that light for a moment. It meant he was still working, at
this hour. Even on the day the water had come, he had gone back to his
own place.

Watching that light, I thought of my mother. I wanted to tell her about
this. That the water had come, that people had dipped their hands in.
But in that moment I knew — she would not come, hearing this news, to
dip her hand in. She had no need. Something in me ached at that thought.
The two people closest to me in this city were, that day, the two people
with the same reason for not being there.

Back at the dormitory, I opened my flask. Half of the distribution
station's water was still inside. I drank it. It tasted the same as
always. And yet that day even that water's taste felt different.
Water that had been held, and water that had come running up out of the
ground — the same water, and yet they reached me with a different
weight.

Last, that night, I had this thought. This water hadn't wet anyone's
throat yet. And yet it had already done something to this city. Nearly
ten people had gathered for no reason at all, and one person had dipped
in a hand. These were things need couldn't explain. I liked those things
that couldn't be explained.

(Continued in Chapter 36.)

Chapter 36. It Runs Down to the Valley

_Previously_ — Water came up in Speon's pit. People gathered. No one
drank it. Only the distribution station attendant dipped in a
fingertip. That night even the water in my own flask felt different.

The water cleared in three days.

I stopped by the pit every day through those three days. The first day
it was muddy. The second day it had settled a little. By the third day
it was clear enough to see the bottom. That day, for the first time,
Speon scooped up a mouthful and drank it.

"…How is it?" I asked.

"…Tastes like water," he said, laughing.

I tried a mouthful too. Cold, with a faint trace of earth still in it.
Close to the well water I used to drink outside. That familiarity
surprised me. Every drop of water I'd had since coming to this city had
passed through the distribution station. Exact, regulation amounts.
This water was just water. Untouched by anyone's hand.

I noticed his answer was exactly the same as Eikon's had been. Tastes
like water. But this time it sounded different. Eikon had said it
because there was nothing surprising in it. Speon said it though there
was. The same words held something different inside.

On the fourth day, Speon set up a small stone marker at the edge of the
pit.

"…What's that?" I asked.

"…I'm going to run the channel from here."

I watched him point his hand out. Not toward the city's inside. Toward
the outside of the city — more exactly, downward.

"…Where's it going?" I asked.

"…The valley."

I stopped for a moment at that.

"…Shouldn't it go toward the city first?" I asked. "The people are
here."

"…The city already has it," Speon said. "There's the distribution
station. Regulation amounts, but it's there."

"…But the valley has none at all."

Speon planted his shovel in the soil and looked at me. Something a
little mischievous showed in his eyes.

"…Well, there's your answer, then."

I liked that answer. But I still wanted to ask more.

"…Still, shouldn't the city come first, in order? It's closer."

Speon thought about that for a moment. Then he said this.

"…Up there isn't dry."

I didn't understand that right away.

"…What do you mean?"

"…No one in the city dies of thirst. There's the distribution station.
It's not that the water's not enough — it's that they don't need water.
That's different from being dry."

"…And the valley?"

"…The valley is really dry. Not because there's no water — because
water's never once gone there."

I turned that distinction over for a long time. The city wasn't dry
because it had no need. The valley was dry because water had never
reached it. On the surface, both places had no water. But Speon saw the
two as different.

I didn't fully understand that, that day. I know now. The city was a
place that had forgotten thirst. The valley was a place that had never
even had thirst to forget. Speon knew exactly which of the two was more
urgent.

That afternoon, Eikon passed by and stopped walking.

"…Where's it going?" she asked, looking at the stone marker Speon had
set.

"…The valley," I said.

Eikon's expression stiffened slightly.

"…Why there?"

"…Speon says up here isn't dry already."

Eikon turned that over a moment.

"…There's no one there. No one who needs water."

"…Seems that way."

"…Then why send it there?"

I looked at Speon at that question. He didn't stop shoveling, and
answered in my place.

"…You don't only give water to people who need it," he said. "You can
make a need, too."

Eikon's face showed she didn't understand.

"…What does that mean?"

"…I don't know either," Speon said, laughing. "It just feels that
way."

Eikon didn't ask further at that answer. She looked once more at the
pit and the channel, and walked off without a word. Watching her back, I
saw, for the first time, that she wasn't certain of something. Only for
a moment.

I couldn't properly read that brief unsteadiness, then. I know now. In
that moment, for the first time, she'd seen something her own logic
couldn't explain — sending water to a place that had no need for it. In
her world, there was no place for a thing like that.

I stood there and turned to look back at the city.

The distribution station, the square, the Curator's building. All
quiet. That quiet looked different to me, this time. Before, that
quiet had looked like peace. That day it looked like something being
held back.

"…Won't the city's people feel hurt?" I asked. "That it's not coming to
them, and going down instead."

"…I asked," Speon said.

I was surprised at that.

"…Asked who?"

"…The distribution station attendant, that official in gray, a few
others."

"…What did they say?"

"…All of them said it didn't matter. That what they had was already
enough."

That answer wasn't strange to me. This city's people always said that.
That it didn't matter. That it was enough. And that wasn't a lie.

That evening, the Curator heard the news and came by as well.

"…I heard it's going to the valley," he said.

"…Yes."

He stood a long while in front of the marker. I tensed for a moment,
thinking he might object. The valley wasn't a light place for him
either.

"…Are you all right with this?" I asked.

"…Yes," he said. "If anything, I think this should have happened long
ago."

"…Why?"

"…I've been going down there alone, all this time," he said. "Without
water. Now I won't be alone anymore."

Something in me stirred at that. This man was receiving a single
channel of water as company for his own sorrow.

"…You won't stop it?" I asked.

"…There's no reason to stop it," he said. "This is the first thing I've
ever not wanted to stop."

He left with only those words. I looked at Speon. Speon said nothing
and picked up his shovel again.

We began cutting the channel together.

Starting from the city's edge. We dug the soil shallow to make a
ditch, and packed the edges with stone. The water began to flow slowly
along it.

I found the work similar to, and yet different from, the digging I'd
done going down into the valley. Back then it had been downward, narrow,
cut deep. This time it was sideways, long, cut far. The blisters on my
palms broke open again. I felt the callus already sitting there and
picked up the shovel again.

"…Is it all right to dig this shallow?" I asked. I felt uneasy that we
were taking the soil so thin.

"…Shallow is what keeps it from collapsing," Speon said. "Dig deep and
it goes faster, but it collapses just as fast. The more you rush a
channel, the less it lasts."

Hearing that, I remembered what the old well-digger had once told me. That
you don't find water — you make it a way. I passed those words along to
Speon.

"…That's exactly right," Speon said, laughing. "I wish I'd known that
old man."

Standing there, I felt strangely steadied that two people who'd never
once met were saying the same thing.

I watched the water flow. At first it was slow. The soil was drinking it
in. But after a day or two, the soil grew wet and packed, and the water
ran faster.

Digging that ditch, I thought of the twelve days I'd spent going down
into the valley. Back then I'd dug down and down. Now the water was
running along that same direction. I wanted to believe that wasn't a
coincidence. I'm still not sure.

The channel reached the city's edge in three days. From there the ground
grew steep. I gathered stones and built up embankments. It had become
work my hands knew by now.

Over those three days I measured the channel's length in my own steps.
The first day, two hundred and thirty steps; the second, four hundred
more; and on the third, it reached the city's edge. Writing those
numbers down again, I remembered the steps I'd counted going down into
the valley. Back then I'd counted my own steps going down. This time I
counted the water's steps, not my own. The direction was the same.

Digging this channel, I found myself remembering something Speon had
told me once before. That in some city he'd seen a bridge only half
finished, and that he'd had three friends, and one of them had helped
finish that bridge. I hadn't asked, then, where that bridge led. Watching
this channel now, I thought this channel and that bridge might be the
same kind of thing. Not something for one city alone, but something that
joins city to city. I'm not certain. I only set the thought down here.

At the city's edge, in front of the slope that led down into the valley,
Speon stopped walking.

"…From here on," he said, "we have to go slow."

"…Why?"

"…The slope's steep. Rush it and the channel collapses. You have to
cut it a little at a time, to make it last."

I listened to that and packed the soil down evenly. Speon set stones as
supports. We worked on that the whole day.

The first tier we cut collapsed. The water surged in all at once and
overran the earthen bank. Speon didn't get angry seeing that. He simply
rebuilt the bank. This time he cut the tier lower and narrower. It took
time for the water to fill that tier before crossing to the next. Rush
it and it collapsed; wait and it crossed over. I learned that rhythm with
my hands, and with my whole body.

Every time the water crossed to the next tier, a short, broken sound
came. A brief trickling, then a stop, then trickling again. Every time I
heard that sound, I could count one more tier finished. By evening I had
heard that sound eleven times.

By evening, the water had reached the first tier of the slope. I stood
there and looked down.

The valley was down there. Still wide, still dry. I thought that
somewhere in that landscape, he must be there. Walking among the bones,
even now.

"…How long will it take?" I asked. "To reach all the way down there."

"…Don't know," Speon said. "Won't know till we get there."

That answer was familiar to me. Speon always said that. And it was
always right.

"…But it will reach, won't it?"

Speon looked at me.

"…It'll reach," he said. Not a voice full of certainty. Just a voice
stating a fact.

"…How do you know?"

"…Water goes down. As long as nothing blocks it."

That sentence was simple, and yet it felt heavy to me. As long as
nothing blocks it. This city hadn't been blocking the water, all this
time. It had just never made a way for it to go. Speon had made that
way. Now the water was only going where it had always meant to go.

Standing there, I thought of something I'd heard from Book Two. Something
Speon had once said, they told me. Water rots when it sits still. It
lives when it flows. I'd taken that, at the time, as only talk about a
well. That day, standing in front of the slope, I understood that it had
been a much larger story than that.

I listened to the water running down. Still a small sound. The sound of
it crossing from the slope's first tier to the next. A trickling sound,
but one that didn't stop.

"…When this water reaches down there," I asked, "what changes?"

Speon slung the shovel over his shoulder.

"…Don't know," he said again. "Won't know till it reaches."

I laughed at that answer. It was an answer no one in this city ever
gave. This city knew every outcome in advance. Speon never knew, right
to the end. And he was never ashamed of not knowing.

The sun went fully down. We sat side by side there and listened to the
water.

"…Cutting more tomorrow?" I asked.

"…Yeah. One tier at a time."

"…I'll help."

"…I know," Speon said. "You don't have to ask."

I laughed at that. I knew things like that about him now too.

I couldn't fall asleep for a long time that night. I imagined the day
the channel would reach the valley. Whether he would see that water.
Whether the bones would really live again, once it reached them. I still
had no answer to that question. But this time, not having one didn't
weigh on me.

I went back over everything that had happened these past three days, in
order. Meeting him in the valley. The question I couldn't answer.
Coming back to the city and finding the view looked different. Eikon's
logic, that sentence I couldn't argue against. The Curator's sorrow, the
wet eyes he'd shown me for the first time. And the water. That night no
one drank it, and one person dipped in a hand.

Laying those four things side by side, I felt for the first time that
everyone I'd met in this city was, in the end, standing in front of the
same thing. He had said it was all right not to know the answer. Eikon
had said she needed no answer because she lacked nothing. The Curator
had grieved even while he knew the answer. I belonged fully to none of
those three, and stood somewhere between all of them.

The sound of water came through the window all through the night. I
listened to it and turned that sentence over again. Up there isn't dry
already. I turned it over a long while, and fell asleep with it.

I still don't know the day this water will reach the valley. But
writing this record now, I know this much: that water does, in the end,
reach it. And when it reaches, I come to stand again in front of the
question I put off longest in this whole book. That is not Part Three's
story. It is Part Four's.

Before I closed my eyes, I looked at my hands, last of all. Blisters and
calluses mixed together on them. Tomorrow I would dig soil with these
hands again. And the day after that too. These hands would carry this
water all the way to the valley. I decided to believe that.

(Continued in Part Four.)

The Dry Valley, Part Four

Breath

Chapter 37. Asked Again

_Previously_ — Speon had run the channel toward the valley. When I asked
why down instead of up, he said, "Up there isn't dry." I turned that
over the whole way back into the city.

In the morning I helped Speon work on the ditch.

The channel now ran all the way to the edge of the city. From there the
ground turned steep, and the water kept slipping sideways. I gathered
stones and built up the banks. My hands knew the work.

"…Going again today." Speon asked it. He wasn't a man who asked me
things much, so the question stood out.

"…Yes."

"…What for."

I couldn't answer him right away. Speon didn't wait for an answer. He
picked the shovel back up and dug.

"…Take some water with you." he said. "Don't go empty-handed."

I didn't ask what he meant by that. I filled the flask.

Walking toward the inner part of the Preserve, I heard the sound of
water.

The first time I'd come into this city, what had struck me strangest was
the absence of sound. There had been no sound of water. Now there was.
Along every alley, faint under everything else, ran the sound of the
channel.

A few of the city people passed by, hearing it. No one stopped. They
didn't need to. But I saw one of them slow down. Just for a moment. Then
that person went back to their ordinary pace.

I didn't take much notice of that brief slowing, at the time. Looking
back now, that was the first tremor I ever saw in this city. A very
small one. One step's worth of a tremor.

I carried the visitation form with me, and at the counter I folded it
back up.

I didn't know, right there, why I folded it. The form always had the
same box on it. Purpose of visit. I stood there a long time holding the
pen, not knowing what to write. Persuasion. I couldn't write that. It
was too honest to count as a purpose.

The clerk looked at me.

"…The purpose box is empty."

"…Just a visit."

"…That's what everyone puts." The clerk smiled. "There are sub-items
listed over there."

I looked at the sub-items. Well-being, counsel, notification, other. I
marked other.

It took me that long to fill in one small box. Looking back now it
seems absurd. But at the time I truly didn't know. What I was going
there to do, that day.

There was no one else in the waiting room besides me.

I was the only one in this city who ever applied for a visit. It had
been that way the first time I came, and it was that way on this, my
thirty-second time. City people had no need of visits. If you wanted to
see a preserved family member, you simply walked over. You didn't get
startled, and you didn't miss them.

I started counting the empty chairs, then stopped. There were always
twelve. No need to count them.

Instead I called up my past visits. The nineteenth, the day I'd tried to
ask why and couldn't. The twentieth, the day I'd learned that I missed
you wasn't an answer. The twenty-first, the day I'd cancelled the
application myself, in front of my brother.

Writing those numbers down here again, I realize all over how faithfully
I'd been counting those visits, all that time. Counting was how I
endured things. But that day, even sitting in the waiting room, I
didn't count. I just sat.

The clerk called my name. I stood up.

My mother welcomed me as she always did.

"…Anemos."

"…Yes."

"…You've come again."

You've come again. I could count that phrase by now. I didn't count
which visit this made. I'd started to, and stopped. At some point I'd
understood that the number told me nothing. To my mother, the first
visit and the tenth were exactly the same.

I sat down across from her. I had no words prepared. For the first time.
For seven years I had always come with something ready.

I counted back through what that preparation had been. At the
nineteenth visit I'd come prepared to ask why. At the twentieth I'd come
to confirm that I missed you wasn't an answer. I always came to confirm
something. Today there was nothing to confirm.

"…I don't have anything to say today." I said.

My mother looked at me. Her expression didn't change much. This person's
expression never changes much. I know that now.

"…Then why did you come."

I hadn't prepared an answer to that question.

"…No reason."

I just talked about the valley.

Not words I'd prepared. I just told her what I'd seen that day. How
Speon had run the water channel downward, how when I'd asked why down
and not up, he'd answered, "Up there isn't dry."

"…A water channel?" my mother asked.

"…Yes. From the well."

"…There's a well in this city?"

I caught the way she said it. Not the same polite form she used when she
spoke about herself. She'd said in this city. As if this city weren't
the place she lived.

That was the first time I noticed that my mother never spoke of this
city as her own. In this city. Over there. My mother always spoke of
this city as though standing one step outside it. I didn't know, that
day, what that meant.

"…Yes. Newly dug. Someone named Speon."

"…Speon." My mother repeated the name. "That's an unfamiliar name."

"…He's an unfamiliar person. Nobody asked him to come, but he came."

My mother smiled, a little, for the first time. Very small.

"…It takes someone like that, for a well to come to be."

I told her about the valley too.

I told her about going down, about seeing traces there dry as bone,
about meeting a man. I didn't say what he'd asked me. That wasn't
something to tell my mother, not yet.

"…Why did you go there." my mother asked.

"…Speon told me to go see it."

That wasn't a lie. But it wasn't the whole of it either.

"…Were you afraid?"

I stopped for a moment at that question. It had been a long time since
my mother had asked me what I was afraid of. No — it was the first
time. In seven years of visits.

I didn't count it, right then. It didn't even occur to me to count it.
I counted it later. Seven years, thirty-two visits, and this question,
once. I still remember that ratio exactly.

"…No." I said. "It was strange."

"…How."

"…The water hadn't reached there. The channel hasn't gotten that far
yet. But even so, something there felt alive."

My mother didn't answer that. She just looked at me for a long time.

"…What kind of person was he, the one you met there." she asked, after
a while.

"…Someone whose age I couldn't guess. He was just walking among the
bones."

"…What did he say."

I stopped there for a moment. The question he had asked me — whether I
thought these could live again — was something I hadn't yet passed on
to anyone.

"…He didn't say much." I said. "We just walked together a little."

That was a lie. A very small one. My mother didn't press.

I took out the water flask.

The one given to me when I left my father's relatives' house, the one
I'd carried for seven years. It was half full inside.

"…This." I said. "It's not this city's water. It's water flowing down
to the valley. I got a little from Speon."

My mother looked at it. She didn't hold out her hand.

"…May I drink it?"

That question startled me. My mother had never needed anything, all
this time. Not water, not food, not sleep. And now she was looking at
the flask and asking a question like that.

At the time I thought it was just a politeness. Now I know. It wasn't
politeness. It was the first time she'd been curious about something.
I didn't catch the difference, that day. In the next chapter, I come
back to what I did that day, not even knowing why myself.

"…No." I said. "Not yet."

My mother nodded. She didn't seem hurt. She simply accepted it, as it
was.

"…I see."

I brought up my brother too, briefly.

"…My brother's doing well." I said. "He couldn't come today. He came
first, earlier."

"…Does he still do that." my mother asked. "Ask if his brother's come."

"…Yes. Every time."

My mother laughed at that. Just for a moment.

"…He's been that way since he was small. He always asked, when you
weren't there."

Listening to that, I remembered again the path where my brother used
to follow me, the spot in front of the third stone wall where he always
stopped. I didn't tell my mother that. Not yet.

Near the end of visiting hours, my mother asked,

"…Weren't you here to persuade me?"

I stopped at that.

"…No."

"…Then what did you come for."

I couldn't answer that question. To be precise, it wasn't that I had
no words for an answer — I didn't know myself what the answer was. That
night I would go looking for it for the first time. It's one of the
most important nights in this book.

"…I don't know." I said honestly. "I just wanted to talk."

My mother looked at me. Something in her eyes was a little different
from usual. I couldn't put my finger on what, at the time.

"…All right." my mother said. "I was glad you talked to me."

I gathered the flask back up.

Standing, I turned back for a moment. My mother was still sitting there.
In the same posture as seven years ago.

"…I'll come again." I said.

"…All right."

It was the same farewell as always. But that day something was
different. I thought about what it was, walking out the door. No answer
came.

I only understood it later. That I'd written other in the purpose box
on the form, that I'd gone without any words prepared, that I'd just
talked about the valley — those had all been the same thing. That was
the first day I hadn't gone there to persuade her. What I had gone
there to do, I would only find out that night.

I ran into the Curator in the hallway.

"…You kept it short today." he said. He always recorded the length of
visits.

"…Yes."

"…Are you all right?"

I raised the flask instead of answering.

"…Will there be a day I can give this to my mother?"

The Curator looked at the flask for a moment. Something crossed his
eyes. I couldn't read what it was, at the time.

"…After release." he said. "Not before then. The body doesn't need it."

"…I know."

"…But you didn't bring that up today."

I stopped at that. I didn't ask how he knew. This man couldn't hear
everything that happened inside the visiting room, but he always read
the face of the person who came out.

"…No." I said. "I didn't, today."

The Curator nodded. He didn't ask further. He just walked past me.

A few steps on, he turned back.

"…Your mother's expression was a little different today." he said.
"I've been recording this for years now, and I've never seen this
before."

"…Different how?"

"…It's hard to explain." the Curator said. "Just — different."

He left it at that and walked on.

I watched his back for a moment. This man always knows something and
doesn't ask. I used to think that was consideration. Now I know. It was
that, and it also meant this man already knew the answer to that
question himself.

I walked to the edge of the city. I could hear the channel running.
Along the ditch Speon had dug, water was flowing down, toward the
valley.

I sat there for a long while.

Eikon passed by, saw me, and came over.

"…Coming back from a visit?" she asked.

"…Yeah."

"…Did you try to persuade her again today?"

I couldn't answer that right away. Eikon knew exactly what I went to my
mother to do. She didn't mock it. She was just curious.

"…No." I said. "Not today."

"…Why not?"

"…I don't know."

Eikon looked at me for a moment. There was no judgment in her eyes. She
doesn't judge people. She lacks nothing, so she has no interest in
measuring others either.

"…That's strange." she said. "After preparing so long."

"…Yeah."

She didn't ask further and went her own way. I watched her go, and ran
back through everything that had happened that day, in order. The
folded form, the empty preparation, my mother's then why did you come,
my I don't know.

That night I realized something strange. In that whole visit, not once
had I said let's go together now. Five words I'd been preparing for
seven years. I hadn't forgotten them. I'd just felt, that day, that the
words weren't needed.

Why I felt they weren't needed, I still didn't know. I would find out
the next day.

The sound of water kept going. Listening to it, for the first time in a
long while, I counted nothing at all.

That night I slept with my head on the flask. It was half full again
inside. The water Speon had told me to take. I didn't know, even then,
exactly why I'd brought it. It was water I couldn't even give my mother.
And yet I hadn't emptied it out. I'd filled it back up.

Before I fell asleep that night I asked myself one thing, once. Had I
really gone today wanting nothing at all. I wanted to answer yes to
that. But the answer wouldn't come.

(Continued in Chapter 38.)

Chapter 38. Why I Came

_Previously_ — I went to my mother without trying to persuade her, and
only talked about the valley and the water. Her expression was
different from usual. That night I asked myself whether I'd really
gone wanting nothing at all. The answer wouldn't come.

I couldn't sleep that night.

I lay with my head on the flask, but my eyes kept opening. I looked at
the ceiling. This room was one the Preserve set aside for people like
me. No furniture in it. No one who needed any.

In that room, that one night, I thought more than I had in the sum of
all my seven years' nights. I still think so now. Some nights are
longer than others.

I closed my eyes and counted back from the beginning again. From the
day I left the settlement, from the day I reached the city after four
days' walk, up through the thirty-second visit.

Somewhere among those numbers there seemed to be one I'd missed. I
didn't know which. It just felt like it was there.

I was a person who counted things. But that night, no matter how much
I counted, no answer came. I learned then, for the first time, that
there were things counting alone could not reach.

Toward dawn I fell asleep briefly and dreamed.

In the dream I was nine years old. Sitting behind the hill. But this
time I wasn't alone. My brother was sitting beside me. Still eight
years old.

"…Brother." my dream-brother said. "Why are you sitting alone."

I couldn't answer. I opened my eyes.

I still remember that dream exactly. The moment I woke from it, I
knew. The nine-year-old me who had sat behind that hill hadn't been
there because he wanted to be alone. He had been there because he was
learning, for the first time that day, how to be alone.

In the morning I went to Speon.

He was already at the ditch. He saw me and handed me a second shovel.

"…Looks like you didn't sleep well." he said.

"…How could you tell."

"…I can tell by the face. Same when you're digging."

I took the shovel and dug at the earth. I'd thought using my body might
help. It didn't.

"…Speon." I said. "That old man, before — he said water isn't
something you find."

"…He said you make it a way."

"…What did that mean, exactly."

Speon stopped digging and looked at me. It was a look I hadn't seen
before.

"…Why are you suddenly asking that."

"…I don't know. No reason."

He went back to digging. The answer didn't come right away.

"…Water is everywhere." he said, after a long while. "Under the
ground. You think it isn't there because you can't see it, but it's all
there, really. Digging a well doesn't mean finding it. It means making
a place for water that's already there to come out."

I turned that over.

"…Then what about me." I said. "Did I come here to find something, or
to make a place for it?"

Speon looked at me again. Longer, this time.

"…That's not a question I can answer." he said. "You have to dig it up
yourself to know."

Hearing that, I set the shovel down. I stood there a long while, just
like that.

For seven years I'd thought I had come to find something. My mother.
My brother. To find what I'd lost.

But my mother had never been lost. She'd been in the city the whole
time. I could see her whenever I wanted. I'd kept looking for someone
who didn't need to be found.

So what had I been looking for?

I followed that question through to the end, for the first time.

The seventeen funerals came back to me again.

I'd written down many times that the old man's had been the ninth. He
was the one who'd taught me to dig wells. A man of few words. For a
while after he stopped, I kept digging that field alone.

At the other eight funerals there had been other people. Relatives,
neighbors, a child I'd played with. Each time I'd watched the people
crying beside me. I'd cried too. But every time, once the crying ended,
I walked home alone.

I counted that fact for the first time that night. Seventeen funerals,
and after them, seventeen roads walked alone. Every time I lost someone,
I left that place alone, every time.

I thought back to that day when I was nine again.

The day I sat alone behind the hill. The day I stayed until the sun
went down. The day I came back to find everyone already gone.

From that day on I was always alone. I grew up in my relatives' house,
watched seventeen funerals, and in not one of them did anyone who cried
beside me stay for long.

I'd written many times that counting was how I'd endured those seven
years. That's true. But it wasn't the whole of it. Even while I
counted, I was alone the whole time. Counting is something you do
alone.

I remembered the years my brother used to follow me. When I went for
water, when I went for firewood. The years he followed so closely it
annoyed me. I hadn't known, then, that I wasn't alone in those years.

I remembered too, what my brother had said, looking up at me.

Brother, you got tall.

I had turned sixteen and my brother was still eight. I hadn't properly
felt, at the time, how large that one sentence was. It meant seven
years of growing up alone.

I had been alone the whole time I was growing. While I got taller,
while I got stronger, while I learned how to go to a funeral. No one
beside me.

I hadn't wanted to undo those seven years. To be precise, the fact that
my mother and my brother were still exactly as they'd been seven years
ago was the force that had dragged me all the way here. If they hadn't
changed, it seemed like I might be able to go back to before, too.

I pushed that thought all the way through. For the first time, I didn't
run from it.

When I came to the city, I believed I had come to wake my family. That
much wasn't wrong. But hidden inside that sentence was another sentence.
I had come to go back to the time when I wasn't alone.

Waking someone and going back are different things. I saw that
difference for the first time there at the edge of the field, shovel
still in hand.

It was a shameful thing. That's how it felt. Inside the work I'd
believed was for my family's sake, I was there too. My own share of it.

Eikon came to mind, all at once.

I don't lack anything. Why would I need more? She'd said that. I hadn't
been able to answer it. I couldn't refute it.

There at the edge of the field, I admitted for the first time that
there was a lack in me too. I lacked something. I lacked someone beside
me. Eikon didn't have that lack. So she had nothing left to want. I
did. That was why I'd come all this way.

I looked straight at that difference for the first time. Eikon's logic
was hard to refute. You can't tell someone with nothing lacking to want
more. But I lacked something. My lack had carried me this far. Without
it, I would never have come to this city at all.

In that moment I felt, for the first time, one of this book's
questions become my own. Is what is preserved different from what is
alive. I didn't know the answer. I only knew, that day, that I lacked
because I was alive, and that I had come because I lacked.

Speon came over again. He must have noticed I hadn't been holding the
shovel long.

"…Dig anything up." he asked.

The question made me laugh. A strange laugh.

"…Yeah." I said. "I thought I came here for my mother."

"…Weren't you?"

"…I was. No — that part's true too, but it wasn't the whole of it. I
came to go back to the time when I wasn't alone."

Speon wasn't surprised at that.

"…What's wrong with that." he said.

"…It's wrong. Because I said it was for my mother, when really it was
for me."

"…It can be both." Speon said. "When I run a channel, I do it for
whoever's thirsty. But it's also true I do it because I like the sound
of water. Both are true. Does that make it a flaw?"

I couldn't answer that right away.

"…Your share being mixed in doesn't make the love for your mother
fake." he said. "But now that you know it, now that you know, you can't
go on pretending you don't."

I remember that as the most exact thing he ever said. When I didn't
know my own share was mixed into my love, I was a person who
persuaded. Once I knew, I couldn't be that anymore.

"…Then what am I supposed to do now." I asked.

Speon shrugged.

"…I don't know either. I'm a well-digger. Not a heart-digger."

"…You just dug one pretty well, though."

"…That was luck." he laughed. A laugh I hadn't seen before. "There's
only one thing I can tell you. Once you've made the way, you have to
wait and see whether the water comes. Digging doesn't make it come
right away."

I held onto that for a long time.

I learned that day, for the first time, that waiting and doing nothing
are different things. I'd thought of waiting as doing nothing, all this
time. It wasn't.

At midday I ran into the Curator. He was carrying papers, headed
somewhere.

"…Your expression is different from yesterday." he said.

"…How's it different."

"…Yesterday it was a face asking something. Today it's a face that's
learned something."

I was struck by that observation. This man had truly spent a long time
reading faces.

"…I learned something." I said. "Why I came here."

"…What is it."

I hesitated for a moment, then said,

"…Turns out I came for myself. I'd thought it was for my mother."

The Curator stopped walking. He looked at me for a long while.

"…It takes many people a long time to learn that." he said. "I learned
it too, doing this work. Even I'm not certain whether I became a
Curator purely for other people's sake."

I couldn't answer that at all. He left it there and walked on.

That was the first time I felt I understood a little of where this
man's sorrow came from. Every day he read, in people's faces, the same
thing I had seen in myself that day. A love with his own share mixed
into it. And even reading it, he kept doing the work.

I picked the shovel back up.

Moving my body helped a little. The earth grew familiar to my hands
again. The channel stretched a little farther downward.

From that day on, I never went to my mother again as a person trying
to persuade her. To be precise, I couldn't. Persuading is pretending to
act for someone else's sake while really driving them toward the
outcome you want. I now knew what I wanted. So I could no longer call
it something done for my mother's sake.

From that day I tried to become someone who went with her, not someone
who persuaded her. I would test the difference between those two, for
the first time, at my next visit.

That evening I met Eikon. She was sitting by the channel.

"…What did you dig up today?" she asked. She remembered what I'd said
the day before.

"…Myself." I said.

"…You dug up yourself?"

"…Yeah. Something a little shameful came out."

Eikon looked at me steadily.

"…What was shameful?"

I laughed for the first time at that.

"…I'll tell you later."

"…Is it a secret?"

"…No." I said. "I just haven't put it into words yet."

Eikon didn't find that answer strange. She didn't much understand why
a person would hide something. She had nothing to hide, herself.

"…Me." she said. "There isn't much I'm curious about. But I'm a little
curious what you dug up."

"…Why?"

"…I don't know." she said. "No reason."

I let that no reason pass without thinking much of it. Eikon said that
often. Being curious without giving a reason. I only thought it was a
turn of phrase she had.

Eikon stood up. She was about to go on her way, then turned back
suddenly.

"…Anemos." she said.

"…Yeah."

"…Wanting to take your mother with you — is that a bad thing?"

I couldn't answer that right away.

"…I don't really know." she went on. "I was just curious what it's
like, wanting something. Looking at you, you always seem like someone
who wants something."

"…It's not bad." I said. "But there's some of me mixed into it. I
learned that today."

"…So will you stop?"

"…No." I said. "That's not a reason to stop."

Eikon listened to that quietly. She didn't ask further and walked away.

I was left alone by the channel.

The sun had fully set. Only the sound of water remained. Listening to
it, I went back through, in order, everything I'd learned that day.
Speon's words, the seventeen roads walked alone, the hill when I was
nine, Eikon's logic and my own lack.

It's still strange to me that I learned all of that in a single day.
Maybe I had already known it, long before. It's only that the courage
to look at it came to me, that day, for the first time.

I opened the flask and drank a little water. It was still water I
couldn't give my mother. But that day the fact didn't weigh on me the
way it had before. For the first time, I simply let myself believe that
someday I would be able to give it to her.

That night, listening to the sound of the channel, I fell asleep for
the first time with my own share acknowledged. The shame was still
there. But the shame didn't stop me. The next day, I applied for
another visit. And I already knew what I would write in the purpose
box on the form.

(Continued in Chapter 39.)

Chapter 39. My Mother's Question

_Previously_ — I came to understand, myself, why I had come to this
city. Not only for my family, but to go back to the time when I
wasn't alone. With that share acknowledged, I applied for another
visit the next day.

In the morning my brother came looking for me.

Lately he often came and waited in front of the room where I was
staying. Not a visit — just there in the yard. He never came inside the
room itself. I didn't ask why.

"…Brother, are you going again today?" my brother asked.

"…Yeah."

"…Should I go too?"

"…No." I said. "I'll go alone today."

My brother nodded at that. No sign of hurt. Eight years old doesn't
hold onto hurt for long.

"…Tell Mom I said hi." my brother said.

Hearing that, I smiled a little. Well-being. I heard the word I would
write on the form that day from my brother, before I thought of it
myself.

I wrote well-being in the purpose box on the form.

Before, it had taken me forever to fill in that box. This time I didn't
hesitate. Well-being. That was the exact word. I wasn't going there
today to get anything.

The clerk took the form and looked at me.

"…You filled that in quickly today."

"…Yes."

I didn't explain further. The clerk didn't ask further either.

Sitting in the waiting room, I went back over what had happened the day
before.

Speon's words, the seventeen funerals, the hill when I was nine. I knew
all of it now. But I had no intention of laying it all out for my
mother today.

I couldn't have put that resolve into exact words, at the time. I can
say it now. That day, instead of going to say something, I wanted to go
to listen to something.

The clerk called my name.

My mother welcomed me as she always did.

"…Anemos."

"…Yes."

"…You've come again."

I knew a different answer to that greeting now. But I held that answer
back too. I just sat down.

My mother studied me for a moment.

"…Your expression is different again, today."

"…Different from yesterday too?"

"…Yes. Yesterday it was a face that had learned something. Today it's
just a face that's here."

Hearing that, I felt all over again how long this person had been
watching me. Someone who notices that my face is different every time.
I'd taken that for granted, all this time. It wasn't a thing to take
for granted.

I didn't talk about the valley, or about the water.

Instead I asked,

"…What did you do today?"

My mother paused at the question. Her face looked as though she'd been
asked something strange.

"…The same as always." she said. "Getting up, sitting, waiting for
you."

"…Is waiting something you do?"

"…Well." My mother smiled. "I'm not sure about that."

I liked that answer. If it had come before, I would have found it sad.
Today I just liked it. It had been a long time since my mother had said
I'm not sure that lightly.

I passed on my brother's message.

"…My brother said to tell you hello."

"…So he knows how to use words like that." my mother smiled. "Who
taught him?"

"…Me, probably."

"…That's not a phrase you used to use either."

I couldn't answer that. It was true. I had almost never used the word
well-being, all this time. I'd never had anyone whose well-being I
needed to ask after.

We sat for a while without saying much.

The room was always the same. The walls, the chair, the position of the
window. My mother had sat in that same spot in that room for seven
years now. With a face that was neither in pain nor bored. I wasn't
unfamiliar with that face anymore.

The silence wasn't uncomfortable. That struck me as strange. For seven
years, in this room, silence had always been a blank I had to fill.
Today it wasn't.

I still remember that silence exactly. Inside it, for the first time,
I was at ease with nothing prepared. That I could be here even with no
words ready, I learned that day, for the first time.

Through the window came the faint sound of water. The ditch Speon had
dug had reached this far by now. My mother listened to it for a moment.

"…That sound, it's often here lately." she said.

"…Yes. It keeps spreading."

"…There was a sound like that when I was young, too." my mother said.
"The well-bucket sound. Do you remember?"

I stopped at that. It had been a long time since my mother had brought
up her own childhood too.

"…I remember." I said. "My brother was scared of that sound."

"…That's right. And you used to let the rope down slowly on purpose."

"…That was a mean thing to do."

My mother laughed at that.

"…But he liked it later, didn't he."

"…Because when that sound came, it meant I was there."

Saying that, I surprised myself a little. I'd thought I had realized
that a long time ago, alone. And now here I was, saying it to my mother.

My mother looked at me again. That look in her eyes must have been the
difference the Curator had mentioned yesterday. I still couldn't have
named it, at the time.

"…Anemos."

"…Yes."

My mother was quiet for a moment. Her face was one of choosing
something. That was a face I'd never seen before, too. This person
always answered right away. She never chose her words.

I can still count that brief silence, even now. It wasn't long. A few
breaths' worth. But seven years fit inside those few breaths.

"…How have you been?"

I couldn't say anything at all, hearing that.

My mother was looking at me. Not a face asking a question back at me.
A face wanting to know.

In seven years I had come to this room more than thirty-two times.
In all those thirty-two visits, my mother had never once asked me that.
If I asked why, she asked why back. If I said I missed you, she said
me too. She always handed back whatever I'd said, unchanged. But not
today. Today she asked, herself.

"…I've been fine." I managed to say.

My voice came out strangely. I didn't know why, at the time.

"…Liar." my mother said. Not an angry tone.

"…What?"

"…I can tell from your face. That's not the face of someone who's been
fine."

I didn't know how to answer that. My mother didn't press for an answer.
She just waited.

"…It's been hard." I finally said. "A lot."

"…How."

Faced with that question, for the first time, I wanted to say it as it
was, not summarize seven years.

"…I was alone." I said. "That was the hardest part. Watching people
die was hard too, but harder than that was always having to walk home
alone afterward."

My mother listened to that quietly. She said nothing. But this time the
silence wasn't a blank to be filled. It was just a listening silence.

"…I learned to dig wells too." I went on. "At fourteen. From a
quiet old man. He stopped too. I started counting from his funeral, the
ninth. It's seventeen, up to now."

"…Seventeen." My mother repeated the number. "You've been counting all
of that?"

"…Yes. Counting became a habit."

"…So that's how you endured the hard parts." my mother said. "By
counting."

I couldn't answer that at all. It was the first time my mother had seen
my counting habit as a way of enduring. I had never called it that
myself, even to myself.

"…I'm sorry." my mother said, after a long while.

That surprised me.

"…For what."

"…For leaving you alone."

"…It's not your fault. You weren't sick."

"…Even so." my mother said. "Sorry is sorry."

I held onto that sentence for a long time. I had never once seen this
person apologize, all this time. The preserved don't feel regret. They
have no lack to regret. But my mother apologized, that day.

I didn't know how to answer that, so I just fiddled with the flask.

"…What about your brother." my mother asked. "Was it hard for him
too?"

"…He was younger than me, so he probably didn't understand much."

"…No." my mother said. "Even an eight-year-old understands. He's just
pretending not to."

Hearing that, I remembered my brother waiting for me in the yard. Tell
her I said hi. I felt, for the first time, that I understood a little
of what had been inside those words.

Near the end of visiting hours, my mother asked again,

"…So, how are you, now?"

"…Now?"

"…Yes. Today. This moment."

I thought about that for a moment.

"…I'm all right." I said. "Because I'm here."

Saying that, I felt, this time, that it was really true. It wasn't
words I'd prepared, and it wasn't said to reassure my mother. It was
just exactly what I felt in that moment.

My mother smiled at that. Not a large smile. But I could tell that
smile was different from before. Before, the smile had simply been
there. This time it had come from something.

Standing to leave, I gathered the flask back up.

"…I'll come again."

"…All right." my mother said. Then she added, "Tell me more about
yourself, next time."

I stopped at that.

Tell me more about yourself, next time. I repeated that sentence to
myself again and again, walking out the door. This person had never
asked anything of me in seven years. She'd had no reason to ask. But
today, for the first time, she asked.

At the door I turned back to look at my mother once more. She had
already turned her eyes to the window. Toward where the sound of water
was coming from.

I remember that back for a long time. The face looking out the window.
I had never once, all this time, seen my mother look out a window that
long. She'd had no reason to look. But that day she was looking at
something.

I ran into the Curator in the hallway. This time he didn't ask first.
He just saw me and nodded.

"…My mother asked me something today." I said first.

The Curator stopped walking.

"…What."

"…How I'd been."

He said nothing at all for a long while, hearing that. I saw something
surging, large, inside that silence.

"…I've never seen a record like that." he finally said. "I've done
this work a long time. This is the first time I've ever heard of one of
the preserved asking something, first."

"…What does this mean?" I asked again.

The Curator closed his eyes for a moment, then opened them. A face
choosing an answer.

"…I don't know exactly, myself." he said. "But if there's one thing
I've learned doing this work, it's that asking something means not
knowing something, and not knowing is something that doesn't happen to
the people here. That's the whole of what I know."

He left it at that and adjusted his grip on the papers. I felt that he
wanted to say more. He didn't.

I walked to the edge of the city, carrying those words with me.

The sound of the channel was running. I sat there, and went back over
my mother's question again. How have you been.

My brother was waiting for me. Crouched at the edge of the yard,
watching the channel.

"…Did you tell her?" my brother asked.

"…Yeah."

"…What did Mom say?"

I thought for a moment about how to answer.

"…She asked." I said. "How I'd been."

My brother tilted his head at that.

"…Doesn't she usually not ask that?"

"…Right. She usually doesn't."

My brother's face didn't understand why that was special. I started to
explain, then gave it up. It wasn't something I could explain to an
eight-year-old.

"…Should I ask you too?" my brother said. "You."

That made me laugh.

"…Sure. Go ahead."

"…How was your day, brother?"

"…It was good." I said. Not a lie.

My brother nodded, satisfied with that answer, and went back to
watching the channel. I sat beside him for a while.

That evening I told Speon about it.

He stopped mid-motion, putting the shovels away.

"…She asked, you said." he asked, as if to confirm.

"…Yes. For the first time."

Speon said nothing for a long while. Then, looking toward the ditch, he
said,

"…It's like this too, when water first starts moving underground. No
sound at all. Just a very small change of direction."

I started to ask exactly what he meant, then didn't. I felt I already
knew, without asking.

"…So what comes next." I asked.

"…I don't know that either." Speon said. "A change of direction
doesn't necessarily mean it makes it all the way here. But if the
direction doesn't change, it never comes at all."

He shouldered the shovel again.

"…Let's stop here for today." he said. "You should rest too."

I write down that question the most carefully of anything in this
record. I didn't fully understand, that day, what it meant. I only
knew: to be curious about something means something is lacking. And my
mother, for the first time in seven years, had come to know that there
was something about me she didn't know.

That night I tried not to think about it any further than that. If I
let it weigh too heavily, it felt like it might break. I decided just
to hold the question, quietly.

(Continued in Chapter 40.)

Chapter 40. Consent

_Previously_ — My mother asked me, for the first time, "How have you
been?" For the first time in seven years she was curious about
something. I decided to hold that question, quietly.

I only held onto that question for three days.

I didn't apply for a visit. My resolve not to persuade her held me back
this time in a different direction. I didn't want to rush the question
into something.

I still think, now, that those three days were the right thing. Some
things disappear if you check them too quickly. I was afraid that
question would disappear.

Over those three days I kept helping Speon dig the ditch.

The channel had now passed the halfway point of the city. Speon dug
alongside me without saying much. He glanced sideways at me sometimes,
but never asked what I was doing.

Only on the evening of the second day did he ask.

"…You still haven't gone."

"…No."

"…Why."

"…I'm not ready."

Speon set down his shovel.

"…Even after water changes direction, it takes time before it comes
out. If you keep tearing at the ground in the meantime, you only block
it. You have to know how to wait, too."

Hearing that, I felt a little relieved. It meant what I was putting off
might not be running away, but waiting. I fell asleep that night holding
onto those words.

On the fourth day, my brother pushed me himself.

"…Brother, when are you going again."

"…Why."

"…I miss Mom."

Hearing that, I knew I couldn't put it off any longer. I had been
spending those three days for myself. My brother hadn't.

"…All right. Let's go today."

We went together.

It had been a long time since my brother had walked beside me. As we
walked, he kept glancing sideways at the channel. I didn't ask whether
the sound of the water scared him. Instead I watched. He wasn't scared.

That day, for the first time, I wondered when my brother had stopped
being afraid of the sound of water. There was no way to find out.
Asking him wouldn't have told me either. Seven years changes a person
that way. Without the person even knowing it themselves.

Walking along, my brother suddenly asked,

"…Brother, what are you going for today?"

"…To see Mom."

"…Not that. Your face isn't the face of someone just going to see
someone."

That surprised me. I hadn't expected to hear something like that from
an eight-year-old.

"…I'm going to ask Mom something."

"…What?"

"…You'll find out when we get there."

My brother didn't press further. He just kept walking beside me.

At the front desk I hesitated a moment over what to write in the
purpose box on the form.

I wrote counsel.

I looked at that word a long while. Counsel is words exchanged to
decide something. For the first time, I had written the exact word.

My brother wrote his own name beside mine. His handwriting was
crooked.

My mother was glad to see the two of us.

"…You came together."

"…Yes." My brother answered first. "Brother brought me."

"…I was the one who said let's go." my brother added. "He was about to
go without me."

My mother laughed at that.

"…That sounds like you."

It had been a long time since I'd seen the two of them talk back and
forth so easily. I stayed a little back from it. Today I had something
else to say.

My brother talked about himself for a long while first.

"…The water's reached the big tree down there now." my brother said.
"I go every day to see how far it's gotten."

"…Is it fun?" my mother asked.

"…Yeah. Yesterday there was a little bug in the water. Mr. Speon says
that's a good thing. It means the water's alive."

"…Living water." my mother repeated the phrase. "That's an interesting
way to put it."

My brother kept going, excited. The nameless weeds blooming by the
ditch, the dry bread Speon sometimes gave him, how the sound of water
was like a lullaby at night.

My mother listened to all of it. Her eyes never left my brother.

Watching that, I felt all over again that this was how she had always
responded to my brother. Listening to a child's words all the way
through. I knew that wasn't a response born of lack. It was just my
mother.

When my brother's story wound down, I looked at my mother.

"…You asked me, last time." I said. "How I'd been."

My mother nodded.

"…I want to answer that properly, today."

My brother went quiet, reading the room. Eight years old, but he knew
when to do that.

This time I didn't summarize.

The winters at the settlement, the seventeen funerals, the well where I
drew water, the old man who taught me to dig. I told it in order. I
left some things out, but I didn't rush.

"…My uncle raised me." I said. "He wasn't a bad person. But since I
wasn't his own child, he only looked after me up to a point. I thought
that was just how it was."

"…It isn't how it should be." my mother said quietly.

"…I know that, now."

I went on — learning to dig wells at fourteen, the old man who'd been a
man of few words, that the ninth funeral had been his. My brother sat
quietly through all of it, just listening.

My mother never once interrupted. Only after hearing it all did she
ask,

"…So now."

"…Now I'm here." I said. "Because I want to see you and my brother
with living bodies again."

Even saying that, I tried to keep last month's self and this month's
self separate.

"…Before, I only thought it was for your sake." I said. "Now I know.
My own share is mixed into it too. I came here because I wanted to not
be alone again."

My mother wasn't surprised at that. Her face was one that had known for
a long time.

"…Is that a bad thing?" she asked.

"…I don't know. But I didn't want to hide it."

My mother looked out the window for a moment. Toward where the sound of
water was.

"…I've been thinking about it too." she said. "After you left, last
time, and the time after that, and the time after that. I kept
thinking."

"…About what."

"…This thing called release."

I stopped breathing at that. I had never brought that word up first.
Not this time either.

I still remember that moment exactly. I heard that word from my
mother's mouth for the first time. Not me — my mother said it first.

"…How did you know." I asked.

"…The Curator told me. That you'd asked, many times."

Hearing that, I thought of the Curator. He had never told me how my
mother was taking the idea. But apparently he had told her side of it.

"…Are you afraid?" I asked.

My mother thought about that for a moment.

"…I'm not even sure what afraid means, anymore." she said. "That's
probably the strange part."

In the meantime, my brother asked quietly,

"…What's release?"

"…It means becoming a living body again." I said. "Then you can get
sick again, and get hurt again."

"…So can you die again too?"

I couldn't answer that right away.

"…Yeah." I managed to say. "You could."

My brother listened to that quietly. He didn't ask anything further.

My mother looked at me again.

"…You came to ask me that, knowing all that?"

"…No." I said. "I didn't come to ask, today. I just came to answer
your question from last time."

My mother nodded at that.

"…But you ended up here anyway."

"…Yes."

The room was quiet for a while. Only the sound of water.

"…I don't." my mother finally spoke. "I don't really know what being
able to die again means. But watching you stand here talking about it,
it doesn't seem like a bad thing."

"…Is that the reason?" I asked.

"…Of course not." my mother laughed. "That's not a reason. It's just a
thought that came to me."

"…Then what's the reason?" I asked.

My mother looked at me for a long time. Then at my brother. Then at me
again.

"…I want to see the two of you standing here, side by side."

That was all. It wasn't a long speech. It wasn't an argument. The
moment I heard that sentence, I knew there was nothing left to ask.

I've copied that sentence out many times since. I tried to change it
into something better. I couldn't. That sentence was right exactly as
it was.

"…Are you consenting?" I asked. My voice shook.

"…Yes." my mother said. "I'll be released."

In that moment I didn't know what to say. I must have had words
prepared, but not one of them came to me.

In that silence I called up all seven years of visits at once. The
faces that had answered why with why. The answers that never changed.
And now, this one sentence, this moment. I understood that all of it
had been pointed toward this moment. But now that I had arrived, I
didn't even know whether I was supposed to feel glad.

My brother asked in my place.

"…So what happens now?"

"…There's a procedure." I managed to say. "They say it's simple."

I remembered what the Curator had told me. That one signature was
enough. That simplicity felt strange, somehow. That undoing seven years
could end with one signature.

"…So will Mom get sick again?" my brother asked.

I couldn't answer that right away.

"…I might." my mother answered in my place. "But that's all right
too."

"…Why is it all right?"

My mother stroked my brother's head.

"…Because being sick is being alive too."

My brother stayed quiet, his face unreadable — whether he'd understood
or not. I carved that answer into myself too.

We left the room and walked for a while without talking.

My brother took my hand. It had been a long time. He used to do it all
the time when he was little, but not these last few days.

"…Brother." my brother said. "Is Mom really going to live with us now?"

"…Yeah. That's how it'll be."

"…Then I want to do that too, later."

I stopped walking at that. My brother didn't seem to know yet what he
meant by it. I decided not to explain it, today.

"…Let's talk about that later." I said.

My brother nodded obediently.

Leaving the room that day, I looked for the Curator.

He was standing at the end of the hallway. It looked like he'd been
waiting.

"…My mother consented." I said.

The Curator nodded at that. Not a surprised face.

"…I knew." he said. "I've seen a few people come out with that face
before."

"…How many times?"

"…Not many." he said. "Truly not many."

"…What happened to them?" I asked.

The Curator hesitated a moment.

"…Most of them were released. After that, it's not our concern
anymore. Once they become living bodies again, they may leave this
city, or they may stay. That belongs to them."

"…Have some left?"

"…There have." he said. "I don't know how they went on to live. I want
to know, and at the same time I feel it might be better not to."

In those words I felt this man's old weariness again.

Walking back to my room with the procedure papers, I met Eikon.

"…Your face is different again today." she said.

"…Yeah. My mother consented."

Eikon stopped walking.

"…Really?"

"…Yeah."

She said nothing for a long while. I tried to read something in her
face. It wasn't envy. It wasn't fear either. Just a face weighing
something.

"…Wasn't she afraid?" she asked.

"…She said she wasn't sure what afraid even means."

"…Same with me." Eikon said. "Is that the same thing, that and this?"

I couldn't answer that right away.

"…I don't know." I said honestly.

Eikon nodded at that answer and went on her way.

That evening I told Speon the news too.

He was sitting by the ditch, watching the water flow.

"…She consented." I said.

Speon's face took a moment to take that in. Then he stood up and put
his hand into the channel.

"…I won't say congratulations." he said.

"…Why not?"

"…Because it's just the beginning. A well isn't finished just because
water comes out. That's when the upkeep starts."

I turned that over. It seemed right.

"…Still, rest a little today." he added. "Days like this don't come
often."

That night I thought back on that sentence again. I want to see the
two of you standing here, side by side. I know that isn't an argument.
If you ask why choose to become able to die again, this sentence isn't
an answer. And yet this sentence was enough.

Of everything in this book, this is the scene I rewrote the most,
while writing it. I wanted to put in a more plausible reason. Every
time, I deleted it again. My mother didn't argue that day. She simply
wanted.

Before I fell asleep that night I thought back, too, on what my
brother had said. I want to do that too, later. I hadn't made any
promise to that yet. I only knew that it was the thing I would have to
face next.

(Continued in Chapter 41.)

Chapter 41. The Release

_Previously_ — My mother consented. "I want to see the two of you
standing here, side by side." Not an argument. She simply said it.
The Curator began preparing the procedure.

The procedure was set for three days later.

The Curator said there was no need to rush, but no need to put it off
too long either. Something in the way he said it let me guess how many
times he'd already been through this.

I didn't count those three days. It was the first time I ever let a
number go.

Through those three days I helped Speon as usual.

If I wasn't working, other thoughts kept crowding in. Digging at the
earth was better than nothing.

"…Nervous." Speon asked.

"…Yeah."

"…What's the most nervous part."

I thought about that question for a long while.

"…That nothing will change." I said. "That after making a decision
this big, once the procedure's over, nothing will actually be
different."

Speon stopped digging at that.

"…No chance of that." he said. "Once water starts coming out, the
ground changes. Even if you can't see it from outside, the inside
changes. People are the same."

On the second day, my brother came and asked me,

"…Brother, is Mom going to get old now?"

"…Probably. That's just how people are."

"…So will Mom get older than me?"

"…She always was. She's the grown-up."

My brother thought about that answer for a long while.

"…I'm fine with it." my brother said. "Even if Mom gets old."

I could tell that wasn't a prepared line. It was just a mind an
eight-year-old had made up on his own.

On the evening of the third day, Eikon came looking for me.

"…Tomorrow, right." she said.

"…Yeah."

"…How do you feel?"

I tried to answer that honestly.

"…I don't really know. Kind of glad, kind of scared."

"…It's strange to me, that you feel both at once." Eikon said. "I only
ever feel one thing at a time. And not even that, often."

I turned that over for a long while.

The day before the procedure, I looked at the papers.

The Curator had shown them to me ahead of time. A single sheet. At the
top, my mother's name and registration number. At the bottom, a line
for a signature.

The content was short. The undersigned consents to the release of the
preservation state, and acknowledges and accepts all resulting physical
consequences thereafter — including illness, aging, and death.

I read that sentence over many times. The whole of this book was
inside that short sentence.

"…This is all of it?" I asked.

The Curator nodded.

"…That's all. There's no reason for it to be complicated. Perpetuity
doesn't judge a person. It asks no eligibility, no reason. If you want
it, this is enough."

"…Then why doesn't anyone do it?"

"…Because no one wants to." he said. "Not because there's anything
blocking them."

I brought the paper to my mother myself.

My brother came too. The air in the room felt different from usual. My
mother saw us come in and straightened where she sat. I felt something
even in that small motion. That this person was getting ready for
something.

My mother took the paper and looked at it for a long while. She didn't
rush.

"…Should I read it to you?" I asked.

"…No. I can read it myself."

My mother really did read it start to finish. I stood beside her
holding my breath.

"…Including illness, aging, and death." my mother read that part
aloud.

"…Yes."

"…This includes all of it."

"…Yes."

My mother set the paper down.

"…I understand."

That was all. She didn't ask anything else.

My brother couldn't bear the silence and asked,

"…Mom, are you scared?"

My mother looked at him and smiled.

"…No. But it is strange."

"…What's strange?"

"…That one sheet of paper decides something this big."

I couldn't answer that. I'd been thinking the same thing myself.

I lay awake again that night.

This time it wasn't anxiety. I just had two feelings at once — wanting
tomorrow to come quickly, and wanting it to come a little later.

That night, for the first time, I understood that those two feelings
weren't fighting each other. Wanting it to come quickly and wanting it
to come slowly were, in fact, two faces of the same feeling. Both came
from the same place: this moment mattering too much.

On the day of the procedure, the room was smaller than I'd expected.

One desk, three chairs. There was one window, facing the direction the
channel ran. I didn't think that was an accident.

The Curator set the papers on the desk.

"…Are you ready." he asked.

"…Yes." my mother said.

"…I'll confirm one thing again." he said. "This procedure cannot be
reversed. After release, you may apply for preservation again, but you
cannot return to exactly the state you are in now. Do you understand."

"…I understand."

"…And this procedure proceeds only by your own will. Here, in this
room, you are signing by your own will, not your family's. Do you
confirm this."

My mother looked at my brother and me for a moment.

"…I confirm it." she said. "This is my will."

I still remember those words exactly. All this time, I'd thought I was
the one leading this. But in that room, my mother said it plainly. This
is my will. In that moment I understood that I was only someone
standing beside her. And I liked that better.

I stood in that room with my brother.

No one came to celebrate. No one from the rest of the city, no one from
our settlement. Just the three of us, the Curator, and one sheet of
paper. That was all.

I didn't find that strange at the time. Thinking back on it now, it is
strange. That no one was there for something this large.

But now I know. This wasn't a thing to celebrate. It wasn't a thing to
grieve, either. It was just one person returning to her own body.

My mother picked up the pen.

Her hand didn't shake. That struck me as strange. That her hand
wouldn't shake, in front of a decision this big.

I only understood later. For a hand to shake, there has to be fear.
My mother didn't properly know that fear yet. Fear too was an emotion
you learn only once you become a living body again.

My mother wrote her name in the signature line.

The sound of the pen touching the paper was the loudest sound in that
room. Hearing it, I only then realized I'd been holding my breath.

Once she'd finished writing her name, my mother set the pen down.

"…It's done." she said. It sounded like something said to herself.

The Curator took the paper and checked it.

"…Confirmed." he said. "I'll begin the procedure now."

He opened a small panel on the wall. Inside was a single simple switch.

"…Is that all it is?" I asked.

"…Yes." he said. "Perpetuity isn't a complicated system. Preservation
and release are both simple. What's difficult isn't the procedure. It's
the decision."

He pressed the switch.

No sound came. No light. I'd expected something dramatic to happen.
Nothing happened.

Strangely, in that moment, I was disappointed. And I was ashamed of
that disappointment. What had I expected? Light bursting out, wind
blowing, some visible proof.

I set that disappointment down honestly, here. At that moment I still
wanted something dramatic. It was a feeling exactly opposite to what
this book means to say. Perpetuity does not seize a person with any
fanfare. Release does not let a person go with any fanfare either. Both
are quiet.

My mother closed her eyes for a moment. Then opened them.

"…How do you feel?" the Curator asked.

My mother couldn't answer that right away. She looked down at her own
hands.

"…Different." she finally said.

"…How?" I asked.

"…I don't know. It just feels different."

My brother took my mother's hand.

"…It's warm." my brother said. "It's different from before."

My mother looked at her own hand again, hearing that.

"…Was warmth always like this." she said, almost to herself.

Hearing that, I reached out and took my mother's hand too. It really
was warm. I had touched my mother's hand at every visit, all this time.
It had been warm then too. But today's warmth was different. I had no
words to explain the difference. It was just different.

The Curator spoke as he put the papers in order.

"…Your registration has now changed. You are no longer a subject of
preservation. You've been converted to a regular resident."

"…What does that mean?" my brother asked.

"…It means Mom's the same as us now." I said.

My brother beamed at that. My mother didn't smile. Her face wasn't
ready to smile yet.

We sat in that small room for a while, just as we were.

No one said congratulations. No one cried. I didn't think that was
strange. This wasn't that kind of thing.

I wanted to write this scene the most quietly of anything in this
book. That day, I truly didn't feel anything large. Joy, fear — those
came later. Inside that room there was just one sheet of paper, one
switch, and three people.

My brother broke that silence first.

"…Are we going home now?" my brother asked.

I stopped at that question. The word home sounded unfamiliar. We hadn't
had a home, all this time. The house at the settlement had passed into
other hands seven years ago, and in this city I had simply been living
in a room the Preserve had given me.

"…Yeah." I managed to say. "We'll have to find somewhere to live
together now."

Hearing that, my mother smiled a little, for the first time.

"…A home." she said. "That's a word I haven't heard in a while."

The Curator watched us as he gathered up the papers. His face carried
the same weary look as always. But something else was mixed into it
too. I couldn't read exactly what it was, at the time.

"…You're not saying congratulations." I said.

"…I don't know that I'm entitled to say something like that." he said.
"I'm the one who builds this procedure. Not the one who goes through
it."

"…Still, you must be glad today."

The Curator was quiet for a moment at that.

"…I am glad." he said quietly. "But it doesn't feel right to show that
too openly, here. This is your day, not mine."

I held onto that for a long time. This man handled even his own
gladness with care. I only understood later that it came from the same
place as the way he handled his sorrow.

The Curator opened the door for us and said,

"…You may go out now."

We left that room. Standing in the hallway, I looked at my mother. She
looked the same. But something was different.

I couldn't put that difference into words, at the time. I understood
it later. From that moment, my mother had become someone who could die
again. Nothing had changed on the outside. But that one fact changed
everything.

The three of us walked down the hallway side by side.

The sound of water came from outside the window. My mother turned her
head toward it.

"…I want to hear that sound up close." she said.

That surprised me. It had been a long time since my mother had said
she wanted something. Or maybe — it had been seven years.

"…Let's go." I said. "Let's get close to it."

We walked to the edge of the city. Our steps were slower than usual. My
mother didn't seem used to her own body yet. A walk that checked
something with every step, at every footfall.

My brother walked beside her, holding her hand. I walked on the other
side. It was the first time the three of us had walked side by side in
seven years.

I didn't count those steps. I didn't want to. Some things only get
smaller when you turn them into numbers.

The closer we got to the ditch, the louder the sound of water grew. My
mother stopped walking.

"…So this is it." she said.

Speon happened to be working on the channel there. He saw us and set
down his shovel.

"…I won't say congratulations." he said to my mother. "I'll give you a
mouthful of water instead."

He filled the flask with water and held it out to her.

My mother took the flask. She wrapped both hands around it.

In that moment I remembered what Speon had said to me a few days
before. I won't say congratulations. Because it's just the beginning. I
only understood then that those words were a principle of his. He must
have said them every time water first came out.

My mother stayed still for a moment, holding that flask. She didn't
drink. She just seemed to be feeling the weight of it in her hands.

I watched that from beside her. The sound of water kept flowing.

My brother said nothing, and neither did Speon. Everyone seemed to know
