that this was a moment that shouldn't be hurried. In that silence, I
thought of the water jar from seven years ago. The jar my mother had
filled and left behind before she went away. I hadn't drunk from it for
three days. Because drinking it meant it would be gone.

The water in my mother's hands now was water that could disappear and
it would still be all right. Because it could be drawn again. I thought
that difference might be the whole of everything that had happened.

(Continued in Chapter 42.)

Chapter 42. Breathing

_Previously_ — My mother was released. One signature, one switch. No
one celebrated and no one cried. Speon offered her a mouthful of
water. My mother stood holding the flask in both hands, not drinking.

My mother blinked.

Once. Twice. Three times.

I saw that too.

My mother moved her fingers, one by one.

Thumb, index, middle, ring, little.

They all moved.

"…They all work." she said.

The wind blew.

My mother drew her shoulders in.

"…Cold." she said.

That word startled me. I hadn't heard it once in seven years.

My brother took off his own coat and put it over my mother's shoulders.

It was too small to cover her fully. She left it on anyway.

"…Thank you." she said.

My mother raised the flask.

Brought it to her mouth.

Drank.

One mouthful.

Her throat moved. I watched that movement. A movement I hadn't seen in
seven years.

My mother took the flask away.

She coughed.

Once, twice, three times. Bending forward each time.

My brother, startled, patted her back.

"…Mom, are you okay?"

My mother raised a hand to say she was fine. She couldn't speak until
the coughing stopped.

Speon said quietly,

"…It's been so long, her throat's startled."

Hearing that, I looked at the flask again. It was half full.

The coughing stopped.

My mother steadied her breath. Breathed in deep, breathed out.

"…The taste of the water." she managed to say.

"…How is it?" I asked.

Instead of answering, my mother drank another mouthful.

This time she didn't cough.

Her throat moved again. A little bigger, a little easier.

My brother stared at that.

"…There's a sound in Mom's throat."

"…Her throat's remembering." Speon said.

My mother touched her throat.

"…It still feels strange." she said.

"…Does it hurt?" my brother asked.

"…No. Just unfamiliar."

One more mouthful.

This time no sound at all. Her throat just moved.

My mother felt that and opened her eyes wide.

"…There. Now it's working." she said.

My mother set the flask down.

Wiped her mouth with the back of her hand. A completely ordinary
motion. I was struck, rather, by that ordinariness.

I still remember that motion exactly. Wiping her mouth with the back
of her hand. A motion more ordinary than anything else, and it was the
biggest event of that day.

We sat down by the ditch.

Sitting, my mother swayed slightly. I reached out and caught her.

"…Are you all right?"

"…Yeah. My legs feel a little strange."

"…Strange how?"

"…Heavy." she said. "Having a body that's all mine — was it always
this heavy?"

My mother dipped her hand into the water.

"…Cold." she said.

"…Do you like the cold?" I asked.

"…I don't know. It's just cold."

My brother dipped his hand in too.

"…It's cold for me too."

"…Your hand already knew that." my mother said.

"…Didn't Mom's hand know?"

My mother paused for a moment at that. She pulled her hand out of the
water, then dipped it back in. Pulled it out, then dipped it back in.

"…I don't think I knew." she said.

The sound of water kept flowing. We sat for a while with our hands in
it, listening to that sound.

I didn't count how long that lasted. I didn't want to.

The sun fell across my mother's face.

She narrowed her eyes.

"…Bright." she said.

She raised a hand to shield the sun.

Under that shade of her hand, my mother closed her eyes for a moment.

Stayed still.

"…What are you doing?" my brother asked.

"…Just feeling it."

"…Feeling what?"

"…The sun."

The wind blew again.

This time my mother didn't flinch.

Her hair scattered in it. She swept it back with her hand.

Speon brought out a piece of dry bread.

"…You must be hungry." he handed it to my mother.

My mother took it and looked at it. Smelled it.

"…Was this what bread smelled like." she said.

She took a bite.

Chewed.

Chewed slowly.

My brother watched. No one rushed her.

She swallowed.

My mother stayed still there for a moment. A face checking something.

"…It's good." she said.

Her voice shook.

My brother laughed at that.

"…Mom's voice sounds weird."

"…It's not weird." Speon said. "It's shaking."

My brother's face didn't quite understand the difference.

My mother finished chewing the bread. Something glinted in her eyes.
Not tears. It might just have been the light catching them that way.

I still can't be sure whether it was tears or not. My mother didn't
cry that day. Her eyes were only a little wet. That's a different
thing.

Finishing the bread, my mother brushed off her hands.

"…Was this what being full felt like." she said.

"…Don't you remember?" I asked.

"…I remember. But feeling it is different."

There was a small scratch on the back of my mother's hand. A mark from
brushing against something on the way. She noticed it and pressed a
finger to it.

"…That hurts." she said quietly. Half surprise, half wonder, in her
voice.

My brother blew a breath on the scratch. A habit he'd had since he was
small.

"…It'll heal." my brother said.

My brother kept laughing beside her.

A laugh with no reason. A laugh that came just because things were
good.

My mother watched that laugh and laughed along with it.

A very small laugh.

Almost no sound at all. The corners of her mouth rose slightly, then
came back down.

I didn't miss it.

In that moment I found myself counting a number. I hadn't meant to. My
body counted before I could stop it.

Seven years.

It was the first laugh in seven years.

I couldn't say anything, right there. Not because my throat had closed
up. I simply had no word to call it.

My brother, not knowing what it was, kept laughing. The sound of that
laugh overlapped with the sound of the water. I heard both sounds at
once.

I write down that laugh the most carefully of anything in this book.
It wasn't a big laugh. There was almost no sound to it. The distance
her mouth moved was probably less than a finger's width. But I knew,
right there, that this one small movement was the whole of what I'd
been waiting seven years for.

I didn't cry. My mother didn't cry either. We just sat by the channel,
drank water, coughed, ate bread, and laughed. That was all. And because
that was all it was, that's the day I still go back to, more often than
any other.

My brother asked,

"…Laugh again, Mom."

My mother shook her head at that.

"…It doesn't work that way." she said. "You need something funny to
laugh."

My brother looked around, trying to find something funny at that. He
couldn't find anything. But the sight of him looking was funny enough
that my mother laughed a little again, once more.

The second laugh.

I counted that one too.

My mother yawned.

Covered her mouth with her hand. Then looked surprised.

"…That was a yawn, wasn't it." she asked.

"…Yes."

"…So this is what being tired is."

My mother rubbed her eyes.

"…I'm sleepy." she said.

That was another word I was hearing for the first time from her.

Standing up, my mother swayed again. This time my brother caught her
hand.

"…I'll help." my brother said.

It was as much help as an eight-year-old could give. Still, my mother
took that hand and stood.

The sun began to set. The sound of water carried more clearly in the
dark.

My mother raised the flask again and drank one more mouthful. This
time she didn't cough.

"…I think I'm getting used to it now." she said.

Walking back toward the room, my mother stopped a few more times.

Once she braced a hand against the wall.

Once she just stood and looked down at her own feet.

"…What are you looking at?" I asked.

"…It feels strange, my feet touching the ground."

Crossing the Preserve's yard, her foot caught on a stone.

My mother staggered slightly.

"…Ah." she made a small sound.

"…Are you all right?"

"…That hurt." my mother said. A surprised face. "So this is what it
means to hurt."

I couldn't answer that at all.

At the door of the room, my mother touched the doorframe with her
hand.

Ran her fingers along the wood grain.

"…Rough." she said.

Inside the room, my mother touched the blanket.

Pressed on it.

"…Soft."

My brother pressed the blanket the same way beside her.

"…I want to touch it too." my brother said. It was a blanket he'd
always touched. But today his face wanted to touch it together with
his mother.

That night the three of us slept in one room for the first time.

A room the Preserve had lent us temporarily. Cramped. But no one
complained about it.

My brother fell asleep first. My mother lay beside him, listening to
his breathing for a long while.

"…Anemos." my mother called my name.

"…Yes."

"…I didn't know a breathing sound could be this loud."

"…It was always like that."

"…But today it sounds different."

I didn't answer for a moment. My mother's breathing and my brother's
breathing sounded, in turns, through the room.

I felt I understood what she meant.

When the listener changes, the same sound is heard differently. I
learned that, that day. My mother had had no need, all those seven
years, to listen to my brother's breathing. A person with no need
doesn't listen. That night, for the first time, my mother needed that
breathing sound.

We laid out the bedding.

My mother was a little awkward about how to lie down. Not sure where to
put her arms, she shifted position a few times.

"…It wasn't like this before." she said.

"…Before, you didn't need to lie down."

"…That's true."

My mother wiggled her feet under the blanket.

"…My feet are cold." she said.

My brother pressed his own feet against hers.

"…My feet are warm."

"…So they are."

My mother put a hand to her chest.

Stayed still.

"…This is it beating, isn't it." she asked.

"…Yes."

"…It's fast."

"…Maybe it's surprised."

"…No." my mother said. "I think it's because it's glad."

My brother fell asleep first. His breathing evened out.

My mother listened to that breathing for a long while.

She reached out and touched my brother's forehead.

"…Warm." she said.

I lay down beside them too.

Three breathing sounds filled the room. I didn't count them.

That night, for the first time, I set the habit of counting down
completely. It was all right not to count. Not because there was no
need to count, but because I simply didn't want to turn that moment
into a number.

The sound of water kept running outside the window. I listened to it
last, and fell asleep.

In the morning I woke to find my mother already awake.

Sitting up, looking down at her own hands.

"…Did you sleep well?" I asked.

"…I don't know. I saw something strange."

"…What was it?"

"…A dream."

I sat up at that.

"…What kind of dream was it?"

"…I don't remember." my mother said. "I just know I saw something."

Hearing that, I was quietly astonished. The preserved don't dream.
They have no need to. That morning, for the first time in seven years,
my mother had dreamed. And she couldn't remember the dream. I thought
even that was enough.

My mother looked at her own hand again. Ran a finger along the lines of
her palm.

"…It's like I'm seeing this for the first time too." she said.

"…It was always there." I said.

"…I know. But it feels like I'm seeing it for the first time, today."

My mother stretched.

Something in her body cracked.

My brother opened his eyes at the sound.

"…What was that sound?"

"…It came from Mom's body." my mother said. A wondering face.

Her stomach made a sound.

My mother pressed a hand to it.

"…Is this something new too?" I asked.

"…Yes." she said. "Was being hungry always something that made a sound
too?"

We got up and opened the door to the room.

The morning sun came in.

My mother walked out into that light. One step, one step, a little
lighter than yesterday.

At the edge of the yard, my mother stopped and looked toward the
channel. The sound of water had kept flowing through the night without
stopping.

"…I guess it ran all night." I said.

"…I suppose it did." my mother said. "Without even sleeping."

"…Water never sleeps."

My mother nodded at that. Then she took a deep breath. It looked as
though I could see the morning air going into her lungs.

I watched her back.

(Continued in Chapter 43.)

Chapter 43. My Brother, Not Yet

_Previously_ — The morning after my mother was released, we went out
to the yard and looked at the water channel. My mother took a deep
breath. I watched her back.

The three of us spent those two days in the room the Preserve had lent
us.

We hadn't even started looking for a house yet. My mother's body still
felt strange to her, and moving any distance was hard. I spent that
time helping Speon with the ditch, and my brother played beside it.

I think those two days were the most ordinary two days in this whole
book. Nothing big happened. And it was exactly that ordinariness that
felt strange. I couldn't even remember the last time the three of us
had spent two days like that, with nothing happening at all.

Two days later, my brother asked.

"…Can I do it now too?"

I had been waiting for that question. And now that I heard it, I
didn't know how to answer.

"…Do what." I asked back. A question meant to buy time.

"…Release."

The three of us were sitting in the yard. My mother spoke first.

"…Why do you want to?"

"…I want to be like you and Mom."

"…Be like us how?"

My brother thought about that for a moment.

"…Sick." my brother said. "You said it yourself. Being sick is still
being alive."

I remembered that I had said that myself, a few days ago. I hadn't
expected my own words to come back to me like this.

"…Is being sick a good thing?" I asked.

"…I don't know. But when Mom was sick, I want to be sick too."

Hearing that, I felt my breath stop. My brother hadn't meant it badly.
It was only a way of saying he wanted to be with us.

I remembered how my brother used to be afraid of the sound of water
when he was small. The rope of the well-bucket unspooling, and the
long silence in between. I remembered once deliberately unspooling it
slower, just to scare him more. My mother had seen it and hit me on
the back. Why would you make something scarier when it already scares
him.

I bring up that memory here for a reason. That day, I didn't want to
make the same mistake in front of my brother. I didn't want to make
something frightening more frightening. But at the same time, I didn't
want to rush something he wasn't ready for either. I swung a long time
between those two.

"…But how is that different from you saying, if you do it, then I
will?" I asked.

My brother's eyes went wide at that.

"…How's it different?"

"…Before, when I asked you, you said if I do it, then you would too.
Isn't this the same thing now? Don't you want it just because your
brother and Mom did it?"

My brother thought about that for a long while.

"…No." my brother said. "Back then it was because I thought you'd like
it. Now I want it myself."

I couldn't tell whether that distinction was real. I couldn't tell
whether an eight-year-old could really make that distinction at all.

In that moment I thought back to Part Two. When my brother said if I
do it, then I will too, I had judged that this was not consent but
obedience, and withdrew the application myself. I still believe that
judgment was right. And now, today, I had to make the same judgment
again.

There was one difference from that time. Back then I had judged it
alone. Now my mother was beside me. I thought that was a good thing. I
didn't want to make this decision by myself.

My mother looked over at me. Her face was thinking the same thing I
was. We confirmed it between us without a word.

My mother sat my brother on her knee.

My brother shifted a little in her lap.

"…So Mom says no too?" my brother asked. His voice had hurt in it.

"…It's not no." my mother said. "It's not yet."

"…What's the difference between not yet and no?"

My brother asked that with a serious face. I saw something in that
face that didn't belong to an eight-year-old. And at the same time, I
saw a stubbornness in it that belonged entirely to one.

My mother was quiet for a moment at the question.

"…Not yet changes, when enough time passes." she said. "No doesn't
change."

"…I think I understand why you want this." she said. "But this is a
different kind of thing."

"…How's it different?"

"…This can't be undone." my mother said. "You're eight years old right
now. But after release, you'll grow up again. Nine, ten, and on. You
don't know yet what it means to grow older."

"…Does my brother know?" he asked.

"…Your brother already grew up that way." my mother said. "You
haven't, yet."

My brother turned that over.

"…So is getting older a bad thing?"

"…No." my mother said. "It's not bad. It's just something you can't
know until you've lived through it."

I listened to that conversation beside them and thought of the
Curator's words.

The consent requirement. Release requires the consent of the
preserved person themselves. I had heard the word consent many times
by then. But it was in front of my brother that I first felt, all the
way through, how heavy that word was. To consent means you have to
know what it is you're consenting to.

"…It's not." I said carefully, "that I'm saying no because you're
eight. It's just that I'm not sure yet whether you really understand
what you want, and really want it."

"…I understood it." my brother said.

"…Understood what?"

"…That I can get sick."

"…That you can die, too?"

My brother had no answer to that at all.

I set that silence down here honestly. I took the silence as proof
that my brother didn't understand it yet. And at the same time, I knew
that even adults don't fully understand death either. I had watched
seventeen funerals and I still don't know.

After a long while, my brother spoke again.

"…What happens when you die?"

I didn't know how to answer that question. My mother seemed the same.

"…I don't know." I said, honestly. "Nobody knows for sure. Even
grown-ups don't."

"…You don't know either?"

"…No. I don't know either."

That answer seemed to put my brother a little at ease, oddly.

And still, I had to make a decision right there. Complete
understanding couldn't be the standard. But some minimum was needed.
My brother didn't seem to have reached even that minimum yet.

"…Brother." my brother said. "I still want to."

"…I know." I said. "But not now."

"…Why."

"…I want to wait until you can be asked again, later, and really,
truly want it for yourself."

My brother's face went dark.

"…I don't like waiting."

"…I know. I didn't like it either."

"…Then why are you making me wait?"

I couldn't answer that right away. My mother answered instead.

"…Because you're precious." she said. "You don't rush what's
precious."

My brother's face still wasn't fully convinced by that.

But he didn't push any further either. He just sat quietly and looked
at the channel.

I don't think that silence was consent. It was just an eight-year-
old's kind of acceptance. And I needed to be clear, that day, that
acceptance and consent are not the same thing.

I sat down beside my brother.

"…Instead." I said. "I'll ask you again every year. Whenever you want
it."

"…Every year?"

"…Yes. Every birthday."

My brother brightened a little at that.

"…So you'll ask me again on my next birthday?"

"…Yes."

"…Promise?"

"…Promise."

My brother held out his little finger. I hooked my finger through his.
A promise from when we were small. I noticed, all over again, how
small that finger was.

"…Mom too." my brother said.

My mother hooked her finger in as well. The three of our fingers
tangled together at once.

I know that pinky promise is a completely different kind of promise
from signing a document. It had no legal force at all. But I don't
think it was lighter than the document. I think, if anything, it was
the opposite.

I wrote this scene more carefully than any other in this book. I felt
the temptation, more than once, to resolve this cleanly — to have my
brother suddenly say something wise beyond his years and convince me,
or to have me convince him with some great argument. I didn't write
either of those scenes. They didn't happen in fact, either.

This book doesn't resolve everything. My brother has not been
released. I believe that was the right decision. And at the same time,
I know that belief could be wrong. I close this chapter holding both
of those at once.

In the afternoon I saw Eikon. I told her about it.

"…My brother said he wanted to be released. I told him no."

Eikon tilted her head at that.

"…Why? Your brother and your mother got to."

"…He's eight. I'm not sure he really understands and really wants it."

"…I don't understand that." she said. "If you want something, don't
you just want it? Why does understanding have to come into it?"

I couldn't answer that right away either.

"…Some things are dangerous to want without understanding." I managed.
"Especially things you can't undo."

"…There's plenty I don't understand either." Eikon said. "But I've
never really wanted much of anything. So I don't really understand
that danger you're talking about."

Something went cold in me hearing that. Eikon had nothing to want, so
there was no danger for her either. My brother had something to want,
and that was exactly what made it dangerous. I confirmed again, right
then, that this difference split their two worlds completely apart.

"…What about you, Anemos." Eikon asked. "Did you understand and want
it? Coming here."

I stopped for a moment at that.

"…No." I said. "I didn't come here with full understanding either. But
I'm an adult. So I carry the responsibility for my own choice, myself."

"…And your brother can't carry it?"

"…Not yet."

Eikon listened to that answer quietly and went on her way.

That evening I told the Curator about it.

"…You did well." he said. "I've seen the opposite far more often — a
parent deciding for a child instead of the child."

"…I'm not his parent."

"…And yet you're standing in that place right now."

I had no answer to that.

"…Could I." I asked. "Could I be wrong about this?"

The Curator thought for a moment.

"…You could." he said. "But I also know it was the best decision you
could make right now. The best isn't always right. But it's better
than what isn't the best."

"…And if I regret it, years from now?"

"…Then you decide again, when that time comes." he said. "If there's
one good thing about this line of work, it's that a decision you
couldn't make today, you can make again tomorrow. Unlike death."

I held on to that for a long time.

I ran into Speon on the way back.

"…Your face is heavy." he said.

"…My brother said he wanted to be released. I told him no."

Speon set down his shovel and looked at me.

"…Good."

"…How can you say that so fast?"

"…I had something similar happen once, a long time ago." he said.
"There was a kid who wanted to learn to dig wells way too early. I
told him no. He was hurt by it."

"…What happened after that?"

"…He came back a few years later. By then he was really ready."

"…What happened to that kid?"

"…He became a well-digger." Speon said. "Like you."

I laughed a little at that.

"…So all I have to do is wait, then."

"…I told you before, waiting isn't the same as doing nothing." he
said. "You're waiting right now. That's work too."

That night I lay awake and listened to my brother's breathing for a
long while after he fell asleep.

His breath was even and slow. I tried not to count it. My body counted
anyway. One, two, three.

My mother was listening beside him too.

"…When will the right time be?" I asked.

"…I don't know." my mother said. "But when it comes, I think we'll
both know."

I decided to believe that.

The next morning, my brother drew something on the wall of the room.

One small line. About the width of a finger's joint.

"…What's that." I asked.

"…A mark." my brother said. "I'm going to count how many days until my
next birthday."

I looked at that line for a long while.

I had always thought I was the only one who counted. That day, for
the first time, I found out my brother had started counting too. Only
what he counted wasn't something that had already passed — it was
something still coming. I thought that difference mattered. I counted
to hold on to what had slipped away. My brother counted to wait for
what hadn't arrived yet.

"…Do you want me to help?" I asked.

"…No." my brother said. "I'm counting this one by myself."

I said nothing back to that. It felt like the right answer.

From then on, my brother drew one more line every morning. Every time
I passed that wall, I counted the lines with my eyes. Once I'd
counted, I never said the number out loud. That number belonged to my
brother.

That year, his birthday was still many months away. I didn't know how
many lines that wall would end up holding. But I knew that each one of
those lines was a way for us to wait.

Writing this chapter, I thought more than once about other endings.
An ending where my brother finally wins the argument and gets
released. An ending where my brother changes his own mind. I wrote
neither. I understood that this isn't a story that ties off every
thread before it ends. Some threads carry over, unresolved, to the
next page.

My brother, that night, and even now as I write this record, is still
a preserved body. Not released. Still eight years old. I don't think
that's a sad thing. There is simply a time that hasn't come yet.

(Continued in Chapter 44.)

Chapter 44. What the Names Mean

_Previously_ — My brother wanted release, but I judged that an
eight-year-old couldn't truly give that consent, and put it off. We
made a pinky promise that I would ask him again every year. The
Curator told me it was the best decision I could make.

Around that time, it had become a habit for us to gather by the
channel each evening.

Me and my brother, sometimes my mother, and Eikon too. Speon always
sat there watching the water run for a while after he finished work,
before he left. That evening was no different. My brother had already
gone in to the room, and my mother, still tired, had gone to rest
early. Only Eikon, Speon, and I were left.

One evening, sitting by the channel, Speon said out of nowhere.

"…Do you two know what your names mean."

Eikon and I looked at him at the same time. We had been sitting there
together.

"…They mean something?" Eikon asked.

"…They do."

I had wondered about that question for a long time. But I had never
asked. I didn't know why myself.

Not from the settlement's paperwork, not from any of the grown-ups
there — I had never once heard what my own name meant. I had just
assumed my parents picked it because it sounded nice. It was the same
with Eikon's name. It had never even occurred to either of us to ask
the other.

I know now. I was afraid to hear the answer. If a name has a meaning,
it means someone made me for that meaning. I didn't know who that
someone was. Not knowing was what frightened me.

"…Tell us." I said.

Speon looked out at the channel for a moment. His face was choosing
something.

"…Anemos." he said. "In an old, old language, it means wind."

"…Wind?"

"…Yes. Not just wind. The thing that cannot be kept. The thing that
can't be held in a container. That meaning comes bundled in with it."

I said nothing at all for a long while after hearing that.

The thing that cannot be kept. In that moment I knew I had lived that
phrase across this entire book. I was a counter. Counting is an
attempt to hold on. I had spent my whole life trying to hold on to
something. And my name had been telling me, from the very start, that
I was a person who could not be held.

"…You don't know who gave it to me?" I asked.

"…I don't know that either." Speon said. "That's how names are. They
outlast the one who gave them."

I didn't ask anything further after that answer. It felt like asking
wouldn't help.

I looked down at the channel. Water and wind shared that much, at
least — neither could be held in a container. In all my time beside
Speon learning to dig for water, I had heard, more than once, that
standing water rots. That it has to flow to stay alive. I thought my
own name might have come from that same place. Something that must
not be kept. Something that has to flow.

In that moment, for the first time, I stopped being ashamed of my
name. I had blamed myself, more than once, for being the kind of
person who couldn't stay close to his family for long. I had even
wondered whether leaving the settlement and coming all this way was
just some restlessness in my nature that couldn't stay in one place.
Hearing the name, I found myself thinking it wasn't a flaw — I had
simply been born with a name like that, and that was all.

"…What about me?" Eikon asked.

Her voice sounded exactly the same as always. I didn't even notice,
then, that it sounded exactly the same as always.

Speon looked at her. He paused for a moment.

"…Eikon." he said. "It means image. Or likeness."

"…What's that mean?" Eikon asked.

"…A form made in the shape of something else." Speon said. "Something
copied over exactly from a thing that already exists."

Eikon's face turned that over.

"…A copy of what?"

Speon didn't answer that question.

I felt something unfamiliar in that silence.

Eikon seemed to feel it too. She asked again.

"…I'm a likeness of something?"

"…That's just what the name says." Speon said carefully. "A name
doesn't necessarily explain the whole of a person."

"…Then why would someone give a name like that?"

Eikon asked it that way. For the first time, I heard something shake a
little in her voice, in that question.

I heard that shake and, even then, didn't know exactly what it was.
It was the first time I'd ever heard Eikon's voice shake.

Speon didn't answer.

Eikon started to ask something more into that silence, and stopped.

She opened her mouth, then closed it again.

I watched that beside her.

It was the first time I had ever seen Eikon fail to answer something.
She always had an answer. I don't lack anything. What do you mean. Why
would I need more. Her answers had always come fast and clear.

That day was not like that.

I write this moment more carefully than any other in this book. I
don't want to attach any explanation to that silence. Eikon heard the
meaning of her own name, and had no reply to it. That's all there is.

The sound of water filled the silence. No one tried to break it.

After a long while, Eikon finally spoke.

"…I just." she said. "I feel kind of strange."

"…Strange how?" I asked.

"…I don't know."

That answer was unfamiliar too. Eikon was a girl who almost never said
I don't know.

Speon quietly got to his feet.

"…Let's leave it here for tonight." he said. "This kind of thing
doesn't get taken in all at once."

He gathered his shovel and left. Only Eikon and I remained.

We sat without speaking for a while.

The sound of water kept running. I didn't know what to say. Ordinarily
Eikon would have been the one to ask something light first, but that
night she was quiet too.

"…Anemos." she said, after a long while.

"…Yeah."

"…How did you feel, hearing what your name meant?"

I thought about that question for a moment.

"…Strange. But the strangeness wasn't bad."

"…For me." Eikon said. "It's strange, but I don't even know if this
is a bad thing or not."

"…Is this the first time?"

"…First time for what?"

"…This feeling of not knowing."

Eikon went quiet again for a long while at that question.

"…I don't know." she said, finally. And she laughed a little at her
own words. Something like a bitter laugh. "I don't even know how many
times I've said I don't know, by now."

I said nothing back to that. Not because I had nothing to say, but
because it felt like silence was the right thing for this moment.

Eikon leaned a little toward the channel.

She looked down at her own face reflected on the water. The current
kept the reflection shaking.

"…Is this me too?" she asked.

"…What is?"

"…The one in the water."

I didn't know how to answer that.

"…Probably close." I said. "But it's water, so it keeps shaking. Your
real face doesn't shake."

Eikon heard that and touched the water lightly with a fingertip. The
reflected face shook harder for a moment, then settled again.

"…Is that what a likeness is?" she asked. "Something that shakes."

"…I don't know." I said. "You'd have to ask Speon."

"…Speon didn't answer either."

I had no answer to that. She was right.

"…You know." Eikon said again, after a long while. "I've never once,
in my whole life, felt like I lacked something. You know that,
Anemos. You've heard me say it, always."

"…Yeah. I know."

"…But right now." she said. "I don't even know if this is lacking
something or not. Whether it's because the name is strange, or
whether something that was already there is only showing itself now."

That distinction was foreign to me. I had always thought lack was just
something you knew. When you're hungry, you know you're hungry. But
Eikon, right now, wasn't even sure what it was that she felt.

In that moment, for the first time, I thought that lack might not
arrive all at once. Speon had once told me it takes time even for
water to change direction underground. I remembered that, right then.
Maybe a person's heart worked the same way.

"…So will I change too, someday?" Eikon asked. Not looking at me — at
the water.

I couldn't answer that right away.

"…I don't know." I said, honestly. "But the fact that you're asking a
question like this tonight — that wasn't true even yesterday."

Eikon said nothing back to that. She just kept looking down at the
water.

Writing this chapter, I felt the temptation more than once to attach
some great meaning to Eikon's silence here. That this was her first
lack. That this was the beginning of her changing. I decided not to
write it that way.

I only set down what I saw, in that place, that day. Eikon heard the
meaning of her own name. And for the first time, had no reply to it.
What that meant, neither she nor I knew.

The sun went down. Eikon stood.

"…I'll go on ahead." she said.

"…Okay."

She walked a few steps, then turned back.

"…Anemos."

"…Yeah."

"…Say my name's meaning once more? Exactly what it was."

"…Image." I said. "Likeness."

Eikon mouthed the words after me. No sound, just the shape of it on
her lips.

Then she walked on.

I watched her back for a long while. I knew, that day, that I had
witnessed the quietest crack in this whole book. Where that crack led,
I didn't know yet, then.

I went back to the room and told my mother about it.

She was sitting on the bedding, mending my brother's clothes. Her hand
was still a little unpracticed at moving the needle.

"…Speon told me what my name meant today." I said.

"…What did he say?"

"…Wind. The thing that can't be kept."

My mother's hand stopped.

"…Your father gave you that name." my mother said.

I was startled by that. It had been a very long time since my mother
had brought up my father.

"…My father did?"

"…Yes. I never knew the meaning myself. He just always said it was a
good name."

"…Why that name of all names, do you think."

My mother thought for a moment, then smiled.

"…Your father was another one who could never stay in one place for
long. That was probably why."

Hearing that, I felt, for the first time, that I knew my father a
little better as a person. I knew almost nothing about him. All I had
ever known was that he'd disappeared when I was five.

"…Did you ask about Eikon's name too?" my mother asked.

"…Yes. It means image. Likeness."

My mother's hand stopped again.

"…That's a different kind of meaning."

"…Yes. She had no answer at all when she heard it."

My mother stopped her sewing for a long while at that.

"…So that's the first time she's ever said she didn't know
something."

I was startled by that. My mother had barely even met Eikon, and yet
she'd named it exactly.

"…How did you know?"

"…Because I was the same." my mother said. "When you asked me that
question, I couldn't say anything either. At first."

Hearing that, something opened up in me all at once.

In that moment I set my mother and Eikon side by side. Both of them
had been women who lacked nothing. Both of them had, at some moment,
met the first time they had no answer to give. My mother changed after
that. Eikon does not know yet.

That day, for the first time, I saw that these two stories held the
same shape. Only my mother's story had already turned the page, and
Eikon's story had only just turned its first.

The next morning, I went to find the Curator and told him about it.

He stopped, mid-paperwork.

"…The meanings of your names." he said. "Who told you?"

"…Speon."

The Curator nodded. There was no surprise on his face.

"…I already knew the meanings of both your names." he said. "They're
written in the registry from the start. I simply didn't think it was
my place to be the one to tell you first."

"…Why not?"

"…The meaning of a name belongs to the one who gave it, not to the one
who keeps the record." he said. "I'm only the one who preserves it."

Something in that answer caught at me.

"…Eikon had nothing to say, when she heard hers." I said.

The Curator set down his pen at that.

"…I thought that might happen." he said quietly.

"…You knew?"

"…I guessed." he said. "I've been reading her file for seven years.
In that whole record, there was never once a moment when she had no
answer to something. Today will be the first."

"…What does that mean?"

The Curator looked out the window, toward the channel.

"…I don't know exactly, myself." he said. "But I've learned one thing,
doing this work. Sometimes, not being able to answer isn't because
there's no answer. It's because the answers have started becoming too
many."

I held on to that for a long time.

That evening I saw Eikon again by the channel.

She looked much like her usual self. She smiled, said hello, asked me
what I'd done that day. I was a little reassured by that ordinariness.

But before she left, she asked.

"…Anemos."

"…Yeah."

"…I said my name over a few more times, when I was alone. Image.
Likeness."

"…And?"

"…It's still strange." she said. "But less strange than yesterday."

I couldn't tell whether that was a good sign or a bad one.

"…Is that it getting better?" I asked.

"…I don't know." Eikon said. "But I was glad you asked me about it.
Better than holding it by myself."

She left those words and walked on. I watched her back. The same
walk as yesterday. But I knew now that something was following behind
that walk.

That night, I thought again, side by side, about my name and hers.
Wind is wind because it cannot be held. Image is image because it
carries over something that already exists. I was born a person who
cannot be held, and she was born a person carrying something within
her. Why those two names came, of all names, to the two of us — that
night, I still didn't know.

(Continued in Chapter 45.)

Chapter 45. Eikon's Postponement

_Previously_ — Speon told us what our two names meant. Anemos: wind,
the thing that cannot be kept. Eikon: image, likeness. Hearing the
meaning of her own name, Eikon had no answer for the first time. That
night I thought about our two names, side by side.

For several days after that, Eikon went on exactly as she always had.

She came to the channel at the same time, sat in the same spot. Her
laugh was the same. I found that, oddly, stranger than if something
had changed. I had expected something big to shift. Nothing shifted
much at all.

I understood something for the first time, then. A person doesn't
change all at once. I had been thinking of this story as one racing
toward a turning point. It wasn't. Some changes arrive at a speed too
slow to see.

The channel kept growing, those same days.

It had crossed half the city and was nearing the edge now. I helped
Speon dig, and came back each evening to the place where the water
sounded. Day to day it all looked the same. But I knew the color of
the soil changed a little each day. To the eye it was still the same
brown, but under the hand there was more moisture in it, little by
little. Speon had taught me how to measure that moisture — by
fingertip, by smell, by weight.

I think Eikon was measuring something the same way. A moisture
invisible to the eye, by fingertip. I didn't understand that clearly
at the time. I only had the sense that something was changing.

My brother went on drawing one line on the wall each of those days
too. I counted the lines every time I passed. Twelve, thirteen,
fourteen. I set that number beside the length of the channel and
looked at the two together. Both were growing a little every day, too
slowly to see.

Still, there were small things.

One day Eikon asked me.

"…What did you do yesterday, Anemos?"

I stopped short at that question. Eikon rarely asked things like that.
She'd always said there was little she was curious about.

"…Dug the ditch. Why?"

"…No reason." she said. "I just wanted to ask."

This time I couldn't let that no reason go by unremarked, the way I
once would have.

Another day she asked this.

"…If I don't lack anything, how can something start making me
curious?"

I didn't know the answer to that.

"…I think you'd know that better than me."

"…I don't know either." Eikon said. "That's why I'm asking."

I turned that answer over for a long time. I had failed to answer
Eikon's questions plenty of times before. But that day was different.
That day, she was asking a question knowing that even she didn't know
its answer.

There was another moment, too.

Eikon didn't come one evening. I worked on the channel longer than
usual, waiting for her. She didn't appear until nearly an hour had
passed.

"…You're late." I said.

"…Yeah." she said. "I was just sitting in my room."

"…Doing what?"

"…Nothing." she said. "I just sat there. I wanted to."

That answer was unfamiliar to me. Eikon had never spent time that way
before. If there was something to do, she did it; if not, she simply
moved on to the next thing without a second thought. Wanting to do
nothing, and doing nothing because of it — I had always thought that
wasn't a thing that happened to someone who lacked nothing.

That day, for the first time, I noticed she had begun spending more
time alone. A person seeks solitude when there's something to sort
through. I'd learned that outside. Someone with nothing to sort
through has no reason to be alone either.

The Curator called me aside around that time.

"…It's about Eikon." he said.

"…What is it?"

"…Nothing's wrong." he said. "But I've been watching her file, and in
the last few days her rate of questions has gone up. We keep records
of that sort of thing."

"…What does that mean?"

The Curator thought for a moment.

"…I've done this work a long time." he said. "Lack usually arrives
this way. Not as one big event, but as questions accumulating one by
one. I call it the way water seeps in. Not pooling all at once, but
drop by drop."

I thought of Speon, hearing that phrase. Two people I'd met in this
city, saying the same thing in different places.

"…So what do we do now?" I asked.

"…Nothing." the Curator said. "We never ask first. Not until the
person wants it themselves. That's the one principle of this whole
procedure. Never rush."

"…Why be so careful about it?"

"…Because once you ask, it can't be undone." he said. "Being asked, in
itself, becomes pressure on a person. I don't want to put that
pressure on her. Waiting is our work too."

"…Then why are you telling me this?"

"…Because you're family, Anemos." he said. "I thought family should
know first. What comes after that is hers alone."

I thought, hearing that, about whether Eikon was someone I could
really be called family to. No answer came. But I didn't dislike the
word.

That evening I passed the news on to my mother.

She had been moving her body a little more easily these days. She
could walk out to the yard on her own now.

"…Eikon's been asking a lot more questions lately." I said. "The
Curator says that's how lack arrives."

My mother's hand stopped at that. She was still mending my brother's
clothes these days.

"…Do you want to help her?" she asked.

"…I don't know. I don't even really know what helping would mean."

"…For me." my mother said. "When you came to me, the hardest thing was
doing nothing at all. You wanted to do something, anything. But I
wasn't ready, then."

Something clicked in me at that.

"…So should I do nothing with Eikon?"

"…Being there beside someone and doing nothing aren't the same thing."
my mother said. "That's something I learned."

A few evenings later, Eikon spoke first.

"…Anemos." she said. "Can I be released too?"

I stopped digging.

"…You can. You're fifteen. It's not like my brother."

"…Then all I need is to consent?"

"…Yes."

Eikon didn't say anything right away, hearing that. She looked down at
the channel for a long while.

"…Are you scared?" I asked.

"…I don't really know what scared is, yet." she said. "But it's
different from before. Before, that word had nothing to do with me at
all. Now it's doing something to me."

I started to ask exactly what that difference was, and stopped
myself. She didn't seem able to explain it yet either.

"…Why did Anemos's mother do it?" Eikon asked.

"…She said, she wanted to see the two of us standing side by side."

"…That's all?"

"…Yes. That was all."

Eikon turned that answer over.

"…I don't have a reason like that." she said. "I have people who want
to see me. My parents. But I can see them even now, without release.
They're here too."

I couldn't argue with that. It was true. My mother's reason was my
mother's own. Eikon needed a different one. I felt the temptation to
make one up for her, in her place. I held back.

"…Then why are you telling me all this now?" I asked.

Eikon was quiet for a long while at that question.

"…I don't know." she finally said. "But lately this thought keeps
coming back to me. A thought I never used to have. I don't even know
why it started. Whether it's because of the name, or whether it was
always there and I'm only seeing it now."

"…Is that why you started wanting to be alone too?"

Eikon looked at me at that. Her face was a little surprised.

"…How did you know?"

"…You came late, a few days ago. You said you just sat there."

"…I guess that's what it was." she said. "I can't explain what it is,
either."

The sound of water filled the space between us.

I put nothing on top of that silence. I didn't want to be the one
persuading her, this time. I already knew that would be the moment my
own wanting got mixed into hers.

That night I thought of something Speon had said a few days before.
Water changing direction doesn't guarantee it will reach here. But if
the direction never changes, it will never come at all. I was
confirming that, again, in front of Eikon.

"…Do you want to be released?" I finally asked. Very carefully.

Eikon looked at me. For a long time.

"…I don't know." she said. "I can't even tell whether I want it, or
whether I'm just curious."

"…Are those different?"

"…Do you know, Anemos?"

I had no answer to that question. I didn't know either.

"…For me." Eikon said. "This is fine, just as it is. Really. You think
I'm pitiable, don't you, Anemos."

"…No." I said. Honestly. "Maybe I did once. Not anymore."

"…Then why?"

"…Because you saying you're satisfied doesn't sound like a lie to me
anymore."

Eikon laughed a little at that. Her usual laugh, the kind I hadn't
seen in a while.

"…But." she said. "Being satisfied and this being everything — I
guess those turned out to be different things. I thought they were
the same."

I stopped breathing at that sentence.

I set this sentence down more quietly than anything else in this
book. I don't want to attach any explanation to it. Eikon had begun to
tell satisfied and everything apart. What that meant, I didn't know
that day. I still don't fully know now.

"…So are you deciding today?" I asked.

Eikon shook her head.

"…No."

"…Why not?"

"…Not yet." she said. "I want to hold on to this question a little
longer. If I answer too fast, I don't think I'll know if it was really
my own answer."

Hearing that, my brother's voice overlapped in my mind. What's the
difference between not yet and no? My mother had answered him. Not yet
changes, when enough time passes.

"…So is this the end of this conversation?" I asked.

Eikon looked straight at me.

"…No." she said.

She paused a moment.

"…Ask me again later."

I heard that sentence several times over, right there, though it had
only been spoken once. It rang again and again inside me.

I know that sentence isn't an answer. It isn't a conclusion either. Of
everything in this book, I rewrote this moment the most. At first I
wanted to make her say something more definite. But that was my own
greed. Eikon, that day, did not close the door. She didn't open it
either. She simply left it standing ajar.

"…I'll ask." I said. "Whenever."

"…Promise?"

I thought of the finger my brother had hooked with mine. I didn't hold
out a finger this time. I said it in words instead.

"…Promise."

Eikon nodded at that. Not so much a relieved face as a face that had
set something down.

"…One thing." she added. "Don't ask too often."

"…Why?"

"…If you keep asking, I'm afraid it'll stop being my answer and start
being the answer Anemos wants."

I had no reply to that. She understood that boundary more precisely
than I did.

"…Then how often should I ask?" I asked.

"…I don't know." she said. "Don't decide that either, Anemos."

I laughed at that. A strange kind of laugh. I knew, in that moment,
that she was handling this postponement far more exactly than I was.

The sun went fully down.

We sat listening to the sound of water for a while without speaking.
Stars came out one by one. The city's lights and the valley's darkness
divided the space between us.

That night, for the first time, I understood what this book was
trying to say — not through words, but through where we sat. I didn't
persuade Eikon. She didn't persuade me either. We just sat in the same
place, with one unanswered question between us.

Eikon rose first.

"…See you tomorrow." she said. The same goodbye as always.

"…See you tomorrow."

She walked off. I watched her back. Her walk was the same as
yesterday's. But I knew now that the walk wasn't the same as
yesterday's at all.

That night I opened the water flask and drank, thinking. I'd spent
this whole book waiting for the moment someone changed for certain.
The way my mother had. But Eikon didn't come that way. She only left a
door open a crack behind her. That crack looked smaller to me than a
closed door. But I know now: a door left open, however little, is a
completely different thing from a door that is shut.

Writing this chapter, I rewrote the ending more than once. I tried
writing an ending where Eikon chooses release in the end. I didn't
like it. I tried an ending where she refuses to the very last. That
wasn't it either. In the end I decided to leave it exactly as it
happened that day. What this book is trying to say was never a
conclusion — it was simply the fact of a door left open.

(Continued in Chapter 46.)

Chapter 46. Being Able to Die Again

_Previously_ — Eikon asked about release, but did not decide that
day. Instead she said, "Ask me again later." I promised her I would.
She left the door neither open nor closed.

Around that time, we found a house.

Two rooms, deep inside the city. Smaller than the room the Preserve
had lent us. But it was ours. The day my mother first crossed its
threshold, she stopped for a moment and rested her hand on the door
frame.

"…A house of our own." she said. "Was it always allowed to be this
small?"

I laughed at that. The house at the settlement had been small too. My
mother seemed to have forgotten.

We spent our first ordinary days in that house.

My mother woke each morning and drank water, my brother played in the
yard, and I still went out to help Speon. In the evenings the three of
us sat together and shared bread. I still hadn't gotten used to that
ordinariness.

I remember those days exactly, even now. Days where nothing happened
at all. I had not had days like that in seven years. I only half knew,
then, how precious that was.

Then my mother began to cough.

At first it was just a dry cough. I thought it might be her throat,
still unused to things. Speon had said the same — that her throat was
just startled, after so long.

But by the third day, the cough hadn't stopped.

That night my mother ran a fever.

I only found out when I touched her forehead. It was hot. Facing that
heat, I didn't know for a moment what to do.

I had seen sick people many times before. At the settlement, across
seventeen funerals. I had thought I knew what to do in front of
someone sick. But that night, for the sole reason that it was my
mother, I knew nothing at all.

My brother woke.

"…What's wrong with Mom?"

"…She has a fever." I said. "She'll be fine."

Saying it, I wasn't sure I believed it myself.

The next morning I carried my mother on my back to the Preserve.

The Preserve had a physician. A place the city's people rarely needed,
since they never fell ill. I opened that door for the first time.

The physician examined my mother. It didn't take long.

"…This is common after release." he said. "The body's starting to use
functions it hasn't used in a long time, and the immune system hasn't
fully found its footing yet."

"…Is it dangerous?" I asked.

"…Not right now." he said. "But we need to watch it. This kind of
thing can turn suddenly."

I stood there for a moment, hearing that.

Can turn suddenly. I knew that phrase. All seventeen funerals had
begun with it. First a cough, then a fever. The year sickness swept
our settlement had begun exactly that way too.

In that moment, for the first time, it truly hit me. My mother was now
a body that could die again. I had known that sentence many times
over. I'd read it in the paperwork, heard it from the Curator,
repeated it to myself more than once. But until I laid my hand on that
hot forehead, I had not truly known it at all.

I brought my mother back home.

I gave her the medicine the physician had given us. I had her drink
water often. That was all there was to do. That was so little, it
frightened me.

"…Brother." my brother asked. "Will Mom get better?"

"…She'll get better." I said.

"…How do you know?"

I had no answer to that question. I didn't know. I truly didn't know.

From that day I didn't leave my mother's side.

I told Speon I couldn't come out for a few days. He nodded without
much comment.

"…Go on." he said. "The ditch isn't going anywhere."

I turned away without even managing to say thank you properly.

Eikon came by that evening.

She didn't come inside. She just stood leaning against the door frame,
looking into the room.

"…I heard your mother's sick." she said.

"…Yeah."

"…Are you worried?"

I looked at her for a moment at that question. It wasn't a question
she would have asked before.

"…Yeah. A lot."

"…I still don't really know what that's like." Eikon said. "But
looking at your face, Anemos, I don't think it's only a bad thing."

I didn't know how to answer that.

"…Why do you think that?" I asked.

"…Because if it were only bad, I don't think you'd keep staying beside
her like that." she said. "Wouldn't you avoid something that was only
bad?"

I couldn't answer that. I had no room, right then, to judge whether
she was right or wrong.

Eikon asked nothing more. She set down a water flask by the door and
left.

"…For your mother." she said. "I don't really know, but I heard people
bring things like this at times like this."

I looked at that flask for a long while. I didn't ask who had told her
that.

Writing this chapter, I thought I should set one thing down honestly.
During the seven years my mother was preserved, I never once needed to
stay by her side. She wasn't sick, she wasn't in danger, she didn't
disappear. The phrase staying by her side didn't even apply. How do
you stay by someone there is nothing to stay for?

But now, my mother was sick. And I did not leave her side. I set
those two sentences beside each other and looked at them for a long
time. I've decided not to explain them in this book. I'll just leave
them side by side.

The nursing was simple.

Touching her forehead, fetching water, tucking in the blanket. I
repeated those simple tasks several times a day. At night I sat beside
my mother and listened to her breathing.

I barely washed those days. I didn't change my clothes. My brother
cooked the meals instead. Clumsy, but edible. Eating that food, I felt
for the first time how heavy it was that there were only the three of
us in this house. At the settlement, the neighbors would have brought
over porridge. Here, we didn't have neighbors like that yet.

Once, at night, my mother gave a small moan. I shot upright at the
sound. It turned out she'd just been lying awkwardly. I couldn't lie
back down after that and sat up until morning.

There were nights her breathing wasn't even. On those nights I
couldn't sleep either.

I was a counter. On those nights I counted her breaths. One, two,
three. When it was uneven, I counted more often. I'd thought, more
than once, that it was a bad habit. But on those nights, there was
nothing else I could do but count.

On the second day, the Curator came.

He looked into the room for a moment from the doorway and didn't come
in.

"…Is there anything you need." he asked.

"…We're all right." I said. "The physician came by."

The Curator nodded. But he didn't leave.

"…To be honest with you." he said. "Every time I see something like
this, something in me goes complicated."

"…In what way?"

"…I kept your mother preserved for seven years. None of this happened
in that time. No sickness, no sleepless nights like this one. That was
the result of my work going well."

I didn't know how to answer that.

"…But now." he went on. "This feels more right, somehow. I still can't
fully explain why."

He left it at that and turned away. I watched his back for a long
while.

On the third night, the fever rose and fell again. I wrung out the wet
cloth fresh, over and over. My hands cracked. I tried not to count
that either.

On the fourth night, the fever climbed higher.

My mother spoke in delirium. Things about the settlement, about the
water jar, names I couldn't make out. I sat beside her, holding her
hand. Her hand was hot.

My brother watched from the doorway and cried. Trying hard not to make
a sound.

"…Brother." he said. "I'm scared."

"…Me too." I said. Honestly, for the first time.

I thought about that moment for a long time afterward. I had played
the adult in front of my brother, all this time. I know, it'll be
fine, words like that. That night, those words wouldn't come. I was
scared too. I didn't hide it. Strangely, that seemed to put my brother
more at ease.

We both slept beside my mother that night. Or more precisely, we
didn't sleep. I counted my mother's breaths until dawn. My brother
fell asleep against my shoulder at some point.

By morning the fever had come down a little.

The physician came and examined her again.

"…I think she's past the worst of it." he said. "But we'll need to
watch her a few more days."

I nearly sank to the floor at that. I only realized then how hard it
had been just to stay standing.

Speon came by that afternoon.

He brought a water flask and dry bread. He set them down without a
word and turned to go, and I stopped him.

"…Speon."

"…Yeah."

"…Is this how it really is? Becoming a body that's alive again."

Speon looked at me for a moment.

"…Yeah." he said. "This is how it is."

"…I'm scared. I didn't know it would be like this."

"…If you'd known." he asked. "Do you think you wouldn't have done it?"

I couldn't answer that question right away.

"…No." I finally said. "I think I'd have done it anyway."

"…Why."

"…I don't know. It's just — having something to watch over gave me
something else, alongside the fear. Something different."

Speon nodded at that.

"…Water's the same." he said. "Once you cut a channel, you also get
the worry of it overflowing. When there's no water, there's no such
worry either. But is that worry a bad thing? I don't think so."

I held on to that for a long time. I'd heard this book's question
asked in different words, more than once by then. Is what's preserved
different from what's alive. I'd tried to find the answer through
argument. But that answer came, that night, not through argument, but
through my hand — laid on my mother's hot forehead.

There is nothing to watch over beside a preserved person. There is
something to watch over beside a living person. Having something to
watch over means being afraid. And I know this too — that without that
fear, I would have had no reason to sit this long beside my mother.

On the fifth day, my mother opened her eyes. Her mind was clear.

"…Anemos." she said. Her voice was weak.

"…Yes."

"…How many days was I like that."

"…Four days."

My mother closed her eyes, then opened them again.

"…Were you here the whole time?"

"…Yes."

"…Did you not sleep at all?"

"…I slept. A little."

My mother's face didn't believe that. In truth I had almost no memory
of really sleeping across those four days.

"…Why." she asked. "You're busy. You have the ditch to dig."

I laughed at that question. A strange laugh.

"…I didn't know back then." I said. "When you were preserved. There
was nothing to watch over, so I didn't need to. But now there's a
reason to watch over you."

My mother listened quietly to that. Her face understood something.

"…So that's what being able to die again means." she said, almost to
herself.

"…Yes."

"…And." she went on. "You staying by my side like this — that's part
of it too, isn't it."

I had no answer to that. It was true.

My brother heard that and ran into the room.

"…Mom!"

"…Hey." my mother smiled. A weak smile, but a real one. "Mom's okay."

My brother took my mother's hand. Didn't let go.

"…I was scared."

"…Me too." my mother said. "But I'm not scared now."

That afternoon, Eikon came again. This time she stepped a few paces
inside the door.

"…Are you all right?" she asked my mother directly.

"…I'm all right." my mother said. "Thank you for coming."

Eikon looked a little flustered at that.

"…I didn't really do anything."

"…You brought the water flask." my mother said. "Anemos told me."

Eikon had no answer to that. I saw something unfamiliar cross her
face. The face of someone who wasn't used to being thanked.

"…Was that really that big a thing?" she asked. Her face was genuinely
curious.

"…It was a big thing." my mother said.

Eikon turned that over for a long while, then left without another
word. Watching her walk away, I knew that another question had come to
settle inside her.

I wanted to leave that as the last line of this chapter. But I'll set
down one more thing after it. I only fell asleep that night. Beside my
mother, with my brother next to me. For the first time while falling
asleep, I didn't count. Not because there was nothing to count. That
night simply wasn't heavy, even without counting.

I wrote this chapter more carefully than any other in this book. I
didn't want to turn it into a story where being sick is a good thing.
My mother was sick, and that was bad. But alongside that badness, there
was something that hadn't been there before. I set both of those down
here.

I stayed by my mother's side for several more days after that. The
fever never rose again. I didn't dig the ditch once in all that time.
Speon never once asked about it. I learned, in my body, that a channel
growing a little slower doesn't mean it will run dry.

(Continued in Chapter 47.)

Chapter 47. How Long Have You Been Here

_Previously_ — My mother fell ill. I didn't leave her side for four
days. While she was preserved there had been no reason to watch over
her. Now there was. Hearing that, my mother smiled quietly. "So
that's part of it too."

After my mother recovered, I spent a few more days at home.

Only once I'd confirmed her body had fully come back did I find room
to think about work outside again. My brother had started going to
something like a school. A new place had opened in the city for living
children, they said. My brother was delighted at the news.

Then, one morning, I went back down into the valley.

There was nothing special about that morning. I woke as usual, my
mother made breakfast as usual, my brother put on his clothes to go to
the new place.

I was packing the water flask when my hand stopped, without my
meaning it to. Up until that instant I'd had no plan to go back to the
valley. But with the flask already in my hand, I realized my heart had
already leaned that way.

"…Not going to the ditch today?" my mother asked.

"…I'm going to go somewhere for a while."

"…Where."

I hesitated for a moment at that question.

"…The valley."

My mother's hand stopped at that. She didn't ask anything more.

"…Be back before evening." she said.

"…Okay."

It's hard to say exactly why.

In the four days my mother was sick, I had come close to the word
dying again. Eikon had left me waiting at a door half open. My brother
kept drawing his lines on the wall. Carrying all of that, I found
myself, without quite meaning to, thinking of that place again.

The place where the bones had been. The place where he was. I'd
thought, once I left that valley, that I would never go back. But one
morning, I simply found myself walking that way.

I went without telling Speon.

On the way I passed the channel. The water was pushing past the edge
of the city now. I followed the flow with my eyes and confirmed again
that it was headed toward the valley. It hadn't reached there yet. But
the direction was clear.

The path down the cliff was the same as before. My body remembered
where to set its feet. I found that strange — I'd only walked it once.

Reaching the bottom, the dry smell came. The same smell as before.
Earth, old wood, and another smell hard to name. I stopped walking for
a moment, taking it in. The scattered shoes, the faded scraps of
cloth, the small bones — all of it was still there. Nothing had been
moved.

I've decided not to describe this valley again in detail. I already
did that once. Writing it again, I felt, would blur the weight of the
first time. But I'll set down one thing. Walking among those remains,
I wasn't afraid, unlike before. Before, this valley had been a strange
land of death. This time it felt like walking to meet a familiar
face.

He was there.

Not in the same spot as before. A little further in, among the dry
trees. He was crouched beside the bones. I couldn't see what he was
doing from a distance.

I walked toward him. Dry things crumbled underfoot with every step.

Up close, he was simply sitting there.

He held nothing in his hands. He wasn't touching the bones either. He
was just sitting beside them, watching the wind carry the soil, little
by little.

"…Back again." he said. Without turning to look at me.

"…Yes."

I sat down beside him.

We said nothing for a while. I went back over everything that had
happened, in order. My mother's release, her laugh, and then the
fever. Eikon's name, and her silence. The lines on my brother's wall.

He didn't hurry that silence.

The wind blew once more. I thought of how that wind was the same word
as my name. The thing that cannot be kept. I thought that wind must
have been carrying this valley's soil, little by little, for a very
long time. And beside it, this man must have been sitting, unmoved,
through all that time.

"…The bones here." I spoke first. "There seem to be fewer than
before."

"…Is that so."

"…Yes. Or am I imagining it."

"…You're not imagining it." he said. "When the water gets closer, the
soil moves. When it moves, the traces move with it."

I thought of the channel again at that. It hadn't reached here yet,
and already something was changing.

"…You asked me something before." I said, speaking first. "Whether you
think these can live again."

"…I did."

"…I said I didn't know."

"…That's right. That was the right answer."

"…Now." I said. "I still don't know. But I think I know something."

He looked at me for the first time, hearing that.

"…What do you know." he asked.

"…That being alive means being afraid." I said. "My mother was sick.
For four days. Those four days were the most afraid I've ever been.
But those four days were more time spent beside my mother than all
the seven years I spent visiting her."

He listened to that quietly.

"…I still don't know what that means." I went on. "But I have the
feeling of knowing something."

"…That's enough." he said. "You don't have to know everything to
know something."

I decided to bring out a question I'd been holding onto for a long
time.

I didn't know exactly when I'd started carrying it. Probably from the
day I first came down into this valley.

"…Can I ask you something." I said.

"…Go ahead."

"…How long have you been here?"

He looked at me.

And laughed.

I'll set down that laugh only briefly. I already knew he was a man
who never got angry. But even so, that laugh caught me by surprise. I
felt something very old inside it. I didn't know what.

"…That's an interesting question." he said.

"…I didn't ask it to be interesting."

"…I know."

"…You're not going to answer?" I asked.

"…No." he said. Briefly.

"…Why not?"

"…Some answers." he said, "outlast the one who hears them. I think
this one would be too heavy for you."

I started to argue with that, and stopped. I couldn't be sure he was
wrong.

"…Then just one thing." I said. "Were you here before I came?"

"…That's the same question." he said. "Just dressed in different
clothes."

I laughed at that. Oddly, I wasn't angry.

"…You're right." I admitted. "But I'm really asking because I'm
curious."

"…I know you're curious." he said. "But being curious doesn't mean
you get an answer."

"…Then how about this." I asked again. "Has anyone else come down
here and asked you something like this? Besides me?"

He looked steadily at me for a moment at that question.

"…There's always been someone asking something like it." he said.

"…What did you tell them?"

"…The same thing I'm telling you now."

I laughed at that answer. I felt like I should be angry, but oddly, I
wasn't.

"…That seems a little unfair." I said.

"…What does."

"…That I'm not the only one getting a special answer."

He laughed out loud at that, for the first time. A short laugh.

"…Wouldn't it be more unfair." he said, "to answer everyone the same
way?"

I turned that over for a long while. It sounded right, and it sounded
wrong, both at once.

We fell quiet again.

The wind blew. Dry soil swirled low across the ground. Watching that
swirl, I thought it was exactly the same sight I'd once seen here
before.

Strangely, I was comfortable inside that silence that day. I'd always
felt frustrated, before, not getting an answer. The Curator, Speon,
this man — all of them, at some point, stopped answering. But that
day, it didn't frustrate me.

"…Can I ask one more thing." I said.

"…Go ahead."

"…Are you going to stay here? Even after the water reaches this far?"

He seemed to think a little longer over that question.

"…I stay where I need to be." he said. "That doesn't change, whether
the water comes or not."

"…And that's here?"

"…For now." he said.

I felt that answer was different from the others. It wasn't a
complete evasion. But it wasn't a complete answer either.

I couldn't pin down that difference precisely, then. I still can't,
even now. But I had the sense that he'd given a little more of himself
away in that question than in the others, that day.

The sun was sinking little by little. The shadows against the valley
wall were growing long.

"…I should get going." I said.

"…All right."

I stood, then looked back at him once more.

"…Can I come again?"

"…No need to ask." he said. "You're going to come again anyway."

I stopped at that.

"…How do you know that?"

He didn't answer. He just laughed. The same laugh as before.

I thought about that laugh the whole way up. I hadn't gotten an
answer that day either. When he'd started being here, whether he'd
still be here — I left the valley without knowing any of it for
certain.

But writing this question down for the third time in this book, I
came to know one thing. Some questions aren't meant to get an answer.
The act of asking itself carries the person holding onto the question
somewhere. That day, in that valley, I climbed out having received not
an answer, but that carrying.

Climbing the cliff, I looked back once.

He was still there. Crouched beside the bones, watching the wind carry
the soil. The same posture as before I'd come.

I held that sight in my eyes for a long while. Then I started walking
again.

On the way back into the city I ran into Speon.

He was adjusting the direction of the channel at its far end. Seeing
me, he set down his shovel.

"…Where'd you go." he asked.

"…The valley."

Speon's hand paused at that.

"…Did you meet him?"

I was surprised by that question. I had never told Speon much of
anything about him.

"…Do you know him?"

"…Know is a stretch." Speon said. "I've crossed paths with him a few
times. Well-diggers run into someone like that, oddly often."

"…What did you two talk about?"

"…Not much." Speon said. "Just a few nods of the head."

"…I asked him today." I said. "How long he'd been there."

"…What did he say?"

"…He just laughed and didn't answer."

Speon nodded at that. His face wasn't surprised.

"…Figured as much." he said.

"…How did you know?"

"…Because I asked him too, once." he said. "A very long time ago."

My eyes went wide at that.

"…What did he say?"

Speon swung the shovel back onto his shoulder.

"…He laughed." he said. "The same as he did with you."

I couldn't say anything for a while, hearing that answer. I had
thought, up till then, that I was the only person to have ever met him
in that valley. I wasn't. I was only the most recent one.

"…Weren't you angry?" I asked. "That he wouldn't answer?"

"…At first, yeah." Speon said. "But I know now. For some questions,
it matters more to keep asking than to get an answer."

I held on to that for a long time.

He picked the shovel back up and went back to the channel. I watched
his back for a moment, then turned my steps toward home. The sun
hadn't set yet. I had kept my promise to be back before evening.

I came home to find my mother making dinner. My brother was excitedly
listing off everything he'd learned at the new place.

"…Where'd you go today, Brother?" my brother asked.

"…The valley."

"…What's there?"

I thought about that question for a moment. Bones, soil, and him. I
didn't know where to begin.

"…There's a man there." I said. "A strange man."

"…Strange how?"

"…He doesn't really answer when you ask him things."

My brother laughed at that.

"…You do that too. To me."

I had no comeback to that. My mother was laughing quietly beside him.

After dinner, I sat alone in my room and went back over what had
happened that day.

How long have you been here. I'm writing that question down for the
third time now. There is still no answer. But I know something now. I
wasn't the only one who ever carried this question down into that
valley. Speon had, and maybe someone else too, carried the same
question and sat in that same place, and come back up. And none of
them, I think, ever got an answer either.

Strangely, that fact comforted me. That not receiving an answer
wasn't something that belonged to me alone. That night I fell asleep
thinking of that laugh again.

I still don't know how long he's been there. Even now, finishing this
book, I don't know. I've stopped finding that strange. Some things can
be kept close without being known. I learned that, that day, and
walked out of the valley.

(Continued in Chapter 48.)

Chapter 48. The Breath

_Previously_ — I went down into the valley and asked him, "…How long
have you been here?" He only laughed and gave no answer. Speon said
he had once asked the same question and gotten the same answer. That
night I fell asleep having learned that some things can be kept
close without being known.

It was about ten days later that the channel reached the edge of the
city.

I was digging soil with Speon that day, same as always. But something
was different that day. The dampness at the tip of the shovel ran
deeper than it had yesterday.

"…We're almost there." Speon said.

"…Where."

"…The edge of the cliff." he said. "Past that is the valley."

I set down my shovel at that and walked to the edge.

The place where the city ended. Beyond it, the ground dropped away. I
thought back to the day, months before, when I'd first stood at this
spot and looked down. I'd known nothing then. Now I knew who was down
there.

The channel had come to a brief stop at that edge. One last mound of
earth still stood in its way.

"…So all we have to do now is break that open?" I asked.

"…Yeah." Speon said. "But we're not breaking it open today."

"…Why not?"

"…To give a little time to get ready." he said. "For the water, and
for people too."

That evening I brought Speon something I'd been building up to say
for a long time.

"…Speon." I said. "Will you teach me how to dig wells. Properly."

Speon stopped putting away his shovels and looked at me.

"…Isn't what you've learned so far enough?"

"…I've only ever helped from the side. I want to learn it properly.
Start to finish."

He looked at me for a long while. It wasn't a judging look. It was a
weighing one.

"…Why." he asked.

I couldn't answer that right away.

"…I don't know." I finally said. "But I want to keep doing this. When
one city's done, I want to go to another. There might be no water
there either."

Speon smiled a little at that.

"…You said you learned from that old man, at fourteen."

"…Yes."

"…What did he tell you?"

"…That you don't find water. You make it a way."

"…I heard something similar myself." Speon said. "From someone else."

"…Who?"

"…That's an old story." he said. "I learned it when I was about your
age too. That person barely spoke either. But once, just once, he told
me this. Water finds its own way even where no one's watching. Whether
a person's looking or not, water just goes to the low ground."

I turned that over for a long while.

"…Where is that person now?"

"…He stopped." Speon said, briefly. "A long time ago."

I didn't ask anything further at that. I thought I understood why he
didn't say the name.

"…That's how the craft gets handed down." Speon said. "The one who
teaches disappears someday, but the teaching doesn't. You got it from
that old man, and I got it from someone else. And now I'm giving it to
you. Someday you'll give it to someone too."

"…Will I be able to teach it?"

"…Don't know." he said. "But that's not something to worry about now.
For now, just learn."

"…Still don't fully know what that saying means?" he asked.

"…I know a little." I said. "But not all of it."

"…Good." Speon said. "If you knew all of it, I wouldn't have anything
left to teach you."

He handed me a shovel. A little longer than the one I'd been using.

"…Let's do this properly, starting tomorrow." he said. "This one was
mine, a long time back."

I took the shovel in my hands. The handle was worn smooth. The shape
of a hand that had used it for a long time was still pressed into it.

Taking that shovel, I thought back to the moment the old man had once
